**H4. Chain per-link downgrade is not compared against link max when no expectation is given**  
`src/computetest/diagnostics.py` · diagnostics.py:256-263 (diagnose_chain link loop) · _correctness_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When expected_speed/expected_width are None (the CLI default for the `chain` command), a link that trained below its maximum speed/width and stayed there is reported 'pass' unless the LBMS/LABS latch happened to fire during the window — but a link that came up degraded before the test and never changed leaves those latches clear, so the downgrade is missed. The single-device diagnose path catches exactly this case via linkstate comparison against max_speed/max_width.  
- **Evidence:** downgraded = bool((expected_speed and ls.speed < expected_speed) or (expected_width and ls.width < expected_width) or bw). With expected_speed=None and expected_width=None, both expectation terms are falsy and only `bw` (the LBMS/LABS change latch) remains. read_link_status returns no max_speed/max_width, so there is no fallback to max. CLI: `--expected-speed`/`--expected-width` default=None (cli.py:174-175); test_ocpdiag.py calls diagnose_chain(... max_seconds=1.0) with no expectation, so the gap is untested.  
- **Fix:** Read the downstream port's device (backend.get_device(li.downstream_bdf)) and fall back to max_link_speed/max_link_width when expected_* is None, matching linkstate.LinkHealth.speed_degraded/width_degraded; or have read_link_status expose max and compare against it.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and concretely reproduced. In diagnostics.py:259-260, `downgraded = bool((expected_speed and ls.speed < expected_speed) or (expected_width and ls.width < expected_width) or bw)`. The `chain` CLI defaults `--expected-speed`/`--expected-width` to None (cli.py:174-175, passed through at cli.py:285-286), so both expectation terms are falsy and only `bw` (the LBMS/LABS latch) remains. `read_link_status` returns a `LinkStatus` (backend.py:103-113) that carries NO max_speed/max_width — confirmed in both the MockBackend (backend.py:526-543) and the real-hw backend (backend.py:359-367) — so there is no fallback to max in the chain loop.

The single-device path is asymmetric and DOES catch this: linkstate.py:56/63 uses `target = self.expected_speed or self.max_speed` (and likewise width), flagging speed_degraded/width_degraded against max even with no expectation.

Reproduction (Mock, switch topology, downstream port 03:00.0 statically trained Gen3 with max_link_speed=4, no LBMS/LABS injected, diagnose_chain with no expectation): chain status = "pass", link reported "Gen3 x16 -> pass"; the single-device diagnose() on the same port returns status "fail" with reason "speed degraded (Gen3)". This is a real wrong-PASS on hardware: a link that came up degraded before the test and never changed leaves LBMS/LABS clear (those latch only on changes after the window's clear_link_bw_status), so the chain misses it. The condition (a marginal lane/connector forcing a low train rate that stays put) is exactly what a chain validation should catch on real hardware where the mock can't.

Not refuted by any guard/test: test_chain.py always passes expected_speed=4/expected_width=16 (via _diag), and the only no-expectation calls (test_ocpdiag.py:438,466 with max_seconds=1.0) assert emitter structure, not the downgrade verdict — so the gap is genuinely untested. Not intentional per conventions: this is a missed FAIL, the opposite of the honesty discipline (it silently PASSes a degraded link rather than refusing to claim a pass). 

Severity high (not critical): it is a wrong PASS verdict, but only on the no-expectation invocation of the `chain` command, and the parallel single-device diagnose path correctly catches the same condition; running chain with expectations (the recommended mode) works correctly. Suggested fix is sound: read backend.get_device(li.downstream_bdf) and fall back to max_link_speed/max_link_width when expected_* is None, mirroring linkstate.LinkHealth.speed_degraded/width_degraded.  

**H5. LBMS/LABS bandwidth-change latch is read at the endpoint, where the spec defines those bits as zero**  
`src/computetest/linkstate.py` · linkstate.py:114-127 (check_link watch loop); reached via diagnostics.py:99 diagnose() passing the endpoint bdf · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** On real hardware, check_link's bw_changed will never latch for an Endpoint (or Switch Upstream Port) device, so a transient speed/width downgrade that recovered before the final snapshot is silently missed — defeating the module's stated purpose.  
- **Evidence:** check_link does `backend.clear_link_bw_status(bdf)` then `ls = backend.read_link_status(bdf)` / `if ls.bw_changed or ls.autonomous_bw: health.bw_changed = True` on the *passed* bdf. diagnose() calls `linkstate.check_link(backend, bdf, ...)` with the device's own BDF, which for every sample-board device is PORT_ENDPOINT (verified: all six read_port_type()==0). Per PCIe Base Spec, Link Bandwidth Management Status (bit 14, LNKSTA_LBMS) and Link Autonomous Bandwidth Status (bit 15, LNKSTA_LABS) are only implemented on Downstream Ports that are Link Bandwidth Notification Capable; they are RsvdZ on Endpoints and Upstream Ports. Notably the sibling chain path in diagnostics.py:254-257 reads link status at `li.downstream_bdf` precisely 'read once at the downstream port' — proving the codebase knows this, yet check_link does not.  
- **Fix:** In check_link (or diagnose), resolve the link-owning downstream port for the target link via backend.link_chain()/read_port_type() and read_link_status()/clear_link_bw_status() against that downstream BDF when computing bw_changed (mirror diagnostics.py's downstream_bdf usage). Alternatively, gate bw_changed on read_port_type(bdf) being a DOWNSTREAM_PORT and document that endpoint-only callers cannot observe LBMS/LABS.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed real defect on the real-hardware path. PCIe Base Spec defines LBMS (Link Status bit 14) and LABS (bit 15) as "not applicable and reserved" for Endpoints and Upstream Ports; they are implemented only on Bandwidth-Notification-Capable Downstream Ports (Root / Switch-Downstream). The spec claim is accurate. RealBackend.read_link_status (backend.py:364-367) decodes bits 14/15 from the *passed* bdf's own config space, and check_link (linkstate.py:115/119/126) arms (clear_link_bw_status) and reads them against whatever bdf it is handed. diagnose() (diagnostics.py:99) passes the device's own bdf, and harness.py:91-92 invokes diagnose per matched endpoint device (GPUs/NVMe, all PORT_ENDPOINT). On real hardware those bits are RsvdZ on an endpoint, so check_link.bw_changed can never latch there — a transient speed/width downgrade that recovered before the final snapshot is silently missed, defeating the module's stated purpose (linkstate.py:3-5). The codebase already knows the correct pattern: topology.py:153 documents downstream_bdf as the port that "owns the link + LBMS/LABS," topology.py:150-152 says LBMS/LABS is read once at the downstream port, and diagnose_chain (diagnostics.py:236, 257) correctly clears/reads bw status at li.downstream_bdf. So the chain path is right while the per-device diagnose/check_link path is wrong. Not refuted by the unit test (test_check_link_watch_catches_downtrain_and_bw_latch passes only because MockBackend.read_link_status at backend.py:542-543 returns _bw_latched regardless of port_type — a mock infidelity that masks the real-hw bug, exactly the high-value category per conventions). Downgraded from critical to high (not upgraded): the bug does not guarantee a wrong PASS because (1) the diagnose_chain path catches downgrades correctly, and (2) check_link's min_speed polling still flags a dip if any poll lands during it; only the recovered-and-not-sampled transient is lost on the per-device path. That makes the LBMS/LABS feature non-functional on real endpoints (wrong value / dead feature on a real-hw path) = high. Suggested fix is sound: resolve the link-owning downstream port via link_chain()/read_port_type() and read/clear at that BDF (mirror diagnose_chain), or gate bw_changed on read_port_type(bdf) being a DOWNSTREAM_PORT and document the limitation.  

**H6. load_config crashes on the documented `bdf` match key (ValueError)**  
`src/computetest/topology.py` · topology.py:87-92 (load_config / to_int) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Any pcie_devices entry whose `match` contains a `bdf` key makes load_config raise ValueError, so the entire test plan fails to load even though `bdf` is an explicitly documented match criterion.  
- **Evidence:** Line 92 applies `to_int` to EVERY match value indiscriminately: `match = {k: to_int(v) for k, v in d.get('match', {}).items()}`, and `to_int` does `int(v, 0) if isinstance(v, str) else v` (line 88). For a bdf string `int('0000:03:00.0', 0)` raises `ValueError: invalid literal for int() with base 0`. Confirmed by running load_config({'pcie_devices':[{'name':'GPU','match':{'bdf':'0000:03:00.0'}}]}) -> RAISES ValueError. Yet matches() supports bdf (line 47 `if 'bdf' in m and dev.bdf != m['bdf']`), the module docstring lists `bdf` as a valid match (line 39 `# any of: bdf, vendor_id, device_id, class_code`), and configs/example_topology.yaml advertises it twice (lines 10 and 37-38: 'matched by ... bdf' / 'or an exact bdf'). No test passes a bdf through load_config; both bdf tests construct DeviceExpectation directly (test_topology_edges.py:15,23), so the gap is uncovered.  
- **Fix:** Coerce only the numeric match keys, not bdf, e.g. `match = {k: (to_int(v) if k != 'bdf' else v) for k, v in d.get('match', {}).items()}` (or whitelist {'vendor_id','device_id','class_code'} for to_int). Add a load_config test that round-trips a bdf match.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced the defect directly: load_config({'pcie_devices':[{'name':'GPU','match':{'bdf':'0000:03:00.0'}}]}) raises ValueError: invalid literal for int() with base 0: '0000:03:00.0' at topology.py:92, because to_int (line 88) does int(v,0) on every string match value, including bdf. The bdf key is an explicitly documented match criterion: module docstring line 39 ('any of: bdf, vendor_id, device_id, class_code'), and configs/example_topology.yaml lines 10 and 38 advertise matching by 'an exact bdf'. matches() (line 47) correctly compares dev.bdf to the raw string m['bdf'] — I confirmed it returns True for a raw bdf string — so the feature would work end-to-end if to_int didn't mangle it; the bug is purely in the loader coercion. Coverage gap is real: the only bdf tests (test_topology_edges.py:15,21-23) construct DeviceExpectation directly, bypassing load_config, and no load_config test passes a bdf, so this path is uncovered (gates still pass). Not intentional: this is not honesty-discipline (it doesn't raise to avoid faking a pass — it crashes on valid documented input), not a real-hw pragma path, and not mock fidelity. The shipped example configs only use vendor_id/class_code in real entries, which is why the example itself loads, masking the bug. Severity high (not critical): it is not a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict, but it is a hard crash that fails the entire test-plan load on a documented config input. Suggested fix (coerce only numeric keys / leave bdf as a string, plus a round-trip load_config bdf test) is correct.  

**H7. One raising functional check aborts the whole test plan on real hardware**  
`src/computetest/harness.py` · src/computetest/harness.py:103-112 (functional_checks loop in run_test_plan) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Each check_* call is made with no exception isolation, so a single RuntimeError/CalledProcessError on real hardware kills run_test_plan and discards the in-memory report plus every not-yet-run check.  
- **Evidence:** for dev in fc.get("nvme", []): add_health("nvme", dev, "smart", nvme.check_nvme(dev)) ... for idx in fc.get("gpus", []): add_health("gpu", str(idx), "health", gpu.check_gpu(idx)) -- and the real-hw paths these reach raise hard, e.g. gpu._real_check_gpu: 'if not shutil.which("nvidia-smi"): raise RuntimeError("nvidia-smi not found")', nvme.check_nvme: 'if not shutil.which("nvme"): raise RuntimeError(...)' and subprocess.run(..., check=True) (CalledProcessError on nonzero exit), plus ethernet._validate_iface(iface) can raise before the docstring even runs. There is NO try/except around any add_health call, unlike diagnostics._try_margin which explicitly catches so 'the rest of the report must stand'.  
- **Fix:** Wrap each per-target check (and ideally each step-2/step-4 diagnose call) in try/except, recording a skip record (status='skip', message=f'{type(e).__name__}: {e}') instead of propagating, mirroring _try_margin. One bad device/handle must not erase the whole station run.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced from the code. In src/computetest/harness.py:102-112, the Layer-2 functional_checks loop calls nvme.check_nvme / gpu.check_gpu / gmsl.check_gmsl / ethernet.check_ethernet / ethernet.check_can directly inside add_health with NO try/except. The cited raises are real: gpu._real_check_gpu raises RuntimeError("nvidia-smi not found") (gpu.py:124); nvme.check_nvme raises RuntimeError on missing nvme-cli (health.py:125) and uses subprocess.run(..., check=True) which raises CalledProcessError on any nonzero device exit (health.py:126-132); ethernet._validate_iface raises ValueError before the mock branch even runs (ethernet.py:78,198 -> 23-25); real ethernet/can paths raise RuntimeError/CalledProcessError. So one degraded-but-enumerable device aborts run_test_plan and every remaining nvme/gpu/gmsl/ethernet/can check plus all step-4 chain diagnostics never run. The asymmetry is the clincher: diagnostics._try_margin (diagnostics.py:108-123) wraps the margining call in try/except specifically so "the rest of the report must stand" — the author knows the pattern but did not apply it to Layer 2, making this an oversight, not intentional design. No test refutes it: tests/test_topology_harness.py runs only against MockBackend (where checks never raise) and has no isolation/partial-survival test.

Two overstatements in the finding, which I downgrade but do not negate the defect: (1) "discards the in-memory report" — records added before the raise are committed to SQLite incrementally (results.py:59 commits per record), so prior records survive on disk when a store is passed; only the in-memory TestReport and not-yet-run checks are lost. (2) "real-hw paths raise hard" leading to a traceback — cli.main() (cli.py:422-468) does catch RuntimeError/ValueError and map to exit codes, so most raises yield a clean error + EXIT_IO/EXIT_USAGE rather than a traceback; HOWEVER subprocess.CalledProcessError is NOT a RuntimeError (verified MRO: SubprocessError->Exception), so a nonzero nvme/nvidia-smi exit escapes all of main()'s handlers and produces an uncaught Python traceback with the wrong exit code — exactly the failure mode _try_margin exists to prevent.

Severity high (not downgraded): it cannot produce a wrong PASS/FAIL on the item actually under test, so it is not critical, but on an unattended station one bad device silently drops all subsequent device-health and whole-chain coverage (incomplete coverage that can masquerade as a shorter clean run) and the CalledProcessError path crashes the CLI with a bad verdict. Suggested fix is correct: wrap each per-target check (and the step-2/step-4 diagnose calls) in try/except recording a skip TestRecord, mirroring _try_margin, so one bad device/handle cannot erase the station run.  

**H8. record() stores non-finite floats as invalid JSON (Infinity/NaN), breaking the dashboard and any strict JSON consumer**  
`src/computetest/results.py` · results.py:52-59 (record); round-trips out via recent() at results.py:69-78 · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When a real DUT skips a BERT (unknown link rate or no AER source), TestRecord.measured contains ber_upper_bound = float('inf'); json.dumps(rec.measured) writes the bare token `Infinity` into the DB, which is not valid JSON per RFC 8259. The Python recent() path tolerates it, but the FastAPI dashboard re-emits it and the browser's JSON.parse rejects it, breaking the /api/results view exactly for the failure/skip cases an operator most needs to see.  
- **Evidence:** results.py: `json.dumps(rec.measured)` (no allow_nan=False / default=). bert.py:204/214/356/366 build verdicts with `float("inf"), float("inf")` for skip; BertResult.to_dict() at bert.py:139 emits `"ber_upper_bound": self.verdict.ber_upper` (= inf), which flows into PcieDiagnostic.to_dict() -> TestRecord.measured. Verified: json.dumps({'ber': float('inf')}) -> '{"ber": Infinity}'; a strict reparse with parse_constant raising fails, and browser JSON.parse('Infinity') throws SyntaxError. dashboard/app.py:71-75 returns s.recent(limit) whose 'measured' carries the reloaded inf.  
- **Fix:** Sanitize before persisting: store via `json.dumps(rec.measured, allow_nan=False)` and pre-convert non-finite floats to null or a sentinel string (e.g. walk the dict replacing math.isinf/isnan with None or 'inf'/'nan'), OR have BertResult.to_dict() serialize ber_upper_bound=inf as None/"inf". This keeps the stored JSON RFC-8259-conformant so every consumer (dashboard, ocp-diag, external tools) parses it.  
- **Verifier:** CONFIRMED defect, with one mechanical correction to the evidence. The core claim holds: results.py:58 does `json.dumps(rec.measured)` with no `allow_nan=False`, so a real-DUT skip (unknown link rate / no AER) persists the bare `Infinity` token, which is invalid JSON per RFC 8259. I reproduced the full chain: bert.py:204/214 build skip verdicts with float("inf"); bert.py:139 to_dict() emits ber_upper_bound=inf; diagnostics.py:85 nests it into PcieDiagnostic.to_dict(); harness.py:97-98 stores it as TestRecord.measured; record() writes '{"ber_upper_bound": Infinity, ...}' to the DB. A strict RFC-8259 consumer (Go/Rust serde/jq/browser JSON.parse) rejects it. No guard (no allow_nan/isinf walk) and no test covers this; recent() silently tolerates it because Python json.loads accepts Infinity.

CORRECTION to the finding's stated mechanism: it claims the dashboard "re-emits it and the browser's JSON.parse rejects it." That is wrong. I exercised the actual /api/results endpoint via Starlette TestClient: Starlette JSONResponse.render() uses json.dumps(..., allow_nan=False) and raises ValueError ("Out of range float values are not JSON compliant: inf") SERVER-SIDE, returning HTTP 500 — the browser never gets a body to parse. (Also note dashboard/app.py:122-123 doesn't even read x.measured, but the 500 still fires because the whole recent() payload, including measured, is serialized.) So the failure is a server-side 500, not a client JSON.parse SyntaxError — arguably worse for the operator. The endpoint-breaking outcome the finding describes is real.

Not intentional, not a pragma real-hw exemption (it's a logic/serialization bug that fires on real-hw skip records), not handled by any guard or test, and not an honesty-discipline case. Severity high is correct: it breaks the /api/results dashboard view and any strict external JSON consumer for exactly the skip/fail records an operator most needs, though it does not flip a PASS/FAIL verdict (so not critical). Suggested fix (serialize non-finite floats as null/sentinel, or json.dumps(allow_nan=False) with pre-conversion, or BertResult.to_dict() emitting None for inf) is sound. The dashboard module lives at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/dashboard/app.py (not the src/computetest/dashboard/app.py path cited in the finding).  

**H9. Ethernet role reports the CONFIGURED preference, not the NEGOTIATED master/slave status**  
`src/computetest/ethernet.py` · ethernet.py:102-104 (check_ethernet real-hw path) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** ethtool prints `master-slave cfg:` (a preference like 'master preferred') BEFORE `master-slave status:` (the actual negotiated role). `re.search` returns the first match, so `role` reflects the configured preference. When cfg and status disagree (the exact misconfiguration an automotive 1000BASE-T1 link check exists to catch), the reported role is wrong.  
- **Evidence:** mlb = re.search(r"master-slave (?:cfg|status):\s*(\S+)", et, re.I) — verified: input with 'master-slave cfg: slave preferred' then 'master-slave status: master' returns role='slave' (took cfg, not status). The good corpus has cfg and status both 'master', so the test never distinguishes them.  
- **Fix:** Match the status line specifically and prefer it: `re.search(r"master-slave status:\s*(\S+)", et, re.I)` (fall back to cfg only if status is absent). Add a corpus/test case where cfg != status to lock the negotiated value.  
- **Verifier:** CONFIRMED. ethernet.py:102 `re.search(r"master-slave (?:cfg|status):\s*(\S+)", et, re.I)` returns the FIRST match. Real Linux ethtool emits `master-slave cfg:` (configured preference) before `master-slave status:` (negotiated role) — the bundled corpus confirms this ordering (corpus/ethtool/6.7/bcm89xx-good/link.txt lines 12-13: cfg on 12, status on 13). I reproduced empirically: feeding `master-slave cfg: slave preferred` + `master-slave status: master` yields role='slave' (the cfg), while a status-targeted regex yields the correct 'master'. I also confirmed that even on the GOOD corpus the regex matches the `cfg` line, so the parser is structurally reading cfg, not status — the test only passes because both happen to be 'master'.

This is a genuine misparse on the `# pragma: no cover - real-hw path` (ethernet.py:94 branch), exactly the kind of parsing bug the project conventions say is high-value because mocks cannot catch it. The role field exists specifically for automotive 1000BASE-T1 master/slave timing (see field comment ethernet.py:36 and tsn/phy.py:28-29 "a mismatched pair never links, so the role is asserted") — so when cfg≠status (a real misconfiguration symptom), the tool reports the wrong negotiated role to the operator.

Test gap is real: both ETH_CASES corpus files have cfg==status==master (test_parsers_corpus.py:69-70 assert role=="master"), so the suite never distinguishes them. Suggested fix (prefer the status line, fall back to cfg) is correct; adding a cfg!=status corpus case would lock it.

Severity stays HIGH but NOT critical: I verified `EthHealth.role` is used only in summary() display and to_dict() reporting; it is NOT in the `checks` dict and does not feed `ok`, so it does not auto-flip a PASS/FAIL verdict. It fits the rubric's "high = ... a real-hw path that misparses" (wrong diagnostic/spec value on real hardware) rather than critical (verdict flip / data corruption).  

**H10. Real-hw subprocess return codes are ignored: a failed ethtool/ip silently yields a degraded-looking PASS-or-FAIL verdict instead of an error**  
`src/computetest/ethernet.py` · ethernet.py:98-107, 205-206 · _error-handling_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `subprocess.run(..., capture_output=True)` is called without `check=True` and `.returncode` is never inspected. If ethtool exits non-zero (interface down, permission denied, unsupported -S), stdout is empty, so up=False/speed=0/errors=0 — the tool emits a confident link/speed FAIL (or, for CAN, a clean ERROR-ACTIVE PASS with tec=rec=0) that is actually a tool failure, misleading a field operator.  
- **Evidence:** et = subprocess.run(["ethtool", iface], capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=10).stdout  (returncode discarded); similarly the `ip -details` call. Contrast: _real_iperf and _real_cable_test do catch errors, but the main link/stats/CAN calls do not.  
- **Fix:** Capture the CompletedProcess and, on non-zero returncode, raise (or record a distinct 'error'/'unknown' verdict) rather than parsing empty stdout into a hardware verdict. At minimum distinguish 'ethtool failed' from 'link genuinely down'.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced from the code. In the real-hw path, ethernet.py:98-99 and :105-106 capture only `.stdout` from `subprocess.run(...)` with no `check=True` and never inspect `.returncode`; same at :205-206 for the CAN `ip -details -statistics link show` call. subprocess.run without check=True does NOT raise on non-zero exit, and a failed ethtool/ip typically writes to stderr leaving stdout empty. Tracing the consequences: (a) Ethernet, empty stdout -> up=False, _parse_speed("")=0, _stat counters=0, giving checks link_up=False/speed_ok=False -> a confident link/speed FAIL; (b) CAN, empty stdout -> the state chain at :207-209 falls through to the final else "ERROR-ACTIVE" (the HEALTHY state), berr regex misses -> tec=rec=0, fd=False, yielding checks not_bus_off=True/error_active=True/low_errors=True -> a fully PASSING CanHealth. So a tool failure (missing `ip`/`ethtool`, interface absent, no CAP_NET_ADMIN/permission denied) is silently reported as a clean ERROR-ACTIVE CAN PASS. That is a wrong PASS verdict, exactly what the project's honesty discipline says to flag ("mislabels a verdict / fakes a PASS"), and it lives inside a real-hw path where mocks cannot catch it.

This is NOT intentional design: a codebase-wide survey shows the established convention is to inspect return codes -- lmt_adapter.py:242-245, bert.py:324-325, ocp_telemetry.py:168-173, and tsn/adapter.py:64-85 all check `returncode != 0` and raise; nvme/health.py and gpu.py use check=True; gmsl/video.py:109 and memory.py:117-118 branch on returncode. The ethernet link/stats/CAN calls are the lone outliers. Even within ethernet.py itself, _real_iperf (:130) and _real_cable_test (:146) wrap subprocess in try/except and map failure to a benign neutral result, while the link/stats/CAN calls do not -- confirming the author knew to handle subprocess failure but missed it here. No upstream guard exists: callers (harness.py:110-112, cli.py:310-311) just consume the verdict.

The finding's claims are factually accurate. I raise severity from medium to high: the CAN branch turns a tool failure into a healthy PASS (a wrong PASS verdict, which the conventions class toward critical), not merely a false FAIL. I stop short of critical because it is gated on the subprocess actually exiting non-zero with empty stdout, the ethtool side only yields the less-dangerous false-FAIL, and it is confined to the untested real-hw path. Suggested fix is sound: capture the CompletedProcess and on non-zero returncode raise (consistent with lmt_adapter/bert/ocp_telemetry) or record a distinct 'error'/'unknown' verdict, so 'tool failed' is never conflated with a hardware result.  

**H11. sample_count and sample_count_bits are inverted and mis-computed vs the OCP pci_lmt schema**  
`src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py` · src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py:167, 177, 188 (from_margin_result); field defs lines 98-99 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The two columns the module promises to keep in lock-step with ocp-diag-pci_lmt are semantically swapped and numerically wrong: it emits sample_count = 1<<sample_count_bits (huge) and sample_count_bits = the raw log exponent, whereas the OCP reference defines sample_count = raw 7-bit register value (small, 0..127) and sample_count_bits = int(pow(2, sample_count/3)) (the derived bit count). It also drops the factor of 3 (2^x vs the spec's 2^(x/3)).  
- **Evidence:** Code: `sample_count = 1 << sample_count_bits` (line 167) with default `sample_count_bits: int = 18` -> emits sample_count=262144, sample_count_bits=18. OCP reference pcie_lane_margining.fetch_sample_count: `sample_count = (ret['margin_payload_status'] & 0x7F)` and `sample_count_bits = int(pow(2, sample_count / 3))`, comment 'MSampleCount Value = 3*log2 (number of bits margined)'. So in pci_lmt the small register value lives in `sample_count` and the large derived bit-count lives in `sample_count_bits` — the exact opposite of this module. Even taking the toolkit's own meaning, 2^18 ignores the /3, where the spec for register value 18 gives 2^(18/3)=64 bits.  
- **Fix:** Match the reference: emit the raw SampleCount register value in `sample_count` (range 0..127) and compute `sample_count_bits = int(2 ** (sample_count / 3))`. On the mock/default path, pick a realistic raw value (e.g. derive from sample_count_bits via the inverse 3*log2) rather than inventing 1<<18, so a real pci_lmt run and a computetest run report comparable numbers in both columns.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced. lmt_adapter.from_margin_result (default sample_count_bits=18, line 132) emits sample_count = 1<<18 = 262144 (line 167) and sample_count_bits = 18. I fetched the upstream OCP reference: in pcie_lane_margining.fetch_sample_count, sample_count = (margin_payload_status & 0x7F) is the raw 7-bit register value (0..127) and sample_count_bits = int(pow(2, sample_count/3)) is the derived bit count (comment: "MSampleCount Value = 3*log2(number of bits margined)"); results.py LmtLaneResult stores both with those semantics. So the two columns are semantically swapped vs the reference AND numerically wrong: this module's 1<<18 also drops the /3 (for a raw value of 18 the spec yields 2^(18/3)=64 bits, not 262144). This directly violates the module's own stated contract (docstring lines 6-10, 73-77: columns "match the OCP pci_lmt column set exactly" so a computetest run is "interchangeable with a pci_lmt run"); a downstream consumer comparing these two columns across the two tools gets contradictory numbers. Not covered by any guard or convention: not a pragma real-hw path, not honesty-discipline (no None/raise), not mock-fidelity. The only test touching these values (tests/test_lmt_adapter.py:121) just mirrors the buggy output (sample_count=1<<18, sample_count_bits=18), so it passes regardless of correctness and provides false confidence. Severity high (not critical): sample_count is informational metadata and does not flip the lane PASS/FAIL verdict, but it is a wrong number / wrong spec value in a schema that claims OCP interchangeability — matching the project's "high = wrong number/spec value" bar.  

**H12. max_voltage_offset is mislabeled as mV and emitted out of the spec/schema range (0.01V register units)**  
`src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py` · src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py:55, 90, 161 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** DEFAULT_MAX_VOLTAGE_OFFSET = 200 is documented and treated as '200 mV', but the OCP pci_lmt / PCIe LMR MaxVoltageOffset column is a raw capability-register value in 0.01V units (valid ~5..50, e.g. google/pcie_lmt example max_voltage_offset: 49 = 0.49V). A consumer ingesting this row reads 200 as 200 x 0.01V = 2.0V, which is also outside the 6-bit field max (63 -> 0.63V).  
- **Evidence:** Code: `DEFAULT_MAX_VOLTAGE_OFFSET = 200         # 200 mV` (line 55), field comment `max_voltage_offset: int              # in mV` (line 90), and `"max_voltage_offset": DEFAULT_MAX_VOLTAGE_OFFSET` (line 161). google/pcie_lmt and PCIe spec encode MaxVoltageOffset in 0.01V units; example shows max_voltage_offset: 49. Note max_timing_offset=50 IS handled correctly (0.01-UI units), making the voltage field the inconsistent one.  
- **Fix:** Express the default in the schema's 0.01V register units (e.g. 49 for 0.49V) and fix the field/comment to say '0.01V units', mirroring how max_timing_offset is documented as 0.01-UI units. Keep the internal step math in mV but convert when emitting the schema field.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py, DEFAULT_MAX_VOLTAGE_OFFSET = 200 is commented "# 200 mV" (line 55), the dataclass field is documented "max_voltage_offset: int  # in mV" (line 90), and this value is emitted directly into the OCP-schema record's max_voltage_offset column (line 161; also reused by ddr5/lmt.py:44 and gmsl/lmt.py:56).

The module's own docstring (lines 6-29) and the test docstring (tests/test_lmt_adapter.py:4-7) state the columns/defaults MUST stay "in lock-step with the OCP pci_lmt CLI." I verified against the OCP/Google pci_lmt reference: its example lane parameters emit max_timing_offset: 50 and max_voltage_offset: 49 as raw Margining Capability register values. Per the PCIe Base Spec LMR fields, MaxTimingOffset is in 1%-UI units (50 = 0.50 UI) and MaxVoltageOffset is in 0.01V units (49 = 0.49V), with the field capped near ~0.5V. So a consumer ingesting this toolkit's row reads max_voltage_offset=200 as 200 x 0.01V = 2.0V — both unit-mislabeled and out of the field's physical range.

The asymmetry the finding cites is real and verified: max_timing_offset=50 is correctly documented "0.50 UI in 0.01-UI units" (line 54) and _step_from_timing_ui converts via max_offset_001ui/100.0 (line 117), matching the OCP example; only the voltage field carries the mV semantic. The internal step math (_step_from_voltage_mv, line 126) is self-consistent in mV, so the verdict logic is unaffected — but the value PUBLISHED to the OCP-portable schema is wrong.

Not intentional/already-handled: the honesty-discipline convention covers fabricated passes and unknown masks, not this; the placeholder comment (lines 151-152) does not excuse emitting a non-conformant value into a column that claims OCP parity. No test guards the value — TestSchemaParity/TestFormatters only check column order and presence, TestFromMarginResult only checks step mapping, and tests/test_lmt_adapter.py:118 hardcodes max_voltage_offset=200 in a fixture, which entrenches the wrong value rather than catching it.

Severity high (not critical): this is a placeholder capability field, not a measured result, and it does not flip a PASS/FAIL verdict (verdicts derive from timing_ui in margining.py). But the module's stated purpose is byte-for-byte OCP schema portability, and it emits a unit-mislabeled, physically-out-of-range number to downstream BigQuery/CSV consumers — a real wrong-spec-value defect. Suggested fix is sound: set the default in 0.01V register units (e.g. 49) and relabel the field/comment as "0.01V units", converting from mV when emitting, mirroring max_timing_offset.  

**H13. ExternalBERT.read_result() raises OverflowError (not InstrumentError) when the error counter returns the 9.9E37 overflow sentinel**  
`src/computetest/instruments.py` · instruments.py:769 (ExternalBERT.read_result) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** On real hardware a saturated or uninitialized BERT error counter returns the IEEE 488.2 overflow sentinel 9.9E37; parse_scpi_float maps that to +inf, and int(inf) raises an uncaught OverflowError instead of the InstrumentError the module promises for every fault.  
- **Evidence:** n_err = int(parse_scpi_float(err)) — verified: ExternalBERT with :READ:ERR? = '9.9E37' raises 'OverflowError: cannot convert float infinity to integer'. The module docstring states 9.9E37 maps to +/-inf 'so a saturated DMM doesn't look like a real reading', and InstrumentError exists so callers can 'except InstrumentError regardless of which layer failed' — this path escapes that contract.  
- **Fix:** Guard the conversion: parse the float first, and if math.isinf/isnan raise InstrumentError(f'BERT error count overflow/invalid: {err!r}') before calling int(). Apply to both n_err and (for safety) the bits read.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced end-to-end through the public API. At src/computetest/instruments.py:769, ExternalBERT.read_result() does `n_err = int(parse_scpi_float(err))`. When the BERT error counter returns the IEEE 488.2 overflow/NaN sentinel 9.9E37 (or -9.9E37) — which the module's own docstring (lines 22-24) documents as what a saturated/uninitialized SCPI instrument returns — parse_scpi_float maps it to ±inf (lines 92-93, by design, does NOT raise), and int(±inf) raises an uncaught OverflowError('cannot convert float infinity to integer'). I confirmed this via the mock transport: `b.transport.set_measurement(':READ:ERR?', '9.9E37'); b.read_result()` raises OverflowError, and likewise for '-9.9E37'.\n\nThis breaks the documented contract: InstrumentError (lines 50-53) exists precisely "so callers ... can except InstrumentError regardless of which layer failed." The OverflowError escapes that contract, so a harness wrapping bench calls in `except InstrumentError` will not catch it and the test step aborts. read_result is the parsing logic shared by the mock and the real _PyVisaTransport (pragma real-hw path at line 196); only the real BERT actually emits the sentinel, so the mock-based tests (test_instruments_hal.py:133-149, which use err=5 and err=0) cannot catch it — exactly the high-value real-hw parsing-bug category.\n\nRefutation attempts failed: (1) no upstream guard — parse_scpi_float intentionally maps to inf rather than raising, deferring overflow handling to a consumer-side .overflow flag that only Reading has, not BertReading; (2) the existing overflow tests (test_instruments.py:221,336) only exercise Reading.overflow on PowerSupply/DMM, where value stays a float and inf is tolerated — none route through int(); (3) not the honesty-discipline pattern (it does not fake a pass, but it raises the WRONG exception type and crashes). Note the bits read (:READ:BITS?) does NOT crash because int() is not applied; instead a saturated bits counter silently yields bits=inf, ber=0.0 — a subtler honesty issue and the rationale for the suggested fix's "for safety" guard on bits, but the primary error-count claim is the solid defect.\n\nSeverity high (not critical): the rubric lists "a real-hw path that crashes/misparses" as high; it does not produce a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict or silent data corruption (it loudly crashes). Suggested fix is sound: parse the float, then if math.isinf/isnan raise InstrumentError(f'BERT error count overflow/invalid: {err!r}') before int(); consider also guarding bits so a saturated bits counter isn't silently reported as ber=0.0.  

**H14. TSNTrafficGenerator.read_counters() raises OverflowError when a stream counter returns the overflow sentinel**  
`src/computetest/instruments.py` · instruments.py:975-977 (TSNTrafficGenerator.read_counters) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** tx/rx/dropped are converted with int(self.query_float(...)); a saturated or N/A counter returning 9.9E37 becomes +inf and int(inf) throws an uncaught OverflowError instead of InstrumentError.  
- **Evidence:** tx = int(self.query_float(f":STREAM{stream_id:d}:TX:COUN?")) — verified: with :STREAM1:TX:COUN? = '9.9E37' read_counters raises OverflowError. Same int(inf) failure mode as read_result; breaks the uniform InstrumentError contract on the real bus.  
- **Fix:** Wrap each conversion in a helper that rejects inf/nan: e.g. _int_count(self, q) -> int that does float = self.query_float(q); if not math.isfinite(float): raise InstrumentError(f'{q} returned non-finite count'); return int(float).  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproduced against the real code. instruments.py:975-977 does `tx/rx/dropped = int(self.query_float(...))`. `query_float` -> `parse_scpi_float` deliberately maps the IEEE-488.2 overflow sentinel 9.9E37 to +/-inf (lines 92-93, documented at line 337). `int(float('inf'))` raises OverflowError. I drove the real TSNTrafficGenerator through _MockSCPI with `:STREAM1:TX:COUN? = '9.9E37'` and read_counters(1) raised OverflowError, not InstrumentError. (The finding's suggested isfinite fix also correctly catches the NaN case, where int() raises ValueError.)

The "uniform InstrumentError contract" claim is grounded in the code's own docstring (lines 50-53): "One exception type so callers (the harness, the power-test step) can `except InstrumentError` regardless of which layer failed." A harness doing `except InstrumentError` around result collection would NOT catch this OverflowError and would crash. The finding's analogy to read_result is also accurate: I reproduced the identical int(inf)->OverflowError on ExternalBERT.read_result (line 769, `int(parse_scpi_float(err))`).

This is not refuted by project conventions: it is a logic bug INSIDE a real-hw path (a real TSN stream counter that is saturated/N-A legitimately returns 9.9E37 over the bus), which the conventions explicitly call out as a high-value finding rather than an untested-pragma non-issue. It is not intentional honesty discipline — the codebase's honesty pattern raises InstrumentError, and elsewhere (measure_resistance, line 671) it explicitly guards `i.overflow` before using a value, showing overflow sentinels are a recognized real input; the two int()-coercion sites simply omit that guard. No test exercises the overflow path for read_counters (test_read_counters only uses finite values 1000/998/2).

Severity high (not critical): it does not flip a PASS/FAIL verdict or corrupt data; it turns a legitimate instrument response into an uncaught, off-contract crash on real hardware — matching "a real-hw path that crashes." Both read_counters (975-977) and read_result's error-count (769) should be wrapped in a finite-check helper that raises InstrumentError.  

**H15. temp<=N check false-FAILs a healthy drive at 0 C or with a missing/zero temperature field**  
`src/computetest/nvme/health.py` · health.py:63 (_apply_limits), interacts with health.py:129-130 · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The temperature check requires 0 < temperature, so a drive whose Composite Temperature converts to exactly 0 C (raw 273 K, or a sensorless drive reporting 0), or whose temperature key is absent (default 0), FAILS the temp check and flips the whole NvmeHealth verdict to FAIL even though the drive is healthy.  
- **Evidence:** f"temp<={max_temp_c}": 0 < smart.get("temperature", 0) <= max_temp_c — with the Kelvin fixup `if smart.get("temperature", 0) > 200: smart["temperature"] -= 273`, a raw 273 K becomes 0, and 0 < 0 is False; a missing key also defaults to 0 and fails. The mock never exercises temperature==0, so the suite stays green.  
- **Fix:** Separate 'temperature present and plausible' from the over-temp gate. Either treat a missing/zero temperature as 'unknown' (raise or report N/A rather than silently FAIL), or change the gate to `smart.get("temperature", 0) <= max_temp_c` and add an explicit sensor-present check (e.g. require the key to exist) so a legitimate 0 C reading passes.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproduced. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/health.py:63 the temperature check is `f"temp<={max_temp_c}": 0 < smart.get("temperature", 0) <= max_temp_c`. The `0 <` lower bound means any healthy drive whose Composite Temperature is at or below 0 C false-FAILs and flips NvmeHealth.ok to FAIL with a misleading `temp<=N` reason (the drive is NOT over temperature).

Reproduced directly: with the real-hw Kelvin fixup at lines 129-130 (`if smart.get("temperature",0) > 200: smart["temperature"] -= 273`), raw 273 K -> 0 C, and `0 < 0` is False -> verdict FAIL. A missing temperature key defaults to 0 -> same FAIL. I also found the defect is broader than the title: any sub-zero reading false-FAILs (e.g. raw 263 K -> -10 C, `0 < -10` is False -> FAIL). This is realistic for this codebase's domain (Zoox automotive/autonomous-vehicle cold-start; operating range goes well below freezing), so it is not an exotic edge case.

Refutation attempts failed: (1) No guard exists - line 63 is the only temperature lower bound and there is no separate sensor-present check. (2) No test catches it. The mock (_mock_smart) only emits 41 C / 96 C; the corpus cases only use 41 C and 47 C (314 K / 320 K); and the Hypothesis property test test_normalize_abbreviated_matches_canonical (test_parsers_property.py:97 with st.integers(0,120) including 0) only asserts canonical-vs-abbreviated _apply_limits EQUIVALENCE, not verdict correctness, so temperature=0 produces False on both sides and the test still passes - it cannot detect the false-FAIL. (3) Not intentional honesty-discipline behavior: a 0/missing/sub-zero reading is silently folded into a hard FAIL labeled as over-temperature, which violates the project's own honesty discipline (don't mislabel a verdict / don't fake a verdict it cannot back) rather than raising or reporting N/A.

This runs on the real-hw path (mock never exercises temp<=0), where mocks cannot catch it - a wrong FAIL verdict on healthy hardware. Severity: high (not critical) - it produces a wrong verdict but the failure mode is conservative (false-FAIL rejecting a good drive / causing needless RMA, not a false-PASS that ships bad hardware). The suggested fix is sound: split sensor-present/plausibility from the over-temp gate (e.g. `smart.get("temperature", 0) <= max_temp_c` plus an explicit key-present check, or report N/A) so a legitimate 0 C or sub-zero reading passes.  

**H16. NMIMT (NVMe-MI message type) packed into wrong byte and wrong bit field**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py` · encode_mi_request (messages.py:127-133); decode_mi_response (messages.py:154) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Per spec/libnvme the NVMe-MI message type goes in bits 6:3 of the NMP byte (byte 1 of the header), with ROR at bit 7 — 'hdr->nmp = (ROR<<7) | (NMIMT<<3) | (csi & 1)'. This module instead puts message_type in bits 0:3 of byte 0 (its only header byte), has no ROR bit at all, and reuses byte 0 for everything. Real hardware will mis-decode the message type and never see a request/response flag.  
- **Evidence:** Comment: '# NMP byte: bits 0..3 = MT, bit 4 = CSI, bits 5..6 = SLOTID, bit 7 = 0' and 'nmp = (int(msg.message_type) & 0x0F) | ...'. libnvme: '(NVME_MI_ROR_REQ << 7) | (NVME_MI_MT_ADMIN << 3) | (ep->csi & 1)'.  
- **Fix:** Build nmp as (ror<<7) | ((message_type & 0x0F) << 3) | (csi & 1); add an explicit ROR/request-response field to MiMessage and set/parse bit 7; decode message_type via (nmp >> 3) & 0x0F.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed against the code and spec. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py the NMP byte is built (line 132-133) as `(MT & 0x0F) | (csi << 4) | (slot_id << 5)` and decoded (lines 154-156) the same way: MT in bits 0:3, CSI at bit 4, SLOTID at bits 5:6, bit 7 forced to 0. The actual NVMe-MI NMP byte (NVMe-MI spec and libnvme nvme-mi.h: `hdr->nmp = (ROR<<7) | (NMIMT<<3) | (csi & 1)`) is: bit7=ROR, bits6:3=NMIMT, bit0=CSI. So this module (a) puts the message type in bits 0:3 instead of 6:3, (b) has no ROR/request-response bit at all (bit 7 hardcoded 0), and (c) invents a 2-bit SLOTID field at bits 5:6 that the NMP byte does not contain. The finding's spec and libnvme characterization is accurate; the code description is accurate.

Not refuted by conventions: this is NOT a mock-only behavior — encode_mi_request/decode_mi_response are the single, canonical wire-framing for NVMe-MI (no other module builds the NMP byte; reset/health/vpd defer to NotImplementedError pending libmctp), and the module docstring states these bytes are 'the on-wire byte string the MCTP transport will send' on real hardware. It is not honesty-discipline (nothing refuses-to-pass here). The only test, test_encode_decode_round_trip, is a self-consistent encode->decode round trip with no spec-defined byte vector, so it passes even though the layout is wrong — exactly the weak-test category. The test class even labels itself 'Covers UNH-IOL v25 test 5.1 — NMP byte field decode,' a spec-conformance claim the layout cannot back.

Note: the module docstring's 'byte 0 = Message Type 0x04' is a separate doc inconsistency — the 0x04 MCTP type byte is correctly owned by the MCTP layer (MctpMessage.message_type), and encode_mi_request's byte 0 is the NMP byte. That part is fine; the real defect is the NMP bit layout + missing ROR.

Severity high rather than critical: on real hardware this produces malformed MI frames (endpoint mis-decodes message type; request vs response indistinguishable at bit 7), a definite wrong wire value in a real-hw-bound path that unit tests cannot catch. It is latent today because no real MCTP station is wired (all real paths NotImplementedError), so it is not currently emitting a wrong PASS/FAIL on connected hardware — that latency is the only thing keeping it from critical.  

**H17. CSI placed at bit 4 instead of bit 0 of the NMP byte**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py` · encode_mi_request (messages.py:132); decode_mi_response (messages.py:155) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Per spec/libnvme CSI is bit 0 of the NMP byte ('ep->csi & 1'). This module shifts CSI to bit 4 on encode and reads it from bit 4 on decode, so a real device's CSI bit (bit 0) is silently ignored and the module's CSI lands in a reserved/other field.  
- **Evidence:** encode: '((msg.csi & 0x01) << 4)'; decode: 'csi = (nmp >> 4) & 0x01'. libnvme nmp construction masks CSI as '(ep->csi & 1)' i.e. bit 0.  
- **Fix:** Encode CSI as '(msg.csi & 0x01)' (bit 0) and decode as 'nmp & 0x01'.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed against the NVMe-MI spec and libnvme. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py the NMP (Message Header) byte is built as bits 0:3 = MessageType, bit 4 = CSI, bits 5:6 = Slot ID (encode line 132: `((msg.csi & 0x01) << 4)`; decode line 155: `csi = (nmp >> 4) & 0x01`, documented at line 127). The real on-wire layout, verified from libnvme src/nvme/mi.c, is `nmp = (NVME_MI_ROR_REQ << 7) | (NVME_MI_MT_MI << 3) | (ep->csi & 1)` — i.e. CSI is bit 0, NMIMT message type is bits 6:3, and ROR is bit 7. So the finding's core claim is factually accurate: this module places CSI at bit 4 instead of bit 0. On a real device, the module would ignore the device's actual CSI (bit 0) on decode and write its CSI into a reserved bit on encode, producing non-conformant frames.

This is not refuted by the project conventions: the function docstrings (lines 124, 141) explicitly state these produce/parse the on-wire byte string the MCTP transport sends — a genuine real-hardware encoder/parser. The only test (tests/test_nvme_mi.py test_encode_decode_round_trip) is a self-consistent round trip that uses the same wrong layout on both sides, so it cannot catch the divergence; per the conventions a spec bug inside a real-hw framing path is exactly the high-value kind to flag. No PASS/FAIL verdict is faked, so this is not critical by the project's definition.

Two caveats worth recording: (1) the suggested fix (encode `msg.csi & 0x01`, decode `nmp & 0x01`) is correct for CSI, but the finding understates the scope — the message type is ALSO misplaced (module uses bits 0:3, spec uses bits 6:3 via `<< 3`) and the ROR request/response bit (bit 7) is entirely absent; a complete fix must relocate MT to bits 6:3 and add ROR. (2) The decode also blindly does `MessageType(nmp & 0x0F)` which, once MT moves to bits 6:3, would need `(nmp >> 3) & 0x0F`. Severity remains high: a wrong spec value on a real-hardware framing path that would misparse a real device's header.  

**H18. Slot ID encoded at bits 5:6 of NMP — not where the spec carries it**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py` · encode_mi_request (messages.py:130-133); decode_mi_response (messages.py:156) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The NMP byte in the spec has no two-bit Slot ID field at bits 5:6 (those bits are part of NMIMT/ROR territory). Control-Primitive Slot ID is carried in the message body, not in these NMP bits. Placing slot_id at bits 5:6 corrupts the NMIMT/ROR region of a real frame and loses the real slot field.  
- **Evidence:** '((msg.slot_id & 0x03) << 5)' and 'slot_id = (nmp >> 5) & 0x03'. With message_type<<3 in libnvme, bits 5:6 belong to NMIMT, so slot_id collides with the type field.  
- **Fix:** Remove slot_id from the NMP byte; carry Slot ID in the control-primitive request data per NVMe-MI Control Primitive layout, or place it in the documented header field.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed spec-conformance defect, and the finding actually understates it. The NVMe-MI Message Header byte (byte 1, what this module calls NMP) has a fixed layout across NVMe-MI 1.x/2.x: bit 7 = ROR (Request/Response), bits 6:4 = NMIMT (NVMe-MI Message Type, 3 bits), bits 3:1 = Reserved, bit 0 = CSI (Command Slot Identifier, 1 bit). The code (src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py:127-133) instead packs message_type into bits 0:3 (4 bits), CSI into bit 4, and a 2-bit slot_id into bits 5:6, with bit 7 forced to 0. The finding's central claim is factually correct: there is no 2-bit Slot ID field at bits 5:6 — those bits are NMIMT territory — and CSI is the only "slot"-like field (a single bit at bit 0), not a separate 2-bit slot. Beyond the slot_id error the finding cites, the entire byte is mislaid: message_type is at the wrong offset and wrong width (4 bits vs spec's 3-bit NMIMT at 6:4), CSI is at bit 4 instead of bit 0, and ROR (bit 7) is omitted entirely. decode_mi_response (line 154-156) mirrors the same wrong layout, so a real device frame would be misparsed (e.g., a response with ROR=1 set in bit 7 would be silently dropped because the decoder masks bit 7 away).

This is not exempt under project conventions. encode_mi_request is a public, exported API documented as producing "the on-wire byte string the MCTP transport will send," and is not marked as a mock-only/pragma path. It is not currently wired into a live transmit path (reset.py, vpd.py, controller_health.py, subsystem_health.py all raise NotImplementedError on the real path), so it cannot corrupt a live frame today — but it is a latent on-wire encoder that will emit malformed NMP bytes the moment it is connected to hardware.

The defect is masked by a weak test: TestMiMessageEncode.test_encode_decode_round_trip (tests/test_nvme_mi.py:126-141) only asserts encode->decode self-consistency and never checks the produced wire bytes against a known-good spec byte sequence, so it passes regardless of whether the layout matches the spec. The test is also mislabeled "Covers UNH-IOL v25 test 5.1 — NMP byte field decode," implying a conformance check it does not perform.

Severity high (not critical): it is a wrong spec value / wrong byte layout in a documented wire encoder, and would misparse real-hardware frames, but it is not yet reachable on a live path, so it cannot currently flip a PASS/FAIL verdict on real hardware. Suggested fix: redo the NMP byte per spec (ROR bit 7, NMIMT bits 6:4, CSI bit 0), drop the invented 2-bit slot_id field, and add a byte-level assertion against a captured/known-good frame in the test.  

**H19. NSHSP Composite Temperature is signed Celsius on the wire, not Kelvin — field name, docstring, and -273.0 conversion are all wrong for real hardware**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/subsystem_health.py` · subsystem_health.py:39,47-48 (composite_temperature_k field + temperature_c property) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The module declares the NSHSP Composite Temperature as Kelvin (`composite_temperature_k`, docstring 'Kelvin (the spec's native unit)') and converts via `- 273.0`, but the NVMe-MI NVM Subsystem Health Status Poll response data structure reports Composite Temperature as a signed (two's-complement) 8-bit value in degrees Celsius, not Kelvin. (The 2-byte Kelvin form is the *in-band* SMART Log Page 02h, a different structure.) When the real parse path is wired, a true 40 C reading would be mislabeled and converted to -233.0 C.  
- **Evidence:** Line 39: `composite_temperature_k: int      # Kelvin (the spec's native unit)`; Line 48: `return float(self.composite_temperature_k) - 273.0`. Demonstrated: feeding a Celsius-native byte of 40 yields `temperature_c = -233.0`.  
- **Fix:** Treat the NSHSP Composite Temperature byte as a signed int8 in Celsius: parse via two's-complement (e.g. `temp_c = byte - 256 if byte >= 128 else byte`) and store as a Celsius field. Drop the Kelvin label/conversion for the NSHSP path; reserve the Kelvin 2-byte decode for the in-band SMART log only. Update the docstring accordingly.  
- **Verifier:** Independently confirmed against the canonical spec/implementation. The NVMe-MI NVM Subsystem Health Data Structure (NSHDS, NVMe-MI Fig. 108) defines Composite Temperature as a SINGLE BYTE in degrees Celsius, two's-complement signed (vendor range not to exceed -60..+127 C). Proof: libnvme's struct nvme_mi_nvm_ss_health_status (src/nvme/types.h) declares it as `__u8 ctemp;` (one byte, not __le16), and the authoritative Code Construct libnvme example prints it directly as `printf("composite temperature: %d°C\n", status.ctemp)` — Celsius, signed %d, no -273 offset. The 2-byte Kelvin form is the IN-BAND SMART Log Page 02h, a different structure — and this very repo proves the distinction: nvme/health.py:129-130 applies `-273` only to the in-band SMART temperature ("nvme-cli reports Kelvin").

Against the cited code (subsystem_health.py): line 39 mislabels the field `composite_temperature_k: int  # Kelvin (the spec's native unit)`; line 48 `temperature_c` returns `float(self.composite_temperature_k) - 273.0`. This is wrong for the NSHSP byte. Reproduced: a real `ctemp` byte of 40 (40 C) wired into this model yields temperature_c = 40 - 273 = -233.0 C. The mock (line 99) stores 314 to be internally self-consistent with the wrong conversion, so the only test (test_nvme_mi_extras.py asserting temperature_c == 41.0) passes vacuously and does NOT catch the spec error. No guard exists.

Not refuted by project conventions: the real parse path raises NotImplementedError (line 127, pragma no cover), so no hardware is misparsed TODAY — but the defect is in the spec-facing data contract (field name/type, documented unit "the spec's native unit", and the conversion math), not merely in untested plumbing. The docstring explicitly invites a station to "wire libmctp + the encoder," at which point every NSHSP temperature reading is ~273 C off and the temperature_threshold health decode is undermined. This is exactly the "wrong spec value / misparse on real-hw path" the conventions say to flag, and the misleading Kelvin contract is not honesty-discipline or intentional design. Severity high is correct (wrong spec/units value that would mislead real-hardware test, latent until the documented wiring step). Note: the sibling controller_health.py (CHSP) has the same `-273.0` pattern; CHSP composite temperature legitimately differs (controller-level), so that file is out of scope for this finding but worth a separate check.  

**H20. Operator note contradicts RSF=0 semantics — could mislead a field engineer into a subsystem-wide reset believing host I/O is safe**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/reset.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/reset.py:3-14, 53-56 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The module simultaneously claims the reset 'reinitializes ONLY the MI endpoint state ... without disturbing the data path' AND that the default RSF=0 (NVM_SUBSYSTEM_RESET) 'resets every controller in the subsystem.' These are mutually exclusive; if RSF=0 resets every controller, the operator note that data-plane NVMe traffic 'is unaffected' is wrong and dangerous.  
- **Evidence:** Header: 'the management endpoint reset re-initializes ONLY the MI endpoint state ... without disturbing the data path the host kernel is driving.' Lines 12-13: 'NVM Subsystem Reset (RSF = 0): resets every controller in the subsystem.' Default: function: ResetFunction = ResetFunction.NVM_SUBSYSTEM_RESET. Operator note line 55-56: 'The data-plane NVMe traffic (the kernel `nvme` driver) is unaffected, but a BMC polling SMART via MI will see one round of timeouts.'  
- **Fix:** Resolve the contradiction: either (a) the command truly only resets the MI endpoint, in which case the RSF=0 description 'resets every controller' is wrong and must be corrected; or (b) RSF=0 genuinely resets every controller, in which case the operator note must warn that host I/O IS disrupted and the default should not be presented as benign. Do not ship guidance that tells an operator a subsystem-wide reset leaves the data path untouched.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed directly from the file text — this is a genuine, demonstrable internal contradiction in operator-facing documentation, squarely in the project's "honesty discipline" dimension. The module makes three statements that cannot all be true: (1) header lines 6-8: the reset "re-initializes ONLY the MI endpoint state — useful for clearing a stuck MCTP transaction without disturbing the data path the host kernel is driving"; (2) lines 12-13: "NVM Subsystem Reset (RSF = 0): resets every controller in the subsystem"; (3) operator note lines 54-56: "The data-plane NVMe traffic (the kernel `nvme` driver) is unaffected." If RSF=0 resets every controller (the explicit description, and the correct NVMe-MI semantic — an NSS/NVM Subsystem Reset tears down all controllers, forcing the host kernel `nvme` driver to re-enable via CC.EN), then the "ONLY the MI endpoint" and "data-plane... unaffected" claims are false and dangerous. The function default is `function: ResetFunction = ResetFunction.NVM_SUBSYSTEM_RESET` (line 46) and `ResetRequest.function` defaults the same (line 34), so the benign-sounding operator note is attached to a default that, by the module's own RSF=0 description, disrupts host I/O.

I tried to refute this: (a) Tests — tests/test_nvme_mi_extras.py:167-189 only assert mechanical behavior (enum codes 0/1, mock returns accepted=True, echoed function). No test validates or contextualizes the operator guidance, so nothing in the suite resolves the contradiction. (b) Guards/context — there is no other text in reset.py or sibling modules that reconciles the two readings; subsystem_health.py uses NSS to mean "NVM Subsystem Status" but does not touch this reset's semantics. (c) Conventions — this is not mock-fidelity, not a pragma no-test complaint (the real path raises NotImplementedError, which I am NOT flagging), and not a passing-gates style nit. It is exactly the "ship guidance that would mislead a field engineer" case the conventions tell me to flag.

Severity: high (not critical). The contradiction ships now and is genuinely misleading in the direction of "default is safe" when it is not. The real-bus path currently raises NotImplementedError (line 60), so no live reset is issued today and there is no immediate data corruption or wrong PASS/FAIL verdict — the harm is latent until libmctp + the request encoder are wired up. That keeps it below critical but firmly at high: it is wrong/dangerous documented semantics on the operator-facing surface of a real-hw command. Suggested fix as written is correct: either correct the RSF=0 "resets every controller" line if the command is truly endpoint-only, or fix the header/operator note to warn that host I/O IS disrupted and stop presenting the subsystem-reset default as benign.  

**H21. RSF enum names/values do not match the NVMe-MI §5.7 Reset command Reset-Function field**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/reset.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/reset.py:25-28 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** ResetFunction is documented as 'RSF field per NVMe-MI §5.7 Table 95' with NVM_SUBSYSTEM_RESET=0 and SUBSYSTEM_RESET_INHIBIT=1, but the NVMe-MI Reset (opcode 0x07) command's RSF field does not define these names/semantics. 'NVM Subsystem Reset' and 'reset inhibit' are properties of the in-band NSSR register / NVM Subsystem Reset behavior, not the MI Reset command's RSF codes. The cited 'Table 95' and the 0/1 mapping appear fabricated/mislabeled and will produce a wrong on-wire encoding when the real path is implemented.  
- **Evidence:** class ResetFunction(IntEnum): """RSF field per NVMe-MI §5.7 Table 95.""" NVM_SUBSYSTEM_RESET = 0; SUBSYSTEM_RESET_INHIBIT = 1  
- **Fix:** Verify the exact RSF field definition against the ratified NVMe-MI 2.1 Reset command table before relying on these codes; correct the enum names and values (or mark them as unverified surrogates per the honesty discipline) so the future request encoder does not put a wrong RSF on the wire. Do not cite a specific table number that cannot be backed.  
- **Verifier:** Verified against NVMe-MI Specification Rev 1.2d (downloaded /tmp/nvmemi.pdf; Figure 104 and section 8.3). The actual Reset command (opcode 07h) request field, per Figure 104, is "Reset Type" in NVMe Management Dword 0 bits 31:24, with the ONLY defined value 00h = "Reset NVM Subsystem" and 01h–FFh = Reserved. The code in /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/mi/reset.py:25-28 is wrong on three counts: (1) it cites "NVMe-MI §5.7 Table 95" — the real figure is Figure 104, so the table number is fabricated/mislabeled; (2) it names the field "RSF"/"ResetFunction" — the real field is "Reset Type"; (3) SUBSYSTEM_RESET_INHIBIT = 1 does not exist — 01h is Reserved, and there is no "NVM Subsystem Reset Inhibit" anywhere in the Reset command. A future request encoder consuming ResetFunction.SUBSYSTEM_RESET_INHIBIT would place a Reserved value (01h) on the wire, which a conformant device rejects with Invalid Parameter. The finding is slightly imprecise in one spot: value 0 IS a real spec value (00h = Reset NVM Subsystem), so the 0-mapping is not fabricated. The module is also worse than the finding states: the docstring frames 07h as a "Management Endpoint Reset" that leaves the data plane "unaffected," but per spec §8.3.1 the Reset command initiates a full NVM Subsystem Reset (resets the entire subsystem, all ports, all controllers — disrupting host I/O); the separate "Management Endpoint Reset" (§8.3.3) is transport-triggered (PCIe conventional / SMBus reset), corroborated by UNH-IOL v25 Group 12 tests. This is not the honesty-discipline "surrogate/None" pattern — the code asserts a confident false spec citation and a wrong value with no "unverified" caveat, and test_reset_function_enum_values asserts the bad value as "the spec's RSF field codes," locking the error in. Severity high (not critical): the real-hw encoder path currently raises NotImplementedError so no wrong bytes hit the wire today, but the enum is the spec-of-record for the future encoder, the value-1 path would mis-encode, and the operator note dangerously understates the command's blast radius. Suggested fix per the finding stands: rename the field to "Reset Type", drop/correct the non-existent inhibit value (only 00h is defined; 01h–FFh Reserved), cite Figure 104 (not "Table 95"), and fix the docstring to state this triggers a full NVM Subsystem Reset.  

**H22. check_eom ignores operator-set EOM thresholds, can emit a wrong PASS**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/checks.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/checks.py:239-240 (check_eom) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** check_eom hard-codes the module-default EOM thresholds (DEFAULT_EOM_UI_MIN/DEFAULT_EOM_MV_MIN) and never reads the per-instance thresholds set via serdes.set_eom_thresholds(), so a link whose worst sub-eye is below an operator-tightened limit is reported eye_open=True (PASS) even though the device's own eom_verdict() correctly FAILs it.  
- **Evidence:** checks.py: `passed = eye_quality_verdict(eye, ui_min=DEFAULT_EOM_UI_MIN, mv_min=DEFAULT_EOM_MV_MIN)`. serdes.py exposes `set_eom_thresholds(mv_min=..., ui_min=...)` writing self._eom_mv_min/_eom_ui_min and a concrete `eom_verdict()` that uses them; the shmoo consumer correctly calls `serdes.eom_verdict(eom)`. Reproduced: MockSerDes(injected_eye_mv=70.0); s.set_eom_thresholds(mv_min=100.0); check_eom(s).checks['eye_open'] == True while s.eom_verdict(s.read_eom(0)) == False (worst sub-eye 64.22 mV < 100 mV).  
- **Fix:** Have check_eom honor the device thresholds: either call `serdes.eom_verdict(eom)` directly (the canonical reuse, matching shmoo.py), or pass `ui_min=serdes._eom_ui_min, mv_min=serdes._eom_mv_min` into eye_quality_verdict. Optionally add explicit ui_min/mv_min kwargs to check_eom that default to the device's per-instance values.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproduced. check_eom (src/computetest/gmsl/checks.py:239-240) hard-codes the module defaults `eye_quality_verdict(eye, ui_min=DEFAULT_EOM_UI_MIN, mv_min=DEFAULT_EOM_MV_MIN)` and never reads the per-instance thresholds written by the public, documented `serdes.set_eom_thresholds(...)` (serdes.py:277-284, stored in `_eom_mv_min`/`_eom_ui_min`). I ran the exact repro: MockSerDes(injected_eye_mv=70.0) + set_eom_thresholds(mv_min=100.0) gives worst sub-eye 64.22 mV; the device's own canonical verdict s.eom_verdict(s.read_eom(0)) returns False, while check_eom(s).checks['eye_open'] returns True and h.ok is True — a silent wrong PASS for an operator-tightened limit.

Refutation attempts all failed: (1) Not already-handled — tests/test_gmsl_eom.py never sets custom thresholds, so it never exercises this path; the set_eom_thresholds tests in tests/test_gmsl_serdes.py:143-155 only call eom_verdict() directly, never through check_eom. (2) Not intentional API divergence — the sibling eye checks check_cxl_retimer (cxl/retimer.py:44) and check_dq_eye (ddr5/eye.py:44) expose explicit ui_min/mv_min kwargs so an operator has a working knob; check_eom exposes NO threshold kwargs, so the ONLY EOM-specific operator knob (set_eom_thresholds) is silently dropped by the check meant to enforce the verdict. (3) The shmoo consumer (gmsl/shmoo.py:81) and the retimer analogue Retimer.lane_status (pcie/retimer/base.py:228) both correctly honor the per-instance thresholds, so check_eom is the outlier, not the convention.

Downgraded from critical to high: it does yield a wrong PASS (meeting the letter of critical), but only when an operator tightens the EOM floor via the setter; the common default-threshold path is correct, and the GMSL3 PAM4 40 mV default is itself spec-aligned. Suggested fix is correct: route through serdes.eom_verdict(eom) (matching shmoo) or add ui_min/mv_min kwargs defaulting to serdes._eom_ui_min/_eom_mv_min. Relevant files: /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gmsl/checks.py (check_eom, lines 229-243), /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gmsl/serdes.py (set_eom_thresholds 277-284, eom_verdict 350-353), /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py:81.  

**H23. Real-mode frame-sync check degenerates to 'all locked'; a true desync passes**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/video.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/video.py:159 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** On real hardware frame synchronization is never actually verified: frame_sync_ok = all(li.locked for li in links) and ('DESYNC' not in addr), but any addr that reaches the real path has already passed _LINK_RE (^\d{1,4}-[0-9a-fA-F]{4}$), which can never contain the substring 'DESYNC'. So the second clause is always True and frame_sync_ok collapses to all(locked) — a deserializer whose links are all locked but whose CSI-2 frames are not aligned (the exact FrameSync failure this module says it exists to catch) reports frame_sync_ok=True and ok=True.  
- **Evidence:** Line 159: `frame_sync_ok = all(li.locked for li in links) and ("DESYNC" not in addr)` with comment '(a "DESYNC" address models loss of sync)'. The 'DESYNC' marker is a mock-only contrivance; on real hw nothing reads an actual frame-sync/line-valid status. Class doc/summary still advertise 'sync'/'DESYNC' and ok requires frame_sync_ok (line 125).  
- **Fix:** On the real path derive frame_sync from an actual hardware signal (e.g. deserializer FSYNC/line-valid status register, or comparing per-link frame timestamps / sequence counters from v4l2 stream output) rather than re-using the all-locked condition. If no such signal is obtainable, set frame_sync_ok=None and raise/skip rather than reporting a synthesized True (matches the project's honesty discipline used for unknown spec masks).  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced from the code. In src/computetest/gmsl/video.py:159, frame_sync_ok = all(li.locked for li in links) and ("DESYNC" not in addr). On the real path, check_deserializer calls check_gmsl(addr, ...) -> _real_check_gmsl, which at line 75 rejects any addr not matching _LINK_RE = ^\d{1,4}-[0-9a-fA-F]{4}$ (raising ValueError before line 159 is reached). I verified with Python that "1-0029-DESYNC" does NOT match _LINK_RE while "1-0029" does, and that no _LINK_RE-matching string can ever contain the substring "DESYNC" (length/charset are incompatible). Therefore on real hardware ("DESYNC" not in addr) is unconditionally True and frame_sync_ok collapses to all(li.locked). The "DESYNC" clause is purely a mock hook (intentional and fine for mock fidelity), but the real path performs zero inter-link frame-synchronization verification: I grepped the whole gmsl package and found no FSYNC/line-valid register read and no per-link frame-timestamp/sequence comparison wired into frame_sync_ok (the sequence_gaps/CRC logic in checks.py/serdes.py is the separate tunneled control channel). Yet the field comment (line 121 "all cameras locked and frame-synchronized"), the docstring ("frame sync across them"), summary() ("sync"), and to_dict()["frame_sync_ok"] all advertise genuine frame sync. A real multi-camera deserializer whose links are all locked with frames flowing per-link but whose CSI-2 streams are cross-link misaligned (the exact MAX96712-class FrameSync failure this module claims to catch) reports frame_sync_ok=True and ok=True. Note: the per-link --stream-mmap timeout (lines 100-111) DOES catch the gross "locked but no frames at all" case, so coverage is partial, not zero — that is the one mitigating guard. But it cannot detect inter-link desync. This is a logic bug inside a # pragma: no cover real-hw path that overclaims a PASS, which the conventions explicitly say is a real high-value finding and the opposite of the project's honesty discipline (it synthesizes True instead of setting None/raising when the real signal is unobtainable). Severity high (not critical) is right: it is a real-hw path that reports an unverified/wrong PASS and would mislead a field tech, but a partial per-link guard exists and it is not blanket data corruption. The suggested fix (read an actual FSYNC/line-valid status or compare per-link frame timestamps; otherwise set frame_sync_ok=None and raise/skip) is appropriate.  

**H24. --get-fmt-video subprocess has a timeout but no try/except — TimeoutExpired crashes the scan**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/video.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/video.py:94-95 · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The format query runs with timeout=10 but is not wrapped in try/except, so if v4l2-ctl hangs (a stuck/locked-but-dead link is exactly the failure mode this module targets) the resulting subprocess.TimeoutExpired propagates out of _real_check_gmsl and aborts the whole deserializer scan, whereas the comparable --stream-mmap call IS guarded and degrades to 'no frames'.  
- **Evidence:** `fmt = subprocess.run(["v4l2-ctl", "-d", video_device, "--get-fmt-video"], capture_output=True, text=True, timeout=10).stdout` — no try/except. Contrast lines 104-111 where --stream-mmap wraps subprocess.run in try/except subprocess.TimeoutExpired: captured = 0.  
- **Fix:** Wrap the --get-fmt-video call in try/except subprocess.TimeoutExpired (and treat a timeout/empty output as w=h=0, which already fails resolution_ok) so one hung device degrades gracefully instead of crashing the multi-link scan.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced from the code. At /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gmsl/video.py:94-95 the `--get-fmt-video` call uses `timeout=10` but is NOT wrapped in try/except, whereas the sibling `--stream-mmap` call at lines 104-111 explicitly catches `subprocess.TimeoutExpired` and degrades to `captured = 0`. Every other real-hw subprocess in the codebase follows the guarded pattern (memory.py:116-120, tegra.py:245, bert.py:322), so the unguarded call is an outlier, not intentional design.

The escape path is real and unhandled at every level. `subprocess.TimeoutExpired` subclasses `SubprocessError`/`Exception` (verified via MRO), NOT RuntimeError/ValueError/NotImplementedError — so the CLI's outer handlers in main() (cli.py:451-468) do NOT catch it; it would surface as an uncaught traceback, not the documented EXIT_UNAVAIL/EXIT_IO. In check_deserializer (video.py:147-157) the multi-link real path loops over links calling check_gmsl(mock=False) with no per-link guard, so one hung camera on a quad/hex part (e.g. MAX96712) aborts the whole loop and discards the verdicts of the healthy links. In harness.run_test_plan (harness.py:107-108) the gmsl health check has no per-item try/except either, so a single hung link aborts the entire test plan.

Technical realism: the author set timeout=10 precisely because a VIDIOC_G_FMT ioctl can block on a wedged V4L2 driver / stuck I2C bus to the deserializer — exactly the locked-but-dead-link failure mode this module targets (the code's own comment at lines 100-102 calls hangs out as the likely failure). The timeout is dead weight without a handler: it converts an infinite hang into an uncaught crash rather than graceful degradation. The tests (tests/test_gmsl.py, tests/test_gmsl_format.py) run only mock mode and never exercise this path, so nothing covers it.

Adjusted to high (from medium): per the project rubric, 'a real-hw path that crashes' is high. The impact is a crash plus loss of all other links' results on a multi-link scan and abort of the whole test plan — more than an edge case. It is not 'critical' because it produces a loud crash, not a silent wrong PASS/FAIL verdict or data corruption. The suggested fix (wrap in try/except subprocess.TimeoutExpired, treat timeout/empty as w=h=0 so resolution_ok already fails) is correct and matches the existing --stream-mmap degradation pattern.  

**H25. lock_status() indexed by position, not by link field — wrong link's lock state on a real backend**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py:77 · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** relocked = serdes.lock_status()[link].locked assumes the returned list is ordered so element [link] has .link == link; the SerDesLink contract does not guarantee ordering or that every index is present, so a real backend returning locks out of order (or only for active links) silently verdicts the wrong link, and an index >= len(list) raises a bare IndexError instead of SerDesError.  
- **Evidence:** Line 77: `relocked = serdes.lock_status()[link].locked`. This is the ONLY positional index of lock_status() in the codebase: serdes.py:293 (negotiated_mode) searches `for ll in self.lock_status(): if ll.link == link`, and checks.py / serdes.py all_locked iterate `for li in links`. The ABC docstring (serdes.py:259-266) only promises 'one entry per info().links' and '0-based link indices', not positional order. The mock happens to build the list in order (serdes.py:441 `for link in range(self._info.links)`), so the test cannot expose this.  
- **Fix:** Look up the lock by field: `lock = next((ll for ll in serdes.lock_status() if ll.link == link), None); if lock is None: raise SerDesError(f"link {link} not present"); relocked = lock.locked` — or reuse serdes.negotiated_mode(link)/a per-link accessor so the wrong-order/missing-link case can't mislabel a corner.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed. src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py:77 reads `relocked = serdes.lock_status()[link].locked` by POSITION, while the SerDesLink contract (serdes.py:259-266, 289) only promises "one entry per info().links" and "0-based link indices" — it never guarantees that element [i] has .link == i.
