This is the lone positional index of lock_status() in the codebase: every other consumer matches by field — negotiated_mode (serdes.py:293-296) iterates `if ll.link == link` and raises SerDesError if absent, and all_locked/all_in_mode/check_serdes_link (serdes.py:357-363, checks.py:71-79) iterate order-independently. The MockSerDes builds the list strictly in order (serdes.py:441 `for link in range(self._info.links)`), so test_gmsl_shmoo.py can never expose the mismatch — and vt_shmoo has no pragma, so this is exactly the "logic bug the mock cannot catch on the anticipated real ADI-register backend" the conventions call high-value.

I reproduced both failure modes against the live package (PYTHONPATH=src):
1. A backend returning lock_status() reversed (still contract-valid: "one entry per links") makes a shmoo of an UNLOCKED link 0 report relocked=True and aeq_reconverged=True — a wrong PASS verdict attributing link 1's lock state to link 0. Worse, the subsequent read_eom(link, direction) keys off the field-correct link, so the lock verdict and the eye read can refer to two different physical links.
2. A backend whose list is shorter than `link` (e.g. only active links reported) raises a bare IndexError, not SerDesError, breaking the documented "readers raise SerDesError" invariant (serdes.py:262-263) and the `except SerDesError` callers rely on.

Not refuted by any guard/test, and not intentional — it is inconsistent with the codebase's own field-based reading of the same contract. Severity high (not critical): it yields a wrong aeq_reconverged PASS/FAIL on a real backend, but only when that backend returns lock_status non-positionally (not guaranteed by contract, yet not the likely default), and it does not corrupt data. Suggested fix is correct: look up by .link field and raise SerDesError when absent (mirroring negotiated_mode), or reuse a per-link accessor.  

**H26. No chamber/rail settle before reading — eye margin attributed to the wrong V/T corner on real hardware**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py:72-86 (vt_shmoo loop) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** After chamber.set_temperature(temp) and psu.set_voltage(volt) the code immediately forces relock and reads the eye, never waiting for the chamber to slew or confirming the rail/temperature reached setpoint, so on real hardware the EOM eye and die temp are read at the PREVIOUS corner's conditions and recorded against the new corner.  
- **Evidence:** Lines 73-80: `chamber.set_temperature(temp)` ... `psu.set_voltage(volt); serdes.force_relock(link); ... die = serdes.read_temperature(); eom = serdes.read_eom(...)`. ThermalChamber.settled() exists for exactly this (instruments.py:918-921: 'poll before trusting a measurement at a new corner') and is never called. A real chamber takes minutes to go -40C<->+105C; the docstring claims it 'Captures the on-die junction temperature at each corner.' The mock returns instantly so the test (test_gmsl_shmoo.py) cannot catch the slew.  
- **Fix:** After set_temperature, poll chamber.settled(tolerance_c=...) with a bounded timeout (and optionally verify psu.measure_voltage() is within tolerance of setpoint) before force_relock/read; raise/record a corner failure if it never settles. Capturing die_temp after settle also makes the recorded die_temp meaningful.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced directly from the code. In src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py:72-86 the vt_shmoo loop does chamber.set_temperature(temp) -> psu.set_voltage(volt) -> serdes.force_relock(link) -> serdes.read_temperature() -> serdes.read_eom(...) with NO wait/poll between programming the setpoint and reading. The purpose-built guard exists but is never used: ThermalChamber.settled(tolerance_c=2.0) at instruments.py:918-921, whose own docstring says "poll before trusting a measurement at a new corner." A grep of all of src/ shows settled() is called nowhere, and vt_shmoo (gmsl/__init__.py exports it) is the only entrypoint — there is no wrapper that pre-settles, and the caller cannot settle because set_temperature is invoked internally per corner. On real hardware set_temperature only programs a setpoint (SOUR:TEMP) that slews over minutes across the AEC-Q100 Grade-2 -40..+105 C range, so the EOM eye and on-die temp are measured while the chamber is still at (or transiting from) the previous corner and recorded against the new corner — exactly the misattribution the finding describes; the docstring even claims it "Captures the on-die junction temperature at each corner," which it does not actually guarantee. The mock cannot catch this: _MockSCPI._respond reflects the last SOUR:TEMP write as the MEAS:TEMP? answer instantly (instruments.py:177-193), and test_gmsl_shmoo.py only asserts the SCPI write strings were sent and that the injected die temp comes back, never any slew/settle behavior. This is a real-hw logic defect of the class the conventions say to flag (a bug inside a real-hw path that the mock structurally cannot expose), not intentional design or honesty-discipline. Severity high is correct: it corrupts the recorded eye-margin/die-temp numbers and can mask a corner whose eye actually closes at temperature (potential false PASS on all_corners_eye_open), but it does not deterministically flip every verdict, so it sits at high rather than critical. Suggested fix stands: after set_temperature, poll chamber.settled(...) with a bounded timeout (optionally also verify psu.measure_voltage() within tolerance), and record/raise a corner failure if it never settles, then read eye and die_temp.  

**H27. Edge-jitter budget uses raw max() of signed samples — a large negative (early) gate transition silently passes**  
`src/computetest/tsn/qbv.py` · src/computetest/tsn/qbv.py:111,115 (check_qbv_gate_timing) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Window-edge jitter is the signed spread between scheduled and actual gate transitions (a gate can fire early = negative or late = positive); using `max(edge_jitter_ns)` measures only the worst late excursion, so a large early excursion that exceeds the budget passes the jitter check.  
- **Evidence:** `max_jitter = max(edge_jitter_ns) if edge_jitter_ns else 0.0` and `"edge_jitter_ok": max_jitter <= max_jitter_ns`. The module docstring calls this 'the spread between scheduled and actual gate transitions', which is inherently signed. Reproduced: `edge_jitter_ns=[-90.0, 5.0]` with default 50ns limit -> `max_jitter_ns=5.0`, `edge_jitter_ok=True`, a 90ns early excursion passes. The sibling module gptp.py (same Sprint 4.2, same author, same 'error vs limit' pattern) correctly does `max_te = max(abs(s) for s in samples_ns)` at gptp.py:61-62, establishing the intended convention.  
- **Fix:** Use `max_jitter = max(abs(j) for j in edge_jitter_ns) if edge_jitter_ns else 0.0` to bound the magnitude of the worst transition in either direction, matching gptp.py's Max|TE| convention.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and independently reproduced. At /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/tsn/qbv.py:111 the code computes `max_jitter = max(edge_jitter_ns) if edge_jitter_ns else 0.0` and at line 115 checks `"edge_jitter_ok": max_jitter <= max_jitter_ns`. The module docstring (lines 7-8) defines edge-jitter as "the spread between scheduled and actual gate transitions" — an inherently signed quantity (a gate can fire early=negative or late=positive). Reproduced by running the function: edge_jitter_ns=[-90.0, 5.0] with the default 50ns limit yields max_jitter_ns=5.0, edge_jitter_ok=True, overall ok=True — a 90ns early (negative) excursion that is 1.8x the budget silently passes, while a symmetric +90ns late excursion correctly fails. No upstream guard normalizes the samples: a repo-wide search shows edge_jitter is referenced only in qbv.py and the test, the parameter is Sequence[float] (not non-negative), and emit_qbv passes raw signed jit values into the series (line 135). The test test_edge_jitter_budget (tests/test_tsn_qbv.py:60-66) only uses positive samples [10.0,90.0], so it would pass identically with or without the bug — it does not exercise the negative path and masks the defect. The sibling module gptp.py (same Sprint 4.2, same LESS_THAN_OR_EQUAL/error<=limit pattern) correctly does max(abs(s) for s in samples_ns) at lines 61-62 with |TE| notation, establishing the intended magnitude-against-budget convention; qbv.py deviates from it. This is not intentional (docstring/data model say signed), not Mock-fidelity (pure reduction function), and not honesty-discipline. The suggested fix max(abs(j) for j in edge_jitter_ns) matches the gptp.py convention. Severity is high: it produces a wrong PASS verdict for a real TAS scheduling defect, conditional on the analyzer feeding signed samples (which the docstring and type say it does).  

**H28. 0.8x Respond window is not in IEEE 802.1Qbu and can wrongly FAIL a conformant device**  
`src/computetest/tsn/preemption.py` · preemption.py:21,71-80 (RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION, run_verify_respond) + module docstring lines 5-7 + docs/tsn/clause99_preemption_coverage_matrix.md:21 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The handshake is failed unless respond_delay_ms <= 0.8 * verify_time_ms, but IEEE 802.1Qbu defines no 0.8x sub-window: the LP/LV state machine accepts a Respond (rRespond) any time before the verifyTime timer expires (and retries up to verifyLimit). A spec-conformant DUT that responds at, e.g., 0.85x verifyTime is therefore reported as a Verify/Respond FAIL, an incorrect conformance verdict.  
- **Evidence:** RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION = 0.8 ... `return respond_delay_ms <= RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION * verify_time_ms`; docstring 'Respond within 0.8x the window'; matrix row 99.5.A titled 'Respond: handshake within 0.8x window' marked Mandatory. No 0.8 factor exists in 802.1Qbu/30.14 — the criterion is simply 'Respond received before verifyTime elapses'.  
- **Fix:** Either change the criterion to respond_delay_ms <= verify_time_ms (the actual spec bound) and drop the 0.8 constant, or, if 0.8x is an intentional project guard-band rather than a conformance limit, rename the constant/notes to clearly mark it as a non-spec margin (e.g. RESPOND_MARGIN_FRACTION) and stop labeling it as the Clause-99 Respond pass/fail criterion in the docstring and coverage matrix.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced directly from the code. preemption.py:21 sets RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION = 0.8 and run_verify_respond (line 80) returns `respond_delay_ms <= RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION * verify_time_ms`. check_preemption calls this directly with no compensating guard, so the 0.8x factor is the sole handshake pass/fail criterion. IEEE 802.1Qbu / 802.3br Clause 99 (and 802.1Q Clause 30.14 management) define the verify/respond bound as simply: an SMD-R Respond received before the verifyTime timer (1-128 ms) expires sets verifyStatus=SUCCEEDED; otherwise retry up to verifyLimit then FAILED. There is no 0.8x sub-window anywhere in the standard. Consequently a spec-conformant DUT that responds between 0.8x and 1.0x of verifyTime (e.g. 9.0 ms against a 10 ms verifyTime — exactly the test_late_respond_fails case at 0.9x) is reported as a Verify/Respond conformance FAIL, an incorrect verdict.

Not refuted/intentional: this is not an honesty-discipline None-raise case. The 0.8x is actively used to compute PASS/FAIL and is explicitly presented as the Clause-99 criterion — preemption.py docstring lines 6-7 ('Respond within 0.8x the window'), the run_verify_respond docstring, and docs/tsn/clause99_preemption_coverage_matrix.md:21 row 99.5.A titled 'Respond: handshake within 0.8x window' marked Mandatory. No comment or naming anywhere flags it as a deliberate guard-band/margin; it is labeled as the spec bound. test_tsn_preemption.py::TestVerifyRespond::test_late_respond_fails (9.0 vs 10.0) also bakes the wrong criterion into the suite, so the test passes even though the code is wrong vs. spec.

Severity: kept at high but noting the direction — the 0.8x is stricter than spec, so it yields false FAILs (rejecting good hardware), not false PASSes that mask a broken DUT. A wrong conformance verdict on a passing device still mis-reports Clause-99 results and would send a field engineer chasing a non-defect, which warrants high (wrong PASS/FAIL verdict), not critical (no data corruption / no broken device passing). Suggested fix is sound: either use respond_delay_ms <= verify_time_ms as the real spec bound, or, if a guard-band is genuinely wanted, rename the constant (e.g. RESPOND_MARGIN_FRACTION) and stop labeling it as the Clause-99 Respond pass/fail criterion in the docstring and the Mandatory matrix row.  

**H29. parse_spd requires only >=14 bytes, so the correct DDR5 ECC byte (235) can never be read**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py` · src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py:36-37 (parse_spd length guard) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The length guard `if len(data) < 14` is sized for the wrong (DDR4) ECC offset. The real DDR5 ECC field is at byte 235, so even after the offset is corrected the parser would still accept a 14-byte buffer and either IndexError or be unable to read ECC. A real SPD5118 read should return the full 1024/512-byte image; a truncated read should be rejected, not silently accepted.  
- **Evidence:** `if len(data) < 14: raise ValueError(f"SPD too short: {len(data)} bytes (need >= 14)")` then accesses up to data[13]. The DDR5 ECC byte (235) is far beyond this minimum.  
- **Fix:** Once ECC moves to byte 235, raise if len(data) < 236 (or the full SPD block length). Keep a clear message distinguishing 'truncated read' from 'not a DDR5 SPD'.  
- **Verifier:** The underlying defect is real and demonstrable. In JESD400-5 (DDR5 SPD), the ECC/bus-extension indication lives at byte 235 (0xEB, "Memory Channel Bus Width", bits 4-3 = "Bus extensions per sub-channel / ECC width"). The code instead reads ecc=bool(data[13] & 0x08) at src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py:42. DDR5 byte 13 (0x0D) is "SDRAM Duty Cycle Adjuster / Partial Array Self Refresh" — wholly unrelated to ECC (byte 13 IS the bus-width/ECC byte in DDR4, which is exactly why the finding calls it a DDR4-era offset). Confirmed via multiple authoritative JESD400-5 references. Consequence on the real-hw path: against a real SPD5118 image, the ECC verdict in check_spd() is derived from an unrelated PASR/duty-cycle bit, so the module-conformance PASS/FAIL on ECC is meaningless. The synthetic 16-byte corpus and its expected.json only pass because both were hand-built around the same wrong byte 13, so the test cannot catch this. The finding's headline impact ("the correct DDR5 ECC byte 235 can never be read") and its truncated-read point are accurate, and the >=14 guard does let a 16-byte stub through (the real corpus is only 16 bytes, not a full 512/1024-byte SPD5118 image). Two framing imprecisions, which I note but do not consider disqualifying: (1) the defect's true location is the wrong byte index at line 42, not the length guard at lines 36-37 — the guard is internally consistent with reading data[13]; (2) the IndexError sub-claim is conditional ("once the offset is corrected"), not current behavior — today data[13] does not IndexError on a 14-byte buffer. Severity stays high: a wrong spec field in a real-hardware SPD parser that feeds a conformance verdict (not critical, because it surfaces only against real SPD images and the immediate effect is a wrong ECC sub-check rather than guaranteed data corruption). Suggested fix should read byte 235 (bits 4-3) for ECC and require the full SPD block length, distinguishing a truncated read from a non-DDR5 SPD.  

**H30. Corpus expected.json is hand-built to satisfy the buggy parser, so tests cannot catch the spec error (tautological corpus)**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py` · corpus/ddr5_spd/synthetic-ddr5-32gb/spd.hex + expected.json; tests/test_ddr5_spd.py:29-30 · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The 'known-good SPD' corpus is a synthetic 16-byte blob crafted so the parser's current (DDR4-layout) decode matches expected.json. test_parses_to_expected asserts parser output == expected, where expected was derived from the parser itself — it validates the code against itself, not against JESD400-5, so it would still pass even though the ECC byte is wrong.  
- **Evidence:** spd.hex is only 16 bytes ('30101201040000000000000000080000'); byte 13=0x08 is set precisely so `ecc=True`. expected.json mirrors the parser output exactly. No independently-sourced/vendor SPD image is used to anchor correctness.  
- **Fix:** Anchor the corpus to a real or vendor-documented DDR5 SPD image (full length, byte 235 populated) and derive expected.json from the JESD400-5 spec, not from the parser. Add a negative case where byte 235 ECC bits and byte 13 disagree to lock in the correct field.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced from the code. The corpus is tautological as claimed: corpus/ddr5_spd/synthetic-ddr5-32gb/spd.hex is a synthetic 16-byte blob ("30101201040000000000000000080000") and expected.json (memory_type:18, module_type:RDIMM, density_gbit:16, ecc:true) exactly mirrors parse_spd()'s output of that blob. tests/test_ddr5_spd.py:29-30 asserts parse_spd(_spd_bytes()).to_dict() == _expected(), where _expected() just reads the parser-derived JSON — it validates the parser against itself, not against JESD400-5. No vendor/spec-anchored SPD image exists (the only corpus dir is the synthetic one). The masked bug is real: spd.py:42 decodes ecc=bool(data[13] & 0x08), a DDR4-style heuristic, and line 35 labels byte 13 as a JESD400-5 field. Per JESD400-5, byte 13 (0x00D) is SDRAM Duty Cycle Adjuster / Partial Array Self Refresh, NOT ECC; ECC presence lives in byte 235 (0xEB, Memory Channel Bus Width, bits 4-3 "bus extensions per sub-channel"). So on a real SPD image read over SMBus/I3C, the ecc verdict reflects DCA/PASR bits, i.e. garbage — and the tautological corpus would still pass. The 16-byte blob does not even contain byte 235, so the true ECC field is never exercised. The docstring's "scaffold/not a full decode" disclaimer covers incompleteness but does not excuse decoding the wrong byte and labeling it a JESD400-5 ECC field. Not a pragma real-hw path (parse_spd is unit-tested pure parsing). Severity high is correct: a weak/circular test that passes even though the code is wrong, masking a wrong-spec-value ECC decode that would mislead a real-hardware RAS verdict. Minor caveat: the finding blends the corpus-quality issue with the parser bug, but its core test-quality thesis is fully demonstrated, so no downgrade. Suggested fix (anchor expected.json to spec/vendor data, full-length image with byte 235, plus a negative case where byte 235 ECC bits and byte 13 disagree) is sound.  

**H31. BUSY return code is 0x0009 but CXL spec Busy = 0x0006 (0x0009 = FW Transfer Out of Order)**  
`src/computetest/cxl/mailbox.py` · src/computetest/cxl/mailbox.py:47 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** CciReturnCode.BUSY = 0x0009 contradicts the CXL CCI Command Return Codes table, where Busy is 0006h and 0009h is 'FW Transfer Out of Order'; a real-hw backend importing CciReturnCode.BUSY to decode a device response would mislabel a Busy as something else and vice-versa.  
- **Evidence:** class CciReturnCode(IntEnum):
    SUCCESS = 0x0000
    BACKGROUND_STARTED = 0x0001
    INVALID_INPUT = 0x0002
    UNSUPPORTED = 0x0003
    BUSY = 0x0009   # spec Busy is 0x0006; 0x0009 is FW Transfer Out of Order  
- **Fix:** Change BUSY to 0x0006 per the CXL CCI Command Return Codes table (Success 0000h, Background Command Started 0001h, Invalid Input 0002h, Unsupported 0003h, Internal Error 0004h, Retry Required 0005h, Busy 0006h). If left provisional, keep it None/raise rather than encode a value known to collide with FW Transfer Out of Order.  
- **Verifier:** Verified against the authoritative CXL CCI "Command Return Codes" table (QEMU's CXLRetCode enum in include/hw/cxl/cxl_device.h mirrors CXL r3.x Table 8-34): Busy = 0x06 and FW Transfer Out of Order = 0x09. The code at src/computetest/cxl/mailbox.py:47 sets BUSY = 0x0009, which is objectively the wrong value and collides exactly with the FW Transfer Out of Order code. The other four enum members are correct (SUCCESS=0x0000, BACKGROUND_STARTED=0x0001, INVALID_INPUT=0x0002, UNSUPPORTED=0x0003), so only BUSY is mis-encoded.

The finding's spec claim is factually correct and the mislabel direction is right: a real-hw/QEMU backend (which the module docstring explicitly says will subclass MailboxTransport) decoding a raw device return code would have a device's 0x0006 Busy fail to match CciReturnCode.BUSY, and a device's 0x0009 FW-Transfer-Out-of-Order falsely match it.

Not excused by project conventions. The honesty-discipline rule is "set unknown values to None and RAISE rather than fake a pass" — here the code instead committed a concrete value that is known-wrong and aliases another defined spec code, which is precisely the kind of misleading encoding the discipline forbids. The "representative subset / confirm against spec before encoding as assertions" docstring caveat covers omission, not encoding a wrong-but-confident value. The .ok / .background_started properties only test SUCCESS/BACKGROUND_STARTED so they are unaffected, and no current code path or test consumes BUSY (maintenance.py uses an independent string-based mock), so there is no present-day wrong PASS/FAIL verdict — this is a latent defect.

Kept at high (= wrong spec value that would misparse on real hardware), consistent with the severity rubric. It is not critical today only because nothing yet decodes raw hardware codes against this enum; once a real backend is wired in, it would mislabel Busy responses. Suggested fix is correct: set BUSY = 0x0006, or leave it None/raise rather than encode a value that collides with FW Transfer Out of Order.  

**H32. UE bit map wrong for bits >=12: InternalError/IDE-Tx/IDE-Rx shifted down by two, IDE-Rx (bit 16) missing entirely**  
`src/computetest/cxl/ras.py` · src/computetest/cxl/ras.py:28-30 (CXL_UE_BITS) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The CXL RAS Uncorrectable Error Status Register places Internal_Error at bit 14, CXL_IDE_Tx_Error at bit 15, and CXL_IDE_Rx_Error at bit 16, with bits 12 and 13 Reserved; the module instead maps bit 12->InternalError, 13->IDETxError, 14->IDERxError and never defines bits 15/16, so on real hardware these uncorrectable errors are mis-decoded and IDE-Rx errors are silently dropped.  
- **Evidence:**     12: ("InternalError", "device internal error"),
    13: ("IDETxError", "CXL IDE Tx error"),
    14: ("IDERxError", "CXL IDE Rx error"),
  ...no bit 15 or 16. CXL spec / kernel pci_regs.h define CXL_RAS_UC_INTERNAL_ERR=BIT(14), CXL_RAS_UC_IDE_TX_ERR=BIT(15), CXL_RAS_UC_IDE_RX_ERR=BIT(16); bits 12-13 are Reserved.  
- **Fix:** Remap to spec: 14:("InternalError",...), 15:("IDETxError",...), 16:("IDERxError",...); leave bits 12-13 undefined (Reserved). Bits 0-11 are already correct.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed against two independent authoritative sources. (1) rasdaemon ras-cxl-handler.c — the exact file the module docstring claims to mirror "verbatim" — defines cxl_aer_ue[] as bits 0-11 (cache/mem parity/ECC/REINIT/encoding/poison/overflow), then Component-Specific/Internal = BIT(14), IDE Tx = BIT(15), IDE Rx = BIT(16), with bits 12-13 reserved. (2) Kernel drivers/cxl/cxl.h defines CXL_RAS_UNCORRECTABLE_STATUS_MASK = GENMASK(16,14) | GENMASK(11,0), independently confirming valid UE bits are 0-11 and 14-16 with 12-13 reserved.

The module's CXL_UE_BITS (src/computetest/cxl/ras.py:28-30) instead maps 12->InternalError, 13->IDETxError, 14->IDERxError and never defines bits 15 or 16. So the three high errors are shifted down by two and IDE-Rx (true bit 16) is silently dropped. Bits 0-11 are correct, as the finding states.

This is a real-hardware impact, not a mock artifact: CXL_UE_BITS is the single shared decode table for both Mock and Real backends. On real hardware, snapshot()/decode_ue()/emit_cxl_ras() would mis-name a real Internal Error (HW bit 14) as "IDERxError", mis-name IDE Tx (bit 15), and entirely omit a real IDE Rx error (bit 16) from the decoded list and the per-bit cxl.ue.* measurements. The pass/fail verdict is not corrupted because it derives from ue_raw != 0 (so a set bit 16 still flags failure), which is why this is high rather than critical. The existing test (tests/test_cxl_ras.py) only checks bit 12 as InternalError, encoding the same wrong mapping, so it locks in the bug rather than catching it. Conventions (honesty discipline, pragma, mock fidelity) do not apply — this is an objectively wrong spec value on a shared decode path. Suggested fix (remap to 14/15/16, leave 12-13 reserved) is correct.  

**H33. Snapshot/RAS tests assert the wrong bit-to-name mapping, so they pass with the buggy table (tautological against the defect)**  
`src/computetest/cxl/ras.py` · tests/test_cxl_ras.py:29-34, 47-48 and tests/test_cxl_emit.py:47-50 · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The tests encode the same off-by-two mapping the source is wrong about (bit 12 -> InternalError, bit 13 -> IDETxError), so they validate the bug rather than the CXL spec and would still pass even after a hardware-correct fix is rejected.  
- **Evidence:** test_cxl_ras.py:29 `CxlRasRegisters(ue_status=(1 << 12), ...)` then :33 `assert "InternalError" in [...]`; :48 `set_sticky(..., 1 << 13)  # IDETxError won't clear`; test_cxl_emit.py:47-50 `ue_status=(1 << 12)` -> `assert "cxl.ue.InternalError"`. No test asserts InternalError at the spec bit 14 or exercises IDE-Rx at bit 16.  
- **Fix:** Rewrite the assertions to use the spec bit positions (InternalError=bit14, IDETxError=bit15, IDERxError=bit16) and add a case for bit 16 so a dropped/mislabeled high bit fails the suite.  
- **Verifier:** CONFIRMED. The source table in src/computetest/cxl/ras.py:28-30 maps InternalError=bit12, IDETxError=bit13, IDERxError=bit14. This is wrong per the canonical CXL spec / Linux kernel and the very source it cites (rasdaemon ras-cxl-handler.c). The Linux kernel header drivers/cxl/cxl.h defines CXL_RAS_UNCORRECTABLE_STATUS_MASK = (GENMASK(16,14) | GENMASK(11,0)), i.e. bits 0-11 valid, bits 12-13 RESERVED, and Internal=BIT(14), IDE_TX=BIT(15), IDE_RX=BIT(16) (also confirmed in rasdaemon: CXL_AER_UE_INTERNAL_ERR BIT(14), CXL_AER_UE_IDE_TX_ERR BIT(15), CXL_AER_UE_IDE_RX_ERR BIT(16)). The source's comment claiming the bits are "verbatim from rasdaemon" is false. The tests encode the same off-by-two mapping in lockstep: test_cxl_ras.py:29/33/34 feed ue_status=(1<<12) and assert "InternalError"; :47-48/:51 use 1<<13 labeled "IDETxError won't clear"; test_cxl_emit.py:47/50 feed 1<<12 and assert "cxl.ue.InternalError". I ran the suite: all 13 tests pass against the buggy table (PYTHONPATH=src python3 -m pytest tests/test_cxl_ras.py tests/test_cxl_emit.py). A spec-correct fix (Internal->14) would FAIL these tests, so the suite would actively reject the correct fix — tautological. No test asserts InternalError at bit 14 or exercises IDE Rx at bit 16. I demonstrated the real-hardware impact: decode_ue(1<<14) returns IDERxError (mislabels a real Internal Error), and decode_ue(1<<15)/decode_ue(1<<16) return [] (real IDE Tx/Rx uncorrectable errors are SILENTLY DROPPED since the table stops at bit 14), while reserved bits 12/13 decode to bogus errors. This is not a mock-fidelity, pragma real-hw, or honesty-discipline exception — the decode runs on actual hardware status words and mislabels/drops uncorrectable errors. Both the finding's specific test-quality claim and the underlying source mapping bug are real. Severity kept at high: as a test-quality finding the tautological tests would block a hardware-correct fix and give false confidence; the underlying decode mislabels and drops uncorrectable RAS errors on real CXL hardware.  

**H34. test_type1_cache_only feeds a Type-1 device hdm_d_capable=True/bi_snoop_capable=True (physically impossible) and so masks the false-FAIL bug**  
`src/computetest/cxl/device.py` · tests/test_cxl_device.py:36-40 (TestCoherency.test_type1_cache_only) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The only Type-1 test passes hdm_d_capable=True, bi_snoop_capable=True for a cache-only device that by definition has no HDM, which is the one input combination that makes the buggy coherency_bridging check pass; no test exercises a realistic Type-1 device (hdm_d_capable=False), so the suite is green while the code would FAIL conformant Type-1 hardware in the field.  
- **Evidence:** h = check_coherency(device_type=CxlDeviceType.TYPE1, claims_cache=True, claims_mem=False, hdm_d_capable=True, bi_snoop_capable=True); assert h.ok. A Type-1 device has no device memory, so hdm_d_capable=True is not a valid real-world state for it.  
- **Fix:** After fixing check_coherency, change the Type-1 test to the realistic case: hdm_d_capable=False, bi_snoop_capable=False and assert h.ok; add a separate Type-2 test that asserts coherency_bridging FAILs when HDM-D[B]/BISnoop is absent.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and independently reproduced. CXL spec semantics: HDM-D / HDM-DB and BI-Snoop (BISnoop) are coherency mechanisms for *device-attached* memory (Host-managed Device Memory). A Type-1 device (CXL.cache only, NO device memory) has no HDM region of any kind, so a conformant Type-1 device reports hdm_d_capable=False, bi_snoop_capable=False. The finding's factual premise — that hdm_d_capable=True is not a valid real-world state for Type-1 — is correct.

The code in src/computetest/cxl/device.py:48,53 treats TYPE1 as cache_capable and then requires `hdm_d_capable and bi_snoop_capable` for any cache_capable device. I ran it: a realistic conformant Type-1 (claims_cache=True, claims_mem=False, hdm_d_capable=False, bi_snoop_capable=False) yields checks['coherency_bridging']=False and ok=False — a false-FAIL verdict on conformant hardware. The sole Type-1 test, tests/test_cxl_device.py:36-40 (test_type1_cache_only), feeds hdm_d_capable=True, bi_snoop_capable=True, which is exactly the one (physically-impossible-for-Type-1) input combination that flips coherency_bridging back to True, so the assertion `h.ok` passes and the bug stays hidden. I confirmed the file's 8 tests are all green. No other caller and no other test exercises a realistic hdm_d_capable=False Type-1 case (test_type2_needs_coherency_bridging covers Type-2 absent-bridging, not Type-1).

This is not intentional design: the module docstring and roadmap item 4.4.8 only call for asserting HDM-D[B]/BISnoop on memory-bearing devices and that 'a Type-3 doesn't claim cache'; nothing justifies requiring HDM-D[B] on a memory-less Type-1. No guard or honesty-discipline convention covers it.

Severity high is correct for this test-quality finding: the weak test masks a real defect whose field consequence is a wrong FAIL verdict (flagging conformant Type-1 accelerators as non-conformant). The suggested fix is sound, though I'd note the suggested 'add a Type-2 absent-bridging FAIL test' already exists (test_type2_needs_coherency_bridging); the genuinely missing case is a realistic Type-1 (hdm_d_capable=False, bi_snoop_capable=False) that should assert ok after the check_coherency bug is fixed.  

**H35. Empty lanes list yields a vacuous PASS — no eye measured but all_lanes_eye_open=True**  
`src/computetest/cxl/retimer.py` · src/computetest/cxl/retimer.py:48-54 (check_cxl_retimer) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Passing lanes=[] (a non-None but empty sequence) makes eyes_ok=all(...) over an empty iterable return True, so all_lanes_eye_open is True and CxlRetimerHealth.ok can be True even though zero lanes were actually probed — a wrong PASS verdict.  
- **Evidence:** lane_iter = range(info.lanes) if lanes is None else lanes  # lanes=[] -> lane_iter=[]\n eyes_ok = all(eye_quality_verdict(...) for lane in lane_iter)  # all([]) == True\nConfirmed at runtime: check_cxl_retimer(rt, expect_part='PT6161L', lanes=[]) -> ok=True, checks={'part_identity_ok':True,'temperature_ok':True,'all_lanes_eye_open':True}. Mirrors CxlRetimerHealth.ok which guards itself with bool(self.checks), but the eye sub-check has no analogous emptiness guard.  
- **Fix:** Materialize the lane list and reject empty/0-lane cases before the all(): e.g. `lanes_list = list(range(info.lanes)) if lanes is None else list(lanes); if not lanes_list: raise ValueError('no lanes to check')` (or set all_lanes_eye_open=False). Add a test for lanes=[].  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed at runtime and in source. /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/cxl/retimer.py:48-49 does `lane_iter = range(info.lanes) if lanes is None else lanes` then `eyes_ok = all(eye_quality_verdict(...) for lane in lane_iter)`. With `lanes=[]` (a valid non-None empty Sequence on a public, documented parameter), `all([])` returns True, so `all_lanes_eye_open=True` and `CxlRetimerHealth.ok=True` despite zero lanes being probed. I reproduced exactly: `check_cxl_retimer(rt, expect_part='PT6161L', lanes=[])` -> ok=True, checks={'part_identity_ok':True,'temperature_ok':True,'all_lanes_eye_open':True}.

Refutation attempts all fail: (1) The `CxlRetimerHealth.ok` guard `bool(self.checks) and all(...)` (retimer.py:30) does NOT mask this — `checks` always has its 3 keys, so it is non-empty; the vacuous truth lives inside the unguarded `eyes_ok` sub-check. (2) No test covers `lanes=[]`; the closest, test_lane_subset (tests/test_cxl_retimer.py:31-34), uses `lanes=[0,1]`. (3) Not intentional — the opposite: the same author explicitly guards the identical "all eyes open" pattern against empty collections elsewhere. gmsl/shmoo.py:88-89 uses `bool(points) and all(p.eye_pass for p in points)` and `bool(points) and all(p.relocked ...)`; ddr5/eye.py:32 uses `bool(self.checks) and all(...)`. The CXL retimer omitting the `bool(...) and` guard is an inconsistent oversight, not deliberate design. (4) This violates the project's honesty discipline (flag anything that fakes a PASS): it reports all_lanes_eye_open=True having opened zero eyes.

Severity held at high (not critical): the wrong-PASS verdict only fires on an explicit `lanes=[]` input; the default/common path (`lanes=None`) iterates `range(info.lanes)` where lanes defaults to 16 and is never 0 in practice, so normal runs are unaffected. Suggested fix is sound — materialize the lane list and reject empty (raise, per honesty discipline) or set all_lanes_eye_open=False, mirroring the `bool(...) and all(...)` guard already used in gmsl/shmoo.py and ddr5/eye.py; add a regression test for lanes=[].  

**H36. inject_general_media_event omits four QEMU-required QMP fields; the call is rejected before any injection occurs**  
`sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py` · sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py:46-50 (CxlInjector.inject_general_media_event) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The cxl-inject-general-media-event QMP command in qemu-system 11.0.0 has eight required members (path, log, flags, dpa, descriptor, type, transaction-type, sub-type); this method only sends path, log, flags, dpa, so on real QEMU the command fails QMP arg validation and never injects an event — the GMER read/clear path it is meant to feed gets no real state.  
- **Evidence:** Code sends only 4 args:
    return self._qmp.execute("cxl-inject-general-media-event", path=path,
                             log=log, flags=flags, dpa=dpa)
Reproduced against /opt/homebrew/bin/qemu-system-x86_64 (v11.0.0). query-qmp-schema reports GMER REQUIRED: ['path','log','flags','dpa','descriptor','type','transaction-type','sub-type']. Issuing the exact 4-arg call returns: {'error': {'class':'GenericError','desc':"Parameter 'descriptor' is missing"}}. Adding the 4 missing fields advances QEMU to 'Unable to resolve path' (i.e. arg shape then accepted), confirming the four omitted fields are the blocker.  
- **Fix:** Add the required parameters descriptor, type, transaction_type (sent as 'transaction-type'), and sub_type (sent as 'sub-type') to the method signature and the execute() call (Python identifiers map to hyphenated QMP keys via **kwargs only if you build the arguments dict explicitly, e.g. self._qmp.execute('cxl-inject-general-media-event', **{'path':path,'log':log,'flags':flags,'dpa':dpa,'descriptor':descriptor,'type':type_,'transaction-type':transaction_type,'sub-type':sub_type})). Expose the General Media-specific optionals (channel, rank, device, component-id) as needed.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced against the cited /opt/homebrew/bin/qemu-system-x86_64 v11.0.0. query-qmp-schema reports cxl-inject-general-media-event REQUIRED members = ['path','log','flags','dpa','descriptor','type','transaction-type','sub-type']; the four extra ('descriptor','type','transaction-type','sub-type') have no `default` in the schema and are mandatory. The code at sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py:46-50 sends only path, log, flags, dpa. Issuing that exact 4-arg call returns {'error':{'class':'GenericError','desc':"Parameter 'descriptor' is missing"}}; sending all 8 required fields advances QEMU to 'Unable to resolve path', confirming the four omitted fields are the blocker — exactly as claimed. On real QEMU the injection is rejected before any event is created, so the cxl/events.py GMER read/clear path the module exists to feed gets no state. Not refuted by conventions: this method has NO `# pragma: no cover - real-hw path`, and the only test (tests/test_cxl_qmp_inject.py:56-65) asserts against a FakeQMPServer that records args without modeling QEMU's required-member validation, so it passes despite the code being wrong against real QEMU (a weak test). It is not the deliberate None-raises honesty pattern. Downgraded from critical to high: QEMU returns a hard QMP GenericError rather than silently faking a PASS, so this is a real-hw path that errors/misbuilds the command (the convention's 'high' = real-hw path that crashes/misparses) rather than producing a falsely-passing verdict. Fix: include descriptor, type, transaction-type, sub-type (built via an explicit args dict so the hyphenated QMP keys are sent), and expose the General-Media optionals (channel, rank, device, component-id) as needed.  

**H37. GMER test passes despite the command being invalid on real QEMU (over-mocked: fake server never validates arguments)**  
`sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py` · tests/test_cxl_qmp_inject.py:56-65 (test_inject_correctable_and_event) and tests/test_qmp_inject.py:65-66 (FakeQMPServer default branch) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The only test of inject_general_media_event asserts just that the command name appears and dpa==0x2000; the FakeQMPServer answers every non-HMP command with {'return': {}} regardless of arguments, so the test would pass even though real QEMU rejects the call for missing required fields — the test gives false confidence that the real-hw RAS-event path works.  
- **Evidence:** FakeQMPServer fallback: `else: conn.sendall(b'{"return": {}}\r\n')` — no schema/required-field check. Test only asserts: `assert 'cxl-inject-general-media-event' in execs` and `_cmd(...)['arguments']['dpa'] == 0x2000`. It does not assert the full required argument set.  
- **Fix:** Assert the complete required-argument set for each cxl-inject-* command (path, log, flags, dpa, descriptor, type, transaction-type, sub-type for GMER), and/or have the fake server validate against the QEMU 11.0 required-field list so a missing field fails the test the way real QEMU would.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed, and the defect is deeper than the finding's test-quality framing. The QEMU v11.0.0 QAPI schema (qapi/cxl.json, tag v11.0.0 — matches FakeQMPServer's greeting major:11,minor:0) defines cxl-inject-general-media-event with required members dpa, descriptor, type, transaction-type, sub-type plus base path, log, flags. But sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py:46-50 inject_general_media_event sends only path, log, flags, dpa — omitting 4 REQUIRED fields (descriptor, type, transaction-type, sub-type), and the method signature physically can't supply them. On real QEMU this command is rejected (GenericError: Parameter 'descriptor' is missing) so no event is injected and QMPClient.execute would raise — the module's promise that the unmodified CXL events path reads real QEMU state fails at runtime. The over-mocked test masks this: FakeQMPServer (tests/test_qmp_inject.py:65-66) returns {'return':{}} for any non-HMP command regardless of arguments, and tests/test_cxl_qmp_inject.py:56-65 only asserts the command name is present and dpa==0x2000, so the suite is falsely green. Not exempted by conventions: no pragma, no TODO/stub marker, no honesty-discipline note; the docstring presents it as working. The other three commands in the file (poison, uncorrectable, correctable) were checked against the schema and are correct, so this is an isolated real bug, not scaffolding. Severity high (not critical): the real-QEMU path errors loudly rather than producing a silent wrong PASS/FAIL or data corruption, but it is a real-hw injection path that misparses/crashes per the conventions' high bar, and it leaves the GMER injection capability non-functional while CI shows green.  

**H38. AUDIT NOTE in the docstring is not discharged: emulator field requirements were not pinned to the actual QEMU schema**  
`sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py` · sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py:10-12 (module docstring AUDIT NOTE) · _honesty_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The module explicitly warns 'confirm QEMU's cxl_type3 actually emulates the ... event types ... this toolkit asserts', but the GMER command was coded to a reduced 4-field shape that the cited QEMU (11.0) does not accept, so the documented audit step was not actually performed for the event path.  
- **Evidence:** Docstring: 'AUDIT NOTE: confirm QEMU's cxl_type3 actually emulates the UE/CE bits, event types, and maintenance commands this toolkit asserts'. The coded GMER call (4 args) does not match the cxl-inject-general-media-event arg-type (8 required members) returned by query-qmp-schema on qemu-system 11.0.0.  
- **Fix:** Pin the command schemas to a known QEMU version (query-qmp-schema output checked into the repo or a schema-version assert), and reconcile each cxl-inject-* arg list against it; note the verified version in the docstring.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced from code + authoritative QEMU QAPI schema. At sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py:46-50 inject_general_media_event builds "cxl-inject-general-media-event" with only 4 members: path, log, flags, dpa. QEMU's qapi/cxl.json requires more: v9.0 requires path, log, flags, dpa, descriptor, type, transaction-type (7); master/recent requires those plus sub-type (8 — matching the finding's "8 required members"). channel/rank/device/component-id are the only optionals. So the call omits the required descriptor, type, transaction-type (and sub-type), and real qemu-system 11.0 QMP rejects a command missing required members — the event-injection feature cannot succeed against the very emulator this module exists to drive.

This is specifically the event path the docstring AUDIT NOTE (lines 10-12) singles out ("event types ... this toolkit asserts"). I confirmed the other three commands are correctly pinned: inject_poison sends path/start/length, inject_uncorrectable sends path/errors, inject_correctable sends path/type — each matches its schema exactly. Only the event command was not reconciled, so the documented audit step was genuinely not discharged for that path.

Not already-handled: there is no schema/version pin or assert, and tests/test_cxl_qmp_inject.py exercises construction only against FakeQMPServer (test_qmp_inject.py), which records commands without any QMP schema validation — so the passing test cannot catch the missing required members. grep confirms no other caller supplies descriptor/type/transaction-type. Not intentional: no stub/partial comment, and the sibling commands being correct shows the intent was schema-faithful construction.

Severity raised from medium to high. The finding frames it as an honesty/undischarged-audit lapse (medium), but the concrete impact is a real-hw path that misparses/crashes: the general-media-event QMP command is malformed and will be rejected by real QEMU 11.0, making CXL event injection non-functional on the path it is built for. That matches the project's "high" rubric ("a real-hw path that crashes/misparses"). The suggested fix (pin a query-qmp-schema snapshot / version assert and reconcile each cxl-inject-* arg list, then note the verified version) is sound and would have caught this.  

**H39. DENSITY_GBIT table is 8x too high — every density read is wrong on real hardware**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py` · spd.py:18 (DENSITY_GBIT), used at spd.py:41 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The DENSITY_GBIT map {0:0,1:4,2:8,3:12,4:16,5:24,6:32,7:48,8:64} does not match the JESD400-5 byte-4 (SDRAM Density per die) bits[4:0] encoding, which is value 1=512Mb(0.5Gb), 2=1Gb, 3=1.5Gb, 4=2Gb, 5=3Gb, 6=4Gb, 7=6Gb, 8=8Gb. Every entry is exactly 8x the true per-die density, so parse_spd reports a density 8x larger than reality.  
- **Evidence:** Module: `DENSITY_GBIT = {0: 0, 1: 4, 2: 8, 3: 12, 4: 16, 5: 24, 6: 32, 7: 48, 8: 64}`. Authoritative memtest86plus parse_spd_ddr5 (system/spd.c, `switch (sbyte & 0x1F)`): 0b00001->512, 0b00010->1024, 0b00011->1536, 0b00100->2048, 0b00101->3072, 0b00110->4096, 0b00111->6144, 0b01000->8192 (Mbit per die). Corpus byte4=0x04 -> spec says 2 Gbit/die; module reports 16. Ratio code/spec = 8.0 for every nonzero entry.  
- **Fix:** Replace with the JESD400-5 per-die table. If Gbit-per-die granularity below 1 matters, store Mbit: DENSITY_MBIT = {1:512,2:1024,3:1536,4:2048,5:3072,6:4096,7:6144,8:8192}. Note this field is density-per-die, NOT module capacity; rename density_gbit accordingly or document it, and do not equate it to the '32gb' module size in the corpus dir name.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed. spd.py:18 `DENSITY_GBIT = {0:0,1:4,2:8,3:12,4:16,5:24,6:32,7:48,8:64}` is used at spd.py:41 to decode JESD400-5 Byte 4 bits[4:0] (SDRAM Density per die). The authoritative encoding is 1=512Mb(0.5Gb), 2=1Gb, 3=1.5Gb, 4=2Gb, 5=3Gb, 6=4Gb, 7=6Gb, 8=8Gb (matches memtest86plus/Linux). I computed code/spec ratio = exactly 8.0 for every nonzero entry, so the table reports density 8x the true per-die value. The corpus confirms it: spd.hex byte[4]=0x04 should be 2 Gbit/die but parse_spd returns density_gbit=16, and expected.json was hand-written to 16 to match the buggy output. The field/docstring explicitly call this "density" (line 10), there is no documentation reframing it as module capacity, and the corpus dir name "32gb" does not equal 16 either, so this is not a deliberate alternate-unit choice. It is not a None/raise honesty case, not a mock-fidelity case, and not a pragma real-hw path — it is pure parsing logic exercised on real hardware reads.

The test suite gives no protection: test_parses_to_expected asserts parse_spd == expected.json where expected.json is itself derived from the buggy table (self-referential), and test_mismatch_fails uses 64 as the "wrong" value without any independent spec anchor. So a corrected table would only break tests because the fixture is also wrong, not because the spec is asserted.

Severity high rather than critical: the certain, demonstrable harm is a wrong reported spec number (8x). A wrong PASS/FAIL verdict only occurs conditionally — check_spd compares parsed-vs-expected, so it stays self-consistent when `expected` is regenerated from the same parser, but if an operator populates `expected.density_gbit` from the actual JEDEC datasheet (e.g., 2 for a 2Gb/die part) a genuinely-conformant module would FAIL density_gbit_matches. Per the project rubric "wrong number/spec value = high"; the conditional verdict-flip path keeps it from being a clean critical. Suggested fix is correct: use the JESD400-5 per-die table (store Mbit if sub-Gbit granularity matters) and regenerate expected.json; note the field is density-per-die, not module capacity.  

**H40. Invented 2-bit slot_id field (bits 5:6) with no spec basis; conflated with CSI**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py:89,130-133,156 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** There is no 2-bit 'Slot ID' (0..3) field in the NVMe-MI message-header byte; the spec field is CSI, a single bit (Command Slot Identifier, selecting one of two command slots). The module both invents slot_id (bits 5:6, validated 0..3) AND carries csi separately, so the dataclass models a wire field that does not exist.  
- **Evidence:** Line 130 `if not 0 <= msg.slot_id <= 3` and line 133 `| ((msg.slot_id & 0x03) << 5)`; line 88 already declares `csi: int = 0  # 0 = primary, 1 = secondary slot`. libnvme has only `(ep->csi & 1)` at bit 0 — no second slot field. The module's bits 5:6 collide with where NMIMT actually lives.  
- **Fix:** Remove the slot_id field entirely and keep only the single-bit CSI at bit 0, matching the spec. If a 'slot' concept is desired for higher layers, name it csi and constrain it to 0..1.  
- **Verifier:** CONFIRMED. The NVMe-MI message header byte (byte 1, "NMP" byte) has no 2-bit "Slot ID" field. Authoritative layout from libnvme's mi.c composes it as `hdr->nmp = (ror << 7) | (nmimt << 3) | (ep->csi & 1)`, i.e. bit0=CSI (single bit), bits1:2=Reserved, bits3:6=NMIMT (4-bit NVMe-MI Message Type), bit7=ROR. The UNH-IOL NVMe-MI conformance plan likewise references only CSI, NMIMT and ROR — no Slot ID.

The module (src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py) invents this field exactly as the finding states: line 89 declares `slot_id`, line 130 validates `0 <= msg.slot_id <= 3`, line 133 packs `((msg.slot_id & 0x03) << 5)`, and line 156 decodes `(nmp >> 5) & 0x03` — while line 88 separately carries the real single-bit `csi`. The docstring (line 17 "Slot ID", line 127 "bits 5..6 = SLOTID") documents a field that does not exist. The finding's key sub-claims all check out: no spec Slot ID, csi carried separately, libnvme uses only `csi & 1` at bit0, and bits 5:6 collide with the real NMIMT (NMIMT occupies bits 3:6, so bits 5:6 are its top two bits).

This is the REAL on-wire encoder (no pragma; the module docstring states real transport runs over mctp.MctpTransport), so this is not mock fidelity or honesty discipline. I reproduced the corruption: encoding NVME_MI_COMMAND(0x01) with csi=1, slot_id=2 yields NMP byte 0x51; a spec-conformant endpoint reads NMIMT=0b1010(10) instead of the intended message type, and there is no ROR bit at all. The existing test `test_nmp_round_trip` (tests/test_nvme_mi.py:127, asserting slot_id==2 round-trips) gives false confidence because encoder and decoder are mutually consistent but both wrong against the wire. No other module consumes slot_id/csi, so the impact is confined to this encoder/decoder — but it is a genuine spec-conformance defect that would malform real OOB management traffic whenever slot_id != 0.

Severity: high (matches the finding). It is a real-hardware wire-framing/spec error that misparses on actual hardware. I keep it at high rather than escalating because the finding is scoped specifically to the invented slot_id field; note that the broader NMP byte layout (MT at bits0:3 vs spec bits3:6, CSI at bit4 vs bit0, missing ROR) is also wrong and warrants its own finding. Suggested fix as written is correct: remove slot_id and keep a single-bit CSI at bit0, and correct the full byte layout to bit0=CSI, bits3:6=NMIMT, bit7=ROR.  

**H41. ROR (Request-or-Response) bit is never encoded or decoded**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py:123-160 (encode/decode), 78-92 (MiMessage) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The NVMe-MI header byte bit 7 (ROR) distinguishes a Request from a Response, but MiMessage has no ROR field and neither encode nor decode touches bit 7. decode_mi_response cannot tell a response from a request, and any received frame with ROR=1 set in bit 7 would have that bit silently dropped (and, given the wrong layout, leak into the bogus SLOTID field).  
- **Evidence:** MiMessage fields are message_type/opcode/csi/slot_id/nmhdr/data/mic — no ror. encode builds nmp without bit 7; decode reads only `nmp & 0x0F`, `(nmp>>4)&1`, `(nmp>>5)&3`. There is no `(nmp >> 7) & 1` anywhere.  
- **Fix:** Add a `ror: int = 0` (or bool) field, set bit 7 on encode (0 for requests, 1 for responses), and parse it on decode so callers can validate the response role per spec §3.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed against the NVMe-MI v2.1 spec (Figure 20, /private/tmp/nvmemi_21.txt lines 2033-2125). The NMP byte (byte 1 of the message) has ROR at bit 7 (0=Request, 1=Response per line 2092), NMIMT at bits 6:3, and CSI at bit 0. In messages.py, MiMessage (lines 86-92) has no ror field; encode_mi_request (line 132) builds nmp with bit 7 forced to 0; decode_mi_response (lines 154-156) only reads `nmp & 0x0F`, `(nmp>>4)&1`, `(nmp>>5)&3`, never bit 7. So ROR is never encoded or decoded, and a function literally named decode_mi_response cannot validate that a frame is a Response (ROR=1) vs a Request. This is the actual on-wire codec ("the on-wire byte string the MCTP transport will send", docstring line 124), a real-hw framing path, not mock scaffolding — so it is not covered by the mock-fidelity/pragma conventions. The round-trip test (test_nvme_mi.py:126) passes only because encode and decode share the same self-consistent layout; it would not catch this against real hardware. Severity stays high per the rubric (wrong spec value on a real-hw path that would misframe/misparse).

Two caveats that do not change the verdict: (1) The evidence's claim that ROR "would leak into the bogus SLOTID field" is factually wrong — decode reads slot_id as (nmp>>5)&0x03 (bits 5-6), so bit 7 is masked out and silently dropped, not leaked. (2) The defect is actually broader than stated: the code's entire NMP layout is wrong vs spec — it places NMIMT at bits 0:3 (spec: 6:3), CSI at bit 4 (spec: bit 0), and invents a 2-bit SLOTID at bits 5:6 (spec has no such field; the Command Slot is the 1-bit CSI selecting slot 0 or 1 only, per lines 1394/2124-2125), and it conflates the MCTPD byte (byte 0, MT+IC) with the NMP byte. The suggested fix (add ror, set bit 7 on encode/decode) is correct but incomplete given the wider mislayout. The headline finding — ROR never encoded/decoded, request/response role indistinguishable — is independently true and demonstrable.  

**H42. Real-hw JSON keys (dataAreas/sizeBlocks, header.version) likely do not match nvme-cli's actual OCP internal-log output**  
`src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py` · ocp_telemetry.py:127-139 (_parse_telemetry) and :89-99 (_MOCK_TELEMETRY) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** On a real drive, _parse_telemetry will silently produce an empty data_areas dict (total_blocks==0) and version=None because nvme-cli's OCP telemetry JSON does not use top-level keys named `dataAreas` with per-area `sizeBlocks`, nor `header.version`.  
- **Evidence:** for name, body in (raw.get("dataAreas") or {}).items(): blocks = body.get("sizeBlocks", 0) ... version = header.get("version"). The standard NVMe/OCP telemetry log header exposes 'Telemetry Host-Initiated Data Area 1/2/3 Last Block' (a last-block index, not a per-area size map) and the telemetry log version lives in 'Telemetry Host Image Version'/header byte fields — not a JSON key literally named version inside a `header` object. Every key here matches only the synthetic _MOCK_TELEMETRY dict, which is the sole input these names were validated against.  
- **Fix:** Pin the expected nvme-cli OCP plugin JSON schema against a captured real-device dump (record the exact key names/casing emitted by the installed nvme-cli version) and parse those keys; if absent, RAISE OcpTelemetryError rather than returning a silently-empty/zero result so a field operator is not handed total_blocks=0 as if it were real telemetry.  
- **Verifier:** Both parts of the finding hold up. (1) The silent-degradation mechanism is directly reproducible from the code: `_parse_telemetry` (ocp_telemetry.py:127-139) does `header = raw.get("header", {}) or {}`, `version = header.get("version")`, and `for name, body in (raw.get("dataAreas") or {}).items()`. If the real JSON lacks those exact keys, it returns version=None and data_areas={} (total_blocks==0) with NO OcpTelemetryError. The tests at lines 42-50 (test_missing_data_areas_is_empty_dict, test_missing_header_version_is_none) explicitly assert this silent empty/None behavior — so there is no guard that would catch a schema mismatch. (2) These key names have only ever been validated against the synthetic mock: corpus/nvme_ocp_internal_log/synthetic-mock-1tb/telemetry.json is byte-identical to _MOCK_TELEMETRY, and the corpus README itself admits "this corpus stays manually curated until a drive-attached station feeds it" — i.e., there is no real-device capture anywhere in the repo. The invented shape {"header":{"version":N}, "dataAreas":{"1":{"sizeBlocks":N}}} does not match nvme-cli's actual OCP/telemetry JSON, which renders the telemetry header with spaced human-readable keys and exposes per-area Last-Block indices (not a sizeBlocks size map) and a telemetry image/version field rather than a nested header.version key. This is not an intentional honesty-discipline path: emit_to_ocpdiag would still emit a PASS diagnosis ("OCP internal-log decoded") while reporting total_blocks=0 and version=None — a misleading PASS handed to a field operator, exactly the failure mode the conventions say to flag. Severity stays high: it silently misreports/zeroes real telemetry on the real-hw path rather than crashing, and the suggested fix (pin against a captured real dump; RAISE when expected keys are absent instead of returning a silently-empty result) is correct. nvme-cli was not installed in this sandbox so I could not capture a live dump to nail the exact real key strings, which is why the finding's own "medium/likely" caveat is appropriate, but the silent-zero defect and the mock-only-validation are concretely demonstrated.  

### Medium (66)

**M1. Device-Status correctable bit (bit 0) is mislabeled 'RxErr' when surfaced through AerSnapshot**  
`src/computetest/aer.py` · aer.py:78-79 (AerSnapshot.correctable) with CORRECTABLE_BITS[0] at aer.py:26 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** On a no-AER device, the Device-Status correctable signal (DEVSTA_CORR == bit 0) is decoded with the AER table, where bit 0 is 'RxErr' (Receiver Error). Device Status bit 0 is a coarse 'a correctable error was detected (no breakdown)' indicator, NOT specifically a physical-layer receiver error, so AerSnapshot.correctable reports a specific PCIe error type the device never actually told us.  
- **Evidence:** Reproduced: a no-AER device with a latched correctable Device-Status bit yields `snap.correctable == [(0, 'RxErr', 'Receiver Error — raw physical-layer symbol error (SI)')]`. The correct coarse name (DEVSTATUS_COR_BITS[0] == 'CorrErrDetected') is only reachable via ErrorReading, which AerSnapshot does not use. Same root cause as the uncorrectable finding (no `source` on AerSnapshot).  
- **Fix:** Same fix: route AerSnapshot's decode through the source-appropriate table (DEVSTATUS_COR_BITS for devstatus) so a coarse correctable is labeled 'CorrErrDetected', not the spec-specific 'RxErr'.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced end-to-end. aer.snapshot() on a no-AER device (aer.py:106-111) falls back to Device Status, producing an AerSnapshot with correctable_raw=DEVSTA_CORR (bit 0) and aer_base=None. AerSnapshot.correctable (aer.py:78-79) hardcodes decode_correctable, which uses CORRECTABLE_BITS where bit 0 = 'RxErr' (Receiver Error). Confirmed runtime output: snap.correctable == [(0, 'RxErr', 'Receiver Error — raw physical-layer symbol error (SI)')], whereas the correct coarse label DEVSTATUS_COR_BITS[0] == 'CorrErrDetected' is only reachable via ErrorReading, which AerSnapshot does not use (AerSnapshot has no source field). Spec-accurate: PCIe Device Status bit 0 'Correctable Error Detected' is a coarse flag distinct from AER Correctable Error Status bit 0 'Receiver Error'; the code's own two-table design documents this. Real impact: diagnostics.py:101 builds PcieDiagnostic.aer_snapshot from aer.snapshot(), and to_dict() (diagnostics.py:83) plus io/ocpdiag.py:337-338 emit these names directly, so real no-AER hardware would record aer.correctable.RxErr — a fabricated physical-layer error type the device never reported. The same root cause makes the uncorrectable path worse: Device-Status NonFatal raw (bit 1) has no entry in UNCORRECTABLE_BITS, so AerSnapshot.uncorrectable decodes to [] while has_uncorrectable is True, causing diagnostics.reasons() (line 47) to emit 'uncorrectable: ' with no type. Not refuted by any guard: no test asserts these labels; the only coarse-label test (test_aer_edges.py:68) uses ErrorReading, not AerSnapshot, and the 'Critical fix' test (line 105) only asserts has_uncorrectable. Not a mock-fidelity/pragma/honesty-discipline exception. Severity stays medium: it does not flip the PASS/FAIL verdict (status uses raw-based has_*correctable, unaffected), but it surfaces a wrong specific spec error type and silently drops the uncorrectable name in field diagnostics and OCP output, misleading a hardware debugger about which PCIe layer failed.  

**M2. Test for the no-AER snapshot fallback only checks has_uncorrectable, not the decoded name list**  
`src/computetest/aer.py` · tests/test_aer_edges.py:105-119 (test_snapshot_without_aer_but_with_devstatus_uncorrectable_is_flagged) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The regression test that guards the Device-Status snapshot fallback asserts only `snap.has_uncorrectable` (which is True purely because raw != 0) and never asserts that `snap.uncorrectable` decodes to a non-empty, correctly-named list. It therefore passes even though the decoded evidence list is empty, so it would not catch the decode-table mismatch in the two findings above.  
- **Evidence:** `assert snap.has_uncorrectable, ("snapshot must fall back to Device Status when AER is absent ...")` — no assertion on `snap.uncorrectable` names. No test in tests/ asserts a devstatus-sourced AerSnapshot's decoded `.uncorrectable`/`.correctable` (grep shows only test_cxl_ras.py asserts a decoded snapshot name, on a different path).  
- **Fix:** Add `assert [n for _, n, _ in snap.uncorrectable] == ['NonFatalDetected']` (and a correctable analogue) so the test fails if the decode table is wrong; this also documents the intended operator-facing label.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and independently reproduced. The regression test tests/test_aer_edges.py:105-119 (test_snapshot_without_aer_but_with_devstatus_uncorrectable_is_flagged) asserts only `snap.aer_base is None` and `snap.has_uncorrectable`. It never asserts the decoded `snap.uncorrectable` name list. I reproduced that on this exact scenario `snap.uncorrectable` decodes to `[]` (empty), so the test passes even though the decoded evidence is empty — exactly the test-quality gap claimed.

Root cause verified: snapshot() falls back to read_errors() for a no-AER device, which yields a devstatus-sourced `uncorrectable_raw = DEVSTA_NONFATAL = 2` (bit 1). It stores that raw into AerSnapshot, but AerSnapshot.uncorrectable (src/computetest/aer.py:82-83) ALWAYS decodes via decode_uncorrectable -> UNCORRECTABLE_BITS (the AER table, whose lowest key is bit 4). Bit 1 is not in that table, so decode returns []. The source-aware ErrorReading.uncorrectable (aer.py:179-181) correctly picks DEVSTATUS_UNC_BITS and returns ['NonFatalDetected'] — I demonstrated this asymmetry directly: ErrorReading(0,2,'devstatus').uncorrectable == ['NonFatalDetected'] but AerSnapshot('x',None,0,2).uncorrectable == [].

The finding's grep evidence is accurate: the only decoded-name assertions in tests are tests/test_aer_edges.py:68-69 (on an ErrorReading from read_errors — a different object/path) and tests/test_cxl_ras.py:33 (CXL RAS, a path with a single consistent UE/CE table). No test asserts a devstatus-sourced AerSnapshot's decoded names, so the snapshot decode-table mismatch is undetected.

Real-world impact (grounds the severity): in src/computetest/diagnostics.py:46-47 and to_dict (lines 83-84), this empty decode surfaces to operators. I ran diagnose() on a no-AER device with a NonFatal Device Status bit and got reasons: ['uncorrectable: '] (empty error name after the colon) and to_dict aer == {'correctable': [], 'uncorrectable': []} despite a real flagged uncorrectable. The PASS/FAIL verdict is still correct (driven by has_uncorrectable), so this is not a wrong-verdict/critical defect — it is a weak test that would still pass while the operator-facing decoded label is silently dropped/wrong. That matches the project's own definition of medium ("a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong"), so medium is the correct severity.

One caveat on the suggested fix: as written it would FAIL against current code (snap.uncorrectable is [], not ['NonFatalDetected']) because the underlying snapshot decode is itself buggy — i.e., adding that assertion would correctly turn red and force a real fix (route AerSnapshot decode through the source, or have the devstatus-fallback snapshot decode against DEVSTATUS_UNC_BITS). That is the desired outcome of the test-quality finding, not a contradiction.  

**M3. Mock worst-lane index uses %16 regardless of link width, so marginal narrow links (x4/x8) can falsely report OK**  
`src/computetest/margining.py` · src/computetest/margining.py:94-96 (_mock_lane_margin) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** For links narrower than 16 lanes, the seeded 'worst lane' index can land >= the actual lane count, so the failure penalty never fires and a marginal narrow link reads OK in mock mode despite an injected marginal BER.  
- **Evidence:** worst_lane = h[1] % 16  ... penalty = 0.18 if (injected_ber > 1e-12 and lane == worst_lane) else 0.0. Verified: for 0000:06:00.0 (x4 NVMe) with injected_ber=1e-9 the worst index is 11 (outside x4); all 4 lanes compute ~0.35-0.38 UI, above the 0.25 limit -> MarginResult.ok is True. The docstring promises 'Make one lane clearly worst on a marginal link', but margin_link only iterates range(n) where n=current_link_width (4 for NVMe), so the penalty lane is unreachable. base alone (0.45 - 6e7*ber) stays >0.25 at moderate BER, so nothing pushes a lane under the limit.  
- **Fix:** Seed the worst lane within the actual width, e.g. pass n into _mock_lane_margin and use worst_lane = h[1] % max(1, n); or compute the worst lane in margin_link after n is known. This keeps the mock's stated 'one weak lane on a marginal link' contract for x1/x4/x8 links, not just x16.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced the core claim in src/computetest/margining.py:94-96. For 0000:06:00.0 (x4 NVMe) with injected_ber=1e-9, all 4 lanes compute ~0.35-0.38 UI, no weak lane appears, and MarginResult.ok is True — despite the module docstring (lines 14-15) and function docstring (line 93) promising "a marginal link shows one weak lane." margin_link only iterates range(n) with n=current_link_width, so the seeded penalty lane is frequently unreachable on narrow links. Quantified across synthetic BDFs: P(a weak lane appears) is ~64% on x16 but only ~22% on x4 and ~6% on x1, so the contract is reliably honored only for x16. The wrong-OK window for a narrow link that rolls no penalty is the BER band roughly (1e-12, ~2.5e-9], where base alone (0.45 - 6e7*ber) keeps every lane above the 0.25 UI limit. The finding's suggested %n fix is directionally valid (raises x4 hit rate to ~68%, x1 to 100%).

Two corrections to the finding's framing, neither of which refutes it: (1) The penalty is actually self-referential — lane L is penalized iff hash(bdf:L)[1] % 16 == L — so even 0000:05:00.0, whose seeded indices include in-width values (3,4), still gets no penalty because no lane self-matches; the deeper cause is the per-lane ~1/16 self-match probability combined with %16 capping reachable matches to lanes 0-15. (2) This logic lives only in the isinstance(backend, MockBackend) branch; real hardware uses _real_margin_lane and is unaffected, so no field-hardware verdict is corrupted. Per project conventions the Mock is meant to faithfully model real dynamics, so a wrong OK on a simulated marginal narrow link is a legitimate fidelity defect, but because it is mock-only and verdict-affecting only in a narrow BER band, medium (not critical/high) is the correct severity.  

**M4. ber --bits with an unproven ("continue") verdict exits 0 and emits an ocp-diag PASS**  
`src/computetest/cli.py` · cli.py:240-245 (ber assess branch) and cli.py:88-94 (_emit_ocp "ber") · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When `computetest ber --bits N` yields a verdict whose status is "continue" (BER NOT yet proven to target at the requested confidence), the CLI still returns EXIT_PASS and the ocp-diag stream records `verdict="ber.math.pass" type_="PASS"` with `step_end("pass")` and a NOT_APPLICABLE-or-PASS testRunEnd — i.e. an unproven result is labeled PASS.  
- **Evidence:** Branch always returns PASS: `v = ber.assess(args.errors, args.bits, ...)`; `_emit(human, obj, ...)`; `_emit_ocp(args, "ber", obj)`; `return EXIT_PASS`. OCP branch hardcodes the verdict: `em.diagnosis(verdict="ber.math.pass", type_="PASS"); em.step_end("pass", step_id=sid)`. Verified empirically: `cli.main(['ber','--json','--bits','1e12','--errors','0'])` -> json status='continue' but rc=0. assess(0,1e6,1e-12,0.95).status=='continue'.  
- **Fix:** In the assess branch, map the verdict status to an exit code (e.g. pass->EXIT_PASS, continue->EXIT_UNAVAIL, fail->EXIT_FAIL) and pass the real status into the OCP diagnosis/step_end (use v.status, not a hardcoded "pass") so the ocp-diag verdict and testRunEnd reflect "not proven" rather than PASS.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced the defect directly. In cli.py:240-245 the `ber` (assess) branch runs `v = ber.assess(...)` then unconditionally `return EXIT_PASS`, and cli.py:88-94 (`_emit_ocp` kind=="ber") hardcodes `em.diagnosis(verdict="ber.math.pass", type_="PASS")` and `em.step_end("pass", ...)` without ever reading `obj["status"]`. Running `cli.main(['ber','--json','--bits','1e12','--errors','0','--ocpdiag',PATH])` (the canonical "continue"/unproven case: CL=0.632 vs target 0.95) yields rc=0 and an ocp-diag stream containing `diagnosis verdict="ber.math.pass" type=PASS` followed by `testRunEnd result="PASS"`. So an UNPROVEN result is labeled PASS in the machine-readable conformance artifact and exit code. This directly violates the project's stated honesty discipline ("mislabels a verdict"; "never claim PASS from a measurement we didn't make" — the same principle the diagnose --no-bert test enforces via EXIT_UNAVAIL). No guard or test covers it: tests/test_cli_commands.py:20-23 exercises this exact arg set but only asserts JSON has fields, never the exit code or ocp verdict. Contrast the sibling bert/diagnose paths which correctly route through _verdict_exit/_verdict_exit_any. Not refuted by mock/pragma/honesty conventions — it is the opposite of honesty discipline. Severity medium (not critical): the human/JSON output is honest (prints status="continue"/CONTINUE), and `ber` is the explicitly hardware-free "confidence math only" command, so it does not gate a live DUT verdict directly; the wrong-PASS only misleads automation that keys on exit code or the ocp-diag verdict/testRunEnd. Suggested fix is sound: map v.status to an exit code (continue->EXIT_UNAVAIL) and pass v.status into the diagnosis/step_end instead of hardcoding "pass".  

**M5. A bad --ocpdiag output path raises an uncaught traceback (bypasses the documented exit-code mapping)**  
`src/computetest/cli.py` · cli.py:420 (main calls _open_ocpdiag before the try) and cli.py:393 (open(args.ocpdiag, "w")) · _error-handling_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `_open_ocpdiag` runs OUTSIDE main's try/except, so when `open(args.ocpdiag, "w")` fails (nonexistent directory, permission denied, or path is a directory) the FileNotFoundError/PermissionError/IsADirectoryError escapes as a raw Python traceback instead of mapping to EXIT_IO (4) — the exact failure mode the module otherwise guards against (cf. the sqlite3.Error fix and the `--db` test).  
- **Evidence:** main(): `_open_ocpdiag(args, argv)` on line 420, then `try:` on line 422. _open_ocpdiag: `args._ocp_stream = open(args.ocpdiag, "w")`. Verified: `cli.main(['--backend','mock','list','--ocpdiag','/nonexistent/dir/stream.jsonl'])` -> `UNCAUGHT FileNotFoundError [Errno 2] No such file or directory`.  
- **Fix:** Move the `_open_ocpdiag(args, argv)` call inside the try block (or wrap the file open in try/except mapping OSError -> EXIT_IO with a clean `error: config/IO:` message). Add a test asserting a bad --ocpdiag path returns EXIT_IO and prints no 'Traceback'.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproduced. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/cli.py, main() calls _open_ocpdiag(args, argv) at line 420, OUTSIDE the try block that starts at line 422. _open_ocpdiag at line 393 does `args._ocp_stream = open(args.ocpdiag, "w")` with no guard. Running `cli.main(['--backend','mock','list','--ocpdiag','/nonexistent/dir/stream.jsonl'])` raises an uncaught FileNotFoundError, prints a full Python traceback to stderr (the station log), and exits with code 1 — NOT the documented EXIT_IO (4). This directly contradicts the module's explicit, tested error-handling contract: tests/test_cli_commands.py:195-205 (test_sqlite_error_maps_to_exit_io) asserts a bad --db path maps to EXIT_IO with "Traceback not in err," and tests/test_cli_commands.py:26-27 asserts a bad plan path maps to EXIT_IO. The --ocpdiag path bypasses exactly this guarding, and no test covers it. The finding's broader claim also holds: PermissionError and IsADirectoryError are siblings of FileNotFoundError under OSError (not subclasses), so the more complete fix is to map OSError->EXIT_IO (the suggested fix notes this). Not intentional — the surrounding code's whole purpose is to avoid tracebacks in station logs and emit documented exit codes. Severity medium is accurate: it is a genuine error-handling/exit-code defect contradicting a tested contract, but it triggers before any test runs (no wrong PASS/FAIL verdict, no data corruption), so it does not reach high/critical.  

**M6. lmt --via-pci-lmt always exits PASS regardless of the reference tool's per-lane margining result**  
`src/computetest/cli.py` · cli.py:318-324 (lmt --via-pci-lmt branch) · _honesty_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The `--via-pci-lmt` path returns EXIT_PASS whenever `call_pci_lmt` returns (i.e. pci_lmt exited 0), even though pci_lmt completes with exit 0 on a finished run while encoding lane PASS/FAIL inside its output JSON; a CI gate keying on `computetest lmt --via-pci-lmt`'s exit code would read 0 as "margining passed" when lanes actually failed.  
- **Evidence:** `payload = lmt_adapter.call_pci_lmt(...); print(payload, ...); return EXIT_PASS  # the reference tool owns the verdict`. test_via_pci_lmt_invokes_subprocess asserts `rc == cli.EXIT_PASS` for an arbitrary returned record `[{"bdf": "0000:03:00.0"}]` with no pass/fail inspection. call_pci_lmt only raises on non-zero process rc, not on in-band lane failures.  
- **Fix:** Either parse the returned JSON/CSV for per-lane pass/fail and map to EXIT_FAIL on any failing lane, or explicitly document (in --help and the README) that --via-pci-lmt's exit code reflects only that the tool ran, not the margining verdict, and have it NOT emit a PASS-implying success that a gate could trust.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed. At cli.py:318-324 the `--via-pci-lmt` branch calls `lmt_adapter.call_pci_lmt(...)`, prints the payload, and unconditionally `return EXIT_PASS` ("# the reference tool owns the verdict"). `call_pci_lmt` (lmt_adapter.py:242-246) only raises on a non-zero PROCESS return code; a completed margining run that finds failing lanes still returns its stdout, so EXIT_PASS is emitted regardless of the per-lane outcome. test_via_pci_lmt_invokes_subprocess (tests/test_lmt_adapter.py:242-263) hard-asserts `rc == cli.EXIT_PASS` for an arbitrary record `[{"bdf": "0000:03:00.0"}]` with zero pass/fail inspection, locking in this behavior.

This trips the project's honesty discipline. The AUTHORITATIVE exit-code contract in docs/reference/cli.md:35-44 states "0 | EXIT_PASS | The verdict was `pass`" and claims it is followed "across every subcommand." So a CI gate keying on the exit code of `computetest lmt --via-pci-lmt CONFIG` will, per the documented contract, read 0 as "margining verdict = pass" — even when lanes failed. Worse, the SAME `lmt` subcommand's native path (cli.py:340-342) DOES compute a real verdict and returns EXIT_FAIL on `not result.ok`, so the two paths have inconsistent and undocumented exit semantics. This is a real-hardware path (it shells out to the real OCP tool), exactly where a misleading PASS-implying exit code matters.

One factual nit in the finding: it says pci_lmt "encodes lane PASS/FAIL inside its output JSON." The documented 24-column schema (lmt_adapter.py:58-67 and docs/research/2026-05-29-roadmap.md:49-56) has no explicit pass/fail column — the lane outcome is implicit in error_count/step/ber vs error_count_limit. This imprecision does not undermine the defect; the verdict is still recoverable from that data, and the finding's alternative remedy (document that the exit code reflects only that the tool ran, not the margining verdict, and stop emitting a gate-trustable PASS) is the correct fix given the cli.md contract. Severity medium is right: it can yield a wrong PASS exit code on real hardware, but only when an operator opts into --via-pci-lmt and a gate trusts the exit code rather than parsing the (faithfully emitted) records; the measurement data itself is not corrupted.  

**M7. ocp-diag testRunEnd is FAIL for setup errors (device-not-found / config-IO / interrupt), conflating test-program faults with DUT failure**  
`src/computetest/cli.py` · cli.py:373 (_EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS) and cli.py:405-411 (_close_ocpdiag) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `_EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS` maps only PASS/FAIL/UNAVAIL; EXIT_NOTFOUND(3), EXIT_IO(4) and rc=130 fall through to `.get(rc, "fail")` so testRunEnd.result becomes FAIL for a missing device / bad config / Ctrl-C — an OCP consumer reads that as the DUT failing, though a run_error artifact (e.g. symptom=device-not-found) was also emitted.  
- **Evidence:** `_EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS = {EXIT_PASS: "pass", EXIT_FAIL: "fail", EXIT_UNAVAIL: "skip"}` (no entries for 3,4,130); `status = _EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS.get(rc, "fail")`. run_end maps unknown statuses to FAIL via `_RUN_RESULT.get(status, "NOT_APPLICABLE")` only catches "skip"-like, so "fail" -> FAIL.  
- **Fix:** Map setup/IO/not-found/interrupt exit codes to a non-FAIL run result (NOT_APPLICABLE/ERROR) — e.g. add EXIT_NOTFOUND/EXIT_IO/130 to a table that yields "skip"/error, so a missing device or aborted run is not recorded as a DUT FAIL while the run_error artifact carries the true symptom.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced the defect empirically.
