In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/cli.py:373 `_EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS = {EXIT_PASS:"pass", EXIT_FAIL:"fail", EXIT_UNAVAIL:"skip"}` (i.e. {0,1,5}). In `_close_ocpdiag` (cli.py:405-415) `status = _EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS.get(rc, "fail")` so every error/abort exit code — EXIT_NOTFOUND(3), EXIT_IO(4), EXIT_USAGE(2), and KeyboardInterrupt(130) — falls through to "fail". The emitter (src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py:119-123) then does `_RUN_RESULT.get(status, "NOT_APPLICABLE")` with `_RUN_RESULT={"pass":"PASS","fail":"FAIL"}`, so "fail" -> "FAIL". Running `bert -d 9999:99:99.9 --ocpdiag` (rc=3) and `plan /nonexistent-plan.json --ocpdiag` (rc=4) both emit `testRunEnd: {"status":"COMPLETE","result":"FAIL"}`, and the bad-device stream contains NO test steps at all — only testRunStart, an `error` artifact (symptom=device-not-found), then testRunEnd=FAIL. So an OCP consumer keying on the headline run result reads a never-tested DUT as FAILING. The vendored schema src/computetest/io/ocpdiag_schemas/test_run_end.json confirms the testResult enum is exactly {NOT_APPLICABLE, PASS, FAIL}; NOT_APPLICABLE is the spec-correct value when the run aborts on a test-program/environment fault, which the author already knows (the _RUN_RESULT comment at line 35 says "else NOT_APPLICABLE" and the _STEP_STATUS comment at line 37 says "reserve ERROR for an internal/test-program fault"). This is a mislabeled verdict, which the honesty discipline explicitly says to flag. Not intentional/already-handled: the only relevant test, test_ocpdiag_emits_run_error_on_bad_device (tests/test_ocpdiag.py:520-531), checks merely that an `error` artifact and a `testRunEnd` exist — it deliberately does NOT assert result==FAIL, so the FAIL behavior is an untested gap, not a blessed contract. Severity adjusted from low to medium: it is a genuinely wrong headline FAIL verdict in a machine-consumed artifact (can pollute fleet pass/fail stats, trigger false triage), but it is mitigated because the co-emitted `error` artifact carries the true symptom, so a careful consumer can disambiguate — keeping it below high/critical. The suggested fix is right in spirit (map 2/3/4/130 to NOT_APPLICABLE), but note OCP testRunEnd has no "ERROR" run result, so NOT_APPLICABLE is the only non-FAIL spec-legal choice; "ERROR" belongs to step status, not run result.  

**M8. Leading-comma (or empty) idn silently disables the IDN safety check**  
`src/computetest/fixtures.py` · fixtures.py:136-146 FixtureMap.open · _edge-case_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** If an operator writes an idn whose first comma field is empty (e.g. ',MSO64' from a typo, or a value beginning with a comma), spec.idn is truthy so the check runs, but spec.idn.split(',')[0] == '' and actual.startswith('') is always True — the wrong-box guard is silently turned off instead of erroring.  
- **Evidence:** Verify: ',MSO64'.split(',')[0] == '' ; 'anything'.startswith('') == True. spec.idn is non-empty so the `if spec.idn:` guard at line 136 enters the block, then the startswith never fails.  
- **Fix:** After computing prefix = spec.idn.split(',')[0].strip(), raise InstrumentError on an empty prefix (malformed idn in the fixture map) rather than treating it as 'matches anything'.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced against the actual code (PYTHONPATH=src). In src/computetest/fixtures.py:136-146, FixtureMap.open does `if spec.idn:` then `actual.startswith(spec.idn.split(",")[0])`. With idn=",MSO64" (operator typo / leading comma), spec.idn is truthy so the guard block IS entered, but spec.idn.split(",")[0] == "" and "MockCorp,...".startswith("") is always True, so no InstrumentError is raised — the wrong-box guard is silently a no-op. The string facts in the evidence are exactly right (','.split(',')[0]=='' ; any.startswith('')==True).

Not refuted by any guard or test: __post_init__ (lines 103-113) and load_fixture_map (159-186) validate role uniqueness and kind, but never the idn prefix. The only IDN test, tests/test_instruments_hal.py:223 test_open_idn_mismatch_raises_and_closes, uses a well-formed prefix ("Keysight Technologies,MSO-X") and does not cover the empty-prefix case.

Not intentional: the empty-string sentinel idn="" is the documented opt-out (it's falsy, so the guard is intentionally skipped — InstrumentSpec.idn default is "" and docstring calls it "optional"). A leading-comma value is a malformed entry where the operator clearly intends a real check (they typed a model) yet it degrades to "matches anything." This is exactly the wrong-box class of bug the docstring (lines 30-33) says the check exists to catch, so a typo turning it off undermines its purpose.

Severity medium is correct, not critical: triggering requires a specific malformed fixture-map entry, and the defect disables a safety/sanity guard rather than directly emitting a wrong DUT PASS/FAIL verdict. The suggested fix is sound: after computing prefix = spec.idn.split(",")[0].strip(), raise InstrumentError on an empty prefix.  

**M9. open_all leaks already-opened VISA sessions if a later instrument fails to open / IDN-mismatch**  
`src/computetest/fixtures.py` · fixtures.py:149-152 FixtureMap.open_all · _error-handling_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** open_all is a dict comprehension that opens instruments in order; if the Nth open() raises (open failure, or IDN mismatch which only closes the failing instrument), the N-1 already-opened instruments are never closed, leaking VISA sessions on a real station.  
- **Evidence:** return {spec.role: self.open(spec.role, mock=mock) for spec in self.instruments}. open() at line 143 only calls inst.close() for the instrument that mismatched; earlier successfully-opened instruments are discarded with no close().  
- **Fix:** Build the dict in a try/except that closes every already-opened instrument before re-raising, e.g. accumulate opened = {} and on exception iterate opened.values() calling close() then raise.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproducible from the code. `FixtureMap.open_all` (fixtures.py:149-152) is a dict comprehension `{spec.role: self.open(spec.role, mock=mock) for spec in self.instruments}` with no try/except. `open()` (lines 125-147) constructs each instrument and calls `inst.open()`; on IDN mismatch it closes ONLY the current failing instrument (line 143) before raising. If the Nth `self.open()` raises — either an IDN mismatch or a real-hardware `inst.open()` failure (transport/VISA open error) — the comprehension aborts and the N-1 already-opened instruments are discarded with no reference and no `close()`, leaking their VISA sessions. Independently verified that nothing mitigates this: the only test, `test_open_all_returns_role_map` (test_instruments_hal.py:232-239), exercises the success path only and closes instruments manually; `test_open_idn_mismatch_raises_and_closes` covers only the single-`open()` case. `open_all` has no callers in src/ and no wrapping context manager, so callers cannot clean up on the partial-failure path because no dict is ever returned. Not intentional/mock-fidelity: the single-`open()` path already cleans up the failing instrument (showing the author cared about leaks), but that care is not extended to already-opened siblings in `open_all`. Severity is correctly medium per the project rubric — this is real error-handling/edge-case mishandling on a real station (exclusive VISA/GPIB/USB resources can stay locked, causing a subsequent retry to fail), but it is not a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict or data corruption (which would be critical/high) and only manifests on the error path. The suggested fix (accumulate an `opened` dict, close all of its values on exception, then re-raise) is correct.  

**M10. IDN-match test is weak: passes whether the code checks the full prefix or only the manufacturer**  
`src/computetest/fixtures.py` · tests/test_instruments_hal.py:223-230 test_open_idn_mismatch_raises_and_closes · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The only IDN-mismatch test uses expected idn 'Keysight Technologies,MSO-X' against the mock default 'MockCorp,Model-1,...', a pure manufacturer mismatch. It would pass identically if the code did a full-prefix match or a manufacturer-only match, so it does not pin the documented wrong-box behavior and cannot catch the model-not-checked gap (Finding 1) or the empty-prefix gap (Finding 2).  
- **Evidence:** InstrumentSpec(..., idn="Keysight Technologies,MSO-X") vs mock idn 'MockCorp,Model-1,SN0001,1.0' — differs in field 0. No test supplies a same-vendor wrong-model idn, and no test asserts the close() actually happened (the comment says 'raises and closes' but only the raise is asserted).  
- **Fix:** Add a test where the mock idn shares the vendor but differs in model (inject via options={'idn': 'MockCorp,Model-2,...'} and spec idn 'MockCorp,Model-1') to pin the intended behavior, and assert inst was closed on mismatch; add a leading-comma idn case once Finding 2 is fixed.  
- **Verifier:** Verified against the actual code. The IDN check in src/computetest/fixtures.py:138 compares ONLY the manufacturer field: `not actual.startswith(spec.idn.split(",")[0])`. The sole mismatch test (tests/test_instruments_hal.py:223-230) uses spec idn "Keysight Technologies,MSO-X" vs the mock default "MockCorp,Model-1,SN0001,1.0", which differs in field 0. I confirmed both possible implementations raise on this input: manufacturer-only ("MockCorp...".startswith("Keysight Technologies") is False) and full-prefix (.startswith("Keysight Technologies,MSO-X") is False) both fail, so the test passes identically under either semantics and does NOT pin the documented manufacturer-only contract. Grepping the test file confirms only two idn references exist (line 220 positive, line 228 negative); no test supplies a same-vendor/wrong-model idn, so a regression that tightened the check to a full prefix would go uncaught, and a regression that loosened it (e.g. the leading-comma case I reproduced: ",Model-1".split(",")[0] == "" and any string .startswith("") is True, silently passing every box) would also go uncaught. The test name "..._raises_and_closes" claims close() is verified, but the body only asserts pytest.raises; close() at line 143 is never asserted (and is hard to assert since open() constructs the instrument internally and does not return it on the raising path). The finding is correctly scoped as test-quality, not a code-correctness claim: the manufacturer-only behavior is intentional and documented (lines 30-33, 139-142), but the test fails to lock that behavior in. This is a genuine weak-test finding ("would pass even if the code were wrong"), matching the medium bar. Minor caveat: the suggested "assert inst was closed" fix is awkward given open()'s internal construction and would require patching the registry/constructor; the more durable fix is adding a same-vendor/wrong-model case and (post-fix) a leading-comma case. Severity stays medium.  

**M11. Link-down severity test only exercises the two casings already in the keyword tuple, masking the case-sensitivity defect**  
`src/computetest/dmesg.py` · tests/test_dmesg_edges.py:6-9 (test_severity_link_down_variants) and tests/test_dmesg.py (no link-down parse-path casing test) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The link-down tests call _severity directly with strings ('Link Down', 'link is down') that happen to be verbatim members of _KEYWORDS, and the parse-path test uses 'Link Down' too. No test feeds parse_events a link-down line whose casing differs from the tuple, so the case-sensitive-filter-vs-case-insensitive-classifier bug (finding #1) passes the suite even though real link-down detection is broken.  
- **Evidence:** test_dmesg_edges.py line 7-8: `dmesg._severity("pcieport ...: Link Down")` and `dmesg._severity("nvme: link is down")` — both bypass parse_events' keyword filter entirely and both match the tuple exactly. test_parse_classifies_link_down_event uses 'Link Down on 0000:04:00.0' which also matches verbatim. A regression where the filter silently drops alternate casings is invisible.  
- **Fix:** Add a parse_events-level test with an alternate casing (e.g. 'PCIe link is DOWN' or 'Link is Down') asserting the event is kept and classified 'link'. This will currently fail, exposing finding #1.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced every claim. In src/computetest/dmesg.py the parse_events keyword filter (line 58: `if not any(k in line for k in _KEYWORDS)`) is case-SENSITIVE against `_KEYWORDS = ("AER", "PCIe Bus Error", "link is down", "Link Down", "link down")`, while `_severity` (line 40: `low = line.lower()`) is case-INSENSITIVE. So a link-down line whose casing differs from those three verbatim tuple variants is silently dropped before classification. Demonstrated: `parse_events('...PCIe link is DOWN')`, `'...Link is Down'`, `'...LINK DOWN'` all return `[]`, whereas the verbatim-casing lines ('Link Down', 'link is down', 'link down') return a 'link' event.

The test-quality claim is fully accurate: (1) tests/test_dmesg_edges.py:7-8 call `_severity` directly with 'Link Down' / 'link is down' — both verbatim `_KEYWORDS` members — bypassing the filter entirely; (2) the only parse-path link-down test (test_dmesg_edges.py:19-22) uses 'Link Down on 0000:04:00.0', again verbatim; (3) test_dmesg.py has no link-down test at all (its parse cases are corrected/non-fatal/fatal AER). grep across tests/ confirms no other test feeds parse_events an alternate-casing link-down line. I ran the suggested-fix assertion (`parse_events('...PCIe link is DOWN')` expects 1 'link' event) and it FAILS on current code (returns []), proving the underlying bug is invisible to the suite as the finding states.

Not refuted by conventions: this is a pure unit-tested parser, not a pragma real-hw path; no guard/test covers the alternate casing; the case-sensitive filter is an unmarked inconsistency, not documented intentional design (the classifier's explicit `.lower()` signals casing-tolerance was intended). Real-world relevance is genuine — kernel/NIC drivers emit phrasings like 'Link is Down' that the classifier would catch but the filter drops, so the dmesg corroboration monitor could silently miss a real hardware link-down. Severity medium is correct per the project rubric ('a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong'); the finding scopes itself to the test gap rather than re-claiming the higher-severity underlying defect.  

**M12. Per-DIMM concentration check silently disabled on legacy EDAC sysfs layout**  
`src/computetest/memory.py` · src/computetest/memory.py:73 (_read_edac dimm/rank glob) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** On kernels using the legacy EDAC sysfs layout (CONFIG_EDAC_LEGACY_SYSFS=y), per-DIMM data lives under mc<N>/csrow<N>/ch<N>_ce_count with no dimm<N>/rank<N> directories, so per_dimm comes back empty and the no_ce_concentration check passes trivially — a hot single stick can hide within the total-CE budget without ever firing the per-DIMM limit.  
- **Evidence:** `for dimm in sorted(glob.glob(f"{mc}/dimm[0-9]*") + glob.glob(f"{mc}/rank[0-9]*")):` matches only the modern layout. _limits computes `worst = max(per_dimm.values()) if per_dimm else 0`, so with empty per_dimm `no_ce_concentration(<=20/dimm)` is `0 <= 20` = always True. total_ce/total_ue from the controller files still work, so the bug is silent (no error, no skip).  
- **Fix:** Add a fallback that globs `mc<N>/csrow[0-9]*/ch[0-9]*_ce_count` (and ch*_ce_count labels) when no dimm*/rank* dirs are found, or at minimum raise/flag when controller CE>0 but per_dimm is empty so the concentration check isn't silently a no-op.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced and verified against the Linux kernel source. At src/computetest/memory.py:73 the glob only matches the modern EDAC layout (mc<N>/dimm<N>, mc<N>/rank<N>) and reads per-DIMM counts from dimm_ce_count/dimm_label (lines 74-75). On a legacy-sysfs kernel (CONFIG_EDAC_LEGACY_SYSFS=y) per-DIMM/channel CE counts live under mc<N>/csrow<N>/ch<N>_ce_count, which this glob never enters, so per_dimm returns empty. I confirmed via kernel source that the controller-level mc<N>/ce_count and mc<N>/ue_count attributes are registered unconditionally (DEVICE_ATTR(ce_count...)/DEVICE_ATTR(ue_count...) in mci_attrs[], no CONFIG guard), so total_ce/total_ue still populate correctly — making the failure fully silent (no exception, no zero-total tell). I then reproduced the consequence directly: _limits(total_ce=80, total_ue=0, per_dimm={}, max_ce_total=100, max_ce_per_dimm=20) yields no_ce_concentration(<=20/dimm)=True because line 58 sets worst=0 when per_dimm is empty, and MemoryHealth(...).ok is True with worst_dimm=None and no history line. The module docstring (lines 8-9) lists per-DIMM concentration as a core pass criterion ("a hot DIMM even within the total budget is suspect"); on legacy sysfs that check is silently a no-op. This is a logic bug inside a real-hw (# pragma: no cover) parser — exactly the high-value class the conventions say to scrutinize, not an intentional honesty-discipline None/raise. No test or guard covers it (all tests exercise only the mock path, which always fills per_dimm). Severity stays medium rather than critical: gross total CE and any UE are still caught via the layout-independent controller files, so the only weakened verdict is the concentration check, and only on the legacy kernel configuration — real and shipping (notably for older/embedded/automotive kernels), but not the highest-impact false-pass.  

**M13. gpu_temp_c reports an offline (-256C) GPU zone instead of falling back**  
`src/computetest/tegra.py` · tegra.py:160 (_build_health) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** gpu_temp is read from the full `thermal` dict, not the offline-filtered `meaningful` dict, so an offline GPU sensor (-256C, which the code elsewhere treats as 'offline, do not gate') is reported verbatim as gpu_temp_c=-256.0 — a garbage value surfaced to the operator/JSON.  
- **Evidence:** thermal includes offline zones (only `meaningful` filters t<=-40). Line 160 reads thermal.get('GPU'). Reproduced: check_tegra(_lines=['GPU@-256C CPU@50C tj@48C']) -> metrics['gpu_temp_c'] == -256.0 even though tj=48C is a valid fallback.  
- **Fix:** Resolve gpu_temp against `meaningful` (post-filter) rather than `thermal`: `meaningful.get('GPU', meaningful.get('tj', max_zone))`, so an offline GPU sensor falls back to tj/max_zone like every other gating path.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/tegra.py:160. `gpu_temp = thermal.get("GPU") or thermal.get("tj") or max_zone` reads the UNFILTERED `thermal` dict. An offline GPU sensor reads -256.0 (defined as "offline... Reported, never gated" by the code's own comments at lines 35-37 and 151), and -256.0 is truthy, so the `or`-chain short-circuits and surfaces it verbatim. Running `check_tegra(_lines=['GPU@-256C CPU@50C tj@48C'])` yields metrics['gpu_temp_c'] == -256.0 and summary() prints the self-contradictory `GPU=-256.0C ... -> OK note[GPU@-256C ignored (offline sensor)]`, despite valid CPU@50C/tj@48C fallbacks.

Not already-handled: the `meaningful` filter (lines 146-153) correctly excludes offline zones for GATING, but line 160 deliberately bypasses it; the note flags the zone as ignored yet the headline metric still reports the garbage value. The closest existing test, test_offline_sensor_ignored, uses an Orin line where GPU@52C is valid and only cpu@-256C is offline, so it never exercises an offline GPU; all 19 tegra tests pass with the bug in place.

Not intentional: the existence of the `or thermal.get("tj") or max_zone` fallback and the two test_gpu_temp_falls_back_* tests prove a fallback chain is the intended behavior — it simply fails for offline values. Bonus same-root-cause latent issue: a legitimate GPU@0C reading is falsy and is also incorrectly skipped (verified: GPU@0C with tj@48C -> gpu_temp_c=48.0). The suggested fix `meaningful.get("GPU", meaningful.get("tj", max_zone))` resolves both.

Severity stays medium (not high/critical): gating uses `max_zone` from the correctly-filtered `meaningful` dict, so this never flips a PASS/FAIL verdict and does not corrupt the verdict path — confirmed ok=True in the repro. It is a genuinely misleading reported number on the real-hw output (JSON + summary), but a simultaneous note tells the operator the GPU is offline, keeping impact below the "wrong spec value / crash" bar for high.  

**M14. Pass-check '0 < max_zone' spuriously FAILS valid sub-zero readings the code itself accepts**  
`src/computetest/tegra.py` · tegra.py:165, f"max_zone<={max_temp_c:g}C": 0 < max_zone <= max_temp_c · _edge-case_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C is -40.0, so any zone reading in (-40, 0] is kept as 'meaningful', yet the pass check requires `0 < max_zone`; a cold board (e.g. automotive cold-start, GPU@-10C) therefore reports a hard FAIL despite being healthy. The `0 <` guard conflates 'no data' (max_zone defaults to 0.0) with 'valid cold reading'.  
- **Evidence:** Line 38 `_MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C = -40.0`; line 150 discards only `t <= -40`; line 159 sets max_zone=0.0 when there is no meaningful data; line 165 `0 < max_zone <= max_temp_c`. Reproduced: check_tegra(_lines=['GPU@-10C CPU@-5C']) -> ok=False, checks['max_zone<=85C']=False.  
- **Fix:** Track whether any meaningful zone was seen separately from the value (e.g. a `have_data` flag or sentinel None), and gate as `have_data and max_zone <= max_temp_c` rather than overloading 0.0 as both 'cold' and 'missing'. The lower bound, if wanted, should be _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C, not 0.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly from the code at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/tegra.py:165. The module deliberately treats any zone reading above _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C = -40.0 as "meaningful" (line 38 + lines 147-153: only `t <= -40` is discarded as an offline sensor, per the Orin -256C comment). Yet the pass-check `0 < max_zone <= max_temp_c` rejects any meaningful reading in (-40, 0]. Verified at runtime: `check_tegra(_lines=['GPU@-10C CPU@-5C'])` -> ok=False, checks['max_zone<=85C']=False, max_zone_c=-5.0 — a wrong FAIL for a healthy cold board. `GPU@0C` also fails because 0.0 is overloaded as both the no-data sentinel (line 159) and a valid cold reading, so the gate cannot tell "cold" from "missing." This directly contradicts the module's own _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C contract and is a real automotive cold-start / cold-chamber qualification scenario for this toolkit. No guard clamps cold readings before the gate (only caller is cli.py:292, which passes through), and no test enshrines this as intended — test_no_samples_fails only asserts the genuine no-data case fails (which a have_data flag would still satisfy). Not refuted by mock-fidelity, pragma, or honesty-discipline conventions. The same `0 <` idiom appears in gpu.py:69 and nvme/health.py:63, but it is benign there (discrete GPUs/SSDs never report sub-zero and use a `.get(key, 0)` missing-data default); tegra is the case where it actually misfires because the module explicitly admits negative readings. Severity kept at medium: it is a genuine wrong-verdict bug, but in the less-dangerous false-FAIL direction (won't pass broken hardware) and only in a sub-zero operating window, so it is edge-case mishandling rather than a critical wrong-PASS or data corruption. Suggested fix is sound: track a have_data flag / None sentinel and gate as `have_data and max_zone <= max_temp_c`, with any lower bound set to _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C rather than 0.  

**M15. Test factory hardcodes sample_count/sample_count_bits and never asserts their relationship, so the inversion/math bug passes the suite**  
`src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py` · tests/test_lmt_adapter.py:121 (_rec defaults); no assertion anywhere · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** No test checks that sample_count is correctly derived from sample_count_bits or that either matches OCP semantics; the only place they appear together hardcodes both, so the schema-parity bug (finding 1) is invisible to the suite that exists precisely to catch upstream drift.  
- **Evidence:** tests/test_lmt_adapter.py:121 `sample_count=1 << 18, sample_count_bits=18,` in a defaults dict; grep for 'sample_count' in the test file returns only the COLUMNS list (line 38) and this hardcoded pair. TestSchemaParity asserts column names/order but not value semantics. Comment at top of test file claims 'columns and defaults assert here MUST stay in lock-step with the OCP pci_lmt CLI' — but value semantics are not asserted.  
- **Fix:** Add a test asserting the OCP relationship (sample_count is the raw 0..127 register value; sample_count_bits == int(2**(sample_count/3))) on records produced by from_margin_result, plus a range check that max_voltage_offset is in 0.01V units within the legal field range. These would fail today and pin the schema contract.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and independently reproduced. The finding's premise (an inversion in sample_count/sample_count_bits) is real: I verified against the OCP reference source at src/pci_lmt/pcie_lane_margining.py (lines 469-476), which computes `sample_count = (margin_payload_status & 0x7F)` (raw 7-bit register field, 0..127, where Value = 3*log2(bits margined)) and `sample_count_bits = int(pow(2, sample_count/3))` (actual bits margined). collector.py:144 uses sample_count_bits as the BER denominator. computetest/lmt_adapter.py:167 does the exact inverse: `sample_count = 1 << sample_count_bits` with sample_count_bits=18, producing sample_count=262144 (far outside the 7-bit field) and sample_count_bits=18 (which should be the bit count, ~262144). Both fields are wrong and mislabeled relative to the OCP schema the module claims drop-in parity with.

The test-quality claim is also correct and reproduced: grepping the entire tests/ tree, `sample_count`/`sample_count_bits` appear only at test_lmt_adapter.py:38 (COLUMNS name/order assertion in TestSchemaParity) and :121 (the `_rec` factory hardcoding `sample_count=1 << 18, sample_count_bits=18`). No test anywhere asserts the OCP value relationship `sample_count_bits == int(2**(sample_count/3))` or that sample_count is a 0..127 register value. Worse, the formatter-test factory bakes in the same inversion as production, so to_json/to_csv round-trip tests carry the wrong values silently. The CLI/ocpdiag tests (lines 196-263) only assert key-sets, CSV header order, and measurement-name patterns — never the values — and cli.py:95-106 emits one OCP measurement per field, so the inverted values flow into artifacts unchecked. The test-file docstring claims columns/defaults stay "in lock-step with the OCP pci_lmt CLI," but value semantics are unasserted, exactly as the finding states. This is not intentional design under the honesty-discipline/mock-fidelity/pragma conventions.

Severity medium is correct: per the project rubric this is "a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong" (medium). The underlying inversion it fails to catch is itself high (wrong numbers + wrong BER denominator on real-hw OCP output), but this finding is scoped to the test gap, so medium is the accurate rating. The suggested fix's secondary max_voltage_offset range check is a minor add-on; the core assertion (pin the sample_count<->sample_count_bits OCP relationship) is sound and would fail today.  

**M16. ExternalBERT BER reads 0.0 (apparent perfect link) when the bits counter overflows to inf**  
`src/computetest/instruments.py` · instruments.py:772 (ExternalBERT.read_result, ber computation) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** ber = n_err / n_bits computes errors/inf = 0.0 when :READ:BITS? returns the 9.9E37 overflow sentinel, so a window with real errors but a saturated bit counter reports BER = 0.0 — a misleadingly clean PHY result.  
- **Evidence:** Verified: :READ:ERR?='500', :READ:BITS?='9.9E37' yields bits=inf, ber=0.0. n_bits>0 is True for inf so the guard 'else 0.0' is skipped, but the division silently produces 0.0 with 500 unaccounted errors.  
- **Fix:** Treat a non-finite bits count as a fault: if not math.isfinite(n_bits) or n_bits <= 0: raise InstrumentError or set ber to float('nan') and flag, rather than letting errors/inf collapse to a passing 0.0.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly as claimed. At instruments.py:772, `ber = (n_err / n_bits) if n_bits > 0 else 0.0`. When `:READ:BITS?` returns the IEEE 488.2 overflow sentinel `9.9E37`, `parse_scpi_float` maps it to `+inf` (line 92-93, documented at lines 22-24). `inf > 0` is True, so the `else 0.0` guard is skipped and `500 / inf` silently yields `ber = 0.0`. Empirical run: errors=500, bits=inf, ber=0.0 — a window with 500 real errors reports a perfect link.

This is NOT refuted by the conventions: (1) `read_result` carries no `# pragma: no cover` and IS unit-tested (test_read_result_derives_ber, test_read_result_zero_bits_yields_zero_ber) — there is simply no test for the overflow case. (2) The sentinel-to-inf scenario is a genuine real-hardware path: a saturated/faulted BERT bit counter returns 9.9E37, which the module is explicitly built to parse. (3) The module's own design intent (lines 22-24) is that the inf mapping should prevent a saturated reading from "looking like a real reading" — and `measure_resistance` (line 671) demonstrates the correct pattern, explicitly checking `i.overflow` before dividing. `read_result` violates that same in-file convention. (4) This is precisely the project's "do NOT mislabel a verdict / fake a PASS" concern: the headline BER metric reads clean while errors occurred; the BertReading.ber docstring ("0.0 when bits <= 0") doesn't even contemplate bits == inf.

Severity stays medium rather than critical: read_result returns raw fields, not a verdict, and there is no production consumer in this codebase that turns BertReading.ber into a pass/fail today (gmsl/checks.py uses ber.assess independently). The true errors=500 count is also retained in the dataclass, so a careful consumer could detect it. So it is a real correctness defect producing a misleading number on a real-hw path, but not a currently-wired wrong PASS/FAIL. Suggested fix is sound — guard with `math.isfinite(n_bits)` (raise InstrumentError or set ber=NaN), matching the existing measure_resistance overflow handling.  

**M17. run_end() can never emit testStatus=ERROR; a test-program fault is reported as a DUT verdict (status=COMPLETE, result=FAIL)**  
`src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py` · src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py:119-123 (run_end); CLI wiring src/computetest/cli.py:373,405-411 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When the harness itself errors out (device-not-found, config/IO error, results-DB error) it emits a run_error artifact and then ends the run with result=FAIL and a hardcoded status=COMPLETE, falsely attributing a DUT failure for a run that never produced a verdict. The module's own stated discipline ('reserve ERROR for an internal/test-program fault') is applied to steps but not to the run.  
- **Evidence:** run_end hardcodes status: def run_end(self, status): return self._write({"testRunArtifact": {"testRunEnd": {"status": "COMPLETE", "result": _RUN_RESULT.get(status, "NOT_APPLICABLE")}}}). CLI maps every non-pass/skip exit to 'fail': _EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS = {EXIT_PASS:'pass', EXIT_FAIL:'fail', EXIT_UNAVAIL:'skip'}; status = _EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS.get(rc, 'fail'); em.run_end(status). So EXIT_NOTFOUND/EXIT_IO (which already emitted run_error 'device-not-found'/'config-io-error') produce testRunEnd{status:COMPLETE, result:FAIL}. A downstream OCP consumer reads that as 'the test ran fine and the DUT failed.'  
- **Fix:** Give run_end an explicit run-status arg (e.g. run_end(result, *, status='COMPLETE')) and have the CLI error handlers pass status='ERROR' with result='NOT_APPLICABLE' when a run_error was emitted (device-not-found, config-io-error, results-db-error). Keep COMPLETE only when a real pass/fail/skip verdict was reached.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced concretely. `run_end()` (src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py:119-123) hardcodes `"status": "COMPLETE"` and only maps the result via `_RUN_RESULT.get(status, "NOT_APPLICABLE")`, so it can never emit `testStatus=ERROR`. The CLI's `_EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS = {0:'pass', 1:'fail', 5:'skip'}` (cli.py:373) omits EXIT_NOTFOUND(3), EXIT_IO(4), EXIT_USAGE(2), and rc=130; `_close_ocpdiag` does `status = _EXIT_TO_OCP_STATUS.get(rc, "fail")` (cli.py:409) in the `finally`, so every harness-level fault falls through to "fail". Running `computetest bert -d 9999:99:99.9 --ocpdiag <path>` exits 3 (device-not-found), emits a run_error with symptom "device-not-found", and then ends the run with `{"status":"COMPLETE","result":"FAIL"}` — a false DUT verdict for a run that never reached a measurement.

This is a real defect, not intentional design. The vendored OCP schema (io/ocpdiag_schemas/test_status.json) defines ERROR in the testStatus enum precisely for an internal/test-program fault, and test_run_end.json defines NOT_APPLICABLE in testResult. The module's own comment (ocpdiag.py:36-37) states it reserves ERROR for an internal/test-program fault, and step_end() (ocpdiag.py:150-151) correctly implements that for steps. The project's reference doc docs/reference/ocp-diag-emitter.md line 160 explicitly documents the canonical mapping "anything else (a test-program error) -> ERROR / NOT_APPLICABLE / UNKNOWN" — but line 52 documents that run_end's testStatus is "always COMPLETE." So the discipline is applied to steps but not the run, exactly as the finding states.

No guard or test refutes it: test_ocpdiag_emits_run_error_on_bad_device (tests/test_ocpdiag.py:520-531) asserts only that an error artifact AND a testRunEnd are present; it does NOT assert the testRunEnd result/status, so it does not catch the mislabel. This is a genuine "mislabels a verdict" honesty defect under the project's own red-flag list, since the run-level OCP stream is the contract for the hyperscale MT pipelines this module targets.

Severity stays medium (not critical/high): it does not produce a false PASS on measured hardware, and the corrective signal is still present (the run_error artifact carries the correct symptom and the CLI exit code is correct at 3/4/5). The harm is bounded to downstream OCP consumers that key off testRunEnd, where a harness/environment fault is miscounted as a DUT FAIL in yield/FA dashboards. The suggested fix (give run_end an explicit status arg and pass status='ERROR', result='NOT_APPLICABLE' from the run_error handlers) is correct.  

**M18. Test enshrines the wrong EID allocation (would pass even if MCTP semantics were corrected)**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py` · tests/test_nvme_mi.py:58-59 (TestMctpEndpointId.test_routable_range) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The test asserts MctpEndpointId(0xF0) and MctpEndpointId(0xFE) are is_reserved, locking in the fabricated 0xF0-0xFE band; it does not test any value in 0xF0-0xFE as routable, so it provides false confidence and would actively block a spec-correct fix.  
- **Evidence:** `for v in (0x01, 0x07, 0xF0, 0xFE): assert MctpEndpointId(v).is_reserved` — 0xF0 and 0xFE are valid assignable EIDs in MCTP, not reserved.  
- **Fix:** Change the reserved set to (0x01, 0x07) only, and add a routable assertion for an upper-range EID, e.g. `assert MctpEndpointId(0xFE).is_routable`.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced and confirmed. tests/test_nvme_mi.py:55-59 (TestMctpEndpointId.test_routable_range) asserts `MctpEndpointId(0xF0).is_reserved` and `MctpEndpointId(0xFE).is_reserved`, and tests NO value in 0xF0-0xFE as routable. This locks in a fabricated 0xF0-0xFE "reserved" band that does not exist in the MCTP specification.

Spec verification: The MCTP base spec (DSP0236) defines the 8-bit EID space as: 0x00 = Null EID, 0x01-0x07 = Reserved, 0x08-0xFE = valid assignable/routable endpoint IDs, 0xFF = Broadcast. There is no 0xF0-0xFE reservation in MCTP. So src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:50-55 is spec-wrong: is_routable returns 0x08-0xEF (should be 0x08-0xFE) and is_reserved includes 0xF0-0xFE (should be only 0x01-0x07). The code comment at lines 26-31 affirmatively claims this allocation is "Per DSP0239" — that is a spec-compliance claim the spec does not back (and the doc number is itself off; the base EID allocation is DSP0236, not DSP0239). The test merely mirrors the buggy production code, so a spec-correct fix would break this test = false confidence, exactly as claimed.

Not refuted: independently reproducible. Not already-handled: no guard/test corrects the band. Not intentional: this is the inverse of the project's honesty discipline — it fakes spec compliance rather than setting an unknown to None and raising.

Severity held at medium (the finding's test-quality dimension): the underlying defect is a wrong spec value (would be "high" if it drove a verdict), BUT is_routable/is_reserved are referenced only by tests (grep across src/ and tests/ shows no production decision path consumes them), so no real-hardware PASS/FAIL verdict is currently wrong today. The medium rating fits the test-quality harm: it cements an incorrect band and would actively block the spec-correct fix. Note the root cause is in production code (the comment + property ranges at mctp.py:26-31,50-55), not solely the test — the suggested fix should also correct is_routable to 0x08-0xFE and drop 0xF0-0xFE from is_reserved, and fix the DSP0239→DSP0236 citation.  

**M19. Coalescing checks only the last-emitted event, defeating per-type coalescing on interleaved types**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/aem.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/aem.py:101-106 (Subscription.deliver) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The coalesce window is supposed to be per event type ('skip if the SAME event type was emitted within the window'), but the implementation only compares against self.sent[-1].event_type and a single global _last_emit_ms, so a duplicate of an event type is wrongly delivered whenever any different event type was emitted in between — even inside the coalescing window.  
- **Evidence:** Docstring line 101: '# Coalesce: skip if the same event type was emitted within the window.' but the guard is `self.sent[-1].event_type == event_type and timestamp_ms - self._last_emit_ms < self.request.coalesce_window_ms`. Reproduced: HEALTH@0 (True), TEMP@100 (True), HEALTH@200 (True) with coalesce_window_ms=1000 — HEALTH@200 is only 200ms after HEALTH@0 (inside the window) yet is delivered because the last emit was TEMP. sent count = 3, when the contract implies 2.  
- **Fix:** Track last-emit time per event type, e.g. a dict `_last_emit_ms_by_type: dict[AsyncEventType,int]`, and coalesce when `event_type in _last_emit_ms_by_type and timestamp_ms - _last_emit_ms_by_type[event_type] < coalesce_window_ms`. Update the entry on emit. Then add a test that interleaves two types within one window and asserts the duplicate is coalesced.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and concretely reproduced. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/mi/aem.py:101-106, the docstring states the contract is per-type ("Coalesce: skip if the same event type was emitted within the window"), but the guard only compares against self.sent[-1].event_type with a single global _last_emit_ms. Reproduced: with coalesce_window_ms=1000, delivering HEALTH@0 (True), TEMPERATURE_THRESHOLD@100 (True), HEALTH@200 (True) yields sent count = 3; HEALTH@200 is only 200ms after HEALTH@0 — inside the window — yet is emitted because the last emit was TEMP. Per the documented per-type contract it should coalesce (count = 2). No guard handles it: test_coalesce_within_window (test_nvme_mi.py:239) uses a single event type so it passes despite the bug; test_sequence_numbers_monotonic (line 273) interleaves types but with coalesce_window_ms=0 (coalescing disabled), so it also misses it — a genuine test gap (weak test that passes even though the code is wrong). Not intentional: the inline docstring, __init__.py:16, and the roadmap all describe per-type coalescing; nothing documents a "consecutive-duplicates-only" design. Not a real-hw/pragma path (no pragma marker; the whole file is unit-tested) — this is the deterministic mock model the conventions say should faithfully model real dynamics, so the divergence is fair to flag. The suggested fix (a _last_emit_ms_by_type dict plus an interleaved-within-window test) is correct.

Downgraded high -> medium: the defect lives in the in-process mock subscription model and causes over-delivery (under-coalescing), not a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict, data corruption, a spec-number error, or a real-hw parser crash/misparse. It also does not drop/hide events. Per the severity rubric this is "edge-case mishandling + a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong," i.e. medium.  

**M20. Coalesce test uses a single event type, so it passes even with the global-last-only bug**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/aem.py` · tests/test_nvme_mi.py:239-253 (test_coalesce_within_window) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** test_coalesce_within_window only ever delivers AsyncEventType.HEALTH, so the global self.sent[-1] shortcut is indistinguishable from a correct per-type implementation; the only test that interleaves types (test_sequence_numbers_monotonic) sets coalesce_window_ms=0 (coalescing disabled). No test can detect the interleaving defect above.  
- **Evidence:** Lines 241-252 deliver HEALTH at 0/500/1500 only. test_sequence_numbers_monotonic (273-283) interleaves HEALTH/TEMP but uses coalesce_window_ms=0. Both pass against the buggy code.  
- **Fix:** Add a test: subscribe to {HEALTH, TEMP} with coalesce_window_ms=1000, deliver HEALTH@0, TEMP@100, HEALTH@200, and assert the second HEALTH is coalesced (len(sub.sent)==2). This fails today and pins the intended per-type semantics.  
- **Verifier:** Verified accurate on all counts. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/tests/test_nvme_mi.py, test_coalesce_within_window (lines 239-253) delivers only AsyncEventType.HEALTH at t=0/500/1500, so the coalesce check at aem.py:102-107 (self.sent[-1].event_type == event_type with global self._last_emit_ms) is indistinguishable from a correct per-type implementation. The only tests that interleave types — test_sequence_numbers_monotonic (273-283) and test_rate_limit_per_second (255-271) — both set coalesce_window_ms=0, disabling coalescing. So no test exercises interleaved types with coalescing enabled, leaving the global-last-only behavior undetectable. I empirically reproduced it: delivering HEALTH@0, TEMP@100, HEALTH@200 with a 1000ms window yields 3 emitted events (HEALTH@200 NOT coalesced) where per-type semantics would yield 2, and the full existing suite passes regardless. The inline comment at aem.py:101 ('skip if the same event type was emitted within the window') states per-type intent that the implementation does not honor for interleaved deliveries, so this is not intentional/honesty-discipline/faithful-mock; it is a genuine test gap masking a behavior bug. The suggested test (subscribe {HEALTH,TEMP}, window=1000, HEALTH@0/TEMP@100/HEALTH@200, assert len==2) fails today and pins the intended semantics. Medium is correct per the project rubric (a weak test that passes even if code were wrong); the bug over-delivers rather than dropping events, so it does not directly flip a hardware PASS/FAIL verdict.  

**M21. mock=False with no transport silently returns fabricated mock VPD data instead of raising**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/vpd.py` · vpd.py:84 (vpd_read predicate `if mock or transport is None`) · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Calling vpd_read(req, mock=False) without a transport returns the hardcoded _MOCK_VPD blob as a VpdReadResult, rather than raising — so a caller who explicitly asked for a real read but forgot/failed to supply a transport gets fabricated FRU data (MockCorp / MockSSD-1TB / SN-MOCK-0001) presented as a genuine device read, with no flag on VpdReadResult indicating its mock provenance.  
- **Evidence:** `def vpd_read(request, transport=None, *, mock: bool = True)` then `if mock or transport is None:` — verified at runtime: `vpd_read(VpdReadRequest(offset=0,length=8), transport=None, mock=False)` returns `b'\x01\x00\x00\x05\x00\x00\x00\xfa'` (mock data). The result dataclass has only `request` and `data` fields, no `from_mock`/source flag. NB: this same `mock or transport is None` pattern is intentional and identical in sibling reset.py:58 and subsystem_health.py:115, so it is a package convention, not a vpd-only slip — which is why this is medium, not critical.  
- **Fix:** When mock is explicitly False and transport is None, raise (e.g. ValueError('mock=False requires a transport')) instead of silently falling back to the mock; or add a `from_mock: bool` field to VpdReadResult so downstream verdicts can never mistake fabricated data for a real read. Align the whole mi/ package together.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced at runtime: `vpd_read(VpdReadRequest(offset=0,length=8), transport=None, mock=False)` returns the hardcoded `_MOCK_VPD` FRU blob (`b'\x01\x00\x00\x05\x00\x00\x00\xfa'`, decoding to MockCorp/MockSSD-1TB/SN-MOCK-0001) as a plain `VpdReadResult`. The predicate at vpd.py:84 is `if mock or transport is None:`, so the explicit operator signal `mock=False` is silently overridden by the `transport is None` short-circuit. `VpdReadResult` (vpd.py:55-59) carries only `request` and `data` — no `from_mock`/source field — so a downstream verdict cannot tell fabricated FRU data from a genuine device read. No test exercises `mock=False` (tests only call `mock=True`), so no guard handles it. The finding's package-convention claim is also accurate: the identical `if mock or transport is None:` pattern appears in reset.py:58, subsystem_health.py:115, and controller_health.py:77.

Not fully refutable as intentional: the docstrings document `transport is None`/`mock=True` as the mock selectors, but the contradictory call `mock=False`+`transport=None` is an out-of-contract caller-error case that the code silently masks with believable fake data instead of raising — exactly the "fabricated data presented as a real read, no provenance flag" pattern the honesty discipline says to flag.

Severity correctly medium, not critical: triggering it requires a contradictory caller (explicit `mock=False` while forgetting the transport); the default `mock=True` and the unimplemented real path (NotImplementedError) mean normal usage and real station integration (which supplies a transport) never hit it. Impact is bounded to a misconfigured real-read harness silently getting fake FRU data. Suggested fix is sound: when `mock` is explicitly False and `transport is None`, raise instead of falling back, and/or add a `from_mock` provenance flag — applied consistently across the mi/ package.  

**M22. healthy() failure branches are completely untested for the controller poll**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/controller_health.py` · tests/test_nvme_mi.py:291-307 (TestControllerHealthPoll) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Only the all-healthy mock path is asserted; none of the three conditions that should break healthy (critical_warning!=0, available_spare<100, percentage_used>=2) has a test, so inverting or breaking any of them would not fail the suite.  
- **Evidence:** The class asserts `chsp.healthy`, `temperature_c == 41.0`, `available_spare == 100`, `critical_warning == 0` on the healthy mock and a controller_id propagation — but there is no negative case. Contrast subsystem_health.py, whose every healthy branch has a dedicated breaking test in test_nvme_mi_extras.py (test_drive_not_functioning_breaks_healthy, test_spare_below_threshold_breaks_healthy, test_percentage_drive_life_used_above_2_breaks_healthy).  
- **Fix:** Add negative tests constructing ControllerHealthStatus directly with critical_warning=1, available_spare=99, and percentage_used=2 and asserting `not healthy`, mirroring the subsystem tests.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and independently reproduced. `ControllerHealthStatus.healthy` (controller_health.py:38-41) ANDs three conditions: `critical_warning == 0`, `available_spare >= 100`, `percentage_used < 2`. A grep of the entire tests/ tree shows `ControllerHealthStatus` is referenced ONLY in tests/test_nvme_mi.py, and `TestControllerHealthPoll` (lines 289-307) has exactly three tests — all positive: `test_mock_poll_is_healthy` asserts `chsp.healthy` plus the mock field values, `test_mock_poll_controller_id_propagates`, and `test_chsp_to_dict_round_trip` asserts `d["healthy"] is True`. There is no test asserting `not healthy`. The contrast cited is accurate: the negative tests test_drive_not_functioning_breaks_healthy / test_spare_below_threshold_breaks_healthy / test_percentage_drive_life_used_above_2_breaks_healthy in test_nvme_mi_extras.py (lines 47, 79, 91) all construct `NvmSubsystemHealth` (subsystem_health.py), a DIFFERENT class — they do not exercise ControllerHealthStatus at all. Mutation analysis: the mock (available_spare=100, percentage_used=1, critical_warning=0) means hard inversions of the first two conditions WOULD be caught by `assert chsp.healthy`, but loosening `percentage_used < 2` (e.g. to `<= 2` or `< 100`) or dropping that comparison entirely would NOT fail any test, since the mock's percentage_used=1 still satisfies the weakened predicate. So the claim "breaking any of them would not fail the suite" is slightly overstated (two of three break-inversions are incidentally caught by the boundary-valued mock), but the substantive gap is real: no negative case pins the failure semantics, and the percentage_used branch in particular is unprotected. Not refutable as already-handled or intentional — these are not pragma real-hw paths and the conventions explicitly call test-quality weaknesses in scope. Severity medium per the project rubric ("a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong"); not higher because the production `healthy` logic is itself correct and this is a coverage/assertion gap, not a wrong verdict on hardware today. Suggested fix is valid: add negative tests constructing ControllerHealthStatus with critical_warning=1, available_spare=99, and percentage_used=2 each asserting `not healthy`.  

**M23. NSS bit definitions assert bit positions/polarity the cited spec section does not clearly support**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/subsystem_health.py` · subsystem_health.py:27-31 (NSS_* constants) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=low · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The NSS (NVM Subsystem Status) bit map is hand-assigned to bits 0..3 with `NSS_DRIVE_FUNCTIONING = 1<<0` commented '1 = controller is responding', but the NVMe-MI NSHSP NSS byte places the Drive Functioning (DF) and Reset Not Required (RNR) flags at higher bit positions, and some revisions reserve bit 0. Because `drive_functioning` (and thus `healthy`) keys off bit 0, a real-hardware NSS byte could be misinterpreted, flipping the PASS/FAIL verdict.  
- **Evidence:** Lines 28-31: `NSS_DRIVE_FUNCTIONING = 1 << 0`, `NSS_RESET_NOT_REQUIRED = 1 << 1`, `NSS_PORT_0_PCI_FUNCTIONING = 1 << 2`, `NSS_PORT_1_PCI_FUNCTIONING = 1 << 3`, with the comment claiming bit 0 = controller responding. The module cites 'NVMe-MI §5.1' but the exact NSS bit table is not reproduced.  
- **Fix:** Cross-check each NSS bit against the exact NSHSP response table in the targeted NVMe-MI revision (confirm DF/RNR/port bit indices and active-high vs active-low) and add a comment quoting the table cell; add a decode test that feeds a real captured NSS byte.  
- **Verifier:** CONFIRMED (with one residual caveat). The code at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/mi/subsystem_health.py:28-31 hand-assigns the NVM Subsystem Status (NSS) byte as NSS_DRIVE_FUNCTIONING=1<<0 (bit 0, commented "1 = controller is responding"), RNR=1<<1, P0=1<<2, P1=1<<3. Independent verification against the NVMe-MI spec contradicts this: NVM Express documentation places the Drive Functioning flag at bit 5, not bit 0 (RNR at bit 4, the port PCIe-link-active bits at 2/3). The field names the finding cites (DF/RNR/P0LA/P1LA) are confirmed real spec fields (libnvme src/nvme/types.h defines struct nvme_mi_nvm_ss_health_status with the nss u8 byte). So the constants as written do not match the spec layout, and the misleading "bit 0 = controller responding" comment is wrong.

Impact: drive_functioning (line 51-53) and therefore healthy (56-61) key off bit 0. A real healthy NSS byte (DF=1 at bit 5) would read drive_functioning=False -> healthy=False (false FAIL); a byte with bit 0 set for an unrelated/reserved reason but DF clear reads as functioning (false PASS). This is a genuine spec-conformance defect in a public, exported decode map.

Why NOT refuted/intentional/already-handled: there is no guard or test that cross-checks against a real captured byte. The mock (_MOCK_NSHSP, line 95-97) emits the same wrong constants and the property reads them back, and tests in tests/test_nvme_mi_extras.py (lines 47-101) were authored with the identical wrong constants, so they pass tautologically — exactly the "weak test that would pass even if the code were wrong" case. This is not honesty-discipline (no None/raise here) nor a deliberate spec mask.

Why medium, not high: the real-hw decode path is unimplemented — poll_nvm_subsystem_health with a transport raises NotImplementedError (line 127, pragma) — so today nothing live decodes a real NSS byte; the wrong map produces no wrong verdict yet. It is a latent wrong-spec-value that ships an incorrect decode table and a wrong comment, and WILL flip the verdict the moment a station wires the real NSHDS decoder per the cited TODO. That is more than a nit but not currently a live wrong PASS/FAIL.

Caveat: the ratified NVMe-MI PDFs returned HTTP 403, so I could not visually confirm the exact P0LA/P1LA indices; however the load-bearing claim (Drive Functioning sits at a higher bit, NOT bit 0) is corroborated by the NVM Express search result stating Drive Functional is at bit 5, which is sufficient to establish the as-written constants are wrong. Suggested fix stands: quote the exact NSHDS table cell per the targeted revision and add a decode test fed a real captured NSS byte.  

**M24. EOM threshold path is untested at any non-default threshold, hiding the bypass**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/checks.py` · tests/test_gmsl_eom.py:20-44 (TestEomCheck) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed

- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproduced. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gmsl/checks.py, check_eom (lines 229-243) hardcodes the module constants: `eye_quality_verdict(eye, ui_min=DEFAULT_EOM_UI_MIN, mv_min=DEFAULT_EOM_MV_MIN)`. It never reads the instance thresholds (`self._eom_mv_min`/`_eom_ui_min`) that `SerDesLink.set_eom_thresholds()` sets (serdes.py:277-284), nor does it call the instance-aware `eom_verdict()` helper (serdes.py:350-353) that shmoo.py:81 uses. So `check_eom` silently ignores `set_eom_thresholds()`, contradicting its own docstring ("verdict reuses the retimer's eye_quality_verdict with GMSL-specific thresholds").

Reproduced directly: MockSerDes(injected_eye_mv=70.0) with set_eom_thresholds(mv_min=100.0) gives a ~64 mV worst sub-eye; eom_verdict() correctly returns False, but check_eom().checks['eye_open'] returns True and h.ok is True — a wrong PASS on a tuned-threshold link.

The test-quality claim is accurate: in tests/test_gmsl_eom.py the TestEomCheck class (lines 20-44) only runs check_eom at module defaults — test_pam4_... at 70 mV (above the 40 mV default) and test_weak_eye_fails_verdict at 10 mV (below it); none calls set_eom_thresholds before check_eom. The only set_eom_thresholds tests (tests/test_gmsl_serdes.py:143-155) exercise eom_verdict() directly, never check_eom(), so the bypass is invisible to the suite. The suggested fix reproduces the divergence and is valid.

Not excused by conventions: check_eom is fully unit-tested (no real-hw pragma), the verdict math runs identically on Mock and real backends, and this is the opposite of the honesty discipline — it fakes a PASS against thresholds a program explicitly configured. Severity medium is correct for the test-quality dimension (a weak test that passes even though the code is wrong); the underlying production wrong-PASS divergence it hides borders on high.  

**M25. check_deserializer real path reads the SAME deserializer sysfs node for every link; cannot detect a single down link**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/video.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/video.py:155-157 (check_deserializer real branch) -> _real_check_gmsl:84-92 · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** On real hardware the per-link loop calls check_gmsl(addr, vid=/dev/video{i}) with the SAME addr for every i, so _real_check_gmsl builds the identical lock_path/err_path (/sys/bus/i2c/devices/{addr}/link_status, .../error_count) for all N links and reports identical locked/error_count for every link — the per-link 'one camera link down' detection that the mock advertises does not exist on hardware.  
- **Evidence:** Real branch: `links.append(check_gmsl(addr, vid, expect_w=expect_w, expect_h=expect_h, frames=frames, mock=False))` passes `addr` (not the per-link `link_id = f"{addr}:link{i}"` built on line 148, which is used ONLY in the mock branch). Inside _real_check_gmsl: `lock_path = f"/sys/bus/i2c/devices/{link}/link_status"` and `err_path = f"/sys/bus/i2c/devices/{link}/error_count"` depend only on `link`==`addr`, identical for all i. The only thing that varies per link is /dev/video{i} (resolution/frames).  
- **Fix:** Read per-link lock/error from per-link sysfs (e.g. a part-specific node like .../link{i}/lock or a register-indexed path) and/or set GmslHealth.link to the per-link identifier so each link's lock/error is distinct. If per-link sysfs is genuinely unavailable for the part, do NOT report a per-link locked/error_count that is actually the aggregate — mark it as derived-from-aggregate so it cannot masquerade as per-link evidence.  
- **Verifier:** The core mechanism is factually correct and reproducible from the code. In check_deserializer (video.py:155-157) the real branch calls check_gmsl(addr, vid, ...) passing addr — NOT the per-link link_id = f"{addr}:link{i}" built on line 148, which is used ONLY in the mock branch (line 153). In fact link_id contains a colon and would fail _LINK_RE (line 75) if passed to the real path. Inside _real_check_gmsl, lock_path (line 84) and err_path (line 89) depend solely on link==addr, so all N iterations read the identical /sys/bus/i2c/devices/{addr}/link_status and .../error_count nodes and produce identical locked/error_count. Worse, line 112 sets GmslHealth.link=addr for every link, so the per-link records aren't even labeled distinctly on hardware. This matches the honesty-discipline item the project says TO flag ("reports a per-link locked/error_count that is actually the aggregate"); the docstring's "best-effort over /dev/videoN" caveat does not disclose that the lock/error signals are aggregate-duplicated. So it is a genuine real-hw defect, not refuted and not fully covered by the documented caveat.

I downgrade from high to medium for two reasons. First, the finding overstates impact: per-link detection is NOT entirely absent on hardware. video_device = /dev/video{i} DOES vary per link, so the resolution_ok and frames_captured checks genuinely discriminate per link — and a down camera link most directly manifests as zero frames on its /dev/videoN, which the per-link --stream-mmap (captured=0 for that link only, lines 104-111) catches. So one camera link going dark would still flip that link's frames_captured check and fail the deserializer overall. What is actually broken is specifically the lock-status and error-count per-link discrimination (two of four per-link checks report the aggregate). Second, this is a real-hw-only (# pragma: no cover) path with an explicit best-effort docstring, so it is not a fake mock PASS and the gates correctly don't exercise it. Net effect: it could mislead a field technician about WHICH link is unlocked / which has errors (wrong per-link evidence), but it does not guarantee a wrong overall PASS/FAIL deserializer verdict — fitting medium (per-link edge-case mishandling / weak per-link evidence) rather than high. Suggested fix stands: either read part-specific per-link register/sysfs nodes, or explicitly mark locked/error_count as derived-from-aggregate so they cannot masquerade as per-link evidence.  

**M26. Unguarded int() on untrusted sysfs/v4l2 text can raise ValueError on real hardware**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/video.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/video.py:92, 99 · _error-handling_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** error_count parsing `int(fh.read().strip() or 0)` and resolution parsing `w, h = int(parts[0]), int(parts[1])` assume strictly decimal content; real sysfs counters/driver output are sometimes hex ('0x0c'), 'N/A', or have trailing units, which makes int() raise ValueError and crash the real path with no diagnostic.  
- **Evidence:** Line 92: `errors = int(fh.read().strip() or 0)`; line 99: `w, h = int(parts[0]), int(parts[1])`. No base-detection, no try/except, no len(parts)>=2 guard (a malformed 'Width/Height' line missing the '/' yields IndexError).  
- **Fix:** Parse defensively: strip non-digits or use int(s, 0) for the counter, guard len(parts) >= 2 before unpacking, and on parse failure fall back to a sentinel that fails the corresponding check (errors->nonzero or w/h->0) rather than raising — or raise a clear diagnostic naming the offending file/line.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed as a real defect in the real-hw path of /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gmsl/video.py (_real_check_gmsl, pragma: no cover - real-hw path; per project conventions a parsing bug inside such a path is a high-value finding, not an untested-pragma nit).

Factual verification:
- Line 92: `errors = int(fh.read().strip() or 0)` is unguarded. The `or 0` only covers the empty-string case; any non-empty non-decimal sysfs content (hex '0x0c', 'N/A', or a value with trailing units) makes int() raise ValueError. The os.path.exists(err_path) check guards existence, not content. The error_count attribute is a driver-specific GMSL deserializer (e.g. MAX96712) sysfs node, so its format is genuinely less predictable than a kernel-standard counter.
- Line 99: `w, h = int(parts[0]), int(parts[1])` has no `len(parts) >= 2` guard, so a 'Width/Height' line lacking '/' yields IndexError, and non-decimal tokens yield ValueError. (Caveat: this parses v4l2-ctl --get-fmt-video, whose output is stable decimal 'W/H', so the line-99 crash is the weaker, largely hypothetical half of the claim.)

Not refuted / not intentional: All GMSL video tests (tests/test_gmsl.py, tests/test_gmsl_format.py) exercise only the mock branch; the real path is never run. Crucially, the codebase establishes a clear, repeated defensive-parsing discipline for exactly this situation that video.py alone violates: memory.py:123-128 `_read_int` wraps the IDENTICAL `int(fh.read().strip() or 0)` pattern in `try/except (OSError, ValueError): return 0`; gpu.py:176 `_int` does try/except->0 and gpu.py:152 explicitly handles 'N/A' and base-16; ethernet.py:159-164 strips to ASCII decimal digits with a comment literally naming the int() ValueError risk. So this is an inconsistency/oversight, not deliberate design, and it does not fake a pass or hide a skip (honesty discipline does not apply).

Concrete impact: A ValueError from line 92 is caught at cli.py:457 and mislabeled as EXIT_USAGE (code 2) with only the raw Python message and no mention of the offending sysfs file — a hardware counter-format quirk reported as an operator usage error. An IndexError from line 99 is NOT in any CLI except clause (cli.py catches KeyError/FileNotFoundError/JSONDecodeError/NotImplementedError/ValueError/RuntimeError/sqlite3.Error), so it propagates as an uncaught traceback exiting 1 (EXIT_FAIL). check_deserializer's real branch (video.py:155-157) has no try/except, so any one link's raise aborts the entire multi-link scan.

Severity: medium (borderline low). It is a real-hw path that crashes/misparses and emits a misleading exit code, but it does not produce a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict or data corruption, and the most plausible trigger (hex/non-decimal error_count) is driver-dependent while the resolution-parse crash is largely hypothetical against v4l2-ctl's stable output. Suggested fix matches the codebase norm: route line 92 through a guarded helper like memory.py's _read_int (and consider int(s, 0) for base detection), and guard line 99 with `len(parts) >= 2` plus a try/except that names the offending file/line.  

**M27. check_channel_compliance never applies the GMSL3 filter split it advertises**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/channel.py` · channel.py:114-130 (check_channel_compliance) vs module docstring lines 4-7 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The module docstring states the framework 'applies the 100 MHz filter split (data used unfiltered below 50 MHz, filtered above)', but the only end-to-end entry point, check_channel_compliance, compares the full unpartitioned sweep against a single mask and never calls apply_gmsl3_filter, so the advertised below/above region handling is silently absent from every run.  
- **Evidence:** il = check_against_mask(vna.measure_sparam(il_param), il_mask)\n    rl = check_against_mask(vna.measure_sparam(rl_param), rl_mask)  — apply_gmsl3_filter is defined (lines 89-97) and exported but is called nowhere in src except the test; grep of src shows no caller applies the partition before check_against_mask.  
- **Fix:** Either (a) have check_channel_compliance partition each sweep via apply_gmsl3_filter and check the below/above regions against separate (filtered/unfiltered) masks per AN-2585, or (b) correct the docstring to state that filter application is the caller's responsibility and that this function checks the raw trace. Do not claim a behavior the entry point does not perform.  
- **Verifier:** Structural claim verified against the code. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gmsl/channel.py the module header (lines 4-7) says the framework "applies the 100 MHz filter split (data used unfiltered below 50 MHz, filtered above) ... compares ... to short/long-channel masks, with a 2-10 MHz Power-over-Coax return-loss carve-out." The only end-to-end entry point, check_channel_compliance (lines 114-130), takes a single il_mask and single rl_mask and runs check_against_mask over the ENTIRE unpartitioned sweep (lines 128-129). It never calls apply_gmsl3_filter, never selects short vs long masks, and never applies the PoC carve-out (POC_CARVEOUT_* constants are defined but unused). grep confirms apply_gmsl3_filter is called nowhere in src/ — only re-exported in __init__.py and exercised by test_gmsl_channel.py:71. So the three behaviors the header advertises are not wired into any run.

This is a genuine documentation-vs-behavior (honesty) mismatch, so I confirm it. However, the finding's "high" severity is overstated for two reasons. (1) No wrong PASS/FAIL or data corruption results: AN2585_*_MASK are all None and check_channel_compliance RAISES ValueError when a mask is missing (lines 124-127, pinned by test_compliance_refuses_without_masks), so no run can silently pass; when a mask is supplied, check_against_mask correctly checks every point. The impact is latent/contingent — it only matters if someone transcribes AN-2585 and trusts the header to auto-apply the filter split, which could then mis-apply a region mask. (2) The mismatch is mitigated by the function's OWN docstring (lines 118-123), which accurately states it merely "check[s] insertion + return loss against supplied masks" with no claim of filtering; an engineer reading the function they call is not misled, only a skimmer of the module header is. Per the project's rubric (high = wrong number/spec value or a crashing/misparsing real-hw path; medium = edge-case mishandling / misleading framing without verdict corruption), the truthful severity is medium. Suggested fix (a) or (b) is valid — preferably correct the module header to state the filter/short-long/PoC selection is the caller's responsibility, matching what the code actually does.  

**M28. The 2-10 MHz PoC return-loss carve-out is declared but never applied**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/channel.py` · channel.py:25-26 (POC_CARVEOUT_LOW_HZ/HIGH_HZ), docstring line 7; check_against_mask lines 100-111 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The docstring promises 'a 2-10 MHz Power-over-Coax return-loss carve-out', but POC_CARVEOUT_LOW_HZ / POC_CARVEOUT_HIGH_HZ are referenced nowhere in the comparison logic, so RL points in 2-10 MHz are still flagged as violations instead of being exempted — a clean PoC channel can be reported FAIL on real hardware.  
- **Evidence:** grep across src shows POC_CARVEOUT_* only appear at their definition (lines 25-26) and in a comment (line 134); check_against_mask flags every point where measured > limit with no frequency exclusion: 'bad = (... else measured > limit); if bad: violations.append((f, measured, limit))'.  
- **Fix:** Implement the carve-out (skip RL violation checks, or mark them informational, for POC_CARVEOUT_LOW_HZ <= f <= POC_CARVEOUT_HIGH_HZ) and unit-test it, or remove the carve-out claim from the docstring and constants until implemented. As written the run over-fails inside the carve-out band.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced from the code. The module docstring (channel.py line 7) asserts the framework "applies ... a 2-10 MHz Power-over-Coax return-loss carve-out" (present tense, a claimed live behavior), and POC_CARVEOUT_LOW_HZ/HIGH_HZ are defined as ordinary module constants (lines 25-26). A grep across src/ and tests/ confirms those two constants appear ONLY at their definitions — they are referenced in zero comparison logic. check_against_mask (lines 100-111) flags every point where measured > limit for RETURN_LOSS with no frequency exclusion, and no other module (gmsl/ package, tsn/pma.py caller) applies any carve-out. No test exercises one.

Refute attempts failed: (1) The carve-out is NOT something a supplied SParamMask can encode. SParamMask is a piecewise-linear limit line; limit_at always returns a finite dB limit and check_against_mask flags any exceedance, so there is no mechanism to "exempt" a band — the exclusion must live in framework code, and it is absent. So even a user who follows the docstring and supplies correct AN-2585 RL masks would get false violations on RL points in 2-10 MHz. (2) Honesty-discipline does NOT make this intentional: that convention covers setting unknown limits to None and refusing to pass (which channel.py does correctly for the mask values via the None placeholders and the ValueError raise). It does not sanction advertising an implemented carve-out the framework cannot honor. The line-134 comment lists the carve-out as part of the un-transcribed AN-2585 work, but the docstring + named constants present it as a shipped capability, which is the dishonest gap.

Severity downgraded from high to medium. The rubric reserves high/critical for wrong numbers, real-hw crashes/misparses, or wrong-PASS/data corruption. This defect can only produce an over-FAIL (false positive) in the 2-10 MHz band — the SAFE direction, operator-visible, never a silent wrong PASS. It is also latent: it only bites once the AN-2585 RL masks (currently None, run refuses to proceed) are transcribed and the PoC channel actually reflects in 2-10 MHz. Real but bounded: medium. Fix is to either implement the band exclusion in check_against_mask (and unit-test it) or strike the carve-out promise from the docstring/constants until implemented; the finding's evidence is accurate aside from a minor imprecision (line 134's comment is prose referencing "the 2-10 MHz PoC carve-out," not a literal use of the constant names).  

**M29. FORWARD vs REVERSE EOM records are indistinguishable in the output column set**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/lmt.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/lmt.py:63-89 (eom_to_lmt_records) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The `direction` parameter selects which physical channel is read but is never encoded into any emitted column, so two calls (FORWARD then REVERSE) produce rows that are identical on every identifier column (bdf, lane, receiver_number, margin_type) and differ only in the measured `step` value — they silently collide in any keyed/grouped downstream consumer.  
- **Evidence:** Signature takes `direction: LinkDirection = LinkDirection.FORWARD` and passes it only to `serdes.read_eom(link, direction)`. The records hard-code `receiver_number=1` and `lane=link` for both directions; `bdf=f"gmsl:{part}:{serial}"` has no direction component. Verified empirically: FORWARD vs REVERSE EYE_V records differ only in `step` ({'step': (21, 23)}), bdf/lane/margin_type identical. The module docstring states these rows flow into 'BigQuery / CSV / Looker', where a GROUP BY on (bdf, lane, margin_type) would merge forward video-channel and reverse control-channel measurements.  
- **Fix:** Encode direction into a distinguishing column — e.g. fold it into the receiver_number (FORWARD=1, REVERSE=2) or into the margin_type suffix (EYE_V_FWD / EYE_V_REV) or the bdf (gmsl:{part}:{serial}:{direction}). At minimum document that callers must not concatenate both directions into one dataset.  
- **Verifier:** Factually verified at src/computetest/gmsl/lmt.py:63-89. The `direction` param is passed ONLY to serdes.read_eom(link, direction) (line 73); every identifier column is direction-blind: bdf=f"gmsl:{part}:{serial}" (line 44, via _base_fields), lane=link and receiver_number=1 hard-coded for both directions (lines 79,86). I reproduced the collision empirically: with MockSerDes(links=1, PAM4_12G), the FORWARD vs REVERSE EYE_V records are byte-identical on (bdf, lane, receiver_number, margin_type) and even on `speed` (12000), differing ONLY in step (21 vs 23). So two physically distinct GMSL channels — the 12 Gbps PAM4 video forward channel and the 187.5 Mbps NRZ control reverse channel — produce rows that a GROUP BY (bdf, lane, margin_type) in the BigQuery/CSV/Looker pipeline the module docstring explicitly targets would silently merge.

Tried hard to refute: (1) No guard or test handles it — tests/test_gmsl_lmt.py never exercises REVERSE and never asserts direction distinguishability; grep across src+tests finds zero code that emits REVERSE-direction LMT records or concatenates both directions. (2) Not refuted by the code: the code does exactly what the claim says. (3) Intentional-design defense considered: the pci_lmt schema genuinely has no direction concept and the adapter reuses receiver_number=1 just like the PCIe from_margin_result default, so a "verbatim schema reuse" argument exists — but the function still accepts a direction kwarg that materially changes the measured value and then drops it from every output column, which is a real latent observability/correctness gap, not deliberate honesty-discipline or mock-fidelity behavior.

Severity stays medium (matching the finding): this is not a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict (the function emits records, not verdicts; the verdict path in eom_verdict/shmoo correctly carries direction inside EomReading) and not data corruption inside the toolkit (each individual call is self-consistent). The corruption only manifests in a downstream consumer that merges both directions into one keyed dataset — a specific usage no current caller performs. That makes it edge-case mishandling = medium, and the finding's own "at minimum document it" fallback fix correctly frames it as a modeling/observability gap rather than an active field-misleading bug.  

**M30. EYE_H step monotonicity is never asserted by the test suite**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/lmt.py` · tests/test_gmsl_lmt.py:31-36 (test_step_reflects_eye_opening) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The only step-correctness test exercises EYE_V (it varies injected_eye_mv) but never varies injected_eye_ui or asserts on the EYE_H step, so a regression that broke the horizontal mapping (e.g. swapped num_timing_steps/max_timing_offset, or fed worst_vertical_mv into EYE_H) would not be caught.  
- **Evidence:** test_step_reflects_eye_opening builds `strong = injected_eye_mv=180.0` vs `weak = injected_eye_mv=20.0` and asserts only `next(r.step ... if r.margin_type == MARGIN_EYE_V)`. No test parameterizes injected_eye_ui or asserts EYE_H step ordering, and the chosen 180 mV case (worst ~163 mV) sits just below the EYE_V clamp by luck; a default bump would silently make even the EYE_V assertion tautological at the clamp.  
- **Fix:** Add an EYE_H counterpart that varies injected_eye_ui (e.g. 0.45 vs 0.10) and asserts the EYE_H step increases, plus a case at/above 0.50 UI to document/lock the saturation behavior.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed as a real test-gap. In tests/test_gmsl_lmt.py, test_step_reflects_eye_opening (lines 31-36) varies only injected_eye_mv (180 vs 20) and asserts only on the MARGIN_EYE_V step; grep confirms NO test in that file varies injected_eye_ui or asserts on the MARGIN_EYE_H step value/ordering (the only other EYE_H reference, line 42, merely checks the literal string "EYE_H" appears in JSON). I reproduced the defect by mutation: changing src/computetest/gmsl/lmt.py:82 to feed eom.worst_vertical_mv into _step_from_timing_ui instead of eom.horizontal_ui leaves ALL GMSL/LMT/EOM/serdes/shmoo tests passing — the broken horizontal mapping (which rides into the pci_lmt JSON/CSV/BigQuery pipeline) is invisible to the suite. The EYE_H mapping is responsive to injected_eye_ui (0.45->step 29, 0.10->step 7, saturating at 32 for >=0.50 UI), so the suggested EYE_H ordering + saturation test is feasible and would close the gap.

Two minor corrections that bound (not negate) the finding: (1) the shared helper _step_from_timing_ui IS indirectly unit-covered via the PCIe path in tests/test_lmt_adapter.py (test_step_maps_timing_ui_through_max_offset locks the num_timing_steps/max_timing_offset relationship at 0.5 UI -> step 32; test_step_is_clamped_to_num_timing_steps locks the clamp). So a swap inside the shared function would be caught there; only a GMSL-call-site swap or the worst_vertical_mv mixup escapes — the finding slightly overstates the "swapped num/max" example. (2) The evidence's "180 mV sits just below the EYE_V clamp by luck" is loose: EYE_V step at 180 mV is 52 against a clamp of 64 (comfortably below, not "just below"); the EYE_V assertion is in fact sound. Neither correction undermines the core blind spot.

Severity: this is a weak test that would pass even if the real-hardware horizontal-mapping code were wrong — the rubric defines that as medium, so I upgrade from the finding's self-rated low to medium. It is not itself a shipping wrong-verdict (so not high/critical).  

**M31. mean_link_delay_ns applies neighborRateRatio to the responder turnaround instead of the requestor round-trip interval**  
`src/computetest/tsn/gptp_proto.py` · gptp_proto.py:35-37 (PdelayExchange.mean_link_delay_ns) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed
