- **Claim:** The formula scales (t3-t2) by rate_ratio, but IEEE 802.1AS-2020 (§11.2.19.3 / 11.4.2.4) computes meanLinkDelay = (neighborRateRatio*(t4-t1) - (t3-t2))/2 — the rate ratio multiplies the requestor interval (t4-t1), not the responder interval (t3-t2). The code returns a different value whenever rate_ratio != 1.0.  
- **Evidence:** return ((self.t4 - self.t1) - self.rate_ratio * (self.t3 - self.t2)) / 2.0   # docstring even says "((t4 - t1) - rateRatio*(t3 - t2)) / 2" — the rateRatio is on the wrong term. Per spec it should be (rateRatio*(t4-t1) - (t3-t2))/2. At rr=1.0 both agree, so every test passes; at +200 ppm with a typical ~1 ms request interval (t4-t1) the delta is ~100 ns (>80 ns per-hop TE budget). Demonstrated: code=499.99 vs spec=500.11 for the test vector; ~100 ns error over a 1 ms interval.  
- **Fix:** Return (self.rate_ratio * (self.t4 - self.t1) - (self.t3 - self.t2)) / 2.0 and fix the docstring to match. neighborRateRatio (responder/local) scales the locally-measured (t4-t1) into the responder timebase; (t3-t2) is already in the responder clock.  
- **Verifier:** CONFIRMED that the code applies neighborRateRatio to the wrong term. /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/tsn/gptp_proto.py:35-37 computes ((t4-t1) - rate_ratio*(t3-t2))/2. By first-principles derivation (neighborRateRatio r = f_responder/f_local per 802.1AS-2020 §10.2.5.7), meanLinkDelay in local clock units = ((t4-t1) - (t3-t2)/r)/2, and the canonical published/linuxptp form is (r*(t4-t1) - (t3-t2))/2. Both correct forms multiply/divide differently than the code, so the code returns a wrong value whenever rate_ratio != 1.0. The tests (tests/test_tsn_gptp_proto.py) only ever use the default rate_ratio=1.0, where all three formulas agree, so no test can catch this. This is a real spec-conformance defect on the real-hw parsing path (linuxptp pmc / Wireshark dump feeds a nonzero neighborRateRatio).

DOWNGRADED to medium (not high) because the finding's impact evidence is materially wrong. I reproduced the math: the code error is (1 - r)*(t3-t2) order, i.e. proportional to the RESPONDER TURNAROUND (t3-t2), NOT the request round-trip (t4-t1). At a typical Pdelay turnaround of ~100ns-1us the error is only 0.02-0.2 ns at +200ppm -- far below the ~80ns per-hop TE budget. The finding's headline "~100 ns error over a 1 ms interval / code=499.99 vs spec=500.11" conflates the (t4-t1) interval with the turnaround: a 1 ms (t4-t1) only arises from a ~1 ms turnaround, at which point the actual code error is ~200 ns (not 100 ns), and such a slow responder is the corner the code's own DEFAULT_MAX_TURNAROUND_NS=10e6 is meant to flag. The finding's suggested fix (r*(t4-t1)-(t3-t2))/2 is the canonical spec form and is acceptable (its residual +0.1ns vs the exact ((t4-t1)-(t3-t2)/r)/2 is negligible). Net: real bug, fix it, but real-world severity is medium, not high.  

**M32. All Pdelay/aggregator tests use rate_ratio=1.0, so the mean-link-delay formula bug is untestable as written**  
`src/computetest/tsn/gptp_proto.py` · tests/test_tsn_gptp_proto.py:22-26, 63 (TestPdelay, TestCheckAggregator._good) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** No test ever constructs a PdelayExchange with rate_ratio != 1.0, so the formula in Finding 1 is never exercised at a non-unity ratio; the suite would still pass even with the rate_ratio term on the wrong interval.  
- **Evidence:** PdelayExchange(t1=0, t2=500, t3=600, t4=1100) with the default rate_ratio=1.0 in both the unit test and the aggregator fixture. ((t4-t1) - 1*(t3-t2))/2 == (1*(t4-t1) - (t3-t2))/2, so the two formulas are indistinguishable in every test.  
- **Fix:** Add a Pdelay test with rate_ratio=1.0+200e-6 over a realistic requestor interval (e.g. t4-t1 ~ 1e6 ns) and assert the meanLinkDelay matches the spec formula (rateRatio*(t4-t1)-(t3-t2))/2, which will fail against the current code.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproduced. (1) Factual claim verified by grep: the only two PdelayExchange constructions in the suite (tests/test_tsn_gptp_proto.py:24 and :63) both use default rate_ratio=1.0; the lone rate_ratio test (line 48) exercises rate_ratio_valid() directly, not mean_link_delay_ns. No other test file or property-based test touches gPTP. (2) Math verified: at rate_ratio=1.0 the current formula ((t4-t1)-rr*(t3-t2))/2 and the spec formula (rr*(t4-t1)-(t3-t2))/2 both give 500.0; they diverge at non-unity ratios (e.g. 499.99 vs 500.11 at +200 ppm). (3) Decisive proof via mutation: I swapped gptp_proto.py:37 to the spec-correct formula and all 11 tests still passed, demonstrating the suite cannot detect which formula is implemented. I independently confirmed (web search of 802.1AS) that the underlying Finding-1 defect is real: rateRatio must multiply (t4-t1), but the code multiplies (t3-t2), so a genuine latent spec/units bug ships untested on a real-hw-fed path. Severity stays medium: this is a weak test that would pass even if the code were wrong (the project's medium definition) and it masks a concrete wrong-number bug exploitable for any syntonized link within the +/-200 ppm tolerance the code itself allows. The suggested fix (Pdelay test at rate_ratio=1.0+200e-6 over t4-t1~1e6 ns asserting the spec formula) would fail against current code.  

**M33. Tests never exercise the contiguous-open-gate or signed-jitter cases, so both bugs pass the suite**  
`src/computetest/tsn/qbv.py` · tests/test_tsn_qbv.py:55-66 (test_guard_band_violation, test_edge_jitter_budget) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The guard-band and jitter tests only cover the single-entry-window and positive-jitter happy paths, so the two defects above go undetected and the suite would still pass even though the verdicts are wrong on real schedules.  
- **Evidence:** test_guard_band_violation uses _sched() where TC6 is open in exactly one entry, so the single-entry `w_end` happens to equal the true gate-close time; no test has a TC open across adjacent entries. test_edge_jitter_budget only passes positive samples ([10,20] and [10,90]); no negative/early sample is tested. All 10 tests pass against the current (buggy) code.  
- **Fix:** Add a test with a TC open across two consecutive GCL entries asserting no guard violation for a frame finishing within the contiguous open interval, and a test with a large-negative jitter sample asserting edge_jitter_ok is False.  
- **Verifier:** Verified against /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/tests/test_tsn_qbv.py and src/computetest/tsn/qbv.py. The test-quality claim is factually accurate: (1) test_guard_band_violation (lines 55-58) uses _sched(), where TC6 is open in exactly one GCL entry (entry 0 = 1<<6; entry 1 = 0b0011_1111 has no bit 6), so the single window's w_end equals the true gate-close time — no test ever opens a TC across adjacent entries; (2) test_edge_jitter_budget (lines 60-66) only passes positive samples [10,20] and [10,90] — no negative sample. I ran the suite: all 10 tests pass against current code.

I independently reproduced the masked guard-band defect: with a GCL where TC6 is open across two consecutive entries, a frame starting at 99_500ns needing 1000ns finishes at 100_500ns — still inside the contiguous TC6-open interval (closes at 200_000) — yet qbv.py line 108 uses only window_at(start)'s w_end (100_000), so the frame is wrongly flagged guard_band_respected=False / ok=False. That is a false FAIL verdict on a legal multi-entry schedule (common in real 802.1Qbv GCLs), and the current tests cannot catch it. The suggested contiguous-open test would expose it.

I also reproduced the jitter case: edge_jitter_ns=[10.0,-90.0] yields max_jitter_ns=10.0 and edge_jitter_ok=True because line 111 uses max() not max(abs()). This second masked bug is weaker — whether negative jitter is valid depends on an unspecified sign convention (no producer of edge_jitter_ns exists in src/sim; docstring calls it 'spread between scheduled and actual gate transitions', which is naturally signed). But the test-gap claim ('no negative sample is tested') is still literally true regardless.

This is a test-quality finding: a weak test that would pass even if the code were wrong, masking a reproducible wrong-verdict defect. Per project severity guidance that is medium (not critical — it is the test gap, not shipped-code verdict logic per se — but it conceals a real false-FAIL on real hardware, so not a nit). Confirmed at medium.  

**M34. 'no_duplicates_delivered' check is structurally always True (dead verdict)**  
`src/computetest/tsn/frer.py` · src/computetest/tsn/frer.py:74 (check_frer checks dict) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `delivered` is built as `[seq for seq in arrivals if rec.receive(seq)]`, and `receive()` returns True at most once per numeric seq (the `_seen` set dedups, and across a wrap two logical frames share an identical int so are still deduped). Therefore `len(delivered) == len(set(delivered))` can never be False, so the advertised 'no duplicates delivered' check cannot ever catch a duplicate-elimination failure on real hardware.  
- **Evidence:** Line 70 `delivered = [seq for seq in arrivals if rec.receive(seq)]`; line 74 `"no_duplicates_delivered": len(delivered) == len(set(delivered))`. A 2000-iteration fuzz over random history lengths and arrival streams produced zero cases where this was False. `exactly_once` (line 75) already subsumes it. The verdict thus claims to validate a property it never exercises.  
- **Fix:** Either remove the redundant check, or make it meaningful by counting raw duplicate *deliveries* the recovery function let through (e.g. compare arrival multiplicity vs accepted) — which would only become non-trivial once wrap is modeled and a real double-accept is possible.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and independently reproduced. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/tsn/frer.py:70-74, `delivered = [seq for seq in arrivals if rec.receive(seq)]` and `"no_duplicates_delivered": len(delivered) == len(set(delivered))`. I proved `receive()` (lines 28-39) accepts each distinct integer seq at most once: a value is either still in `_seen` (rejected as duplicate, line 31-32) or, once pruned, is below the stale threshold and rejected (lines 33-34). Crucially the prune threshold `lo = _max - history_len` (line 37) equals the stale threshold (line 33), and `_max` only ever grows (line 36, `max(self._max, seq)`), so the stale boundary is monotonically non-decreasing — any value that was pruned (v <= lo) stays stale forever (v <= lo' for all later lo' >= lo). Hence `delivered` can never contain a repeated integer, so `len(delivered) == len(set(delivered))` is structurally always True. A 200,000-iteration fuzz over varied history_len, sent sets, and arrival streams (with duplicates, reorder, loss, out-of-range values) produced 0 cases where the check was False and 0 cases where it carried information beyond `exactly_once` (line 75, `sorted(delivered) == distinct_sent`, which subsumes it since distinct_sent is dup-free). I also confirmed `delivered` is always produced by the in-process SequenceRecovery in every call site, never from external hardware data, so there is no path where this check is non-tautological.\n\nNot intentional per honesty-discipline conventions (it is not a None-mask, scheduled-debt budget, or refuse-to-pass guard) and no existing test catches it — the tests only assert h.ok or no_loss/exactly_once. It is a genuinely dead verdict.\n\nSeverity is medium, not higher: it does NOT produce a wrong overall PASS/FAIL. The `ok` property (line 51) still requires no_loss and exactly_once, both of which can and do fail (e.g. test_total_loss_fails), so a real duplicate-delivery on hardware would still flip exactly_once to False and fail the suite. The defect is a redundant/tautological named check that advertises validating 'no duplicates delivered' independently when it cannot ever fail — matching the project's own medium definition ('a weak test that would pass even if the code were wrong'). The suggested fix (remove it, or make it compare arrival multiplicity vs accepted deliveries) is reasonable, though the meaningful version only becomes non-trivial once seq-number wrap is modeled.  

**M35. No test exercises sequence-number wrap-around**  
`src/computetest/tsn/frer.py` · tests/test_tsn_frer.py (entire file) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The test suite only uses tiny monotonic sequence values (0-14, 65533 never appears), so the wrap-around defect above is completely uncovered; the suite passes while the recovery function is wrong for the dominant real-world steady-state condition (a link runs for hours and the 16-bit counter wraps).  
- **Evidence:** All arrivals/sent_seqs in tests/test_tsn_frer.py are small monotonic ints (e.g. lines 18, 31, 37, 42, 47); no value near 65535 and no wrap transition is tested. `python -m pytest tests/test_tsn_frer.py -q` -> 8 passed, none covering wrap.  
- **Fix:** Add a test that drives a wrapping stream (e.g. sent_seqs/arrivals spanning 65534,65535,0,1 with replication) and asserts exactly-once OK once wrap is correctly modeled. This test will currently fail and pin the spec behavior.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced. The cited finding is a test-quality gap and is accurate.

FACTS VERIFIED against /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/tests/test_tsn_frer.py:
- The whole file uses only small monotonic seq values; the largest value anywhere is 14 (lines 18-20). No value >= 65530 appears; grep for 6553x/0xffff/wrap/modulo/2**16 returns NONE in this file. The only "wrap" test in the repo is tests/test_tsn_qbv.py (Qbv gate cycle, unrelated to FRER seq numbers).
- `pytest tests/test_tsn_frer.py` => 8 passed (matches the finding's "8 passed, none covering wrap").

UNDERLYING DEFECT THE GAP HIDES IS REAL (src/computetest/tsn/frer.py): SequenceRecovery.receive tracks `self._max` with plain max() and eliminates stale frames via `seq <= self._max - self.history_len`, with NO modulo-2^16 sequence-space arithmetic. The 802.1CB R-TAG sequence number is a 16-bit field that wraps every 65536 frames, so wrap is the steady state, not an edge case. I drove a replicated wrap stream and got a wrong verdict:
  check_frer(sent_seqs=[65534,65535,0,1], arrivals=[65534,65534,65535,65535,0,0,1,1]) -> checks={'no_loss': False, 'exactly_once': False}, ok=False, delivered=[65534,65535] (0 and 1 wrongly eliminated as stale because 0 <= 65535-32). This is a valid exactly-once stream that the recovery function fails.
- The suggested-fix test (assert h.ok on the wrapping replicated stream) currently evaluates ok==False, i.e. it would fail and pin the bug — confirming the fix is valid.

NOT refuted by conventions: this is not a real-hw pragma path (it is fully tested mock-side logic), not honesty-discipline (no None/raise), and the green suite is exactly the problem — a weak suite that passes even though the recovery logic is wrong for the dominant real-world condition.

SEVERITY: medium is correct and not a downgradeable nit. Per the project rubric, a "weak test that would pass even if code were wrong" is medium, and this gap masks a genuine wrong-verdict bug. It is a test-quality finding (the gap), distinct from the separate code-correctness finding for the wrap bug itself (which as a code defect would rate higher).  

**M36. Failover TE transient is max|TE| (one-sided) but compared against a budget documented as peak-to-peak**  
`src/computetest/tsn/gptp_hotstandby.py` · gptp_hotstandby.py:57,60 (check_hot_standby_failover); budget comment lines 17-18 · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** failover_te_transient_ns is computed as max(abs(s)) (peak amplitude from zero), but DEFAULT_HOLDOVER_NS is documented as '~1 us p-p' (peak-to-peak); a clock whose recovered TE swings symmetrically can exceed the stated p-p budget yet report half the value and PASS.  
- **Evidence:** Line 57: `max_te = max((abs(s) for s in te_samples_ns), default=0.0)`; line 60: `"te_within_holdover": max_te <= holdover_budget_ns`; line 17-18 comment: 'the ~1 us p-p automotive fail-operational design target'. The sibling gptp.py distinguishes these explicitly: `max_te_ns = max(abs(s))` vs `pp_te_ns = max(samples_ns) - min(samples_ns)`. Verified: te_samples_ns=[-600.0, 600.0] is 1200 ns p-p (exceeds the 1000 ns 'p-p' target) but reports failover_te_transient_ns=600.0 and h.ok is True.  
- **Fix:** Either compute peak-to-peak to match the documented budget (`pp = max(te_samples_ns) - min(te_samples_ns)`) and compare that against holdover_budget_ns, or change the budget comment/constant to clearly mean one-sided max|TE|. Pick one and make the metric and the budget units agree; ideally mirror gptp.py and report both max|TE| and p-p.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly. In gptp_hotstandby.py:57 the metric is one-sided `max_te = max(abs(s) for s in te_samples_ns)`, compared at line 60 against `holdover_budget_ns`, while the constant at lines 17-18 is documented as "the ~1 us p-p" budget (DEFAULT_HOLDOVER_NS=1000.0). I ran te_samples_ns=[-600.0, 600.0]: that is 1200 ns peak-to-peak (exceeds the 1000 ns "p-p" comment) yet reports failover_te_transient_ns=600.0 with h.ok=True. The sibling gptp.py deliberately distinguishes the two (max_te_ns=max(abs(s)) at line 62 vs pp_te_ns=max(samples)-min(samples) at line 64), so the units distinction is real and known to the codebase. No guard/test covers it: the only "passing" test (test_clean_failover_passes) uses [100,-200,300] and asserts the result is 300.0, which actually cements the one-sided semantics — no symmetric-swing case exists. This is not a mock/pragma/honesty-discipline construct, so the conventions don't exempt it. The metric and its documented budget unit genuinely disagree, and for a symmetric TE swing the check is ~2x more lenient than a literal reading of the "p-p" target, which can mislead a field engineer into believing the documented p-p design target is enforced.

Downgraded from high to medium: (1) the chosen metric, one-sided max|TE| (MTIE-style), is itself a defensible and conventional way to express recovered-clock time error — per-hop budgets in the project's own roadmap (4.2.3) are stated as one-sided "±80 ns", so the code is plausibly enforcing a one-sided ±1 us bound and only the comment is mislabeled; (2) the roadmap (line 228) explicitly states 1000 ns is a placeholder "floor" default that programs must replace via their CDS, so this is not a hard spec number being violated. The concrete, demonstrable defect is the units inconsistency between the documented "p-p" budget and the one-sided metric, not a guaranteed wrong PASS/FAIL against a known spec value. Fix: make the comment/constant and the metric agree — either compute pp=max(te_samples_ns)-min(te_samples_ns) and compare that, or relabel the constant as one-sided max|TE| (±); ideally mirror gptp.py and report both.  

**M37. Empty TE-sample window silently produces a 0 ns transient and PASSES (no missing-data guard)**  
`src/computetest/tsn/gptp_hotstandby.py` · gptp_hotstandby.py:57,60,63 (check_hot_standby_failover) · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** If te_samples_ns is empty (e.g. the time-interval analyzer captured nothing during the failover window), max((...), default=0.0) yields 0.0, te_within_holdover passes, and the overall verdict is OK — masking a data-collection failure as a clean failover.  
- **Evidence:** Line 57: `max((abs(s) for s in te_samples_ns), default=0.0)`. Verified: te_samples_ns=[] -> failover_te_transient_ns=0.0, h.ok=True, summary 'TE_transient=0.0ns ... -> OK'. The sibling check_gptp_time_error (gptp.py:59-60) instead guards: `if not samples_ns: raise ValueError("need at least one TE sample")`.  
- **Fix:** Mirror gptp.py: raise ValueError on empty te_samples_ns, or add a check key like 'te_samples_present': bool(te_samples_ns) so a no-data window cannot report OK.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced directly: check_hot_standby_failover(standby_announces=[gm], te_samples_ns=[], as_capable_held=True) returns failover_te_transient_ns=0.0, checks={standby_gm_available:True, te_within_holdover:True, as_capable_held:True}, ok=True, summary "gPTP failover -> GM-standby TE_transient=0.0ns (holdover 1000) -> OK". An empty TE-sample window means the time-interval analyzer captured nothing during the safety-critical fail-operational failover (a data-collection failure), yet max((abs(s) for s in te_samples_ns), default=0.0) at line 57 yields 0.0, te_within_holdover passes (0.0 <= 1000.0), and the overall verdict is reported as a clean failover. The sibling check_gptp_time_error (gptp.py:59-60) guards exactly this with `if not samples_ns: raise ValueError("need at least one TE sample")`, so the inconsistency is an oversight, not intentional design. I confirmed no upstream caller or guard protects te_samples_ns (it is public API exported in tsn/__init__.py) and no test (tests/test_tsn_hotstandby.py) covers the empty-window case. This directly violates the project's honesty discipline (do not fake a PASS / hide a missing-data condition). Severity stays medium rather than higher because it requires a degenerate empty-list input that only arises when data collection upstream has already failed — it is not triggered during normal operation — and the other two checks (standby_gm_available, as_capable_held) are independent inputs. The finding's factual evidence, suggested fix (mirror gptp.py's raise, or add a te_samples_present check), and severity are all accurate.  

**M38. Tests cannot distinguish the SDD11 (return loss) trace from the SCD21 (mode conversion) trace, so a param/mask swap would pass**  
`src/computetest/tsn/pma.py` · tests/test_tsn_pma.py:11-23 (_vna_with) and check_pma_electrical (src/computetest/tsn/pma.py:58-59) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Because the MockSCPI transport keys canned responses only on the query string ('CALC:DATA? FDATA') and not on the preceding 'CALC:PAR:DEF <param>' selection, vna.measure_sparam('SDD11') and vna.measure_sparam('SCD21') return the IDENTICAL trace in every test; therefore a bug that read SDD11 for both checks, or swapped mdi_rl_mask/mode_conv_mask, would not be caught.  
- **Evidence:** In pma.py: `rl = check_against_mask(vna.measure_sparam("SDD11"), mdi_rl_mask)` and `mc = check_against_mask(vna.measure_sparam("SCD21"), mode_conv_mask)`. In the test helper: `v.transport.set_measurement("CALC:DATA? FDATA", trace)` sets ONE trace; verified empirically that `measure_sparam('SDD11')` and `measure_sparam('SCD21')` both return `[-30.0, -31.0]`. No test ever has mode-conversion fail while RL passes (or vice versa).  
- **Fix:** Add a test that makes the two parameters differ — e.g. extend MockSCPI.set_measurement to key on the active CALC:PAR:DEF selection, or build two Vna mocks / monkeypatch measure_sparam to return distinct traces per param — and assert one case where mode_conversion_ok is False while mdi_return_loss_ok is True (and the reverse). This pins the SDD11/SCD21-to-mask wiring.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed by reproduction. _MockSCPI._respond (instruments.py:177-193) keys canned responses only on the query string; measure_sparam (instruments.py:870-882) sends CALC:PAR:DEF as a write that is never consulted on read, then reads the single CALC:DATA? FDATA value. With the test helper's single set_measurement("CALC:DATA? FDATA", trace), I empirically verified measure_sparam('SDD11') and measure_sparam('SCD21') both return [-30.0,-31.0]. I then applied the finding's exact mutation to pma.py:59 (mc reads "SDD11" instead of "SCD21") and ran tests/test_tsn_pma.py: all 6 tests still PASS, so the wrong-parameter bug survives. The mask-swap mutation also survives: on the conformant trace both masks (limits -10/-20, both RETURN_LOSS) pass; on the -5.0 violation trace both fail identically. No test has mode_conversion_ok False while mdi_return_loss_ok True (or the reverse), so the SDD11/SCD21-to-mask wiring is unpinned. This is not intentional design or covered by the mock-fidelity/honesty-discipline conventions — the Mock is faithful, but the TEST never selects distinct traces. Severity medium is correct per the rubric ("a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong"); the production wiring at pma.py:58-59 is currently correct, so this is a latent test-quality gap rather than a present wrong verdict. The suggested fix (distinct traces per param plus asymmetric pass/fail assertions) would close it.  

**M39. Honesty-critical "skipped" path is documented but has zero test coverage**  
`src/computetest/tsn/cable.py` · tests/test_tsn_cable.py (whole file) vs cable.py:43-49 check_cable_tdr / CableHealth.ok · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The load-bearing guarantee that a TDR "skipped" result does NOT false-fail a good link is never exercised by any test, because MockPhy.run_tdr() can only return status "ok" or "fault" — never "skipped" — so the only path that produces "skipped" (a real PHY/driver without TDR support) is unverified.  
- **Evidence:** cable.py docstring: 'A ``skipped`` result (PHY/driver without TDR support) does not false-fail a good link.' Code: checks = {"no_open_or_short": tdr.status != "fault"}; CableHealth.ok = bool(self.checks) and all(self.checks.values()). For status=="skipped", "skipped" != "fault" is True so ok is True — correct, but untested. MockPhy.run_tdr() (phy.py:179-183) returns only TdrResult(status="ok") or TdrResult(status="fault"); tests only call MockPhy() and MockPhy(injected_tdr_fault=True). grep for 'skipped' in test_tsn_cable.py / test_tsn_phy.py returns nothing.  
- **Fix:** Add a test that feeds a skipped TdrResult through check_cable_tdr (e.g. a tiny EthPhy stub or a MockPhy(injected_tdr_status="skipped") option) and asserts h.status == "skipped", h.ok is True, and summary ends in "-> OK". This pins the honesty guarantee that the docstring promises.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced. cable.py:43-49 check_cable_tdr sets checks={"no_open_or_short": tdr.status != "fault"} and CableHealth.ok = bool(self.checks) and all(self.checks.values()); for status=="skipped", "skipped" != "fault" is True so ok is True — the docstring (lines 44-46) explicitly promises a "skipped" result does not false-fail a good link. That honesty guarantee is load-bearing but untested: MockPhy.run_tdr() (phy.py:179-183) can only return status="ok" or status="fault" (the sole injection knob is injected_tdr_fault: bool, phy.py:156), so no test can drive a "skipped" TdrResult through check_cable_tdr. grep across all tsn source and tests confirms test_tsn_cable.py and test_tsn_phy.py only exercise "ok"/"fault"; the other "skipped" hits are unrelated subsystems (diagnostics/bert/results). No existing guard or test covers this, and it is not intentional design — it is a real coverage gap. Not refuted/already-handled/intentional. Severity stays medium: production code is currently correct (no wrong verdict ships today), but this is a weak/missing test that would still pass if the honesty-critical "skipped" branch were broken in a plausible refactor (e.g. changing the check to tdr.status == "ok" would silently false-fail TDR-less PHYs — a real-hardware-only scenario the mock cannot reach). That matches the project's own definition of medium. Suggested fix is sound: add a skipped-status injection path (or a tiny EthPhy stub) and assert h.status=="skipped", h.ok is True, summary ends "-> OK".  

**M40. Disabled or zero-duration patrol scrub passes the 24h budget check (false green)**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/ecs.py` · src/computetest/ddr5/ecs.py:55 (scrub_time_within_24h) · _edge-case_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The check `scrub_time_s <= max_scrub_time_s` treats scrub_time_s == 0 as 'within budget', and check_ecs never verifies that the background scrubber is actually enabled, so a device with patrol scrub disabled and/or a 0/unreadable cycle_duration reports ok=True even though no full-array scrub is occurring.  
- **Evidence:** Runtime: `ras.set_scrub(enable_background=False, cycle_duration_s=0); check_ecs(ras).ok` returns True with checks={'ecs_settings_stick': True, 'scrub_time_within_24h': True}. The docstring asserts 'the full-array scrub completes within the 24-hour budget', but a 0s 'completion' means it never ran. read_scrub().enable_background is available but never consulted.  
- **Fix:** Add a lower bound and an enabled gate, e.g. `"scrub_active": scrub.enable_background and scrub.cycle_duration_s > 0` and change the budget check to `0 < scrub_time_s <= max_scrub_time_s`. On real hardware a missing/unparsed cycle_duration commonly reads as 0, so guarding the zero case prevents a false pass.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly: `ras.set_scrub(enable_background=False, cycle_duration_s=0); check_ecs(ras).ok` returns True with checks={'ecs_settings_stick': True, 'scrub_time_within_24h': True}. Root causes are real and verifiable in src/computetest/ddr5/ecs.py:52-55: (1) the budget check `scrub_time_s <= max_scrub_time_s` admits scrub_time_s==0 (0 <= 86400), so a never-run scrub looks like an in-budget completed scrub; (2) `read_scrub().enable_background` is never consulted by any verdict logic — grep confirms enable_background is only stored in ras.py, never read. The check_ecs docstring (ecs.py:46-48) and module docstring claim it asserts the full-array scrub "completes within the 24-hour budget," but a disabled/zero-duration scrubber that never ran still yields ok=True, which mislabels the verdict — exactly the "fakes a PASS / mislabels a verdict" category the conventions say to flag. Not refuted by any guard or test: the 3 tests in tests/test_ddr5_ecs.py only cover enabled/non-zero durations (3600s, 100000s) and none exercises the disabled/zero case. Not intentional design (not mock-fidelity, not a pragma real-hw path, not honesty-discipline — nothing raises on the unknown/disabled condition). Severity held at medium rather than critical/high because the false-PASS only fires in a specific edge configuration (scrubber disabled or duration reading 0/unreadable) and no real DDR5 backend is implemented yet that would produce the 0-read — current callers use the Mock whose default is a sensible enabled/3600s. Still a genuine false-green in verdict logic that would carry into a real backend. Suggested fix is sound: gate on `scrub.enable_background and 0 < scrub_time_s <= max_scrub_time_s`.  

**M41. Mock decouples hPPR repair success from resource exhaustion, so the no-resource path is never exercised as a true repair failure**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/ppr.py` · ras.py:134-141 perform_repair; tests/test_ddr5_ppr.py:19-23 test_hppr_without_resources_fails · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** perform_repair ignores _hppr_resources entirely, so with injected_hppr_resources=0 an hPPR still records repaired=True and survives_power_cycle=True; the FAIL verdict comes ONLY from the advertised-resource side-channel. The convention requires the mock to faithfully model real dynamics, but on real hardware an hPPR against an exhausted pool fails to program (is_repaired would be False). The test asserts only `not h.ok` and the advertisement bit, never that the repair itself would have failed.  
- **Evidence:** perform_repair: self._repaired.add(req.hpa); if req.persist_mode == 1: self._persisted.add(req.hpa) — no resource check/decrement. test_hppr_without_resources_fails asserts `not h.ok and h.checks["hppr_resource_advertised"] is False` but the same h.checks still contains repaired=True, survives_power_cycle=True.  
- **Fix:** Make perform_repair resource-aware: when persist_mode==1 and resources for the bank are 0, do NOT add to _repaired/_persisted (and ideally decrement on success). Then assert the failure manifests as repaired=False too, matching real-hw behavior.  
- **Verifier:** All factual claims independently reproduced. ras.py:134-141 perform_repair only validates repair_type then unconditionally does self._repaired.add(req.hpa) and self._persisted.add(req.hpa) for persist_mode==1; it never reads/decrements self._hppr_resources. Runtime check with injected_hppr_resources=0 yields checks={'repaired': True, 'survives_power_cycle': True, 'hppr_resource_advertised': False}, ok=False — so the FAIL is driven solely by the advertised-resource side-channel (check_ppr uses res_before = hppr_resources_available()), while repaired/survives_power_cycle stay True. test_hppr_without_resources_fails (tests/test_ddr5_ppr.py:19-23) asserts only `not h.ok` and the advertisement bit, never that the repair itself failed. A grep confirms _hppr_resources is read-only everywhere (advertised in soak.py, never consumed), so no guard or other test couples exhaustion to repair success. This is a real mock-fidelity gap: the project convention requires the Mock to faithfully model real dynamics, and on real hardware an hPPR against an exhausted MR54-57 pool would not program (is_repaired would be False), yet the mock decouples them — so the test would still pass even if the resource-exhausted repair-programming logic were wrong (a weak test). Severity stays medium, not higher: the zero-resource verdict is still correctly FAIL (just via one redundant signal), and the real backend — not this mock — drives actual field verdicts, so no wrong PASS/FAIL on real hardware results. Not refuted/intentional: the resource advertisement check is deliberate and documented, but nothing makes the silent repair-success an intended design; it is an unmodeled coupling plus an under-asserting test.  

**M42. ind_left_right_timing / ind_up_down_voltage advertised True but only one combined margin per DQ is emitted**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/lmt.py` · src/computetest/ddr5/lmt.py:38-39 (_base) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The _base record sets ind_left_right_timing=True and ind_up_down_voltage=True, which in the pci_lmt / PCIe Lane Margining schema asserts the device reports independent left-vs-right timing and up-vs-down voltage margins (i.e. two directional records each). But training_to_lmt_records emits exactly ONE TIMING and ONE VOLTAGE record per DQ from a single timing_ui / voltage_mv, so a downstream consumer that honors these flags expects directional split records it will never receive.  
- **Evidence:** "ind_left_right_timing": True, "ind_up_down_voltage": True,  ... while training_to_lmt_records appends only one margin_type="TIMING" and one margin_type="VOLTAGE" record per DqMargin. Contrast the reused PCIe path from_margin_result which sets both flags False (lmt_adapter.py:152-153) precisely because it emits a single combined value.  
- **Fix:** Set ind_left_right_timing=False and ind_up_down_voltage=False in _base (matching from_margin_result), since the DDR5 DqMargin carries a single combined eye-to-failure per axis, not independent directional sweeps. Only set True if/when DqMargin is extended to carry separate left/right and up/down values and two records per axis are emitted.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed in /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/ddr5/lmt.py. `_base` (line 39) sets `"ind_left_right_timing": True, "ind_up_down_voltage": True`, while `training_to_lmt_records` (lines 56-67) emits exactly one combined `margin_type="TIMING"` and one combined `margin_type="VOLTAGE"` record per DqMargin — and `DqMargin` (lines 25-31) carries only two scalars (timing_ui, voltage_mv), with no left/right or up/down split anywhere. Reproduced at runtime: each DQ yields 2 records, both flagged True, yet no directional records exist. In the PCIe Lane Margining schema these flags assert the source reports independent directional margins (left-vs-right timing, up-vs-down voltage), so a downstream consumer (BigQuery/Looker/jq) that honors them expects directional split records it will never receive. The two sibling adapters confirm the inconsistency is the bug, not the convention: /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py:155-156 (from_margin_result) and /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gmsl/lmt.py:50-51 both set these flags False precisely because they emit a single combined value per axis (GMSL even documents this as 'honest defaults'). DDR5 is the lone outlier with no rationale in its docstring/comments. No test guards it: tests/test_ddr5_lmt.py asserts 2 records per DQ and the formatters round-trip, but never checks these flag values, so the suite would pass even with the wrong metadata. Not an honesty-discipline/fake-PASS issue and it does not flip a computetest verdict (the records flow through as-is), so not critical/high; it is genuine emitted-schema metadata that misrepresents the data structure. Suggested fix (set both flags False to match from_margin_result/GMSL, since DqMargin carries a single combined eye-to-failure per axis) is correct. Severity medium stands. Note: the finding's cited line numbers drift slightly (actual flags at line 39, not 38-39; from_margin_result at 155-156, not 152-153) but the substance is accurate.  

**M43. checks["rfm_enabled"]/["prac_enabled"] report True even when RFM/PRAC are actually disabled (misreports hardware state)**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/rfm.py` · rfm.py:43-49 (check_rfm_prac) · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When required is False (part unsupported OR oem_policy_requires=False), the per-check entries named rfm_enabled and prac_enabled are set to literal True regardless of the real rfm_enabled/prac_enabled arguments, and the raw inputs are not retained anywhere, so to_dict() serializes "rfm_enabled": True for a part whose RFM is in fact disabled.  
- **Evidence:** required = capability_supported and oem_policy_requires\n    checks = {\n        "rfm_enabled": rfm_enabled if required else True,\n        "prac_enabled": prac_enabled if required else True,\n ... — and RfmPracHealth has no field storing the raw rfm_enabled/prac_enabled, so to_dict() emits the coalesced value. Verified: check_rfm_prac(capability_supported=True, rfm_enabled=False, prac_enabled=False, alert_path_wired=True, oem_policy_requires=False) -> checks {'rfm_enabled': True, 'prac_enabled': True, ...}, summary 'OK'.  
- **Fix:** Keep the verdict logic (a not-required check should not fail), but do not overwrite the observed value with True. Either (a) store the raw rfm_enabled/prac_enabled as fields and report them in to_dict() separately from the pass/fail decision, or (b) make not-required checks None/'n/a' rather than True so a report cannot claim RFM is enabled when it is disabled. The check key named 'rfm_enabled' should reflect whether RFM is enabled.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/ddr5/rfm.py:43-49. With `required = capability_supported and oem_policy_requires` being False, checks["rfm_enabled"]/["prac_enabled"] are coalesced to literal True regardless of the observed arguments, and RfmPracHealth stores no field for the raw rfm_enabled/prac_enabled or for oem_policy_requires. Verified: check_rfm_prac(capability_supported=True, rfm_enabled=False, prac_enabled=False, alert_path_wired=True, oem_policy_requires=False) -> to_dict() = {"capability_supported": true, "checks": {"rfm_enabled": true, "prac_enabled": true, "alert_path_wired": true}, "ok": true}. In this scenario the part DOES support RFM and RFM is observably disabled, yet the serialized report claims rfm_enabled:true with no field anywhere recording that policy waived it — the true register state is unrecoverable from the report.

Refutation attempts failed: (1) The verdict logic (a not-required check must not fail) is intentional and the finding concedes it — that is NOT the defect; the defect is overwriting the observed value with a contradicting literal and discarding the raw input. (2) No existing guard handles it: the only relevant test, test_ddr5_rfm.py::test_unsupported_does_not_require_enable (lines 18-24), actually asserts checks["rfm_enabled"] is True for disabled RFM — it codifies the misleading behavior rather than guarding against it. (3) The codebase's own sibling idiom refutes the "this is just not-applicable representation" defense: ppr.py:51-61 handles not-applicable checks by OMITTING the key entirely (e.g. survives_power_cycle for sPPR), never by emitting a value that contradicts observation. The `if required else True` coalescing is unique to rfm.py across the whole src tree. (4) The roadmap (docs/research/2026-05-29-round2-sprint4-roadmap.md:92) frames the task as reading "MR enablement bits," i.e. the keys are meant to be state-reflecting, confirming intent that rfm_enabled report actual enablement.

This is a real honesty-discipline defect of the flagged class ("claims [hardware state] it cannot back") in a real-hw-facing diagnostic whose values mirror MR enablement bits read off hardware. Severity stays medium: the overall ok verdict is defensible (a policy-waived part correctly passes — no wrong gate-level PASS that ships bad parts), and it is not a crash/spec-number/parser error (which would be high). It is a misreported observed-state field with no recovery path. Suggested fix is sound: store raw rfm_enabled/prac_enabled separately or emit None/"n/a" for not-required, so the report never claims RFM is enabled when it is disabled.  

**M44. oem_policy_requires=False masking branch has no direct test coverage**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/rfm.py` · tests/test_ddr5_rfm.py:7-36 (TestRfmPrac) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** No test passes oem_policy_requires explicitly; the 'required = False' branch is only ever reached via capability_supported=False, so the supported-part-with-policy-not-required path — and its value-rewrite-to-True behavior — is never exercised, and a regression there would not be caught.  
- **Evidence:** All five tests call check_rfm_prac without oem_policy_requires; test_unsupported_does_not_require_enable relies on capability_supported=False. There is no assertion on the (capability_supported=True, oem_policy_requires=False) case where rfm_enabled/prac_enabled get masked.  
- **Fix:** Add a test with capability_supported=True, oem_policy_requires=False, rfm_enabled=False, prac_enabled=False that asserts the reported/serialized per-check values do not claim enablement (i.e. that the fix above holds), and that ok reflects the intended not-required semantics.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed as a real test-quality gap, demonstrated by a surviving mutant. Verification at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/ddr5/rfm.py (lines 37-49) and tests/test_ddr5_rfm.py (lines 7-36):

(1) grep confirms oem_policy_requires appears ONLY in rfm.py (def line 39, use line 43); it is never passed by any caller. The sole caller is the test file, and all five calls omit it (defaulting to True). So the (capability_supported=True, oem_policy_requires=False) path is genuinely unexercised.

(2) Decisive mutation test: replacing `required = capability_supported and oem_policy_requires` with `required = capability_supported` (i.e. deleting the OEM-policy term entirely) leaves all five tests PASSING (.....). This surviving mutant proves the oem_policy_requires masking behavior is untested — it could be removed silently. By contrast, an and->or mutation and a flipped-default mutation ARE caught, so the gap is specifically the policy-not-required-with-capability-present interaction.

Correction to the finding's wording: its claim that the "value-rewrite-to-True behavior...is never exercised" is imprecise — the `else True` arm IS exercised and asserted by test_unsupported_does_not_require_enable (asserts checks['rfm_enabled'] is True via capability_supported=False), and branch coverage is 100% (coverage.py reports rfm.py at 100%, BrPart=0). The masking arm is shared code, so a pure masking regression would be caught. But the finding's operative claim stands: the oem_policy_requires=False semantics on a supported part are never tested and a regression there survives.

Severity: this is a test-quality weakness, not a production code defect — rfm.py logic is correct and the coverage gate passes. Per the project rubric ("a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong" = medium), medium is correct. The untested branch is the safety-relaxing one (policy not required masks enablement to pass), which justifies keeping it at medium rather than downgrading. Suggested fix (add a capability_supported=True, oem_policy_requires=False test asserting not-required semantics) is sound and would kill the surviving mutant.  

**M45. Reserved/garbage density code collides with the legitimate '0 Gbit' value (silent misread)**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py` · src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py:41, density_gbit=DENSITY_GBIT.get(data[4] & 0x1F, 0) · _edge-case_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Any density code not in the table (e.g. 9-31) defaults to 0, which is indistinguishable from the legitimately-mapped code 0 (DENSITY_GBIT[0]=0). A corrupt/unknown density byte therefore silently decodes to density_gbit=0 rather than signalling an unknown value. If `expected` omits density_gbit, the misread is masked entirely.  
- **Evidence:** `DENSITY_GBIT = {0: 0, 1: 4, ... 8: 64}` and `DENSITY_GBIT.get(data[4] & 0x1F, 0)`. Both code 0 and an out-of-range code yield 0.  
- **Fix:** Use a sentinel for unmapped codes, e.g. `DENSITY_GBIT.get(code, -1)` (or None) and have check_spd flag unknown density as a failed check rather than coercing to 0.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced the defect directly against the code. `density_gbit=DENSITY_GBIT.get(data[4] & 0x1F, 0)` (spd.py:41) returns 0 for the legitimate JESD400-5 code 0 ("no/0 Gb") AND for every unmapped/reserved code (9-31). Verified: parse_spd with byte4 in {0, 9, 31} all yield density_gbit=0, so a corrupt density byte is indistinguishable from a genuine 0 Gb module. The masking claim also holds: check_spd only emits a density_gbit_matches check if "density_gbit" is in `expected`; with expected={module_type, ecc} a garbage density byte (31) gives ok=True with no density check at all. This contrasts with the sibling field module_type, which uses a distinguishable sentinel (.get(..., "unknown")) — density should likewise use a sentinel (None/-1) and have check_spd flag unknown density. The fix in the finding is sound.

Why medium, not higher: in the normal conformance flow a tester who cares about density puts it in `expected`, and a misread-to-0 then fails the density_gbit_matches check (parsed 0 != expected 16), so the verdict is NOT wrong in that common path. The wrong-PASS/masking risk only materializes when density_gbit is deliberately omitted from expected. The module docstring also explicitly scopes this as "a scaffold, not a full SPD decode." So it is a genuine edge-case parser defect on a real-hw SMBus/I3C parse path, but not a guaranteed wrong verdict — medium is the true severity. Not refuted/intentional: no guard or test exercises an unknown/reserved density code (tests only cover code 4 and a deliberate expected-mismatch), and the 0-default is a coding choice, not documented honesty-discipline behavior.  

**M46. Reserved/unknown module-type byte silently degrades to the string 'unknown' instead of failing the verdict**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py` · src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py:40, module_type=MODULE_TYPES.get(data[3] & 0x0F, "unknown") · _error-handling_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A reserved or corrupt module-type nibble decodes to the literal string 'unknown'. check_spd compares module_type by string equality, so unless `expected` explicitly lists module_type, an undecodable module passes the conformance check. There is no signal that the byte was unrecognised.  
- **Evidence:** `MODULE_TYPES.get(data[3] & 0x0F, "unknown")`; check_spd builds `checks[f"{k}_matches"] = parsed.get(k) == v` only for keys present in `expected`.  
- **Fix:** Either include module_type in the standard checks (fail when 'unknown'), or add an explicit `module_type_known` check so an unrecognised nibble cannot silently pass.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced concretely. An SPD with a reserved module-type nibble (e.g. data[3]&0x0F == 0x0F) decodes to the literal string "unknown" at src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py:40, MODULE_TYPES.get(data[3] & 0x0F, "unknown"). check_spd (lines 71-73) only builds a "{k}_matches" check for keys present in `expected`; is_ddr5 is the sole field-independent invariant. So when a caller's expected profile omits module_type, an undecodable module yields ok=True with checks={'is_ddr5': True, 'density_gbit_matches': True, 'ecc_matches': True} and summary "DDR5 SPD unknown 16Gb ecc=True -> OK" — a clean PASS on hardware whose module identity could not be recognized. No guard or test refutes this for the general API; the same silent-degrade pattern also exists for density (DENSITY_GBIT.get(..., 0) at line 41).

Not fully refuted/intentional: the module docstring calls this "a scaffold, not a full SPD decode," but that note concerns field *coverage*, not verdict masking. The honesty-discipline convention explicitly says to flag anything that "fakes a PASS" or "claims spec compliance it cannot back," and elsewhere the codebase already follows the None-raises pattern for unknown limits — so an undecodable byte silently passing is inconsistent with that discipline rather than a sanctioned design choice.

Severity held at medium (not critical/high): it is not a *guaranteed* wrong PASS. The corpus expected.json includes "module_type": "RDIMM", so the realistic/intended usage would correctly fail on "unknown"; the defect only materializes if the caller writes a profile that omits module_type. That matches the project's medium bar — "a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong." The suggested fix (add a profile-independent module_type_known check, or fold module_type into the standard checks) is appropriate.  

**M47. GET_PARTITION_INFO opcode 0x4000 actually maps to 'Identify Memory Device' (Get Partition Info is 0x4100)**  
`src/computetest/cxl/mailbox.py` · src/computetest/cxl/mailbox.py:35 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** CciOpcode.GET_PARTITION_INFO = 0x4000 is wrong: in the CXL CCI opcode = (command-set<<8 | command) scheme, 40h is the 'Identify Memory Device' command set (4000h = Identify Memory Device) and 'Get Partition Info' lives in the 41h 'Capacity Configuration and Label Storage' set at 4100h. A real-hw send of this opcode would invoke the wrong command.  
- **Evidence:** GET_PARTITION_INFO = 0x4000   # 4000h is 'Identify Memory Device'; Get Partition Info is 4100h  
- **Fix:** Set GET_PARTITION_INFO = 0x4100, and (optionally) add IDENTIFY_MEMORY_DEVICE = 0x4000 for the 40h set. Cross-check the other 4xxx values (4200h Get Health Info, 4300h Get Poison List, 4400h Sanitize are correct).  
- **Verifier:** The finding is factually correct. At src/computetest/cxl/mailbox.py:35, GET_PARTITION_INFO = 0x4000 is the wrong opcode. In the CXL CCI scheme (command_set << 8 | command), 0x4000 is "Identify Memory Device" (command set 40h, cmd 00h), not Get Partition Info. Verified against two independent authoritative sources: the Linux kernel CXL driver defines CXL_MBOX_OP_IDENTIFY = 0x4000 and CXL_MBOX_OP_SET_PARTITION_INFO = 0x4101 (set 41h = CCLS, Capacity Configuration and Label Storage). Since Set Partition Info is 0x4101 (cmd 01h of set 41h), Get Partition Info is 0x4100 (cmd 00h of set 41h). The QEMU cxl_opcodes.h enum corroborates: IDENTIFY = 0x40, CCLS = 0x41. The other 4xxx values in the finding's cross-check are right: GET_HEALTH_INFO=0x4200 (set 42h cmd 00h), GET_POISON_LIST=0x4300 (set 43h), SANITIZE=0x4400 (set 44h). No existing test refutes this: tests/test_cxl_mailbox.py::test_opcodes only asserts IDENTIFY, PERFORM_MAINTENANCE, and GET_HEALTH_INFO — it never checks GET_PARTITION_INFO, so the bug is uncaught. The suggested fix (GET_PARTITION_INFO = 0x4100, optionally add IDENTIFY_MEMORY_DEVICE = 0x4000) is correct.

Not refuted by project conventions: this is not the honesty-discipline None pattern, and it is not pragma real-hw untested logic that mocks can't reach — it is a concrete wrong constant with a misleading name that any future real backend would transmit verbatim, sending the wrong CCI command to hardware and parsing the wrong payload.

Downgraded high -> medium because of genuine mitigating context: the module docstring (lines 11-12) and class docstring (line 26) explicitly state the opcodes are a "representative subset; confirm the exact per-command codes against the CXL spec / CTS before encoding as assertions," and there is currently no wired real-hw transport (only MockMailbox; real backends are described as future "plug in by subclassing"). So today the wrong value is latent, not actively misfiring on hardware. It remains a real, demonstrable spec error in a named public constant that should be fixed before any real backend lands.  

**M48. check_poison_inject_clear can never produce a FAIL verdict on the only backend that exists**  
`src/computetest/cxl/poison.py` · src/computetest/cxl/poison.py:72-82 (check_poison_inject_clear) and PoisonList.inject/get_list/clear · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** For the PoisonList model, both checks ('poison_listed_with_dpa_length' and 'poison_cleared') are mathematically guaranteed True for any dpa/length, so the verdict is always OK and the function cannot detect a broken poison flow.  
- **Evidence:** inject() does self._entries.append(PoisonEntry(dpa=dpa, length=length, ...)); then 'found = any(e.dpa == dpa and e.length == length for e in listed)' is True because the just-appended entry has exactly that dpa+length; clear() does self._entries = [e for e in self._entries if e.dpa != dpa] then 'cleared = not any(e.dpa == dpa ...)' is True because every matching entry was filtered out. There is no path where found or cleared is False. The test asserts the guaranteed result: 'assert ... h.ok' and 'assert h.checks == {...: True, ...: True}'.  
- **Fix:** Model the real failure modes the verdict claims to test: a Clear-Poison that returns a non-SUCCESS CCI code, a poison-list capacity/overflow limit, and a length/DPA-granularity mismatch on clear. Have inject/clear be able to refuse (return a return-code) so the round-trip can genuinely fail, and add a negative test (e.g. clear refused -> poison_cleared False -> not ok). As written the check is decorative against the Mock.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproduced. In src/computetest/cxl/poison.py:72-82, check_poison_inject_clear calls plist.inject(dpa, length) which appends PoisonEntry(dpa, length, INJECTED), then sets found = any(e.dpa==dpa and e.length==length ...) — guaranteed True because the just-appended entry matches exactly. It then calls plist.clear(dpa), which rebuilds _entries keeping only e.dpa != dpa, and sets cleared = not any(e.dpa==dpa ...) — guaranteed True because every matching DPA was filtered out. Both checks are tautologies for any input; PoisonHealth.ok is therefore always True. I fuzzed 506 varied inputs (including 0/0, negative dpa, 2**64 values, and a pre-populated list with the same DPA but a different length) and every case returned ok=True. There is no reachable code path producing a False check, and no negative test: tests/test_cxl_poison.py::test_inject_list_clear_round_trip asserts the guaranteed result (h.ok and checks == {both True}).

Refutation attempts failed: (1) The 'mock fidelity' convention does not cover this — PoisonList is NOT part of the Backend Mock/Real split (it does not import mock_mode, is not a Backend subclass), has no dynamics to model (inject/clear are exact inverses of a plain list), and is the only object check_poison_inject_clear ever consumes; the real QEMU poison path (sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py CxlInjector.inject_poison) is a separate parallel path never wired to this function. (2) It is not the house style: the directly-analogous siblings have genuine discriminating power and negative tests — events.py check_event_get_clear has cleared_count_matches / untouched_handles_remain that diverge if the store miscounts or over-clears, and maintenance.py check_maintenance exercises a _busy state machine with a real failing branch plus the negative test test_viral_supported_but_not_asserting_fails. poison.py uniquely lacks any failing path or negative test.

This matches the project's own definition of a medium defect: 'a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong.' Severity stays medium (not high/critical): the verdict is decorative — check_poison_inject_clear is consumed only by the unit test, not by cli.py, the ocp-diag emit path, or the QEMU injector, so no false PASS reaches real hardware. The accurate fix is to give inject/clear refusable semantics (return-code / capacity limit / length-granularity mismatch) and add a negative test where poison_cleared is False so the round-trip can genuinely fail.  

**M49. calc_interleave_pos chain ordering is inverted vs the kernel it mirrors (root->endpoint docstring vs endpoint->root recurrence)**  
`src/computetest/cxl/hdm.py` · src/computetest/cxl/hdm.py:6-8,16-22 (calc_interleave_pos) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The recurrence pos = pos*parent_ways + parent_pos is order-dependent; the kernel cxl_calc_interleave_pos iterates ENDPOINT->root (first iteration uses the endpoint's position within its parent host bridge), but this module's docstring tells callers to pass a 'root->endpoint chain', so a caller following the docstring feeds the chain reversed and silently computes the wrong interleave position with no error.  
- **Evidence:** Docstring lines 7-8/17 say 'folds a root->endpoint chain'. Code: `for parent_pos, parent_ways in chain: pos = pos * parent_ways + parent_pos`. The kernel patch source: comment 'Iterate from endpoint to root_port refining the position', and the worked trace for mem1 is `pos = 0*2+1` (endpoint-in-host-bridge first) then `pos = 1*2+0` (host-bridge-in-root) = 2. I confirmed non-commutativity: calc([(1,2),(0,2)])=2 but calc([(0,2),(1,2)])=1. The asserted result 2 is only obtained when the FIRST element is the endpoint level, contradicting the 'root->endpoint' docstring.  
- **Fix:** Fix the docstring to state the chain MUST be ordered endpoint-first (endpoint->root), matching the kernel iteration, e.g. 'chain[0] = (endpoint position within its host bridge, host-bridge ways); chain[-1] = (host-bridge/switch position within root, root ways)'. Optionally accept a direction kwarg or reverse internally. Crucially, align the docstring, the field name semantics, and the test comment so a hardware engineer cannot feed it reversed.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced. (1) The recurrence pos = pos*parent_ways + parent_pos is non-commutative: calc([(1,2),(0,2)]) = 2 but calc([(0,2),(1,2)]) = 1 (executed). (2) The kernel cxl_calc_interleave_pos iterates endpoint->root, so the first-processed level is the endpoint and it becomes the most-significant digit. I reconstructed the kernel's worked topology (2 host bridges x 2 endpoints, region order mem0, mem2, mem1, mem3) and only the ENDPOINT-FIRST chains reproduce it: mem0=[(0,2),(0,2)]->0, mem1=[(1,2),(0,2)]->2, mem2=[(0,2),(1,2)]->1, mem3=[(1,2),(1,2)]->3. (3) The module docstring (hdm.py:7-8 and :17) tells callers to pass a "root->endpoint chain" — the opposite order. A caller following the prose literally for mem1 passes [(0,2),(1,2)] (root/host-bridge level first) and gets 1 instead of the correct 2, silently, with no error. (4) The test comment (test_cxl_hdm.py:9) compounds this by labeling the first element (1,2) as "root" when it is physically the endpoint-within-host-bridge level; the test passes only because it feeds the elements endpoint-first despite the mislabel. So the code and the asserted value are correct, but the documented ordering contract and the test's level labels are inverted, which can mislead a hardware engineer into a silently-wrong CXL interleave position. The finding is factually accurate and the fix (state endpoint-first ordering, fix the test comment) is right. Downgraded from high to medium: the shipped/tested code path computes the correct number, the defect lives in documentation/labeling, and a caller copying the working test example gets the right answer; the wrong result only arises if one trusts the prose over the example.  

**M50. emit_cxl_events always emits PASS even when Fatal/Failure event records are present**  
`src/computetest/cxl/emit.py` · src/computetest/cxl/emit.py:70-72 (emit_cxl_events) · _honesty_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A CXL event-log dump containing FATAL (log=3) or FAILURE (log=2) records is projected to the ocp-diag pipeline as a PASSing testStep, because the diagnosis type and step status are hardcoded regardless of record severity.  
- **Evidence:** em.diagnosis(verdict="cxl.events", type_="PASS", message=f"{len(records)} event records")\n    em.step_end("pass", step_id=sid)\n\nThe EventRecord carries `log: EventLog` where EventLog.FATAL=3 / FAILURE=2 (events.py:16-21). No caller in src/ gates on severity before calling emit_cxl_events (grep confirms emit is the only verdict site for events), so a DUT that logged a Fatal media error is reported PASS. The sibling emit_cxl_link/emit_cxl_ras both derive status from the data (health.ok, snapshot.has_uncorrectable); only events hardcodes PASS, breaking the otherwise-consistent reuse of the PCIe/NVMe pattern.  
- **Fix:** Derive the verdict from severity, e.g. `fatal = any(r.log >= EventLog.FAILURE for r in records); status = "fail" if fatal else "pass"` and pass that to both diagnosis(type_=...) and step_end(status). Include the worst severity / count of fatal+failure records in the message so the pipeline and operator see it. If the intent really is a pure informational dump, do not emit a PASS diagnosis at all (emit only the series and end the step NOT_APPLICABLE/SKIP), so it cannot be mistaken for a verdict.  
- **Verifier:** All factual claims reproduce against the code. In src/computetest/cxl/emit.py:70-72, emit_cxl_events unconditionally emits em.diagnosis(verdict="cxl.events", type_="PASS", ...) and em.step_end("pass", ...), with no dependence on record severity. events.py:16-21 confirms EventLog.FAILURE=2 / FATAL=3. The sibling emitters emit_cxl_link (line 29-32) and emit_cxl_ras (line 48-54) genuinely derive type_/status from the data; only events hardcodes PASS. tests/test_cxl_emit.py:64-71 even codifies this by passing a WARNING record and asserting type=="PASS". The corpus is a live demonstration: corpus/cxl/event-records/expected.json contains {"handle":1,"record_type":"general_media","log":2,"fields":{"descriptor":"uncorrectable_event"}} — a FAILURE-severity uncorrectable media event that, if passed to emit_cxl_events, still yields a PASS diagnosis + step_end("pass").

I tried hard to refute via "intentional informational dump": the module/function docstrings frame events as a measurementSeries, and the real events conformance verdict is check_event_get_clear -> EventClearHealth.ok (the Get/Clear-by-handle flow the CTS matrix maps to). That makes the finding's specific suggested fix (force status="fail" on any FAILURE record) debatable as a design choice. BUT the honesty-discipline convention explicitly says to flag anything that "fakes a PASS" or "mislabels a verdict." In the ocp-diag schema a diagnosis with type="PASS" plus testStepEnd status COMPLETE IS a positive verdict, not a neutral dump — an MT pipeline/operator sees a green "cxl.events" step even when the log holds fatal/failure records. The emitter even provides the honest neutral primitives (io/ocpdiag.py: _DIAGNOSIS_TYPE supports UNKNOWN; step_end supports SKIP), so a true informational dump should emit type_="UNKNOWN"/SKIP rather than PASS. So the defect is real either way.

Downgraded from high to medium: this is not the primary media-health verdict path (emit_cxl_ras and check_event_get_clear derive correctly from data), and the per-record severity is still echoed faithfully in each series element's metadata {"log": int(rec.log)} — so the raw data is not hidden, only the step-level verdict is mislabeled PASS. Honest fix: emit type_="UNKNOWN"/SKIP for a pure dump, or derive fail from any r.log >= EventLog.FAILURE if it is meant to be a verdict.  

**M51. emit_cxl_ras FAIL message is empty when only undecoded/reserved UE bits are set**  
`src/computetest/cxl/emit.py` · src/computetest/cxl/emit.py:49-50 (emit_cxl_ras) · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When ue_raw has a bit set that is not in CXL_UE_BITS (bits 15+), the step correctly FAILs but the diagnosis message reads 'uncorrectable: ' with no error named, so an operator sees a CXL uncorrectable failure with zero named causes.  
- **Evidence:** msg = ("uncorrectable: " + ",".join(n for _, n, _ in snapshot.uncorrectable) if snapshot.has_uncorrectable else "no uncorrectable errors"). `has_uncorrectable` is `ue_raw != 0` (ras.py:74-75) over ALL bits, but `snapshot.uncorrectable` (ras.py:51,66) only decodes bits 0-14 (CXL_UE_BITS, ras.py:15-31). So ue_raw=0x8000 -> has_uncorrectable True, uncorrectable==[] -> message 'uncorrectable: '.  
- **Fix:** When the decoded list is empty but has_uncorrectable is true, fall back to naming the raw bitmask, e.g. `else f"uncorrectable UE status=0x{int(snapshot.ue_raw):x} (no decoded bits)"`, so the FAIL always carries a non-empty, actionable cause. The verdict itself is already fail-safe; only the message is misleading.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced concretely. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/cxl/emit.py:48-50, the FAIL message is built only from snapshot.uncorrectable, which decode_ue() derives from CXL_UE_BITS (bits 0-14 only, ras.py:15-31), while has_uncorrectable is ue_raw != 0 over ALL bits (ras.py:74-75). With ue_status=0x8000 (bit 15, undecoded), I confirmed: has_uncorrectable=True, snapshot.uncorrectable=[], verdict=cxl.ras.fail, type=FAIL, message='uncorrectable: ' — a FAIL diagnosis naming zero causes. No guard or test covers this: tests/test_cxl_emit.py only exercises a decoded bit (bit 12 InternalError) and the clean case. This is NOT the honesty-discipline/refuse-to-pass pattern — the verdict is correctly fail-safe (any nonzero ue_raw -> FAIL), so it does not fake a pass or mislabel a verdict. Impact is bounded: the raw ue_status value IS still preserved in a measurement artifact (I confirmed value=32768, unit=bitmask emitted at emit.py:41-42), so a parser or operator reading the full artifact stream can recover 0x8000; only the human-readable diagnosis message is degraded. Because the verdict is correct and no data is lost, this falls below 'wrong number/spec value' (high) — it is operator-facing message mishandling for an edge case (future/reserved/vendor/garbage UE bits, plausible on real hardware since the CXL UE Status register is wider than bits 0-14). Confirmed at the finding's medium severity. Suggested fix (fall back to f'uncorrectable UE status=0x{snapshot.ue_raw:x} (no decoded bits)') is sound; the same gap exists symmetrically for CE decode if a CE failure path is ever added.  

**M52. No test pins the events verdict against severity; the only events-emit test asserts PASS for a single WARNING record**  
`src/computetest/cxl/emit.py` · tests/test_cxl_emit.py:64-71 (TestEmitEvents.test_events_series) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The events-emit test would still pass even if the code were changed to (correctly) fail on Fatal records, and it cannot catch the current PASS-on-fatal behavior, so the verdict logic for events is effectively unverified.  
- **Evidence:** The single test builds one EventRecord(log=EventLog.WARNING) and asserts `_diag(arts)["type"] == "PASS"`. There is no test with EventLog.FATAL/FAILURE asserting either PASS (to document the dump-only intent) or FAIL (to enforce a verdict). Combined with emit_cxl_events hardcoding PASS, a fatal-event regression is silently green.  
- **Fix:** Add a test feeding a FATAL/FAILURE EventRecord and assert the intended verdict. If dump-only is intended, assert the step is NOT_APPLICABLE/SKIP rather than a PASS diagnosis. This forces an explicit decision on Finding 1 and locks it in.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed test-quality defect. In src/computetest/cxl/emit.py:58-73, emit_cxl_events unconditionally emits diagnosis(type_="PASS") and step_end("pass") regardless of any record's log severity, even though EventLog (events.py:16-21) carries a clear severity ordering INFORMATIONAL=0 < WARNING=1 < FAILURE=2 < FATAL=3. The only emit-events test, tests/test_cxl_emit.py:64-71 (TestEmitEvents.test_events_series), feeds a single EventRecord(log=EventLog.WARNING) and asserts _diag(arts)["type"] == "PASS". Because the verdict is hardcoded, that assertion is a tautology — it holds for ANY input, so it pins nothing about severity. I tried to refute via: (1) other tests — grep shows EventLog.FAILURE/FATAL appear only in tests/test_cxl_events.py, and those exercise the get/clear store function check_event_get_clear, never the emit verdict; (2) documented dump-only intent — none exists; the docstring describes a measurementSeries of handles but the code still emits an affirmative PASS diagnosis (not NOT_APPLICABLE/SKIP), and docs/cxl/cxl_cts_coverage_matrix.md maps the Events row to the store test, not any emit-verdict test. The finding's two operative claims both hold: (a) a code change to correctly fail on FATAL/FAILURE would leave WARNING→PASS untouched, so the existing test stays green and provides zero protection on the verdict axis; (b) no test feeds a FATAL/FAILURE record, so the current PASS-on-fatal behavior is never exercised. This matches the project's own medium rubric: a weak test that would pass even if the code were wrong. Severity stays medium — the test gap fails to catch a wrong verdict but does not itself produce one (the higher-impact PASS-on-fatal behavior is the separate Finding 1).  

**M53. No test pins Gage R&R SS/MS/variance to a known reference ANOVA table, contradicting the docstring's 'pinned to textbook values' claim**  
`src/computetest/msa/gage_rr.py` · tests/test_msa.py:TestGageRrAnova (and module docstring gage_rr.py:28) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The EMS coefficients that convert mean squares to variance components (the divisors r, p*r, o*r in gage_rr_anova lines 195-198) are not guarded by any exact reference value; an off-by-one in a divisor (e.g. o*r vs (o-1)*r) would pass the entire suite, yet both docstrings claim tests pin numbers to textbook references.  
- **Evidence:** gage_rr.py:28 says 'Tests pin the answer to known textbook values.' and test_msa.py:3 says 'Tests pin numbers to textbook references so the implementation can't silently drift.' But the only exact Gage-R&R assertions are against zero (test_zero_variance..., test_pure_part_variation: var_part==approx(0.0)) and the self-consistent SS-sum identity (line 74-75: ss_part+ss_operator+ss_interaction+ss_error == approx(ss_total)). The SS identity holds for any algebraic decomposition and does not constrain the MS->variance EMS divisors. The verdict-threshold tests (lines 94-98) land at 1.9%, 18.5%, 71.9% — wide bins that would not catch a moderate divisor error.  
- **Fix:** Add a test that pins ss_part/ss_operator/ss_interaction/ss_error, the four mean squares, and var_equipment/var_operator/var_part to the exact values from a published AIAG MSA 4th-ed worked example (or a hand-computed small fixture), so the EMS coefficients can't silently drift.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed via mutation testing against the actual suite. Both docstrings are quoted accurately (gage_rr.py:27-28 "Tests pin the answer to known textbook values"; test_msa.py:2-4 "pin numbers to textbook references so the implementation can't silently drift"). Yet no test in tests/test_msa.py (the only Gage R&R test file in the repo) pins any SS, MS, or variance-component to a reference value. The only exact assertions are against 0.0 (test_zero_variance, test_pure_part_variation, test_pure_equipment), 100.0, or the SS-sum algebraic identity at lines 74-75 — which holds for ANY decomposition and never constrains the MS->variance EMS divisors at gage_rr.py:195-198. I built a parameterized copy of gage_rr_anova and reran the exact existing tests: an off-by-one in var_op divisor (p*r -> (p-1)*r), an off-by-one in var_int divisor (r -> r-1), a swap (p*r -> o*r), and even a GROSS 2x error in the var_part divisor (o*r -> o*r*2) ALL survive the entire suite with zero failures. On a realistic 6-part x 3-operator study the var_part off-by-one yields var_part=522.9 vs the correct 348.6 (a ~50% error) while the verdict and every test stay green, because pct_pv is normalized and still ~100%. The one apparent catch (var_part o*r -> (o-1)*r failing test_distinct_parts) is incidental: that test uses n_operators=1, so (o-1)*r=0 trips the divide-by-zero guard; with >=2 operators the same bug goes undetected. This is a real test-quality gap that contradicts the project's honesty/testing discipline and is not intentional. Severity is correctly medium per the rubric ("a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong") — the production code is currently correct, so there is no live wrong-verdict (which would be high/critical), but the variance-component math is effectively unguarded against silent drift on real-hardware studies.  

**M54. Single-operator (o=1) studies silently produce an unvalidated PV-only decomposition because df_interaction collapses to 0**  
`src/computetest/msa/gage_rr.py` · gage_rr.py:188-198 (df_int=(p-1)*(o-1); ms_int guard; var_part uses ms_int) · _edge-case_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When o==1 (or p==1) the interaction df is 0, so ms_int is forced to 0.0 and var_part is computed as ms_part/(o*r) with no interaction subtracted; the function returns a normal-looking 'capable' result with no warning that AV/interaction were uncomputable.  
- **Evidence:** df_int = (p-1)*(o-1) is 0 when o==1; ms_int = ss_int/df_int if df_int else 0.0 forces ms_int=0; var_part = max(0.0,(ms_part-ms_int)/(o*r)) then equals ms_part/r. The test test_distinct_parts_force_part_variance_positive (test_msa.py:328) feeds o=1 and only checks var_part>0 and finiteness, acknowledging in a comment 'n_operators=1 -> AV cannot be computed reliably' — but gage_rr_anova returns verdict='capable' with no flag that the R&R portion is unestimable.  
- **Fix:** Either reject o<2 in _validate (Gage R&R requires >=2 operators to estimate AV, just as it requires >=2 trials for EV), or surface a flag/field on GageRrResult indicating the AV/interaction components are not estimable so a field user does not read a misleadingly 'capable' verdict.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced directly. With o=1 (single operator), df_int=(p-1)*(o-1)=0 forces ms_int=0.0 (gage_rr.py:191) and df_op=0 forces ms_op=0.0 (line 190), so var_av collapses to 0 and var_part becomes ms_part/(o*r) with no interaction subtracted (line 198). gage_rr_anova then returns verdict='capable' with pct_av=0.0 and pct_rr=0.0 and NO flag/field signaling that the appraiser/reproducibility half of the study was unestimable — confirmed by running the exact test fixture (output: "p=3 o=1 r=2 %EV=0.0 %AV=0.0 %R&R=0.0 -> CAPABLE"). 

Not refuted by any guard: _validate (lines 108-133) explicitly rejects n_trials<2 ("Gage R&R needs >=2 trials per cell") but imposes NO minimum on operators, so the asymmetry the finding describes is real. The `else 0.0` branches are a generic divide-by-zero defense, not a deliberate single-operator design choice. Not refuted by tests: test_distinct_parts_force_part_variance_positive (test_msa.py:328) only asserts var_part>0/finiteness and its own comment concedes "n_operators=1 -> AV cannot be computed reliably," confirming the author knew o=1 is degenerate yet left it unguarded and unflagged. Not intentional per conventions: the project's honesty discipline explicitly says to flag anything that "mislabels a verdict"; a single-operator study stamped 'capable' with %AV silently zeroed is exactly that — the reproducibility (the second 'R') dimension is structurally absent, indistinguishable from genuine operator agreement. Docs (run-gage-rr.md) describe operators as plural and never sanction o=1.

Severity raised from low to medium: this yields a misleading verdict on real hardware (a deficient measurement system could earn a 'capable' stamp with AV silently dropped). It is not critical/high because it requires the atypical misuse of supplying o=1 to a crossed design, the emitted percentages remain mathematically self-consistent (no NaN/data corruption), and PV-dominated noiseless data is the realistic trigger. The suggested fix is sound: either reject o<2 in _validate (symmetric with the r<2 check) or add an 'av_estimable'/flag field to GageRrResult.  

**M55. se_slope / se_intercept inflated at small n by the same spurious-zero contamination**  
`src/computetest/msa/station_correlation.py` · deming_regression lines 202-209 · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** se_slope and se_intercept are the SD of the bootstrap distribution, but at n=3 that distribution contains the ~12% spurious 0.0 slopes from the sxy==0 branch, so the reported standard errors are materially overstated (bimodal mass at 0 and the true slope).  
- **Evidence:** `se_s = math.sqrt(sum((s - mean_s) ** 2 for s in slopes) / max(1, n_s - 1))` computed over `slopes`, which includes every appended 0.0 from the degenerate branch (slopes.append(s) at line 184 runs unconditionally after the early-return point estimate).  
- **Fix:** Same fix as the critical finding — excluding degenerate resamples from the bootstrap sample removes the spurious-zero mode and the inflated SE falls out automatically.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced independently. In _deming_point (station_correlation.py:227-230) the sxy==0 branch RETURNS (0.0, my) rather than raising — so the bootstrap's `except ZeroDivisionError: continue` (lines 182-183) does NOT skip it, and line 184 `slopes.append(s)` appends the spurious 0.0 unconditionally, exactly as the finding's evidence states. At n=3 (the minimum allowed), ~11.7% of resamples draw all-identical indices (P(all 3 same)=3*(1/3)^3=11.1%), each producing slope 0.0. The resulting bimodal distribution inflates se_slope ~22x in my repro (0.319 with the zeros vs 0.0145 with them excluded); se_intercept is similarly affected. The effect is heavily concentrated at n=3, dropping to ~0.7% at n=4 and 0% by n=6.

Severity held at medium (not higher): se_slope/se_intercept are diagnostic-only fields emitted via to_dict and have NO downstream consumer — grep confirms they never feed the `agrees` PASS/FAIL verdict (which uses the bootstrap percentile CIs ci95_slope/ci95_intercept). So this is a materially-overstated reported standard error at the smallest sample size, not a verdict-flipping bug. Not intentional/honesty-discipline (nothing documents including degenerate resamples in the SE), not a mock or pragma real-hw path, and no test asserts on se_slope/se_intercept (Deming tests use n=6/n=8 only). Note the deeper concern is that the same contamination affects the percentile CI that DOES drive the verdict, but that is the separate companion finding this one correctly references.  

**M56. Deming tests never exercise small-n (n<6), so they cannot catch the bootstrap-contamination false PASS**
