`src/computetest/msa/station_correlation.py` · tests/test_msa.py TestDemingRegression (lines 148-182) and TestStationCorrelation (lines 188-219) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Every Deming/station-correlation test uses n=6, 8, or 10 data points. The sxy==0 bootstrap-contamination false PASS only manifests at n=3 (the minimum). The suite therefore passes even with the verdict-corrupting bug present, giving false assurance for the smallest-sample field scenario.  
- **Evidence:** test_perfect_agreement uses 6 points; test_systematic_offset and test_pure_scale_factor use 8; test_three_identical_stations uses gold=range(10). No test uses n=3, 4, or 5. `python3 -m pytest tests/test_msa.py` passes 100% despite the n=3 false PASS demonstrated above.  
- **Fix:** Add a regression test at n=3 with a pure scale factor (e.g. y=1.5*x) asserting `not r.agrees` and that the slope CI does not collapse to (0, slope); add a station_correlation test with 3 golden DUTs and a 1.5x-biased station asserting `not p.ok`.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced both halves of this test-quality finding.

UNDERLYING BUG (real, verdict-corrupting): In src/computetest/msa/station_correlation.py, deming_regression's bootstrap (lines 170-200) resamples paired (x,y) WITH replacement. At n=3, degenerate resamples that pick all-identical points make _deming_point's sxy==0 branch (lines 227-230) return slope 0.0. I counted 117/1000 such contaminated resamples for x=[1,2,3], y=[1.5,2.25,4.5]. This drags the 2.5th-percentile slope down to 0.0, so ci95_slope collapses to (0.0, 1.5) — which contains 1.0 — and ci95_intercept=(0.0, 4.5) contains 0.0. Result: a pure 1.5x scale factor (true DISAGREE) reports r.agrees=True, a false PASS. Via station_correlation, a 3-DUT study with a 1.5x-biased station yields p.ok=True for ('bad','good_A') and ('bad','good_B'). The bug manifests ONLY at n=3 (the minimum the len(x)<3 guard at line 163 permits); at n>=4 with clean data it self-corrects (ci95_slope=(1.5,1.5), agrees=False). n=3 is a fully supported, realistic minimal correlation study — no higher guard exists.

TEST GAP (the actual finding): Verified the cited line numbers. TestDemingRegression uses n=6 (line 150), n=8 (lines 158,169); TestStationCorrelation uses n=10 (lines 191,201). No Deming/station test uses n=3,4,5. `python3 -m pytest tests/test_msa.py` passes 32/32 with the false PASS fully present. So the suite gives false assurance for the smallest-sample field scenario, which is exactly the only scenario the bug corrupts.

Not refuted (data sizes and 100% pass confirmed factually), not already-handled (no guard/test covers n=3), not intentional (the code emits a wrong PASS verdict — opposite of the honesty-discipline convention which RAISES rather than fake a pass). This matches the project's own definition of medium: "a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong." The bug it fails to catch is itself critical-grade, but as a test-quality finding medium is correctly calibrated. The suggested fix (regression tests at n=3 asserting not agrees / not ok, and that the slope CI does not collapse to (0, slope)) is appropriate and would catch it.  

**M57. _to_date returns a datetime for YAML timestamps, crashing date arithmetic on real hardware configs**  
`src/computetest/msa/calibration_registry.py` · calibration_registry.py:140-144 (_to_date), used at lines 131-132 · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When a YAML value carries a time component (e.g. `next_due: 2026-01-15 12:00:00`), PyYAML parses it to datetime.datetime; because datetime is a subclass of date, the `isinstance(v, date)` guard passes and _to_date returns the datetime unchanged. Subsequent `next_due - today` (today is a plain date) raises TypeError, crashing is_expired/days_until_due/require — the exact CI gate that decides station readiness.  
- **Evidence:** `def _to_date(v) -> date:\n    if isinstance(v, date):\n        return v\n    return date.fromisoformat(v)` — verified: `isinstance(datetime(2026,1,15,12,0,0), date)` is True, and `datetime(...) - date(2026,1,1)` raises `TypeError: unsupported operand type(s) for -: 'datetime.datetime' and 'datetime.date'`.  
- **Fix:** Normalize datetime down to a date before the bare-date check: `if isinstance(v, datetime): return v.date()` then `if isinstance(v, date): return v`; or `return v.date() if isinstance(v, datetime) else (v if isinstance(v, date) else date.fromisoformat(v))`.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproduced. At calibration_registry.py:140-144, `_to_date` checks `isinstance(v, date)` and returns v unchanged; since `datetime.datetime` subclasses `datetime.date`, a YAML value carrying a time component is returned as a datetime. I verified with PyYAML: `next_due: 2026-01-15 12:00:00` parses to `datetime.datetime(2026,1,15,12,0)`, `isinstance(..., date)` is True, and `datetime(2026,1,15,12,0) - date(2026,1,1)` raises `TypeError: unsupported operand type(s) for -: 'datetime.datetime' and 'datetime.date'`. This propagates through days_until_due (line 46), is_expired (line 43), require (line 87, the station-readiness CI gate), expired, and due_within — all of which subtract/compare against a bare `today: date`. The suggested fix (normalize via `v.date()` when isinstance datetime) is correct.

Adversarial checks: Not refuted — the technical behavior is real. Not already-handled — no guard normalizes datetime, and the tests (tests/test_msa.py:264-281) only exercise bare-date YAML and string-date JSON; none feed a timestamped value, so the gates pass while the path is broken. Not intentional — the `_to_date` docstring shows the author meant to accept "a date (YAML 1.2 native) or an ISO string" and simply missed the datetime-subclass subtlety; this is not honesty-discipline or mock-fidelity design.

Severity held at medium (not high): the trigger requires off-schema input. The documented schema (docstring lines 28, 100-110) and the bundled configs/calibration_registry.example.yaml use bare YYYY-MM-DD exclusively, which parse to datetime.date and work fine. A time component is outside the documented format, and the failure mode is a loud TypeError crash, not a silent wrong PASS/FAIL or data corruption. But it is a genuine real-hw config-parser robustness bug: PyYAML silently accepts a human-written timestamp and the readiness gate then crashes instead of failing gracefully or normalizing — medium per the rubric (edge-case input mishandling on a real-hw path).  

**M58. Length guard >=14 lets ECC read pass while the real ECC byte (235) is missing**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py` · spd.py:36-37, 42 · _error-handling_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** parse_spd only requires len>=14 then reads data[13] for ECC; once the offset is corrected to 235 a 14-byte buffer would IndexError. Even today, accepting a 16-byte 'SPD' and emitting confident memory_type/density/ecc verdicts on a fragment is misleading — a real DDR5 SPD is 1024 bytes.  
- **Evidence:** `if len(data) < 14: raise ValueError(...)` then `ecc=bool(data[13] & 0x08)`. The shipped corpus is only 16 bytes and passes. No upper-context check that the buffer is a plausible DDR5 SPD.  
- **Fix:** Require the SPD to be long enough for every byte actually read (>=236 once ECC moves to byte 235), or guard each optional field and set ecc=None when truncated rather than returning a definite False.  
- **Verifier:** The load-bearing claim is factually correct and is a real spec/parsing bug. In JESD400-5 DDR5 SPD, byte 13 (0x00D) is "SDRAM Duty Cycle Adjuster / Partial Array Self Refresh" — NOT ECC. ECC/bus-extension lives in byte 235 (0xEB, "Memory Channel Bus Width", bits 4-3). spd.py:42 computes `ecc=bool(data[13] & 0x08)`, reading the wrong byte. The module docstring states this parser decodes the SPD "read over SMBus / I3C-Basic from the SPD5118 hub" against real DDR5 modules, so on actual hardware `data[13]` holds duty-cycle-adjuster bits and the ecc verdict (and the `ecc_matches` conformance check in check_spd) would be effectively arbitrary — a wrong field decode / potential wrong PASS-FAIL on a path meant for real hardware.

The synthetic 16-byte corpus (`corpus/ddr5_spd/synthetic-ddr5-32gb/spd.hex` = 30101201040000000000000000080000) is hand-built so each field lands at the offset the parser reads (0x08 at byte 13), so test_ddr5_spd.py passes and actively masks the offset bug rather than exercising a real SPD. Byte 2 (DDR5 key 0x12), byte 3 (module type nibble) and byte 4 (density) are correctly placed per spec; only the ECC offset is wrong.

Adjacent claims are partly weaker: (1) the "16-byte fragment passes / no upper-bound DDR5-plausibility guard" point is partly intentional — the docstring explicitly calls this "a scaffold, not a full SPD decode" and there is no SPD-CRC check (crc.py is link CRC, unrelated), so accepting the synthetic fragment is by-design; (2) the IndexError-on-14-bytes claim is conditional on adopting the fix (today the len>=14 guard is self-consistent with the wrong offsets, so no IndexError occurs now). Confirmed verdict rests on the wrong-byte spec bug, which is real and independently reproducible from the code + JESD400-5.

Severity held at medium (not raised to high): the bug is a real spec misparse, but there is currently NO real-hardware caller wired up — no SPD5118/SMBus EEPROM reader exists in src; parse_spd is only ever fed the synthetic corpus today. So field blast radius is latent rather than active. The suggested_fix should both move ECC to byte 235 AND raise the length guard to cover every offset actually read (>=236) to avoid the IndexError the finding correctly anticipates.  

**M59. Round-trip tests are self-consistent and cannot detect the wrong NMP layout**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py` · tests/test_nvme_mi.py:126-160 (TestMiMessageEncode) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** test_encode_decode_round_trip only asserts decode(encode(x)) == x; since encode and decode share the same (wrong) bit constants, the test passes regardless of whether the layout matches the spec. No test asserts the absolute byte value of the NMP byte against a spec-derived golden vector, so the critical layout bug is invisible to the suite.  
- **Evidence:** The test sets csi=1, slot_id=2 and asserts decoded.csi==1, decoded.slot_id==2 — a pure round-trip. There is no assertion like `wire[0] == 0x09`. The CRC test at line 175-179 does use a real golden vector (0xE3069283), proving the technique was available but not applied to the NMP byte.  
- **Fix:** Add a golden-vector test asserting the exact NMP byte (e.g. NVME_MI_COMMAND, csi=1 -> wire[0]==0x09 after the layout fix) and a decode test against a hand-built spec-conformant frame, so field placement is pinned to the spec rather than to the encoder.  
- **Verifier:** The finding's core test-quality claim is factually correct and reproduces. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/tests/test_nvme_mi.py:126-141, test_encode_decode_round_trip asserts only decode(encode(x)) == x (decoded.csi==1, decoded.slot_id==2, etc.), and encode_mi_request / decode_mi_response in messages.py:123-160 share the identical bit constants (MT bits 0-3, CSI bit 4, SLOTID bits 5-6) in both directions. I confirmed encode/decode are mutual inverses (the test passes) and that grepping the whole test file finds NO assertion against the absolute NMP byte value (e.g. no `wire[0] == ...`); the only golden vectors are the CRC ones at lines 177/179 (0xE3069283, RFC 3720). So the round-trip test would pass for any self-consistent layout, spec-conformant or not — matching the project's own definition of a medium-severity weak test ("a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong"). HOWEVER, the finding overstates by asserting an established "wrong NMP layout"/"critical layout bug": it never demonstrates the layout is actually wrong, and its own suggested golden value (wire[0]==0x09 for csi=1) is inconsistent with the code, which produces 0x11 for NVME_MI_COMMAND(0x01)+csi=1+slot_id=0 (and 0x51 for the round-trip case). The 0x09 example is internally muddled and not shown to be spec-correct, so the "critical layout bug" framing is unsubstantiated. The verifiable, accurate part is purely a test-design gap (a tautological round-trip with no spec-pinned golden NMP vector), which is genuinely medium, not critical. Suggested fix (add a golden NMP-byte assertion and a decode-from-hand-built-frame test) is sound and would close the gap.  

**M60. Gen6 FEC burst-length histogram over-counts pre-FEC symbol errors by ~0.4%**  
`src/computetest/backend.py` · src/computetest/backend.py:650-656 (MockBackend.read_fec_stats burst histogram) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The per-bucket share weights sum to 1.004 (not 1.0) because buckets 7 and 8 both fall through to the final 0.002 branch, so the histogram — presented as a breakdown of pre_fec_symbol_errors — sums to MORE than pre, and the leftover reconciliation only adds (never trims) so the overshoot persists.  
- **Evidence:** share = (0.6 if b==1 else 0.25 if b==2 else 0.10 if b==3 else 0.04 if b==4 else 0.008 if b==5 else 0.002). For buckets [1..8] this is 0.6+0.25+0.10+0.04+0.008+0.002+0.002+0.002 = 1.004. The reconcile `if pre > counted: hist[8] += (pre - counted)` only handles a shortfall. Live mock run: pre_fec_symbol_errors=2561687 but sum(histogram)=2571932 (extra 10245). bert.py reports both the headline pre count and this histogram side by side (explain() line 119-123, to_dict line 149), so a consumer summing the histogram disagrees with the headline. It never flips a pass/fail (FEC counters don't gate the verdict), hence medium not critical.  
- **Fix:** Make bucket 8 the explicit remainder rather than giving 7 and 8 the same fallthrough weight: e.g. assign explicit shares for b in 1..7 summing to <1, then set hist[8] = pre - sum(hist[1..7]) (clamped >=0). Add a test asserting sum(histogram.values()) == pre_fec_symbol_errors. The existing test_gen6_burst_histogram_is_length_keyed only checks key types and that bucket 1 dominates, so it passes despite the overshoot.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced end-to-end through the actual MockBackend.read_fec_stats path (PYTHONPATH=src, gen6 device, injected_pre_fec_symbol_rate=1e-5, elapsed=1.0): pre_fec_symbol_errors=2561687 but sum(burst_length_histogram)=2571932 — an overshoot of 10245 (0.400%), exactly matching the finding.

Root cause (src/computetest/backend.py:646-656): buckets=[1..8]; the share expression assigns 0.6/0.25/0.10/0.04/0.008 for b in 1..5 and a fallthrough else=0.002 for everything else — so buckets 6, 7 AND 8 each get 0.002, summing to 0.6+0.25+0.10+0.04+0.008+0.002*3 = 1.004. (Minor inaccuracy in the finding: it says "buckets 7 and 8 fall through"; it is actually 6, 7, and 8. The 1.004 total and reproduced numbers are correct regardless.) Each bucket is round(remaining*share) where remaining=pre is never decremented, so counted ≈ pre*1.004. The reconcile at line 655 is `if pre > counted: hist[8] += (pre - counted)` — only an additive shortfall fix; on overshoot (counted>pre) it does not fire and never trims, so the surplus persists.

Not intentional design: the use of a `remaining`/leftover variable plus the comment "Anything left over goes to the >=8 bucket" and the one-directional reconcile show the author intended the shares to sum to <=1.0 and the histogram to partition `pre`. Bucket 6 silently inheriting the same 0.002 as 7/8 (and no explicit bucket-6 share) is an unintended weight, not a modeling choice. So this is a genuine off-by-0.004 bug, not mock fidelity.

Impact is real but bounded: bert.py surfaces both the headline pre_fec_symbol_errors and the histogram side by side in explain() (lines 119,123) and to_dict() (lines 146,149), and io/ocpdiag.py:282-292 emits pre count and each burst_len_N as separate measurements — so a consumer summing the buckets disagrees with the headline by 0.4%. It does NOT gate any pass/fail verdict (FEC counters carry no verdict-deciding validator; fber has its own separate validator). The existing test test_gen6_burst_histogram_is_length_keyed only checks key types and that bucket 1 dominates, so it passes despite the overshoot — confirming the test gap the finding cites. Medium is correct: internally inconsistent mock data that could mislead a histogram-summing consumer, but no wrong PASS/FAIL on real hardware. Suggested fix (make bucket 8 the explicit remainder and add a sum==pre test) is sound.  

**M61. Throttle hex parse only guards ''/'N/A' and crashes on any other unexpected token**  
`src/computetest/gpu.py` · gpu.py:152 · _error-handling_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** int(f[10], 16) is only protected against the literal strings '' and 'N/A'; any other non-hex value (e.g. a column-shift artifact, or an unforeseen nvidia-smi token) raises an uncaught ValueError that aborts the entire real-hw check rather than degrading safely.  
- **Evidence:** throttle_bits = int(f[10], 16) if f[10] not in ("", "N/A") else 0 — no try/except; the only guarded sentinels are empty string and 'N/A'.  
- **Fix:** Wrap the parse in try/except ValueError returning 0 (or a small helper _hex(s)), so an unparseable throttle field degrades to 'no throttle reported' instead of crashing the whole GPU verdict.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed real defect in a real-hw path (src/computetest/gpu.py:152, inside _query_nvidia_smi which is `# pragma: no cover - real-hw path`).

The line is `throttle_bits = int(f[10], 16) if f[10] not in ("", "N/A") else 0`. The guard only excludes the bare strings "" and "N/A". But nvidia-smi under `--format=csv,noheader,nounits` emits BRACKETED sentinels for unavailable fields — `[Not Supported]`, `[N/A]`, `[Unknown Error]` — none of which match the guard. I verified in Python that `int(t, 16)` raises ValueError for every one of these tokens (including the bracketed `[N/A]`, which is distinct from the guarded bare `N/A`). The fields are .strip()'d (line 151) so whitespace isn't the issue; the bracket characters are.

There is no try/except around this parse, and the call site harness.py:106 (`gpu.check_gpu(idx)`) has no wrapper, so an unparseable throttle field raises an uncaught ValueError that aborts the GPU health verdict (and propagates up the harness loop) instead of degrading to "no throttle reported."

This is not refuted/handled/intentional: notably, EVERY other field in the same function (f[1]..f[9]) is parsed through the sibling helpers `_int()`/`_float()`, which DO wrap in `try/except (ValueError, TypeError)` and degrade to 0/0.0 — `_int('[Not Supported]')` returns 0. The throttle field is the lone exception, so this is an inconsistency/oversight, not a deliberate design choice, and it is not an honesty-discipline raise (it crashes, it does not refuse-to-pass intentionally). clocks_throttle_reasons.active returning `[Not Supported]`/`[N/A]` is real nvidia-smi behavior on older, MIG, or virtualized GPUs.

The claim is factually accurate. Adjusting severity from the filed "low" up to "medium": per project conventions a real-hw path that crashes/misparses is high-impact, but this is conditional on the throttle field being unavailable (the common healthy case returns `0x0...` and parses fine), so it sits between low and high. Suggested fix is sound — route f[10] through a try/except helper (e.g. `int(f[10], 16)` wrapped like `_int`) so an unparseable token degrades to 0.  

**M62. _RAIL_RE misses JetPack 5/6 (AGX/Orin) power-rail format `VDD_IN 4067mW/4067mW` — every rail silently dropped on the primary automotive Jetson**  
`src/computetest/tegra.py` · tegra.py:44 (_RAIL_RE) parsed in parse_tegrastats:99 · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** On JetPack 5.x/6.x (AGX Orin, Orin Nano/NX) tegrastats emits power rails as `<RAIL> <inst>mW/<avg>mW` (e.g. `VDD_IN 4067mW/4067mW`), but _RAIL_RE requires `(\d+)/(\d+)` with the slash immediately after the first integer, so the `mW` between the number and the slash makes it match nothing and rails_mw comes back empty on real Orin hardware.  
- **Evidence:** _RAIL_RE = re.compile(r"\b([A-Z][A-Z0-9_]{2,})\s+(\d+)/(\d+)(?=\s|$)"). Tested against a real JP5 line `... VDD_IN 4067mW/4067mW VDD_CPU_GPU_CV 600mW/600mW VDD_SOC 1397mW/1397mW` -> _RAIL_RE.findall(...) == [] (empty). The ORIN test fixture uses the OLD `VDD_GPU_SOC 1191/1191` form, which is the Nano/JP4-era format, so the test never exercises the mW/mW form the docstring claims to support for Orin.  
- **Fix:** Broaden the rail regex to tolerate an optional unit on each side of the slash, e.g. r"\b([A-Z][A-Z0-9_]{2,})\s+(\d+)\s*mW?/\s*(\d+)\s*mW?\b" (or accept `mW` suffix optionally) and add a real JP5/JP6 Orin fixture (`VDD_IN 4067mW/4067mW`) to test_orin_line so the supported-format claim is actually tested. Power is non-gated so no verdict flips, but the 'per-rail power reported' feature is currently dead on Orin.  
- **Verifier:** CONFIRMED. _RAIL_RE at tegra.py:44 is `r"\b([A-Z][A-Z0-9_]{2,})\s+(\d+)/(\d+)(?=\s|$)"`, requiring the slash immediately after the first integer. I reproduced the bug: running findall against the documented/real Orin form `VDD_IN 4067mW/4067mW VDD_CPU_GPU_CV 600mW/600mW ...` returns [] (empty), so parse_tegrastats:99 yields rails_mw == {} and _merge folds nothing. The format claim is verified against authoritative sources: NVIDIA's r36.4.4 (JetPack 6) Tegrastats doc documents the rail format as `VDD_GPU 10687mW/9815mW` (mW suffix between number and slash); real AGX Orin JP5.0.2 output shows `VDD_GPU_SOC 7968mW/7969mW`; and the de-facto reference parser jetson_stats/jtop deliberately uses `r'\b(\w+) ([0-9.]+)(\w?)W?\/([0-9.]+)(\w?)W?\b'` with an OPTIONAL unit suffix precisely to tolerate this. The docstring (line 11) explicitly advertises `VDD_*/VIN_* on Orin`, yet the only Orin fixture (test_tegra.py:23, `VDD_GPU_SOC 1191/1191`) uses the unit-less form; grep confirms no `<n>mW/<n>mW` data sample exists anywhere in src or tests, so the supported-format claim is never tested. This is reached only via the real-hw collector (_collect_tegrastats, pragma no-cover) feeding raw lines into parse_tegrastats, so unit tests cannot catch it — a parsing bug inside a real-hw path, the high-value class the conventions call out. NOT refuted by mock fidelity, pragma, or honesty discipline, and not handled by any guard or test. Downgraded from high to medium for two reasons: (1) per the project's own conventions, power is explicitly REPORTED-NOT-GATED (lines 16-17, 164-168 — checks cover only temp/throttle/critical-XID), so there is NO wrong PASS/FAIL verdict and no gated-data corruption (the finding concedes this); (2) the regex is incomplete rather than wholly wrong — Nano POM_5V_* and older r34/r35-era output use the unit-less N/N form the regex matches. Net impact: on the primary automotive Jetson (Orin / JetPack 5-6) the advertised per-rail power telemetry silently returns empty, defeating a documented feature but never flipping a verdict. Suggested fix (tolerate optional mW on each side, add a real JP5/JP6 mW fixture) is correct.  

**M63. gpu_temp falsy-0 fallthrough: GPU@0C mislabels reported GPU temperature as tj/max_zone**  
`src/computetest/tegra.py` · tegra.py:160 (_build_health) `gpu_temp = thermal.get("GPU") or thermal.get("tj") or max_zone` · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A valid GPU reading of 0.0C (idle/cold iGPU; 0.0 > _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C so it is kept as meaningful) is falsy under `or`, so the chain skips it and reports tj or the max zone as gpu_temp_c, mislabeling the GPU temperature in metrics/summary.  
- **Evidence:** With thermal={'GPU':0.0,'tj':47.0}, max_zone=47.0: `thermal.get("GPU") or thermal.get("tj") or max_zone` -> 47.0 (verified), not 0.0. Same `or` falsy bug would also fire on a GPU@0C-only line. gpu_temp_c is reporting-only (not in checks), so no verdict flips, but the surfaced number is wrong.  
- **Fix:** Use explicit membership: `gpu_temp = thermal["GPU"] if "GPU" in thermal else thermal.get("tj", max_zone)` (and treat tj the same way), so a legitimate 0.0C reading is preserved.  
- **Verifier:** Mechanically reproduced at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/tegra.py:160. `gpu_temp = thermal.get("GPU") or thermal.get("tj") or max_zone` uses `or`, so a legitimate GPU reading of exactly 0.0C (which passes the `_MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C = -40.0` floor and is kept as meaningful) is falsy and falls through. End-to-end via check_tegra(_lines=["GPU@0C tj@47C CPU@45C"]) yields metrics["gpu_temp_c"] == 47.0 (the tj value) rather than the GPU's own 0.0C; thermal_c correctly stores GPU:0.0, so the surfaced gpu_temp_c metric and summary() line are mislabeled.

Not refuted/already-handled: the fallback tests at tests/test_tegra.py:89-95 only cover the ABSENCE of GPU/tj zones, never a falsy GPU value, so no guard or test catches this. Not intentional: the `or` idiom is a convenience, not a deliberate design (the author explicitly modeled implausible sensors via the -256C/PMIC sentinel logic, showing intent to handle sensor edges correctly).

Severity held at medium, not raised to high: (1) gpu_temp is reporting-only — grep confirms it feeds only summary() and metrics["gpu_temp_c"], never the checks dict (lines 164-168), so no PASS/FAIL verdict flips (the finding concedes this); (2) the trigger is the narrow exact value 0.0C, which is implausible as a real idle iGPU temperature on a Jetson (the iGPU shares the warm LPDDR die and idles ~25-45C, not 0.0C). This matches the rubric's "edge-case mishandling / a weak test that would pass even if the code were wrong" exactly — the existing tests do still pass with the bug present. Suggested fix (explicit `"GPU" in thermal` membership) is correct.  

**M64. max_zone<=85C check false-FAILs a cold-but-healthy chip whose hottest meaningful zone is between -40C and 0C**  
`src/computetest/tegra.py` · tegra.py:165 `f"max_zone<={max_temp_c:g}C": 0 < max_zone <= max_temp_c` · _edge-case_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The `0 < max_zone` lower bound is meant to fail the empty/no-samples case (max_zone==0.0), but it also fails any run where every meaningful zone reads sub-zero. -40C..0C is inside the AEC-Q100 grade-1 automotive operating range, so a cold-chamber Jetson with the hottest zone at e.g. -5C would report `max_zone<=85C` = False and ok=False despite being thermally healthy.  
- **Evidence:** meaningful={'GPU':-5.0,'CPU':-10.0} -> max_zone=-5.0 -> `0 < -5.0 <= 85.0` == False (verified). The no-samples sentinel and a real cold reading are conflated by the same `0 <` guard.  
- **Fix:** Distinguish 'no data' from a real cold reading: gate on whether `meaningful` is non-empty (a separate `has_data` flag) rather than reusing 0.0 as a sentinel; then the temperature check becomes `max_zone <= max_temp_c` and a `samples_present` check covers the empty case.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced from the actual code at tegra.py:165. `check_tegra(_lines=['GPU@-5C CPU@-10C'])` yields checks={'max_zone<=85C': False, ...}, ok=False, max_zone_c=-5.0, with EMPTY notes — i.e. a wrong FAIL verdict on a thermally healthy chip, and the CLI (cli.py:296) maps th.ok straight to EXIT_FAIL, so the false-fail propagates end-to-end. The root cause is exactly as claimed: line 159 uses max_zone=0.0 as the no-data sentinel, and the test_no_samples_fails test (test_tegra.py:97-100) confirms the `0 < max_zone` lower bound in line 165 exists to fail that empty case. But the offline-sensor filter at line 150 only drops zones with t <= _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C (-40.0), so a real reading like -5C is deliberately kept as 'meaningful' yet then fails the temperature gate — an internal contradiction producing a discontinuity at exactly 0C (verified: -0.5C fails, +0.5C passes), which has no physical or spec basis. Nothing rescues the verdict (no note, no other guard). This is NOT intentional design (no docstring/comment/spec states a positive minimum operating temperature; the only documented floor is -40C for offline rejection, which argues sub-zero-above--40 readings should be valid) and is not a mock-fidelity or pragma exception. Severity stays medium: it is a wrong FAIL verdict, but the failure direction is safe (false-fail, not false-pass — it won't ship bad hardware) and the precondition is narrow (die zones must read sub-zero, requiring a cold chamber plus a near-idle non-self-heating SoC, i.e. an automotive cold-soak/cold-start qualification run). The suggested fix (track a separate has_data/samples_present flag and drop the `0 <` lower bound) is correct.  

**M65. Self-test verdict (self_test_passed) real path has no corpus replay test, unlike smart-log/id-ctrl**  
`src/computetest/nvme/health.py` · health.py:169-196; tests/test_parsers_corpus.py (no nvme self-test case) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The smart-log and id-ctrl real branches are exercised through real-format captures in test_parsers_corpus.py (Kelvin fixup, avail_spare/percent_used aliases all asserted). The self-test real path — _real_self_test_log and _real_poll_self_test, which produce the self_test_passed PASS/FAIL check added to checks{} — has zero replay coverage; only the mock (which hard-codes result=0/9) is tested. Any of the key-name/masking issues above would not be caught.  
- **Evidence:** corpus/nvme/2.11/samsung-pm9a3/ contains only id-ctrl.json and smart-log.json; NVME_CASES in test_parsers_corpus.py replays only smart-log/id-ctrl. start_self_test/poll_self_test tests use mock=True only.  
- **Fix:** Add a corpus capture nvme/<ver>/<case>/self-test-log.json (one passing, one failing entry) and a parametrized replay test asserting result code, in_progress, and passed through self_test_log(device, mock=False).  
- **Verifier:** Verified against the actual code, tests, corpus, and design doc. CONFIRMED on all points. (1) Code: _real_self_test_log (health.py:169-177) and _real_poll_self_test (188-196) are the real-hw branches that produce checks["self_test_passed"] (line 141); both carry `# pragma: no cover`, so the coverage gate cannot flag them. (2) Tests: every self-test test (tests/test_nvme.py:16-30, tests/test_nvme_format.py:20-48) calls with mock=True/default-mock only. The mock self_test_log (162-165) hard-codes result=9 if "BAD" else 0 and never runs the real parser's key-aliasing (`Self-test Log` vs `logs`, `result` vs `Self Test Result`) or the `in_progress = cur not in (0,0xF)` masking that determines `passed`. (3) Corpus: confirmed no self-test-log.json exists under corpus/nvme/* (only id-ctrl.json + smart-log.json in 2.10/samsung-pm9a3, 2.11/samsung-pm9a3, 2.11/used-stock-drive); NVME_CASES in test_parsers_corpus.py replays only smart-log/id-ctrl. (4) The disparity is real and contrary to project intent: the smart-log/id-ctrl real branches ARE replay-covered (Kelvin fixup + avail_spare/percent_used aliases asserted in test_nvme_corpus_decodes), and docs/realpath-simulation.md:176-182 explicitly lists `self-test-log` among the captures the parser corpus should replay. So this is the documented drift-defense being incomplete, not intentional pragma/honesty-discipline design. Could not refute via any guard, alternate stub, or convention. Severity held at medium: it is a weak-coverage gap (a key-name/masking bug in the untested parser would silently flip the self_test_passed verdict on real hardware yet still pass the suite) rather than a presently-demonstrated wrong verdict — matching the project's medium definition ("a weak test that would pass even if code were wrong"). The suggested fix (add a passing + failing self-test-log.json capture and a parametrized replay through self_test_log(device, mock=False)) is the correct remedy.  

**M66. Empty device set folds to PASS (exit 0) in `diagnose` — zero coverage reported as success**  
`src/computetest/diagnostics.py` · diagnostics.py:126-129 (diagnose_all) consumed by cli.py:280 via _verdict_exit_any · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When backend.list_devices() returns an empty list (enumeration failure / no PCIe devices found), diagnose_all returns [] and cli._verdict_exit_any over an empty generator returns EXIT_PASS (0), so `computetest diagnose` claims PASS having tested nothing.  
- **Evidence:** diagnose_all: targets = bdfs if bdfs is not None else backend.list_devices(); return [diagnose(...) for bdf in targets]  -> [] when no devices. cli.py:121 _verdict_exit_any: 'if any(s=='fail' ...): return EXIT_FAIL; if any(s in skip...): return EXIT_UNAVAIL; return EXIT_PASS' — both any() are False over [], so EXIT_PASS. No guard for an empty result set.  
- **Fix:** In cli's diagnose path (or _verdict_exit_any), treat an empty result set as EXIT_UNAVAIL/skip (nothing measured is not a pass), and print a warning that no devices matched/were enumerated.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced end-to-end. `diagnostics.diagnose_all(backend, None, ...)` returns [] when `backend.list_devices()` is empty (diagnostics.py:128-129), and `cli._verdict_exit_any` over an empty sequence falls through both `any()` checks to `return EXIT_PASS` (cli.py:125-129). Running `cli.main([..., "diagnose", "--no-bert", "--no-margin"])` against a zero-device backend returns rc=0, prints only a blank line to stdout, and emits NO warning to stderr that nothing was tested. There is no empty-set guard in diagnostics.py, cli.py, or select_backend (which picks RealBackend whenever /sys/bus/pci/devices is a directory, even if empty). The real-hw path is genuinely reachable: RealBackend.list_devices (backend.py:268-269) is `glob.glob(f"{self._sys_root}/*")`, which silently returns [] (no exception) when sysfs is missing/misconfigured (e.g. wrong COMPUTETEST_SYSROOT, container without sysfs) or genuinely empty — so an enumeration failure folds to a silent PASS. This directly contradicts the codebase's own honesty discipline ("we never claim PASS from a measurement we couldn't/didn't make"): PcieDiagnostic.status returns "skip" for a single untested device (diagnostics.py:36-41) and tests/test_cli_commands.py:42-49,80-85 assert one untested device exits EXIT_UNAVAIL(5), not PASS. The zero-device case is the lone path that escapes that rule. No test covers it (test_diagnose_all_covers_every_device uses the populated mock board). Not refuted, not intentional, not already-handled. Severity medium is correct: it produces a misleading PASS exit code that would fool a CI gate or operator into thinking a board passed when nothing was tested, but it requires an enumeration failure / empty-or-misconfigured sysfs to trigger rather than mis-verdicting a device that was actually measured. Suggested fix stands: in the diagnose path (or _verdict_exit_any), treat an empty result set as EXIT_UNAVAIL and print a warning that no devices matched/were enumerated.  

### Low (58)

**L1. Burst-histogram test cannot catch the over-count — passes even though the model is internally inconsistent**  
`src/computetest/backend.py` · tests/test_bert_fec.py:75-83 test_gen6_burst_histogram_is_length_keyed · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The only burst-histogram test asserts keys are ints >=1 and that hist[1] dominates; it never checks that sum(hist.values()) equals pre_fec_symbol_errors, so the 1.004-share over-count (finding above) is invisible to the suite — the test would pass even if the histogram double-counted every error.  
- **Evidence:** assert all(isinstance(k, int) and k >= 1 for k in hist.keys()) ; if any(v > 0 ...): assert hist[1] >= max(...)  # no conservation/sum check  
- **Fix:** Add an assertion that sum(hist.values()) == r.pre_fec_symbol_errors (conservation of counted bursts), which would have flagged the share-sum bug.  
- **Verifier:** All factual claims are independently reproducible. The test tests/test_bert_fec.py:81-83 asserts only (a) keys are ints >=1 and (b) hist[1] dominates; it never checks sum(hist.values()) == pre_fec_symbol_errors. I ran the production code (src/computetest/backend.py:625-661 MockBackend.read_fec_stats) with the test's exact device config (injected_pre_fec_symbol_rate=1e-5): pre_fec_symbol_errors=6605 but sum(hist.values())=6630 — a +25 over-count (~1.004x), exactly matching the share-sum 0.6+0.25+0.10+0.04+0.008+0.002+0.002+0.002 = 1.004 applied as round(remaining*share) with remaining held constant at pre. Across pre in 0..4999, 4776/5000 values over-count, 0 under-count; in every over-count case hist[1] still dominates, so the test's assertions always hold. The cited test passes today, and it would also pass on a fully-doubled histogram — confirming the conservation gap. The suggested fix (assert sum(hist.values()) == r.pre_fec_symbol_errors) fails against current output, so it would indeed flag the bug.

Severity stays low (not the medium that 'weak test' might suggest): the burst_length_histogram is a mock-only synthetic surrogate, explicitly documented as 'a model — real silicon would surface a vendor burst counter,' and it is never consumed by any verdict or reported number (bert.py uses it only for explain() display and to_dict() serialization; fber_estimate derives from post/flits, not the histogram). So the over-count the test cannot catch is a cosmetic bookkeeping inconsistency in mock data with no PASS/FAIL or spec-value impact and no real-hw path. Genuine but minor test-quality gap; adding the conservation assertion is a reasonable hardening.  

**L2. A real port whose PCIe-cap walk fails is silently treated as endpoint, dropping its link from the downgrade check**  
`src/computetest/topology.py` · topology.py:171-183 (analyze_chain) with backend.read_port_type (backend.py:391-395) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=low · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** analyze_chain trusts read_port_type; if a real root/switch-downstream port's find_cap returns None, read_port_type returns PORT_ENDPOINT, so analyze_chain creates no ChainLink for that link and the per-link speed/width downgrade check never runs there — a downgrade on that link would go unflagged.  
- **Evidence:** Line 181 `if types[parent] in DOWNSTREAM_PORTS:` is the sole gate for emitting a ChainLink. backend.read_port_type returns PORT_ENDPOINT when find_cap is None (backend.py:392-394). PORT_ENDPOINT is not in DOWNSTREAM_PORTS (backend.py:88), so a misclassified downstream port yields zero links for its segment; diagnose_chain only iterates the links analyze_chain returns (diagnostics.py:256). The mock always sets port_type explicitly so tests never hit this, and it is partly an upstream (backend) robustness issue, but the consequence is a quietly missing downgrade verdict on real hardware.  
- **Fix:** Out of scope to fully fix here, but consider having analyze_chain log/annotate when a non-leaf BDF reports PORT_ENDPOINT (an implausible port type for a parent in a chain) so a cap-walk failure surfaces instead of silently producing zero links.  
- **Verifier:** The finding's factual chain is accurate and independently reproducible from the code. topology.py:181 `if types[parent] in DOWNSTREAM_PORTS:` is the sole gate that emits a ChainLink. backend.py:391-395 `read_port_type` returns PORT_ENDPOINT (0x0) when `find_cap(CAP_PCIE)` is None. PORT_ENDPOINT is not in DOWNSTREAM_PORTS=(PORT_ROOT, PORT_SWITCH_DOWNSTREAM) (backend.py:88). diagnostics.py:256 iterates only the links analyze_chain returns. So if a real root/switch-downstream port's legacy-cap walk fails, that segment yields zero ChainLinks and its per-link speed/width/LBMS-LABS downgrade verdict silently vanishes with no annotation — a quietly missing verdict, which the project's honesty discipline cares about.

Not refuted: I could not find any guard. Tests (test_topology_harness, test_chain, test_diagnostics_format) all build mocks that set port_type explicitly, so this path is never exercised. test_real_no_pcie_cap_fallbacks (test_backend_contract.py:119-126) confirms the PORT_ENDPOINT fallback is deliberate, but it only tests the backend in isolation — it does not cover the analyze_chain interaction the finding flags. Not already-handled, not intentional design (the silent drop is a side effect of the PORT_ENDPOINT fallback, not a deliberate choice).

Severity stays low (matching the finding) for two reasons: (1) the precondition is a config-space PCIe-cap-walk failure on a port the kernel already enumerated as a bridge with children in the realpath chain — unlikely on functioning hardware, though not impossible on the malfunctioning hardware this tool exists to catch; (2) blast radius is contained — the members loop (topology.py:169-176) still emits a ChainMember for that BDF regardless of type, so per-BDF AER error reporting and the BERT signal for that segment are preserved; only the speed/width downgrade verdict for that one link is lost. The suggested fix (log/annotate when a non-leaf BDF reports PORT_ENDPOINT) is reasonable and in-scope for topology.py.  

**L3. Stale 'running' heartbeat left behind when any check raises**  
`src/computetest/harness.py` · src/computetest/harness.py:67-68 and 128-129 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** store.heartbeat('idle', ...) is only reached on the success path; if any check raises (see finding above) the exception propagates past it, so the station is left advertising heartbeat status='running' forever, and ResultStore.__exit__ only calls close() with no final heartbeat.  
- **Evidence:** Start: 'if store: store.heartbeat("running", "executing test plan")'. End (only on clean return): 'if store: store.heartbeat("idle", "PASS" if report.ok else "FAIL")'. ResultStore.__exit__ in results.py is 'def __exit__(self, *exc): self.close()' — no heartbeat reset. The dashboard colors a station live/dead purely by age_s but shows status, so a crashed run reads as 'running' until age ages it out.  
- **Fix:** Wrap the body in try/finally and emit a terminal heartbeat in the finally (e.g. 'error'/'idle') so a crashed plan does not masquerade as still running; or have ResultStore.__exit__ stamp a final heartbeat.  
- **Verifier:** Code citations are all accurate. harness.py:68 sets heartbeat("running",...) at the start; the matching heartbeat("idle", "PASS"/"FAIL") at lines 128-129 sits AFTER the entire check body and BEFORE the only return, so it is reached only on a clean return. There is no try/finally. ResultStore.__exit__ (results.py:49-50) only calls close() — no terminal heartbeat. The path is genuinely reachable: ethernet.check_ethernet raises ValueError on a malformed iface name (ethernet.py:25) even in mock mode (a plausible operator config typo), and real-hw paths raise RuntimeError when nvidia-smi/ethtool are absent (gpu.py:124, ethernet.py:96); any such exception propagates past lines 128-129. The real CLI flow (cli.py:344-353: `with ResultStore(...) as store: run_test_plan(...)`) hits exactly this — the with-block exit only closes the connection, so the persisted heartbeats row stays status='running' forever. No existing test covers the crash path (test_heartbeat_upsert_and_stations only exercises the happy-path upsert), and this is not deliberate design (checks raising IS intentional per the honesty discipline, but the resulting stale heartbeat is an unhandled side effect, not a documented choice).

I downgrade from medium to low because the stated harm is overstated by the evidence. The dashboard (dashboard/app.py:116) colors the station dot live/dead purely by age_s < 15, so a crashed station correctly turns RED within 15s — it does NOT masquerade as alive. The only residual defect is that the status TEXT permanently reads 'running' instead of 'error'/'crashed', so an operator looking at an already-flagged-dead station sees a misleading last-known status. This is a genuine observability/honesty gap but: it produces no wrong test PASS/FAIL verdict, corrupts no data (records written before the crash remain accurate), and the primary live/dead signal is already correct via age. The suggested fix (try/finally emitting a terminal 'error'/'idle' heartbeat, or stamping one in __exit__) is reasonable.  

**L4. Final heartbeat detail reports 'PASS' for an all-skip (incomplete-coverage) plan**  
`src/computetest/harness.py` · src/computetest/harness.py:128-129 (heartbeat) vs TestReport.ok:42-44 · _honesty_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** report.ok is all(r.status != 'fail'), so a plan whose only non-pass records are 'skip' (e.g. BERT skipped: no AER source) writes heartbeat detail 'PASS', while the CLI exit logic (_verdict_exit_any) correctly maps the same statuses to EXIT_UNAVAIL=5 (incomplete coverage).  
- **Evidence:** harness: 'store.heartbeat("idle", "PASS" if report.ok else "FAIL")' with 'def ok(self): return all(r.status != "fail" for r in self.records)'. cli._verdict_exit_any: 'if any(s in ("skip","unavailable","incomplete")...): return EXIT_UNAVAIL'. The two disagree for an all-skip run. Impact is limited because the dashboard surfaces per-record statuses from recent(), not this detail string.  
- **Fix:** Compute the detail with the same three-way logic as _verdict_exit_any: 'PASS' only if no fail and no skip, 'INCOMPLETE' if any skip, else 'FAIL', so the station label cannot claim PASS on uncovered tests.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and reproduced. TestReport.ok = all(r.status != "fail") (harness.py:43-44), so an all-skip plan (e.g. BERT skipped: no AER source) yields ok==True, making harness.py:129 write heartbeat detail "PASS". The same statuses through cli._verdict_exit_any (cli.py:121-129) return EXIT_UNAVAIL=5 (incomplete coverage). I reproduced this directly: a single skip record gives ok=True, detail="PASS", summary "[PASS]", yet cli exit code 5. The two disagree, which is an honesty-discipline label mismatch (a verdict claiming PASS over uncovered tests). The divergence is actually slightly broader than the finding states: TestReport.summary() (harness.py:57) also prints the final "[PASS]" for an all-skip run, and that string is emitted to the operator's stdout at cli.py:349 — so a human sees exit-code 5 alongside a "[PASS]" summary. Severity stays LOW, correctly: (1) the authoritative machine verdict (exit code) is correct at 5; (2) the same summary text shows every record as "[SKIP]" plus "=> 0 pass, 0 fail, N skip", which is self-evidently not a real pass; (3) the heartbeat detail string is NOT rendered by the dashboard — app.py:116 displays x.status ("idle"/"running"), not x.detail (it only appears in the /api/summary JSON, not the UI). No real-hardware PASS/FAIL verdict is corrupted. The suggested three-way fix (PASS only if no fail and no skip) is reasonable and would align the label with the exit code and honesty discipline. Note the existing test test_report_ok_when_no_fail (test_results_report.py:23-26) explicitly asserts ok is True and "[PASS]" for a pass+skip mix, so this behavior is currently codified as intended-but-inconsistent; a fix would need that test updated.  

**L5. Docstring inverts the JSON/YAML relationship and the fallback gives a cryptic error without PyYAML**  
`src/computetest/fixtures.py` · fixtures.py:159-174 load_fixture_map (and docstring lines 162-164) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The claim 'YAML 1.2 is a strict JSON superset, so plain-JSON YAML files load via the JSON path' is inverted (JSON is a subset of YAML, not vice versa). The shipped configs/fixture_map.example.yaml is NOT valid JSON (comments, unquoted scalars), so on a station that installed computetest without the [yaml]/[dev] extra, load_fixture_map falls into `except ImportError: data = json.loads(text)` and raises a confusing json.JSONDecodeError instead of a clear 'install pyyaml'.  
- **Evidence:** try: import yaml; data = yaml.safe_load(text) except ImportError: data = json.loads(text). PyYAML is only in optional 'yaml' and 'dev' extras (pyproject dependencies = []), so a bare install has no yaml; json.loads on the example YAML fails with a parser error pointing at e.g. the leading comment line, not at the missing package.  
- **Fix:** In the except ImportError branch, attempt json.loads and on json.JSONDecodeError re-raise as InstrumentError('reading <path> requires PyYAML; install computetest[yaml]') so the field message is actionable; fix the docstring to say JSON is a subset of YAML.  
- **Verifier:** The core runtime defect is real and reproducible. pyproject.toml has `dependencies = []` with pyyaml only in the optional `yaml`/`dev` extras (lines 14, 18), and the shipped configs/fixture_map.example.yaml is not valid JSON (it has comments, unquoted scalars, and block sequences). On a bare `pip install computetest`, load_fixture_map (fixtures.py:167-172) hits `except ImportError: data = json.loads(text)`; I reproduced the result: `json.JSONDecodeError: Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)` — a cryptic message pointing at the leading comment, not "install pyyaml". The existing test test_example_yaml_loads_and_validates (test_instruments_hal.py:246) runs under the dev env where pyyaml IS present, so it always takes the yaml path and never exercises this fallback — nothing refutes it. The codebase's own sibling function _load_plan (cli.py:361-366) handles the identical case CORRECTLY by raising RuntimeError("PyYAML not installed; use a .json config or pip install pyyaml"), proving the actionable-error pattern is the intended house style and that load_fixture_map's silent fallthrough is a genuine inconsistency, not intentional design. The suggested fix (catch JSONDecodeError in the except-ImportError branch and re-raise an actionable error) is valid. CAVEAT: the finding's secondary claim that the docstring is "inverted" is factually WRONG — "YAML 1.2 is a strict JSON superset" is the correct, standard phrasing and is equivalent to "JSON is a subset of YAML"; that half of the suggested fix should be ignored. Severity stays low: the impact is a confusing error message on an off-happy-path install, not a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict or data corruption, and the error still surfaces (operator can recover).  

**L6. Link-down keyword filter is case-sensitive but severity classifier is case-insensitive — most link-down wordings never reach classification (potential wrong PASS)**  
`src/computetest/dmesg.py` · src/computetest/dmesg.py:22, parse_events line 58 vs _severity lines 40/47 · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** parse_events keeps a line only if a _KEYWORDS substring matches case-sensitively (`any(k in line for k in _KEYWORDS)`), but _severity lowercases before testing link-down. Any link-down wording whose casing is not verbatim in the tuple (e.g. 'Link is Down', 'LINK DOWN', 'PCIe link down') is dropped by the filter, so _severity's 'link' branch is effectively unreachable for those, and the chain diagnostic (which fails on severity=='link') will not fail.  
- **Evidence:** _KEYWORDS = ("AER", "PCIe Bus Error", "link is down", "Link Down", "link down") ; line 58: `if not any(k in line for k in _KEYWORDS): continue` (case-sensitive) ; line 40: `low = line.lower()` then line 47: `if "link is down" in low or "link down" in low: return "link"`. Verified: 'something: Link is Down' and 'PCIE LINK DOWN' are dropped by the filter (kept=False) even though _severity would label them 'link'. diagnostics.bad_kernel_events includes `e.severity == "link"`, so a missed link-down line = a chain that passes when it should fail.  
- **Fix:** Make the keyword filter case-insensitive and use a single source of truth: e.g. `low = line.lower(); if not any(k in low for k in _KEYWORDS_LOWER): continue` with the tuple stored lowercased, and have _severity test the same set. This collapses the three link-down spellings into one robust check and removes the filter/classifier casing mismatch.  
- **Verifier:** The mechanical defect is real and reproducible. In src/computetest/dmesg.py, parse_events (line 58) gates lines case-SENSITIVELY: `if not any(k in line for k in _KEYWORDS): continue`, while _severity lowercases (line 40) before testing the link branch (line 47). I empirically confirmed that `something: Link is Down` and `PCIE LINK DOWN` are dropped by the filter (kept=False) yet _severity would label them "link". diagnostics.py:167 keys bad_kernel_events on `e.severity == "link"`, so a dropped link-down line would not fail the chain. So the filter/classifier casing inconsistency exists, and _severity's link branch is partly unreachable for non-canonical casings.

However, the finding overstates impact ("most link-down wordings never reach classification" / high severity). The wordings the actual Linux kernel emits for PCIe link events DO match the verbatim-cased keywords: the pciehp hotplug driver logs `... Link Down` and other drivers log `link is down` — I verified both parse correctly to "link" (parse_events returns ['link']). The cited "broken" strings (`Link is Down`, `LINK DOWN`, `PCIe link down`) are not wordings mainline Linux actually emits for PCIe link-down; the module's docstring, tests (test_dmesg.py, test_dmesg_edges.py), and mock are all built around real pcieport/nvme/AER lines that match. _severity's lowercasing is defensive belt-and-suspenders, not a contract the filter must mirror for any real kernel string. So with real dmesg output, no real link-down line is dropped and there is no demonstrable wrong-PASS on hardware today.

Net: a genuine but low-impact robustness/consistency bug (two paths disagree on casing; brittle to future/vendor wording changes), not a high-severity field-impacting wrong verdict. The suggested fix (single lowercased source of truth shared by filter and classifier) is reasonable hardening. Downgraded high -> low.  

**L7. Cable-test 'pair' field type differs between mock (int) and real (str), so to_dict consumers see inconsistent types**  
`src/computetest/ethernet.py` · ethernet.py:136 (_mock) vs 150 (_real_cable_test) · _api-consistency_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Mock emits faults[].pair as int 0; the real path emits faults[].pair as a string (regex group, e.g. 'a'/'b'). A downstream consumer of to_dict()/JSON that keys or compares on pair will behave differently against mock vs real hardware.  
- **Evidence:** _mock_cable_test: {"pair": 0, ...} (int) ; _real_cable_test: {"pair": m.group(1), ...} (str). Verified mock pair type == int.  
- **Fix:** Normalize to one type (e.g. always the pair letter as str, or always an index int) in both paths so serialized results are comparable.  
- **Verifier:** Factually accurate. At /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/ethernet.py:136 the mock emits faults[].pair as int 0 ({"pair": 0, ...}); at line 150 the real path emits faults[].pair as a str (regex group m.group(1) from r"pair (\w)..."). The two code paths therefore serialize pair as different JSON types. This is NOT intentional/mock-fidelity-by-design: the sibling TSN cable-test code uses a STRING pair letter in its own mock (src/computetest/tsn/phy.py:182 -> faults=[{"pair": "A", ...}]) and tsn/cable.py documents that ethernet.py and the TSN path return "the same status/faults/distance shape" — so the string-letter form is the intended convention and ethernet.py's mock int 0 is the outlier, a real mock-fidelity gap. Impact is genuinely low, exactly as the finding self-rates: the pair field is never consumed by any internal logic (summary() reads only faults[0]['code'] and ['distance_m'] at line 52; CLI and all check/verdict logic ignore pair), so there is no wrong PASS/FAIL verdict and no data corruption. The only effect is on a hypothetical external to_dict()/JSON consumer that keys/compares on pair across mock vs real. Real defect, but cosmetic/API-consistency only; severity stays low.  

**L8. gpu_temp_c fallback uses truthiness: a real 0C GPU reading is silently discarded**  
`src/computetest/tegra.py` · tegra.py:160 (_build_health), gpu_temp = thermal.get("GPU") or thermal.get("tj") or max_zone · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When the GPU zone legitimately reads 0.0C (cold/idle), the `or` chain treats 0.0 as falsy and falls through to tj or max_zone, so metrics['gpu_temp_c'] reports a different zone's temperature than the actual GPU.  
- **Evidence:** Line 160: `gpu_temp = thermal.get("GPU") or thermal.get("tj") or max_zone`. Reproduced: check_tegra(_lines=['GPU@0C tj@47C CPU@50C']) -> metrics['gpu_temp_c'] == 47.0, i.e. the real GPU value of 0.0C is dropped and tj is reported instead.  
- **Fix:** Distinguish 'absent' from 'zero' explicitly, e.g. `gpu_temp = thermal['GPU'] if 'GPU' in thermal else thermal.get('tj', max_zone)` (or build a meaningful-only lookup and use `... if 'GPU' in meaningful else ...`).  
- **Verifier:** Mechanically confirmed at tegra.py:160. `gpu_temp = thermal.get("GPU") or thermal.get("tj") or max_zone` uses Python truthiness, so a GPU zone reading of exactly 0.0C is falsy and falls through. Reproduced: check_tegra(_lines=['GPU@0C tj@47C CPU@50C']) -> metrics['gpu_temp_c'] == 47.0 (reports tj, not the real 0.0C GPU). I verified the trigger is exactly 0.0C only: GPU@-5C correctly yields -5.0 and GPU@0.1C yields 0.1 (negatives/other values are truthy and pass through). The existing tests test_gpu_temp_falls_back_to_tj / _to_max_zone only cover the GPU-absent case, never a 0.0C GPU, so this edge is untested. The finding's claim about what the code does is accurate.

I downgrade severity from medium to low, however, because real-world impact is minimal: (1) gpu_temp_c is a REPORTED metric only — grep confirms it feeds only summary() (line 78) and metrics (line 170) and NEVER any pass/fail check. The thermal gate uses max_zone, computed correctly from the `meaningful` dict (lines 155-159), which is unaffected by this bug. So there is no wrong PASS/FAIL verdict and no qualification-gating data corruption (per the rubric, those would be critical/high). (2) The trigger is an implausibly narrow edge — an on-die Jetson iGPU sharing LPDDR on a powered board reading exactly 0.0C is physically unrealistic; the module's own offline threshold is <= -40C. The result is a cosmetically wrong displayed/reported number at a near-impossible input, not a wrong verdict. The suggested fix (`thermal["GPU"] if "GPU" in thermal else thermal.get("tj", max_zone)`) is correct and worth applying for robustness, but this is low severity.  

**L9. _collect_tegrastats readline() has no read timeout; a stalled tegrastats hangs the collector**  
`src/computetest/tegra.py` · tegra.py:228-247 (_collect_tegrastats) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** proc.stdout.readline() blocks indefinitely with no per-read or overall deadline; if tegrastats stalls or never emits a line (driver issue, wrong JetPack arg), the collection loop blocks forever on the DUT with no timeout escape (the wait(timeout=2) only applies during teardown, which is never reached).  
- **Evidence:** Lines 236-240: `for _ in range(...): line = proc.stdout.readline(); if not line: break; lines.append(...)`. There is no timeout around readline; teardown's wait(timeout=2) at line 244 is only reached after the read loop completes.  
- **Fix:** Bound total collection with a wall-clock deadline (e.g. samples*interval_ms plus margin) and break out / terminate if exceeded, or use select/poll on proc.stdout with a timeout per readline.  
- **Verifier:** Verified against tegra.py:228-247. The claim is accurate. `proc.stdout.readline()` (line 237) has no timeout and is wrapped in a `try` whose `finally` teardown (`proc.terminate()` + `proc.wait(timeout=2)`, lines 242-244) only runs after the `try` body exits. A blocking `readline()` neither completes nor raises, so the `finally` is never reached during a stall — the `timeout=2` governs only teardown latency after a successful read loop, exactly as the finding states. I confirmed there is no escape elsewhere: the CLI (cli.py:292) calls check_tegra with no wrapping deadline, and tegra.py has no signal.alarm/select/poll. If tegrastats starts but never emits a newline (driver stall, wedged tool, wrong --interval), the real-hw collector blocks forever on the DUT. This is not refuted by the pragma convention: the project explicitly treats logic/robustness bugs inside `# pragma: no cover` real-hw paths as high-value, and the codebase itself defends against this exact class of hang elsewhere — bert.py:319 uses `subprocess.run(timeout=seconds+30)` with a TimeoutExpired->RuntimeError handler, and ethernet.py:97-99 sets `timeout=10` with the comment "a wedged/flaky PHY can wedge ethtool indefinitely." So bounding subprocess I/O against a hung DUT tool is an established convention this collector violates. The mock and _lines paths are unaffected, so there is no false-PASS or data-corruption risk; the failure mode is a wedged collector that never returns. That makes it a liveness/robustness defect, not a correctness one (no wrong number, no misparse, no wrong verdict, no crash), so the self-assigned `low` severity is correct — above a pure nit because it can hang a real test run with no escape, but below high/critical per the impact rubric. Suggested fix (wall-clock deadline of ~samples*interval_ms + margin, or select/poll per read) is sound and matches the codebase's own pattern.  

**L10. error_count_limit parameter is accepted and CLI-wired but silently ignored on the native path**  
`src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py` · src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py:133 (from_margin_result signature); cli.py:329 · _api-consistency_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** from_margin_result declares error_count_limit and the CLI passes args.error_count_limit into it, but the function body never references the parameter, so `computetest lmt --error-count-limit N` (native path) has zero effect on the output — an operator-visible no-op.  
- **Evidence:** Signature: `error_count_limit: int = DEFAULT_ERROR_COUNT_LIMIT,` (line 133); grep shows error_count_limit is never used in the body (only referenced again at line 238, inside call_pci_lmt). CLI: `records = lmt_adapter.from_margin_result(result, backend=backend, receiver_number=args.receiver_number, error_count_limit=args.error_count_limit)` (cli.py:327-329).  
- **Fix:** Either drop the parameter from from_margin_result (and the CLI call) so the no-op flag isn't implied, or actually use it — e.g. record it for provenance, or stop emitting error_count=0 / ber=0.0 unconditionally and gate verdicts on the limit. At minimum document that it is accepted only for signature symmetry.  
- **Verifier:** Mechanics confirmed. `from_margin_result` (src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py:133) declares `error_count_limit: int = DEFAULT_ERROR_COUNT_LIMIT` but its body (lines 144-191) never references it; grep confirms the only lmt_adapter.py uses are the docstring at line 111 (a different function), this signature, and `call_pci_lmt`'s own signature/use (lines 225, 238). The CLI defines `--error-count-limit` with help text "OCP pci_lmt -e: stop a lane at this many errors (default 63)" (cli.py:212-214) and passes `args.error_count_limit` into the native call at cli.py:329. So on the native margining path the flag is accepted and wired but has zero effect — an operator-visible no-op. It is honored only on the `--via-pci-lmt` path (cli.py:321 -> call_pci_lmt -e). The native `_emit_ocp(args, "lmt", records)` (cli.py:338) doesn't capture it either, so it isn't even recorded for provenance. No existing guard, test, or comment documents/justifies the asymmetry; tests only exercise the limit on the subprocess path (test_lmt_adapter.py:172, 260).

NOT intentional per conventions: nothing fakes a pass or hides a skip — but nothing explains the dead native parameter either. Severity downgraded from medium to low: this is API/UX hygiene, not a correctness defect. The native path always emits error_count=0/ber=0.0, which is correct by the documented "last passing margin" semantics (no step-by-step sweep exists yet, per the module docstring lines 26-29), so there is genuinely nothing for a limit to gate against today — no wrong verdict, no data corruption, no spec/units error, no crash. The flag only becomes meaningful once step sweeps land. Suggested fix stands: drop the param from the native call, or document it as accepted only for signature symmetry / capture it for provenance.  

**L11. run_log()/step_log() do not validate severity against the OCP log enum; a bad caller value silently produces a schema-invalid artifact**  
`src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py` · src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py:125-128 (run_log), 230-234 (step_log) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** severity is emitted as severity.upper() with no validation, but the OCP log schema restricts it to {INFO,DEBUG,WARNING,ERROR,FATAL}. A caller passing 'warn', 'critical', or 'trace' emits an invalid enum that breaks downstream OCP consumers, and the schema-conformance test would not catch it because it only exercises the internal callers (which all use valid values).  
- **Evidence:** def run_log(self, severity, message): return self._write({... "log": {"severity": severity.upper(), "message": message}}). log.json: severity enum is [INFO, DEBUG, WARNING, ERROR, FATAL]. validator() by contrast guards its value via _scalar; severity gets no equivalent guard.  
- **Fix:** Validate severity.upper() against a module-level _LOG_SEVERITIES set and raise ValueError on anything outside it (mirroring validator()'s scalar guard), or map common aliases (warn->WARNING, critical/crit->FATAL).  
- **Verifier:** Factually accurate. /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py:127 (run_log) and :234 (step_log) emit `severity.upper()` with no enum validation, while the vendored schema /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/io/ocpdiag_schemas/log.json restricts severity to {INFO,DEBUG,WARNING,ERROR,FATAL} with `additionalProperties:false`, so an out-of-enum value would produce a schema-invalid artifact. The schema-conformance test (tests/test_ocpdiag.py:563-588) only calls run_log("INFO", "smoke"), so it would not catch a bad value. The asymmetry the finding cites is real: validator()/measurement()/series_element() all guard their value via _scalar and raise, but severity gets no equivalent guard.

However, the impact is correctly rated LOW and is latent, not active. I checked every caller in the entire repo (grep across src/ and tests/): all are internal to ocpdiag.py and pass hardcoded valid uppercase literals ("WARNING","ERROR","INFO"), plus the single test caller uses "INFO". No code path today can emit an invalid severity. Unlike the verdict mappings (_RUN_RESULT/_STEP_STATUS/_DIAGNOSIS_TYPE) which use .get(...,default) and safely coerce unknown statuses to NOT_APPLICABLE/SKIP/UNKNOWN, severity is a free-form log annotation, not a verdict — so this does NOT fake a PASS, hide a skip, or mislabel a verdict (the honesty-discipline concern is not triggered). The Emitter class is technically public, so a hypothetical future external caller could trip it and have a strict downstream OCP consumer reject the log line, which is the genuine (minor) gap. Not refuted, not already-handled, not intentional: a real defensive-coding gap with low field impact.  

**L12. lane_status() reports error_count=0 always; docstring claims an error read that never happens**  
`src/computetest/pcie/retimer/base.py` · base.py:225-238 (Retimer.lane_status) · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The base lane_status() advertises "combining eye + a 0-duration error read" and populates LaneStatus.error_count, but it performs no error read and hardcodes error_count=0, so every lane on every backend (including a future real vendor subclass that does not override the method) reports zero link errors regardless of actual link health.  
- **Evidence:** Docstring: "Per-lane health summary combining eye + a 0-duration error read." Body: `return LaneStatus( lane=lane, linked=eye.eye_ui > 0 and eye.eye_mv > 0, error_count=0, eye_pass=eye_ok, ...)` — there is no call to run_prbs_bist or any error counter; error_count is the literal 0. LaneStatus is exported in __init__.py ("LaneStatus" in __all__), so consumers may trust .error_count.  
- **Fix:** Either (a) make lane_status actually source an error count (e.g. an abstract read_error_count(lane) the subclass implements, or a documented short BIST window), and have it raise/return None where unknown rather than 0; or (b) drop error_count from LaneStatus / mark it Optional[int]=None and correct the docstring so it no longer claims an error read. Do not let a structurally-zero value masquerade as a measured error count on real hardware.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced from the code. In src/computetest/pcie/retimer/base.py:225-238, Retimer.lane_status() hardcodes error_count=0 (literal, line 233) and performs no error read, yet its docstring (line 226) claims it combines "eye + a 0-duration error read." No subclass overrides lane_status (grep confirms it is defined once in the ABC; neither MockRetimer nor AriesRetimer overrides it), so every backend — including any future real vendor subclass — reports error_count=0 regardless of link health. LaneStatus declares error_count as a non-Optional int, docstrings it as "error count," and is exported in __all__, so external consumers can trust a structurally-fake zero. This also contradicts the class's own stated invariant (line 173-174: "never silently return zeros"), and the docstring's "0-duration error read" is self-contradictory since run_prbs_bist raises RetimerError for duration_s <= 0. The finding is factually correct on all counts and is not covered by tests (test_retimer.py:162-182 only assert eye_pass/note, never error_count) — so it is a real honesty/API-shape defect, not intentional design or an untested-pragma path. I downgrade medium->low because the blast radius is limited: no current src consumer reads LaneStatus.error_count, and the actual lane verdict (eye_pass) is computed correctly and independently of error_count, so there is no current wrong PASS/FAIL or data corruption on real hardware (which medium/high/critical would require). The fix is sound: either source a real count (raise/None where unknown) or make error_count Optional[int]=None and correct the docstring.  

**L13. lane_status().linked derived from eye opening > 0, not from real link/training state**  
`src/computetest/pcie/retimer/base.py` · base.py:232 (linked=eye.eye_ui > 0 and eye.eye_mv > 0) · _correctness_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** "linked" is inferred solely from the eye opening being nonzero rather than from actual link-training/lock state, so a lane that returns a small but nonzero eye while not trained would be reported as linked=True; conversely the base contract has no real link-state source.  
- **Evidence:** `linked=eye.eye_ui > 0 and eye.eye_mv > 0` — there is no read of train/lock status; the only inputs are the eye floats from read_eye. The ABC docstring promises "Lane indices ... bounded" and that readers raise on "link not trained", but lane_status itself does not consult link state.  
- **Fix:** Define linked from an explicit link-state signal (a read_link_status or the eye verdict semantics documented as a proxy). At minimum document that 'linked' here means 'eye opening is nonzero', not 'PCIe LTSSM trained', so a field operator does not read it as link-up.  
- **Verifier:** Verified at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/pcie/retimer/base.py:232. The finding is factually accurate: `linked=eye.eye_ui > 0 and eye.eye_mv > 0` derives "linked" solely from the eye floats returned by read_eye, with no consultation of LTSSM/training/lock state. The Retimer ABC (lines 164-242) genuinely exposes no link-state source — only read_eye/read_eq/read_temperature/set_loopback/run_prbs_bist; there is no read_link_status. BISTResult has a `locked` flag but lane_status never calls BIST. So a lane returning a small-but-nonzero eye while untrained would be reported linked=True. The concern is reinforced (not refuted) by the LaneStatus docstring at line 129, which explicitly labels this field "link state" — i.e. the code claims a semantic it cannot back from the available inputs, touching the project's honesty discipline.

However, severity stays LOW (matching the finding): (1) the actual gating verdict, eye_pass, is computed correctly via eye_quality_verdict against real thresholds and is unaffected; (2) the `linked` field is currently DEAD — grep shows it is set in exactly one place and never read by any downstream code or asserted by any test (tests only check eye_pass and note). So no real PASS/FAIL verdict is driven by `linked` today; the risk is purely a field operator misreading the value as PCIe link-up. Not critical/high (no wrong gating verdict, no data corruption, no real-hw crash/misparse). Suggested fix is reasonable: either document `linked` as "eye-opening nonzero, not LTSSM-trained" or source it from an explicit link-state read. (Aside: the same method hardcodes error_count=0 while its docstring calls it "a 0-duration error read" — a similar but out-of-scope mislabel.)

`src/computetest/pcie/retimer/base.py` · tests/test_retimer.py:162-182 (TestMockRetimerLaneStatus / TestEyeThresholds) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed

In tests/test_retimer.py the LaneStatus tests are: test_lane_status_pass_with_good_eye (lines 163-167, asserts only s.eye_pass and s.note == "") and test_lane_status_fail_with_bad_eye (lines 169-173, asserts only not s.eye_pass and "below" in s.note), plus TestEyeThresholds (lines 177-182, asserts eye_pass only). Not refuted by any guard or test; not an intentional honesty-discipline construct (error_count=0 silently returns 0 rather than raising). Relevant files: /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/tests/test_retimer.py and /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/pcie/retimer/base.py.  

**L15. Wire-up docstring references a _read_register method and COMET_I2C_DEVICE env var that do not exist in the code**  
`src/computetest/pcie/retimer/aries.py` · aries.py:17-21 (module docstring) vs aries.py:87 (_open_sdk) · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed

Replace the ``_read_register`` body with the SDK call.' — but grep across src shows COMET_I2C_DEVICE appears only in the docstring (never os.environ-read) and there is no def _read_register; the only SDK seam is `def _open_sdk(self)` at line 87, whose own docstring instead names `ariesSDK.PT5161L(comet=..., addr=self.address); aries.init()`.  
- **Fix:** Make the docstring match the code: change step 3 to 'Replace the _open_sdk() body with ariesSDK.PT5161L(comet=..., addr=self.address).init() and implement the per-method register reads', and either consume COMET_I2C_DEVICE inside _open_sdk (e.g. pass it as the comet transport path) or drop the env-var instruction.

The finding's factual claims hold: (1) grep across all .py files shows `_read_register` appears ONLY in the module docstring (line 18); there is no `def _read_register` — the actual SDK seam is `_open_sdk()` at line 87. (2) `COMET_I2C_DEVICE` appears ONLY in the docstring (line 17) and is never read; the module imports only `importlib.util` and `typing.Any` (no `os` import), so it is impossible for any env var to be consumed. The `transport`/`self.transport` field exists but never feeds a device-path lookup. So the "To enable on a real station" steps 2 and 3 point an engineer at an env var that is ignored and a method that does not exist — documentation drift relative to the code seam.

Not refuted: the inaccuracy is reproducible from the code. Not already-handled: no guard or test makes the docstring correct. Not intentional under the honesty-discipline convention: that convention concerns refusing to fake a PASS/hide a skip, which this docstring does not touch.

Severity correctly stays low (no downgrade needed below low, no upgrade warranted): this is an NDA-gated, fully stubbed boundary where every telemetry method raises NotImplementedError, so it can never produce a wrong PASS/FAIL or corrupt data on real hardware. Mitigating it further: `_open_sdk()` carries its OWN accurate inline instruction at line 104-106 ("Replace with ariesSDK.PT5161L(comet=..., addr=...).init()") naming the correct method, so an engineer wiring up the real path lands on correct code-level guidance at the exact seam even if the top-of-file docstring is stale. The suggested fix (align step 3 to `_open_sdk()` and either consume or drop COMET_I2C_DEVICE) is appropriate.  

**L16. self-test percent default of 100 can report 'complete' when the completion key is absent/renamed**  
`src/computetest/nvme/health.py` · health.py:176 (_real_self_test_log) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** raw.get("completion", 100) defaults the reported percent to 100 (i.e. 'done') when nvme-cli omits or renames the key. Real nvme-cli JSON has historically used 'current_completion' rather than 'completion', so on those versions this would always report 100% while in_progress is still derived correctly from current_operation — the percent shown to an operator is misleading though the pass/fail verdict is unaffected.  
- **Evidence:** return {"percent": raw.get("completion", 100), ...}; the cur key is read as raw.get("current_operation", 0) but the percent key is 'completion'. No corpus capture covers self-test-log JSON, so the real key name is unverified.  
- **Fix:** Default the percent to 0 (not 100) when unknown, and read both key spellings, e.g. raw.get("completion", raw.get("current_completion", 0)); add a self-test-log corpus capture to pin the real key names.  
- **Verifier:** Verified at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/health.py:176 (_real_self_test_log). The code is exactly `return {"percent": raw.get("completion", 100), "in_progress": in_progress, ...}` — so when nvme-cli omits/renames the `completion` key the reported percent defaults to 100 ("done"). The evidence is independently corroborated:

1. Corpus gap is real: /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/corpus/nvme only contains id-ctrl.json and smart-log.json (versions 2.10/2.11) — there is NO self-test-log capture anywhere in the repo, and no doc/string pins the key names. The real key spelling is genuinely unverified in-tree.

2. Key-name mismatch is plausible/likely: real nvme-cli `self-test-log -o json` emits the completion field as "Current Device Self-Test Completion", not the bare `completion` the code reads (and likewise "Current Device Self-Test Operation" vs the code's `current_operation`). The per-entry array key `Self-test Log` and `Self Test Result` the code reads ARE correct, so result decodes, but percent falls to its default. Under any such mismatch percent silently reports 100.

3. No guard/test catches it: the mock branch (health.py:164) returns a literal `{"percent": 100, ...}` and never exercises the real `.get` default; test_nvme.py / test_nvme_format.py only assert the mock shape. So this is not already-handled.

4. Not intentional per conventions: honesty discipline argues AGAINST defaulting an unknown completion percentage to "100% complete" — the sensible unknown default is 0 (or None), as the finding's suggested fix proposes.

Severity stays low: the percent field is display-only — `passed` is driven solely by `in_progress` and `result`, so this specific default cannot by itself flip a PASS/FAIL verdict, matching the finding's own scoping.
