(Worth noting for the broader audit, though out of scope for this finding: the same wrong-key root cause on `current_operation` at health.py:172 would default `in_progress` to False and could yield a false PASS — a more serious, separate issue. But as scoped, this finding is a genuine, correctly-rated low-severity real-hw display defect.)  

**L17. Test for non-dict data-area body is tautological — input value is already 0**  
`src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py` · tests/test_nvme_ocp_telemetry.py:51-55 (test_non_dict_data_area_body_treated_as_zero_blocks) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The test feeds {"3": 0} and asserts data_areas == {"3": 0}, which passes whether the code reads the int value or forces it to 0; it does not actually verify the 'treated as zero' behaviour it names, so the int-coercion branch could silently drop a non-zero value without the test failing.  
- **Evidence:** r = ocp._parse_telemetry("/dev/nvme0", {"logId": 7, "dataAreas": {"3": 0}})\n        assert r.data_areas == {"3": 0}  
- **Fix:** Use a non-zero non-dict body to disambiguate, e.g. feed {"3": 20} and assert the intended semantics (either {"3": 0} if dropping is intentional, or {"3": 20} if the value should be kept). The current input cannot distinguish the two implementations.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed as a real test-quality weakness. At tests/test_nvme_ocp_telemetry.py:51-55 the test feeds {"3": 0} and asserts data_areas == {"3": 0}. The production parser (src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py:134) does `blocks = body.get("sizeBlocks", 0) if isinstance(body, dict) else 0`, forcing non-dict bodies to 0. I reproduced that for body=0 this is indistinguishable from a hypothetical alternate that reads the int directly (int(0) == 0); only a non-zero non-dict body (e.g. 20) would distinguish "coerced to 0" from "value kept," and there is no such test (grep shows the only non-dict-body test is this one). So the test name "treated_as_zero_blocks" is not actually exercised — the int-coercion branch could be changed to keep the value and this test would still pass. The finding's claim is factually accurate and not refuted by any other test, guard, or corpus case (the synthetic corpus uses dict bodies). It is not "intentional" per conventions: the test plainly intends to verify coercion but fails to. The suggested fix (use {"3": 20}) is correct. I keep severity at low rather than the rubric's "weak test = medium" because the production code is actually correct, the affected values feed informational ocp-diag measurements rather than any pass/fail verdict, and the documented real-world case (nvme-cli returning plain ints for empty areas) genuinely involves zero/empty areas — so the false-assurance gap carries minimal real-hardware impact.  

**L18. Spurious 0xF0-0xFE reserved EID band misclassifies valid MCTP endpoint IDs**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:49-55 (is_reserved / is_routable), comment lines 26-31 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The code treats EIDs 0xF0-0xFE as Reserved and caps the routable range at 0xEF, but per MCTP (DSP0236 §8.2 / DSP0239) the assignable endpoint-ID space is 0x08-0xFE with only 0xFF (broadcast) reserved at the top — there is no 0xF0-0xFE reserved band, so a legitimately-assigned EID such as 0xF5 is wrongly reported is_reserved=True and is_routable=False.  
- **Evidence:** is_reserved returns `(1 <= self.value <= 7) or (0xF0 <= self.value <= 0xFE)` and is_routable returns `0x08 <= self.value <= 0xEF`; the comment block declares `0xF0-0xFE — Reserved` and `0x08-0xEF — Routable endpoint addresses`. MCTP defines 0x01-0x07 reserved, 0x08-0xFF valid, 0xFF=broadcast — nothing reserved between 0xEF and 0xFE.  
- **Fix:** Make is_routable cover 0x08-0xFE and drop the 0xF0-0xFE clause from is_reserved (keep only 0x01-0x07). Update the docstring allocation block to `0x08-0xFE — Routable; 0xFF — Broadcast`.  
- **Verifier:** Spec-conformance defect confirmed. Per MCTP DSP0236 Table 2 (Special endpoint IDs) — corroborated by DMTF docs, the Linux kernel MCTP docs, and the Intel/NVIDIA/openbmc libmctp implementations — the 8-bit EID space is: 0x00 Null, 0x01-0x07 Reserved, 0x08-0xFE assignable to endpoints, 0xFF Broadcast. There is NO reserved 0xF0-0xFE band. The code at src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:50-55 wrongly classifies 0xF0-0xFE as reserved (is_reserved) and caps routability at 0xEF (is_routable), so a legitimately-assigned EID like 0xF5 is reported is_reserved=True / is_routable=False. The docstring at lines 29-30 states the wrong allocation, and the test at tests/test_nvme_mi.py:58-59 actively asserts 0xF0/0xFE are reserved — locking in the wrong behavior rather than guarding against it (a weak test, not a real guard). Not intentional under any project convention (no spec-mask=None honesty pattern, not a real-hw pragma path).\n\nDowngraded from medium to low: grep shows is_reserved/is_routable have ZERO consumers in src/ outside their own definitions and the unit test, so they currently feed no PASS/FAIL verdict and no real-hardware routing/assignment decision. The blast radius is limited to incorrect documentation, a wrong-locking test, and any future caller that trusts these properties. Suggested fix is correct: is_routable should cover 0x08-0xFE, drop the 0xF0-0xFE clause from is_reserved (keep only 0x01-0x07), and fix the docstring to '0x08-0xFE Routable; 0xFF Broadcast'. (Minor nit: the finding's evidence prose mislabels 0xFF as 'valid' assignable; 0xFF is broadcast-only and 0x08-0xFE is the assignable range — but the substantive claim is correct.)  

**L19. Docstring claims a fragmentation/reassembly + header-framing layer the module does not implement**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:4-13 (module docstring) vs whole module · _honesty_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The docstring states the module 'models the framing layer (encode + decode of headers, EIDs ...)' and references 'an optional fragmentation+reassembly layer', but there is no header serialization/deserialization, no SOM/EOM/packet-sequence/TO-tag handling, and no reassembly anywhere — MctpMessage is a plain dataclass and MockMctpTransport just shuttles message objects through a Python list. message_tag and integrity_check are stored but never serialized or validated.  
- **Evidence:** MockMctpTransport.send appends to self.sent and recv pops self._responses.pop(0); no bytes are ever produced/parsed. __len__ returns `len(self.body) + 1  # +1 for the type byte` yet no type byte is ever emitted.  
- **Fix:** Soften the docstring to say the module defines the post-reassembly message model + transport ABC + an in-memory mock, and that per-packet header framing / fragmentation+reassembly are the concrete binding subclass's responsibility (real-hw, out of scope here).  
- **Verifier:** Verified against /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py. The module docstring (lines 10-11) states "This module models the framing layer (encode + decode of headers, EIDs, control vs message-type discrimination)" and line 6 references "an optional fragmentation+reassembly layer." A grep for to_bytes/from_bytes/encode/decode/SOM/EOM/sequence/reassembly/fragment finds those terms ONLY inside docstrings/comments — there is zero byte serialization or header (de)parsing in the module. MctpEndpointId only range/role-validates an int; MctpMessageType is an enum; MctpMessage is a plain dataclass; MockMctpTransport.send appends MctpMessage OBJECTS to a list and recv pops them (lines 156-166) — no bytes are ever produced or parsed. message_tag is range-checked (line 88) but never serialized, and integrity_check is stored but never read/validated/emitted anywhere (grep confirms only its declaration). __len__ (line 92) returns len(body)+1 "for the type byte" yet no type byte is ever emitted. The only real encode/decode in the NVMe-MI stack lives in messages.py (encode_mi_request/decode_mi_response) operating on MI payloads, not MCTP headers/EIDs, and the package __init__ correctly labels mctp as "transport" and messages as "framing." So the docstring's specific claim of header/EID encode-decode is a capability the code cannot back. No guard or test refutes this (tests only cover EID range, tag range, and an object-level mock round-trip). Under the project's honesty discipline this is a legitimate, if minor, finding; it is not a protected pragma/None-raise/scheduled-debt pattern. Severity stays low: it is a documentation overstatement that could mislead a developer but does not cause a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict, a wrong spec number, or data corruption on real hardware. The suggested fix (soften the docstring to "post-reassembly message model + transport ABC + in-memory mock; header framing/reassembly are the concrete binding subclass's job") is correct.  

**L20. Module docstring frame layout contradicts the code and the spec**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/messages.py` · messages.py:14-21 (module docstring) vs encode_mi_request:127-134 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The docstring claims 'byte 0: Message Type (always 0x04 for NVMe-MI)' and 'byte 1: NMI MJD + CSI + Slot ID', but the code never emits 0x04 in byte 0 (it emits the MI message type), and packs CSI/SlotID into byte 0 not byte 1. The documented framing matches neither the implementation nor the real spec, so a reader trusting the docstring will mis-cite spec conformance.  
- **Evidence:** Docstring: 'byte 0: Message Type (always 0x04 for NVMe-MI)' / 'byte 1: NMI MJD + CSI bits + Slot ID + Reserved'. Code emits 'bytes([nmp, msg.opcode])' where nmp carries MT|CSI|SlotID in byte 0 and opcode in byte 1 — no 0x04 byte, no NMHDR at bytes 2..5 distinct from the layout described.  
- **Fix:** Rewrite the docstring to match a corrected implementation: byte0 = 0x84, byte1 = NMP (ROR/NMIMT/CSI), byte2 = MEB flag, byte3 = reserved, then opcode/dword fields, MIC last.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced from the code. The module docstring at messages.py:14-21 states "The on-wire framing per spec §3:" then lists "byte 0: Message Type (always 0x04 for NVMe-MI)" and "byte 1: NMI MJD + CSI bits + Slot ID". The actual encoder (encode_mi_request, lines 127-137) emits byte 0 = nmp = (MT & 0x0F) | (CSI<<4) | (SlotID<<5) and byte 1 = msg.opcode. So: (a) byte 0 is never the constant 0x04 — it carries the MI message type (e.g. 0x01 for NVME_MI_COMMAND) plus CSI+SlotID; (b) CSI/SlotID are packed into byte 0, not byte 1 as the docstring claims; (c) the opcode byte (byte 1) is omitted from the documented layout entirely. The inline comments (lines 39-40, 127) and decode_mi_response (lines 154-159) agree with the code, not the docstring, so the docstring is the outlier. The 0x04 value is actually the MCTP message-type byte, which mctp.py shows is owned by the MCTP layer (MctpMessage.body is "the message data after the 8-bit MCTP message type byte") — it is not part of the frame this module encodes. The docstring also doesn't match the real NVMe-MI NMP layout (spec NMP: bit7=ROR, bits4:3=NMIMT, bit0=CSI + 3 reserved bytes). So the documented frame matches neither the implementation nor the spec, and it over-claims "per spec §3," which the honesty convention flags. The finding is factually correct. However, this is purely a documentation/honesty inaccuracy: encode/decode are internally self-consistent and round-trip correctly (test_encode_decode_round_trip passes), so there is no wrong PASS/FAIL verdict, no data corruption, and no runtime/parsing bug on real hardware. Real-world blast radius is limited to a reader mis-citing spec conformance from the docstring. That is below the project's "medium" bar (edge-case mishandling / weak test), so I downgrade to low. The suggested fix correctly diagnoses the doc problem but its proposed byte values (0x84/NMP bits) describe the real spec and would also imply changing the code's bit layout; the minimal correct fix is to rewrite the docstring to match the actual implementation and drop the unbacked "per spec §3" framing.  

**L21. check_eom crashes on an empty EOM vertical_mv where worst_vertical_mv is guarded**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/checks.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/checks.py:238 (check_eom, height_mv=max(eom.vertical_mv)) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** If a real backend's read_eom returns an empty vertical_mv list (a register misparse / N-A read), check_eom raises ValueError('max() arg is empty') even though EomReading.worst_vertical_mv defensively returns 0.0 for the same input — an inconsistency that turns a degraded read into an unhandled crash instead of a clean fail.  
- **Evidence:** checks.py builds `height_mv=max(eom.vertical_mv)` with no empty guard; serdes.py `worst_vertical_mv` does `min(self.vertical_mv) if self.vertical_mv else 0.0`. Confirmed max([]) raises ValueError while EomReading(vertical_mv=[]).worst_vertical_mv == 0.0.  
- **Fix:** Guard the max: `height_mv=(max(eom.vertical_mv) if eom.vertical_mv else 0.0)`, or have check_eom raise a SerDesError with a clear message when vertical_mv is empty (contract violation) rather than letting a bare ValueError escape.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced the factual core. At src/computetest/gmsl/checks.py:238, check_eom builds `height_mv=max(eom.vertical_mv)` with no empty guard, while EomReading.worst_vertical_mv (serdes.py:143) returns `min(self.vertical_mv) if self.vertical_mv else 0.0`. I verified empirically: max([]) raises ValueError, and EomReading(vertical_mv=[]).worst_vertical_mv == 0.0. So the asymmetry the finding describes is real and demonstrable — on the same empty input, the accessor that actually drives the verdict (eye_mv=worst_vertical_mv) degrades to 0.0 (which would cleanly fail the mv_min threshold), while the informational height_mv assignment crashes with a bare ValueError. No guard or test handles the empty case (tests/test_gmsl_eom.py only exercises 3- and 1-element lists), so it is not already-handled.

Severity stays LOW, not higher, for three reasons grounded in the project conventions: (1) An empty vertical_mv is a CONTRACT VIOLATION, not a normal degraded read — the SerDesLink ABC docstring (serdes.py:263-265) mandates read_eom returns one entry per PAM4 sub-eye (3) or 1 for NRZ, and that readers raise SerDesError 'never silently return zeros'. A conformant backend never produces []. (2) There is currently no real read_eom backend; only MockSerDes exists, and it always emits >=1 entry (serdes.py:466-470), so this is forward-looking about a hypothetical buggy backend rather than a live crash. (3) Per the severity rubric and honesty discipline, this produces NO wrong PASS/FAIL and NO data corruption: on bad input the code crashes loudly rather than faking a verdict, and height_mv is purely informational (eye_quality_verdict at base.py:155-158 only reads eye_ui and eye_mv, not height_mv). The only legitimate defect is error-hygiene: a bare ValueError escapes where callers expect to `except SerDesError` (the unified-exception contract), so the suggested fix to raise SerDesError (or guard the max) is reasonable. Confirmed as a minor robustness/exception-type inconsistency, low severity.  

**L22. Mock tests never exercise the per-link real-path label/sysfs divergence, so the link-discrimination bug is invisible to the suite**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/video.py` · tests/test_gmsl.py:10-19, tests/test_gmsl_format.py:36-39 · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Every check_deserializer test runs in mock mode where link_id=f'{addr}:link{i}' and the i==0 BAD heuristic give correct per-link behavior; nothing tests that the real path (which passes the same addr and reads one shared sysfs node) would produce the same per-link discrimination, so the high-severity real-path defect passes CI silently.  
- **Evidence:** test_deserializer_one_link_down_fails asserts `not d.links[0].locked` — only achievable in mock via `bad = "BAD" in addr and i == 0`; the real branch (lines 155-157) is # pragma: no cover and exercised by no test or fake.  
- **Fix:** Add a verified fake / contract test that drives _real_check_gmsl with per-link sysfs fixtures (distinct lock/error per link) to prove the real path discriminates links and labels them per-link; this would have caught findings #1 and #2.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and factually accurate. In tests/test_gmsl.py the only per-link discrimination test (test_deserializer_one_link_down_fails, lines 10-13) asserts `not d.links[0].locked`, which is reachable solely via the mock heuristic at src/computetest/gmsl/video.py:150 (`bad = "BAD" in addr and i == 0`) combined with the per-link label `link_id = f"{addr}:link{i}"` (line 148). The real branch (lines 155-157, `# pragma: no cover`) instead calls `check_gmsl(addr, vid, ...)` passing the bare `addr` for every link, so _real_check_gmsl (lines 71-113) reads ONE shared sysfs node `/sys/bus/i2c/devices/{addr}/link_status` (and error_count) for all N links and stamps every GmslHealth.link with `addr`, not a per-link id. I reproduced this: mock yields labels ['...:link0','...:link1',...] with locked=[False,True,True,True], while the real-equivalent call stamps .link='1-0029' for all four links and would read identical lock/error state for each — it cannot discriminate links and mislabels them. I confirmed no test or fake drives the GMSL real path: grep shows zero `mock=False` GMSL tests, and tests/test_parsers_corpus.py drives the `# pragma: no cover` real branches only for NVMe and Ethernet (monkeypatching subprocess.run/shutil.which) with no GMSL fixture. So the test-quality gap is real: the precedent corpus-replay pattern exists project-wide but GMSL is absent, leaving the real-path link-discrimination/labeling defect invisible to CI. This is NOT merely "a pragma path lacks a unit test" (which the conventions say not to flag) — it is a real logic bug inside a pragma path plus the specific test blind spot that hides it, which the conventions explicitly call high-value. Kept at low: as a standalone test-quality item its own impact is minor; the substantive harm lives in the referenced real-path defect (findings #1/#2), and the finding correctly frames it that way rather than overclaiming.  

**L23. test_compliance_with_supplied_masks primes one trace for both reads, so it cannot catch IL/RL param-swap or per-param mask bugs**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/channel.py` · tests/test_gmsl_channel.py:80-89 · _test-quality_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The compliance test sets a single CALC:DATA? FDATA response (-15,-16) reused for both the il_param (SDD21) and rl_param (SDD11) reads; the same -15/-16 trace passes both an IL floor of -20 and an RL ceiling of -10, so the test would still pass if check_channel_compliance swapped the two masks or measured the wrong param.  
- **Evidence:** v.transport.set_measurement('CALC:DATA? FDATA', '-15.0,-16.0'); ... il = INSERTION_LOSS [(1e6,-20),(2e6,-20)]; rl = RETURN_LOSS [(1e6,-10),(2e6,-10)]; both pass on the same trace, so an il/rl mask transposition is invisible.  
- **Fix:** Prime distinct traces per param (the mock keys responses by query, but both params share CALC:DATA? FDATA, so distinguish via mask values that only pass for the correct param), or assert il_res.kind == 'insertion_loss' and rl_res.kind == 'return_loss' plus a trace that fails if swapped.  
- **Verifier:** Factually accurate. The mock transport (instruments.py:129-131, _meas keyed on uppercased query) makes both measure_sparam("SDD21") and measure_sparam("SDD11") issue the identical query "CALC:DATA? FDATA" (instruments.py:878), so both reads return the single primed trace -15.0,-16.0. With the test's IL floor of -20 (INSERTION_LOSS fails if measured<limit) and RL ceiling of -10 (RETURN_LOSS fails if measured>limit), -15/-16 clears both. I traced the swap scenario: because both params read the same trace, swapping il_mask/rl_mask or il_param/rl_param inside check_channel_compliance (channel.py:128-129) leaves both results passing, so this test cannot catch an orchestration-level transposition. The finding is correct that test_compliance_with_supplied_masks is a happy-path wiring test blind to a mask/param swap.

Tempering this to low/nit (which matches the finding's own "low"): the substantive comparison-direction logic is NOT untested — TestComparison.test_insertion_loss_violation_below_limit and test_return_loss_violation_above_limit (test lines 44-60) independently exercise each MaskKind's pass/fail direction with distinct sweeps and distinct masks, so a real sign/direction bug in check_against_mask would be caught. The only unguarded surface is the trivial 2-line param->mask wiring in check_channel_compliance. Also note the finding's suggested fix to assert il_res.kind=='insertion_loss' would NOT catch a swap, since kind is copied from the mask object the test passes in (check_against_mask sets kind=mask.kind.value, channel.py:110); only the suggested "distinct traces" approach actually closes the gap. So this is a genuine but minor test-quality weakness, not a verdict-correctness or real-hardware defect.  

**L24. PRBS `locked` can report True with a catastrophic error count, and TSN has no BER verdict to override it**  
`src/computetest/tsn/phy.py` · src/computetest/tsn/phy.py:192 (MockPhy.run_prbs_bist) · _correctness_ · conf=medium · verdict=uncertain  
- **Claim:** `locked=self._prbs_errors < 1_000_000` reports a locked/good PRBS window for up to 999,999 errors, and unlike the GMSL analogue there is no tsn check that turns (error_count, bits) into a pass/fail BER verdict — so `locked` is the only exposed signal and it can mask a badly degraded link.  
- **Evidence:** phy.py: `locked=self._prbs_errors < 1_000_000`. The GMSL mirror (gmsl/serdes.py:490) uses the same heuristic, BUT gmsl/checks.py:146 separates it: `checks = {"prbs_locked": r.locked, "ber_proven": v.status == "pass"}` where `v = assess(errors=r.error_count, bits=r.bits, ...)`. No equivalent `check_prbs_*` exists in the tsn package (tsn/__init__.py exports only PhyPrbsResult, never a verdict fn), so a caller that trusts `locked` alone gets a pass with ~1e6 errors.  
- **Fix:** Either (a) add a tsn-side PRBS/BER verdict helper mirroring gmsl.checks.check_prbs_ber that feeds error_count/bits to computetest.ber.assess, or (b) document on PhyPrbsResult.locked that it is a coarse sync flag, NOT a BER verdict, and that callers must run the BER math on (error_count, bits).  
- **Verifier:** All concrete factual claims are correct and independently verified. src/computetest/tsn/phy.py:192 is verbatim `locked=self._prbs_errors < 1_000_000`, so `locked` is True for up to 999,999 errors. The GMSL mirror at src/computetest/gmsl/serdes.py:490 uses the identical heuristic, and gmsl/checks.py:128-151 (`check_prbs_ber`) does separate the signals into `{"prbs_locked": r.locked, "ber_proven": v.status == "pass"}` by feeding error_count/bits to computetest.ber.assess. I confirmed via grep that NO equivalent check_prbs_* / assess call exists anywhere in src/computetest/tsn/ — there is no tsn adapter.py PRBS consumer, pma.py never touches PRBS counts, and tsn/__init__.py exports only the PhyPrbsResult dataclass plus PhyPrbsPattern, never a verdict function. So the GMSL-vs-TSN asymmetry is genuine.

However, this is a missing-helper / API-completeness gap, NOT a demonstrable correctness defect. Per the honesty-discipline convention, a defect requires the code to fake a PASS, hide a skip, or mislabel a verdict. tsn/phy.py does none of these: `PhyPrbsResult` truthfully exposes raw `error_count` and `bits`, and `locked` is honestly named as a sync flag (not `ok`/`pass`). I searched the whole codebase and found NO code path where `locked` actually drives a PASS/FAIL verdict — no `*Health.ok`, no CLI, no adapter consumes it. The "masks a badly degraded link" scenario therefore depends entirely on a hypothetical external caller choosing to trust `locked` alone, which the API neither forces nor implies; it cannot be reproduced from existing code. The 1_000_000 threshold matches the GMSL mock and plausibly models real PRBS-lock loss (intentional mock fidelity). The suggested fix (b) — a docstring note that `locked` is a coarse sync flag, not a BER verdict — is reasonable, and the symmetry-restoring helper (a) is a nice-to-have. But the rubric's "medium = weak test that would pass even if code were wrong" does not fit: no existing test or check is weakened. Downgraded to low because the factual basis is solid but the asserted real-world impact (a wrong verdict) is not present in any current code path.  

**L25. TdrResult.ok treats any unexpected/garbage status (not just 'skipped') as OK**  
`src/computetest/tsn/phy.py` · src/computetest/tsn/phy.py:71-72 (TdrResult.ok) · _edge-case_ · conf=high · verdict=uncertain  
- **Claim:** `ok` returns True for every status except the exact string 'fault'; a future real MDIO/ethtool backend that emits an unrecognized status (e.g. 'error', 'unknown', '') would be silently verdicted as a passing cable.  
- **Evidence:** `@property def ok(self) -> bool: return self.status != "fault"`. The docstring enumerates only "ok" | "fault" | "skipped", but anything off that list also passes. check_cable_tdr (tsn/cable.py:48) inherits the same logic: `no_open_or_short = tdr.status != "fault"`.  
- **Fix:** Make `ok` a whitelist of safe statuses (e.g. `return self.status in ("ok", "skipped")`) so an unexpected/error status from a real backend fails closed rather than open.  
- **Verifier:** The code description is factually correct: src/computetest/tsn/phy.py:71-72 is literally `return self.status != "fault"`, and cable.py:48 mirrors it (`no_open_or_short = tdr.status != "fault"`). So any non-"fault" status passes — a blacklist, not a whitelist. BUT the real-world impact claimed is hypothetical. The only TDR producers in the codebase are MockPhy (emits exactly "ok"/"fault") and the canonical real-hw ethtool path src/computetest/ethernet.py:_real_cable_test (lines 140-152, # pragma: no cover - real-hw), which provably emits ONLY "ok", "fault", or "skipped" — there is no "error"/"unknown"/"" path anywhere. TdrResult is explicitly designed to mirror that exact 3-value shape. "skipped" passing is documented, intentional honesty-discipline design (check_cable_tdr docstring: skipped "does not false-fail a good link"). Thus the suggested whitelist `in ("ok","skipped")` would behave identically to today for every status the system actually generates; no current verdict — mock or real-hw — is wrong. The finding's own wording ("a FUTURE real MDIO/ethtool backend that emits an unrecognized status") concedes it is contingent on a backend that does not exist. Per the project's severity rules (critical/high = a wrong verdict on real hardware) there is no present-day false PASS. This is a legitimate-but-minor defensive-coding / fail-open hardening suggestion, not a reproducible defect; I cannot confirm a concrete bug, so I leave it uncertain at low (effectively nit/low) severity. The suggested fix is harmless and reasonable hardening if a non-conforming backend is ever added.  

**L26. PRBS bits test is tautological — mirrors the implementation formula, can't catch a units error**  
`src/computetest/tsn/phy.py` · tests/test_tsn_phy.py:70-74 (test_prbs_bist) · _test-quality_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The assertion `r.bits == 1000 * 1e6` re-expresses the exact code formula `speed_mbps * 1e6 * duration_s` rather than an independently-derived physical bit count, so a conceptual units bug (e.g. mis-scaling Mbps) that changed the formula in both places would still pass.  
- **Evidence:** Code: `bits = self._info.speed_mbps * 1e6 * duration_s` (phy.py:189). Test: `assert r.bits == 1000 * 1e6 and r.locked`. The test hard-codes the same `1000 * 1e6` literal; there is no check that bits == line_rate_bps * duration for a non-1.0 duration or a 100BASE-T1 (100 Mbps) part.  
- **Fix:** Assert against an independent expectation, e.g. `MockPhy(speed_mbps=100).run_prbs_bist(duration_s=2.0).bits == 100e6 * 2.0`, exercising both a different speed and a non-unit duration so a scaling regression is caught.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced the core mechanism. test_prbs_bist (tests/test_tsn_phy.py:70-74) only calls run_prbs_bist with duration_s=1.0, a multiplicative identity, and asserts r.bits == 1000 * 1e6 reusing the same 1000 literal as the injected speed. I confirmed against the code (src/computetest/tsn/phy.py:189, bits = speed_mbps * 1e6 * duration_s) that two distinct regressions pass this assertion unchanged: dropping the duration factor (bits = speed_mbps * 1e6 -> 1000000000.0) and dividing instead of multiplying by duration (1000*1e6/1.0 -> 1000000000.0). No other PRBS test (test_prbs_explicit_pattern, test_prbs_high_errors_unlock) checks bits with a non-1.0 duration or non-1000 speed, so the duration dimension and the speed-tracking of bits are genuinely unpinned. The finding is a legitimate test-quality gap and the suggested fix (assert MockPhy(speed_mbps=100).run_prbs_bist(duration_s=2.0).bits == 100e6 * 2.0) would close it. Two caveats keep this at low rather than higher: (1) the 'can't catch a units error' framing is overstated — the literal 1e6 DOES pin the Mbps->bps scale factor, so changing 1e6 to 1e3 in the code would fail the test; only the duration dimension and speed-coupling are blind. (2) The affected code is the deterministic MockPhy arithmetic, not a real-hw parser, so blast radius is small. Severity stays low as the finding itself rated it.  

**L27. BMCA dataset comparison omits offsetScaledLogVariance (steps-removed / stepsRemoved tiebreak)**  
`src/computetest/tsn/gptp_proto.py` · gptp_proto.py:56-64 (bmca_elect) and AnnounceMsg fields 45-54 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** IEEE 1588 / 802.1AS dataset comparison orders by (priority1, clockClass, clockAccuracy, offsetScaledLogVariance, priority2, clockIdentity); the code (and AnnounceMsg) drops offsetScaledLogVariance, so two GMs that tie on accuracy but differ in clock variance would be ordered only by priority2/clockIdentity, diverging from the standard tiebreak.  
- **Evidence:** min(announces, key=lambda a: (a.priority1, a.clock_class, a.clock_accuracy, a.priority2, a.clock_identity)) — no offset_scaled_log_variance field exists on AnnounceMsg.  
- **Fix:** Add offset_scaled_log_variance to AnnounceMsg and insert it into the comparison key between clock_accuracy and priority2 to match the 1588 comparison algorithm; document if intentionally simplified.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and independently reproduced from the code. bmca_elect (gptp_proto.py:62-64) orders by (priority1, clock_class, clock_accuracy, priority2, clock_identity), and AnnounceMsg (gptp_proto.py:45-53) has no offset_scaled_log_variance field. The IEEE 1588 / 802.1AS data set comparison ordering does include offsetScaledLogVariance between clockAccuracy and priority2, so the finding's cited spec ordering is accurate and the omission is real. No guard, alternate path, or test covers the variance tiebreak (tests in test_tsn_gptp_proto.py:29-40 only vary priority1). It is NOT excused by project conventions: the docstring (lines 57-58) positively asserts this is the 'IEEE 1588 dataset comparison' and enumerates the exact incomplete key with no note that variance is intentionally dropped — so it is an undocumented simplification that mildly overclaims spec compliance, not the honesty-discipline None-raises pattern. Severity stays low: impact is bounded to the narrow tie case where two GMs match on priority1/clockClass/clockAccuracy but differ in variance, in which case this offline conformance helper (fed by pmc/Wireshark dumps) would elect a different, less-stable GM than the standard prescribes — a wrong-verdict corner case, slightly above a pure nit. The suggested fix (add the field and insert it into the key between clock_accuracy and priority2, or document the simplification) is correct.  

**L28. check_fragmentation does not verify a preempted frame actually has a continuation (single SMD-S 'fragment' passes)**  
`src/computetest/tsn/preemption.py` · preemption.py:83-103 (check_fragmentation) · _edge-case_ · conf=low · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A list of exactly one SMD-S fragment of arbitrarily tiny size returns (True, []): fragments[:-1] is empty so the floor check is skipped, and there is no continuation. The function is documented to 'validate a fragmented frame' but accepts an unfragmented single mPacket as valid, and never asserts >=1 continuation exists.  
- **Evidence:** `for f in fragments[:-1]:` skips the only/last element; check_fragmentation([Fragment('SMD-S0', 1)], add_frag_size=3) returns (True, []). Per 802.3br a preempted frame is a start fragment plus >=1 continuation; a single SMD-S with no SMD-C is not a fragmented frame.  
- **Fix:** If the function is meant to validate that preemption occurred, add a check that len(fragments) >= 2 (a start plus at least one continuation) and emit a note like 'no continuation fragment'; otherwise tighten the docstring to state it accepts a degenerate single-fragment input as trivially valid.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/tsn/preemption.py:83-103. check_fragmentation([Fragment('SMD-S0', 1)], add_frag_size=3) returns (True, []), and check_preemption(...fragments=[Fragment('SMD-S0',1)], add_frag_size=3).ok is True. The `for f in fragments[:-1]` slice (line 98) excludes the sole/last element, so the floor check is skipped, and nothing asserts a continuation exists. The docstring (lines 85-87) explicitly says the function validates "a fragmented frame: a single SMD-S start then SMD-C continuations" — but a lone SMD-S mPacket with no SMD-C is not a fragmented frame under 802.3br (preemption yields an initial fragment plus >=1 continuation). So the code accepts a degenerate, sub-floor, unfragmented single packet as a valid fragmented frame — a wrong PASS for that input that contradicts its own contract. Not refuted: no guard catches it. Not already-handled: the existing tests (test_tsn_preemption.py:88,94) pass single-fragment lists but only assert the handshake check, never asserting fragmentation_valid should be False — the edge case is untested, not validated as intended. Not intentional: the docstring contradicts the behavior, so it is not deliberate honesty-discipline design. Severity stays low (matching the finding): this is an edge-case input a conformance operator would rarely feed deliberately, and the more important floor/SMD-sequence checks still work for realistic >=2-fragment frames; it does not fake a spec mask or mislabel a mainline verdict.  

**L29. Respond-window boundary is not pinned by tests; 0.8 could drift undetected**  
`src/computetest/tsn/preemption.py` · tests/test_tsn_preemption.py:32-43 (TestVerifyRespond) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** test_late_respond_fails uses delay 9.0 against window 10.0 and test_success uses 5.0; neither exercises the 0.8x boundary, so the fraction could be changed to anything in roughly (0.5, 0.9) without a test failing. The tests do not actually verify the documented 0.8x criterion.  
- **Evidence:** Only data points: (delay 5.0, pass) and (delay 9.0, fail) against verify_time 10.0. A boundary case delay=8.0 (expected pass) / delay=8.01 (expected fail) is absent.  
- **Fix:** Add boundary assertions: run_verify_respond(10.0, respond_delay_ms=8.0, got_respond=True) is True and run_verify_respond(10.0, respond_delay_ms=8.0001, got_respond=True) is False, so the constant is actually exercised.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and accurate. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/tsn/preemption.py the handshake is `respond_delay_ms <= RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION * verify_time_ms` with RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION = 0.8 (line 21, 80). The tests in tests/test_tsn_preemption.py TestVerifyRespond (lines 32-43) only exercise delay=5.0 (expect pass) and delay=9.0 (expect fail) against verify_time=10.0; the related check_preemption tests reuse the same 5.0/9.0 values. I computed the drift window directly: delay 5.0 passing requires fraction >= 0.5, and delay 9.0 failing requires fraction < 0.9, so RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION could be set to anything in [0.5, 0.9) and every test stays green — the documented 0.8x criterion is genuinely not pinned. A grep across tests/ and src/computetest/tsn/ confirms no other test exercises a delay near the 8.0 boundary. The suggested fix (delay=8.0 -> True, delay just above 8.0 -> False) correctly pins the constant, since 0.8*10.0 evaluates exactly to 8.0 and the comparison is `<=`. This is NOT one of the intentional conventions (no pragma, no honesty-discipline None, not mock fidelity) — it is a real, if low-impact, test-coverage gap. Severity stays low: the production code is correct, so there is no wrong PASS/FAIL on real hardware today; the only risk is an undetected future regression to the constant. Refutation attempts failed — no guard or other test covers the boundary, and the claim is factually correct about what the code and tests do.  

**L30. No boundary-condition tests for link-up time and SQI thresholds**  
`src/computetest/tsn/pma.py` · tests/test_tsn_pma.py:43-53; check_pma_electrical (src/computetest/tsn/pma.py:61-62) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The checks use `<= max_linkup_ms` and `>= sqi_min`, but the suite only exercises clearly-passing (20 ms, SQI 7) and clearly-failing (500 ms, SQI 2) values, never the exact boundary (linkup == max_linkup_ms, sqi == sqi_min), so an off-by-one flip to `<`/`>` would not be detected.  
- **Evidence:** `"link_up_time_ok": phy.read_link_up_time() <= max_linkup_ms` and `"sqi_ok": phy.read_sqi() >= sqi_min`; tests inject injected_linkup_ms=20.0/500.0 and injected_sqi=7/2 — none at 100.0 ms or SQI 5.  
- **Fix:** Add cases at exactly the boundary: injected_linkup_ms=100.0 with default max_linkup_ms (expect link_up_time_ok True) and injected_sqi=5 with default sqi_min (expect sqi_ok True), plus 100.001 / SQI 4 just-over-the-line cases.  
- **Verifier:** Accurate and independently reproduced. check_pma_electrical (src/computetest/tsn/pma.py:61-62) uses inclusive comparisons: `phy.read_link_up_time() <= max_linkup_ms` (default 100.0) and `phy.read_sqi() >= sqi_min` (default 5). The PMA suite (tests/test_tsn_pma.py:29,45,51) injects only clearly-passing (linkup 20.0, SQI 7) and clearly-failing (linkup 500.0, SQI 2) values — never the exact boundary (linkup==100.0 or SQI==5). I also checked the broader suite: tests/test_tsn_phy.py exercises the same operators via linkup_ok/sqi_ok (phy.py:141,145) but again only with off-boundary values (20.0/500.0; SQI 3 vs thresholds 2/5; set_thresholds 500.0 vs 600.0). So no test in the repo pins either value to the threshold, and a `<=`->`<` or `>=`->`>` off-by-one mutation in pma.py:61-62 (or phy.py:141/145) would survive undetected. This is a real, demonstrable test-quality gap, not refuted by any guard or other test. It is NOT an intentional-design / honesty-discipline / pragma exemption — it's about test coverage, and the gates (coverage fail_under=95) measure line/branch coverage, not boundary/mutation coverage, so they don't catch this. Severity stays low: the production code is currently correct (inclusive operators are the right semantics for "within budget" / "clears the floor"), there is no present wrong verdict or data corruption, and the issue is purely defense-in-depth against a hypothetical future regression. Suggested fix (add boundary cases at linkup==100.0/SQI==5 expecting pass, plus just-over cases) is appropriate.  

**L31. test_summary_and_to_dict only checks a literal substring, not the rendered verdict**  
`src/computetest/tsn/cable.py` · tests/test_tsn_cable.py:18-22 test_summary_and_to_dict · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The summary assertion `assert "cable TDR" in h.summary()` is near-tautological ("cable TDR" is a hardcoded prefix that can never change with logic) and never checks that summary() actually renders the FAIL verdict or the @3.5m distance, so summary() could mislabel a fault as OK or drop the distance and this test would still pass.  
- **Evidence:** assert "cable TDR" in h.summary() — the only summary check; for a fault, summary() returns 'cable TDR status=fault @3.5m -> FAIL', none of which (status, @3.5m, FAIL) is asserted.  
- **Fix:** Assert the meaningful pieces: e.g. assert h.summary() == "cable TDR status=fault @3.5m -> FAIL" (or assert 'FAIL' in it and '@3.5m' in it), so a regression that mislabels the verdict or drops the distance is caught.  
- **Verifier:** Verified against the actual code. test_tsn_cable.py:20 asserts only `assert "cable TDR" in h.summary()`. In cable.py:35, `summary()` returns `f"cable TDR status={self.status}{dist} -> {state}"`, so `"cable TDR"` is a hardcoded prefix that is always present regardless of status, distance, or verdict — the assertion is near-tautological. I ran the code and confirmed `summary()` renders `'cable TDR status=fault @3.5m -> FAIL'`, yet none of `status=fault`, `@3.5m`, or `FAIL` is asserted from the rendered string. The summary() method has real logic untested in its output: the `state` ternary ("OK" vs "FAIL") and the `dist` conditional. A regression that flipped the verdict label or dropped the distance in summary() would not fail this test. No other test covers the rendered summary string: test_open_fault_fails_with_distance only checks the underlying properties (h.ok, h.status, h.fault_distance_m), and test_summary_and_to_dict's to_dict() check exercises a different code path. So this is NOT already-handled. It is not an intentional convention either (no pragma/honesty-discipline rationale applies). Severity kept at low rather than medium: summary() is a human-readable reporting string, while the machine-readable PASS/FAIL verdict (h.ok/h.status/to_dict) IS asserted elsewhere, so a summary regression would mislead a field reader but not corrupt the programmatic verdict. The suggested fix (assert h.summary() == "cable TDR status=fault @3.5m -> FAIL", or assert 'FAIL' and '@3.5m' substrings) is correct and would catch such regressions.  

**L32. ECC tests are internally consistent with the mock and would pass even if real EDAC mapping were wrong**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/ecc.py` · tests/test_ddr5_ecc.py:12-32 · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The tests drive the same MockDdr5Ras whose inject_ce/inject_ue hardwire exactly the counter the checks read, so every assertion is a tautology over the mock's internal consistency; none would catch a real-backend bug in CE/UE attribution. This is acknowledged Mock design, hence low, but there is no contract/fake-vs-real cross-check for this subsystem.  
- **Evidence:** h = check_ce_reporting(MockDdr5Ras(...), dimm="DIMM_A1")
assert h.checks == {"ce_counter_incremented": True, "correct_dimm_attribution": True, "no_spurious_ue": True}  
- **Fix:** Add a negative-path test (e.g. a mock variant where inject_ce mis-attributes or drops the CE) asserting the relevant check goes False, so the assertions exercise FAIL detection rather than only the happy path.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced and confirmed at the cited location (tests/test_ddr5_ecc.py:12-32) against src/computetest/ddr5/ecc.py and ras.py. The tautology claim is factually correct: MockDdr5Ras.inject_ce(dimm) increments self._ce[dimm], and read_edac() returns sum(self._ce.values()) plus dict(self._ce) — the same counter the inject writes. So check_ce_reporting's three checks (ce_counter_incremented, correct_dimm_attribution, no_spurious_ue) are structurally guaranteed True for the mock, and likewise for check_ue_reporting. The happy-path assertions cannot fail regardless of real EDAC-attribution correctness. The only FAIL test, test_fail_summary_branch (lines 36-39), hand-constructs EccReportHealth(checks={"ce_counter_incremented": False}) purely to hit summary()'s FAIL-string branch; it never drives the check_* functions to emit a False check, so the production delta/attribution logic is never exercised in a failing direction. The sub-claim "no contract/fake-vs-real cross-check for this subsystem" is also accurate: tests/test_backend_contract.py runs Mock-vs-Real (over a fixture sysfs tree) only for the PCIe backend; DDR5 RAS has only MockDdr5Ras with no RealDdr5Ras and no contract test, despite ras.py's docstring claiming a real backend reads the same sysfs nodes. All four ECC tests pass as written. This is not refuted by any guard/test, and it is not purely intentional Mock design — the gap is in test coverage of the check functions' own logic, not in the Mock's deliberate internal consistency. Severity is correctly low: it is a test-quality weakness (a regression in check_ce/ue_reporting's comparison logic would go uncaught), not a wrong hardware verdict, data corruption, or a faked PASS. The suggested fix (a mock variant whose inject mis-attributes/drops the CE, asserting the relevant check flips to False) is the right remediation. Kept at low per the finding's own honest scoping.  

**L33. persist_mode is not validated; any value != 1 silently routes to the sPPR verdict**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/ppr.py` · ppr.py:51-61 check_ppr; ppr.py:24-25 PprHealth.mode · _error-handling_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A caller passing persist_mode=2 (or any int other than 0/1) is silently treated as sPPR: the `else` branch runs and `mode` returns 'sPPR' (it only special-cases ==1), and perform_repair does not persist it, so the address 'reverts' and the run reports a PASS labeled sPPR for an invalid request rather than rejecting it.  
- **Evidence:** if persist_mode == 1: ... else: checks = {"repaired": ..., "reverts_on_power_cycle": not survives}  and  return "hPPR" if self.persist_mode == 1 else "sPPR". No validation that persist_mode in (0,1).  
- **Fix:** Validate at the top of check_ppr: `if persist_mode not in (0, 1): raise Ddr5RasError(...)`. The Linux mem_repairX persist_mode ABI is strictly 0/1.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly as claimed. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/ddr5/ppr.py, check_ppr (lines 41-62) only special-cases persist_mode == 1; any other int falls into the else branch (sPPR path). Running check_ppr(MockDdr5Ras(), addr=0x1000, persist_mode=2) yields ok=True, mode="sPPR", checks={"repaired": True, "reverts_on_power_cycle": True}, summary "DDR5 PPR addr=0x1000 sPPR -> OK", and to_dict carries persist_mode=2 with mode "sPPR". Same for 99 and -1. There is NO validation: perform_repair in ras.py (line 137) only validates repair_type, MemRepairRequest does not validate persist_mode, and no caller/CLI guards it (check_ppr is only invoked from tests). PprHealth.mode (ppr.py:25) returns "hPPR" only for ==1, so it mislabels the verdict for invalid input. This is not intentional and not mock-fidelity: the same module validates sibling inputs the same way the fix suggests (ras.py:91-93 ECS threshold "must be one of ...", ras.py:125-126 unknown DIMM, ras.py:137-138 repair_type "must be ppr|sparing"), all raising Ddr5RasError; persist_mode is the lone unvalidated enum despite the Linux mem_repairX ABI being strictly 0/1. Per the honesty-discipline convention (flag anything that mislabels a verdict / reports a PASS for an out-of-domain request), this is a real finding, and the suggested fix matches the codebase pattern.

Downgraded from medium to low: the defect is only triggerable by a caller passing an out-of-domain int (a programming error upstream), not by any hardware/field condition or valid run. No valid (0/1) input produces a wrong verdict and no data is corrupted; the mock's behavior on valid runs is correct and matches the passing tests. Impact is limited to a misleading label/PASS on caller-supplied invalid input on an internal keyword argument.  

**L34. Negative / failing DQ training margin silently maps to step 0 instead of being flagged as a failure**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/lmt.py` · src/computetest/ddr5/lmt.py:60-66 -> lmt_adapter._step_from_timing_ui:119 / _step_from_voltage_mv:127 · _edge-case_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A DQ with zero or negative trained margin (a failing/closed eye) produces step=0 (clamp via max(0,...)), and the record carries error_count=0, ber=0.0 — indistinguishable from a DQ with a tiny-but-passing margin. Nothing in the emitted record signals that the eye is closed.  
- **Evidence:** step=_step_from_timing_ui(m.timing_ui, ...) where _step_from_timing_ui does `return max(0, min(num_steps, step))`; the DDR5 record then sets sample_count=0, error_count=0, ber=0.0 unconditionally. A DqMargin(timing_ui=-0.1) yields step 0, error_count 0, ber 0.0.  
- **Fix:** These records carry no verdict, so this is a consumer-trust issue rather than a faked pass. Either document that DqMargin must hold the passing margin (>0) only, or guard training_to_lmt_records to raise/flag when timing_ui<=0 or voltage_mv<=0 (e.g. a failing DQ) so a closed eye is not silently emitted as step 0 with zero errors.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly: training_to_lmt_records([DqMargin(timing_ui=-0.1, voltage_mv=-5)]) yields TIMING/VOLTAGE records with step=0, error_count=0, ber=0.0 — identical to a tiny-but-passing margin (timing_ui=0.001 also gives step=0). The clamp max(0,...) in _step_from_timing_ui/_step_from_voltage_mv (src/computetest/lmt_adapter.py:119,127) collapses any non-positive margin to step 0, and src/computetest/ddr5/lmt.py:62,67 set error_count=0/ber=0.0 unconditionally. So the claimed behavior is factually accurate and not guarded by any test (test_ddr5_lmt.py only exercises positive margins).

However the severity is overstated. I traced every consumer: the only consumers are the to_json/to_csv export formatters; there is NO PASS/FAIL verdict logic anywhere that interprets step/error_count/ber for these DDR5 records, and LmtLaneRecord has no verdict column at all. So nothing is mislabeled, no PASS is faked, and no skip is hidden — the honesty-discipline triggers do not apply. The finding's own claim and suggested_fix concede this: 'These records carry no verdict, so this is a consumer-trust issue rather than a faked pass.' Additionally, step=0 is a defensible representation of a closed eye in pci_lmt step semantics (largest surviving step for a failed eye is genuinely 0), and error_count=0 mirrors the documented sibling model in from_margin_result (lmt_adapter.py:169-171: the adapter projects the LAST passing trained margin, not a sweep). DqMargin documents its fields as the 'eye to failure' i.e. a measured (non-negative) margin; a negative/zero margin is an out-of-contract input that would not normally reach this post-training telemetry projection. Real-world impact is limited to ambiguous telemetry (a marginal ~0 eye looking like a tiny passing eye) for an input that shouldn't occur, with no verdict corruption and no data corruption — that is low/nit, not medium. The suggested guard or a one-line docstring clarification would be a reasonable polish, but this does not mislead a hardware PASS/FAIL decision.  

**L35. Negative or sentinel retry count silently passes the budget check**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/crc.py` · src/computetest/ddr5/crc.py:48 (check_crc_parity, crc_retries_within_budget) · _edge-case_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A negative crc_retry_count (e.g. a -1 parse-error sentinel from a host-readable counter read) is treated as 'within budget' and yields a PASS, masking an upstream readback failure.  
- **Evidence:** "crc_retries_within_budget": crc_retry_count <= max_retries  — with DEFAULT_MAX_CRC_RETRIES = 0, crc_retry_count = -1 gives -1 <= 0 == True, so the check passes. The docstring states the count comes 'from a host-readable counter', and a CRC retry counter is an unsigned register; a negative value can only arise from a failed/garbage read, yet the function accepts it as healthy.  
- **Fix:** Treat negative counts as invalid: either raise on crc_retry_count < 0, or change the check to 0 <= crc_retry_count <= max_retries so a sentinel/garbage value fails the verdict rather than passing it.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced at src/computetest/ddr5/crc.py:48. With DEFAULT_MAX_CRC_RETRIES=0, calling check_crc_parity(..., crc_retry_count=-1) yields checks['crc_retries_within_budget']=True and overall ok=True (verified via PYTHONPATH=src python3). There is no guard against negative/sentinel counts, and tests/test_ddr5_crc.py only exercises counts of 0, 2, and 5 — no negative case — so nothing handles it. The behavior is real: a CRC retry counter is an unsigned register, so a negative value can only be a garbage/parse-error read, yet it is accepted as healthy. This is not intentional honesty-discipline design; it is a missing input-validation guard, and the suggested 0 <= count <= max_retries fix is sound. However severity stays low (edge-case): check_crc_parity is a pure verdict function with NO internal caller that reads a host counter and emits a -1 sentinel (confirmed via grep of src — the only references are the definition, dataclass, and __init__ export). The docstring explicitly instructs callers to 'pass 0 for the config-only case,' so the -1 sentinel scenario is hypothetical and requires a contract-violating caller; on valid hardware data the verdict is correct. It is a reasonable defensive hardening, not a wrong PASS on real data.  

**L36. test_summary_and_to_dict asserts the dict value but never asserts the verdict (.ok)**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/crc.py` · tests/test_ddr5_crc.py:30-32 (test_summary_and_to_dict) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The in-budget case (crc_retry_count=2, max_retries=10) is never asserted to be .ok=True, so a regression that inverted the budget comparison (e.g. >= instead of <=) would not be caught here.  
- **Evidence:** assert _good(crc_retry_count=2, max_retries=10).to_dict()["crc_retry_count"] == 2 — only checks the echoed count, not checks["crc_retries_within_budget"] or .ok; no positive test exists where retry_count>0 but <= a non-default budget is asserted to pass.  
- **Fix:** Add an assertion that _good(crc_retry_count=2, max_retries=10).ok is True and that checks["crc_retries_within_budget"] is True, giving a positive boundary case for the budget comparison.  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed as a real test-quality gap, though the finding's headline example is slightly imprecise. In tests/test_ddr5_crc.py:30-32, test_summary_and_to_dict feeds the only non-default budget (crc_retry_count=2, max_retries=10) but asserts only to_dict()["crc_retry_count"] == 2 (the echoed count, which is constant regardless of the verdict). No test ever asserts .ok is True or checks["crc_retries_within_budget"] is True for a retry_count>0, within-budget case. I empirically proved the gap: mutating crc.py:48 from `crc_retry_count <= max_retries` to `crc_retry_count <= 0` (which entirely ignores the max_retries parameter) leaves all 5 tests passing — so the verdict effect of a non-default max_retries budget is untested. Caveat on the finding's evidence: the specific >= mutation it cites is actually CAUGHT by test_retries_over_budget_fails (5 >= 0 -> True -> ok would wrongly be True, failing that assertion), so that example is wrong; but the core claim (no positive boundary test for retry_count>0 within a non-default budget) holds. Severity stays low: the untested path is the non-default max_retries config-only counter (the module is often config-only and the default-budget=0 path is well covered by the other four tests), so this is an edge-case weak test, not a wrong PASS/FAIL on the primary path. Suggested fix is sound: assert _good(crc_retry_count=2, max_retries=10).ok is True and checks["crc_retries_within_budget"] is True.  

**L37. Tests never exercise the latency-optimized-vs-standard asymmetry that the default would mis-verdict**  
`src/computetest/cxl/link.py` · tests/test_cxl_link.py:31-34 (test_64gt_latency_optimized_ok) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The only latency-optimized test passes expect_flit=CXL_256B_LATENCY_OPT explicitly, so it cannot catch the finding above (default expect_flit FAILing a latency-optimized link). There is no test asserting how a better-than-default flit is handled, so the suite would pass even if the asymmetric behavior is wrong.  
- **Evidence:** h = _good(speed_gt=64, flit_mode=FlitMode.CXL_256B_LATENCY_OPT, expect_flit=FlitMode.CXL_256B_LATENCY_OPT); assert h.ok — expectation is overridden to match, masking the default behavior.  
- **Fix:** Add a test that calls check_cxl_link with flit_mode=CXL_256B_LATENCY_OPT and the DEFAULT expect_flit, and assert the intended verdict (pass if hierarchy semantics are adopted, or an explicit documented FAIL if exact-match is the deliberate contract).  
- **Verifier:** The test-quality claim is factually accurate and independently reproducible. In src/computetest/cxl/link.py:59, flit mode uses exact equality `flit_mode == expect_flit` (default `CXL_256B_STD`), while speed/width use `>=` (lines 57-58) — a real asymmetry. So a link negotiating the better `CXL_256B_LATENCY_OPT` mode FAILs flit_mode_ok under the default `expect_flit`. tests/test_cxl_link.py:31-34 (test_64gt_latency_optimized_ok) is the ONLY latency-optimized test and it overrides `expect_flit=CXL_256B_LATENCY_OPT`, so it never exercises the default path; the evidence quote matches the code verbatim. I confirmed no other test does either: test_cxl_emit.py:35,40 only use CXL_256B_STD and PCIE_68B with default expect_flit, and no other call site in tests/ or src/ passes CXL_256B_LATENCY_OPT with the default. The suite therefore passes regardless of whether the asymmetric exact-match contract is right — a genuine coverage gap masking an arguably-incorrect default (the 4.4.3 roadmap and the link.py docstring frame it as "degrade to a *lower* flit mode = fail", implying a hierarchy, which the == does not honor). Not refuted by any guard/test; not intentional honesty-discipline (no spec mask is at None here). Severity stays low: real but minor — in practice a field caller checking a 64 GT/s latency-optimized link sets expect_flit explicitly (as the existing test shows is idiomatic), so the default-mismatch exposure is narrow and does not by itself produce a wrong verdict in normal usage. The suggested fix (add a test with default expect_flit asserting the intended verdict) is appropriate and would also force a decision on the underlying asymmetry.  

**L38. PoisonList.clear() ignores length, so it cannot model length-scoped / partial-cacheline poison clears**  
`src/computetest/cxl/poison.py` · src/computetest/cxl/poison.py:47-48 (PoisonList.clear) · _correctness_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Real CXL Clear Poison (and cxl-cli/ndctl) clears poison for a DPA over a specific length at cacheline granularity; this clear() removes every entry whose dpa matches regardless of length and has no length parameter, so clearing one cacheline of a multi-cacheline poisoned span is modeled as fully clearing the span — the opposite of real hardware.  
- **Evidence:** def clear(self, dpa: int) -> None: self._entries = [e for e in self._entries if e.dpa != dpa]. inject() accepts arbitrary length and even non-64B-aligned dpa/length, but clear() drops the whole entry on a dpa match; check_poison_inject_clear then verifies 'not any(e.dpa == dpa ...)' which always passes, hiding the length mismatch.  
- **Fix:** Give clear() a length parameter and clear at cacheline granularity (or at minimum require an exact dpa+length match to remove an entry, and split entries on partial overlap). Validate that inject length/dpa are 64-byte (cacheline) aligned, mirroring the real Clear Poison granularity, so the round-trip exercises the real constraint.  
- **Verifier:** The code observation is factually accurate. In src/computetest/cxl/poison.py:47-48, `clear(self, dpa: int)` takes no length and removes every entry whose `dpa` matches regardless of length, and `check_poison_inject_clear` (line 80) asserts `not any(e.dpa == dpa ...)`, which the clear's own logic trivially satisfies — so the round-trip cannot detect a length/granularity mismatch, and a partial-cacheline clear would be modeled as a full-span clear, unlike real CXL Clear Poison. No existing guard or test covers this: test_clear_only_targeted_dpa only checks dpa selectivity, and no test injects a multi-cacheline span and clears one cacheline.

HOWEVER, severity is overstated. This is a self-contained in-memory model ("In-memory CXL poison list"), not a real-hardware parser. It is never fed actual cxl-cli/ndctl/QEMU poison-list output — the round-trip injects into and clears from the same in-process list, so the check only confirms the toolkit's own model is internally consistent. The actual hardware-facing path is sim/qemu/cxl_inject.py CxlInjector.inject_poison(path, start, length), which DOES correctly carry length through the cxl-inject-poison QMP command (verified by test_cxl_qmp_inject.py:41-47). So no real-hardware PASS/FAIL verdict, spec number, or parse depends on this clear() granularity; nothing in the field is misled. Per the project's severity rubric (critical = wrong verdict on real hardware; high = wrong number/spec value or real-hw misparse), this does not qualify. It is a fidelity/scope gap in a mock plus a tautological round-trip step — a genuine but low-impact weakness. The suggested fix (length-scoped clear with cacheline-aligned validation and entry splitting on partial overlap) would improve model fidelity but is not required to avoid misleading a hardware test today.  

**L39. check_maintenance is exercised only against the self-consistent Mock; tests cannot detect a wrong spec mapping**  
`src/computetest/cxl/maintenance.py` · tests/test_cxl_maintenance.py:18-41; maintenance.py whole module · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Every test drives check_maintenance with a fresh MaintenanceDevice(), the only implementation, whose perform() is hard-wired to return exactly the strings the checks compare against. The 'well-behaved flow' test is therefore a closed loop that passes by construction: it confirms the Mock agrees with itself, not that any spec contract holds. No test wires the maintenance flow to mailbox.py's CCI codes or to a real/QEMU backend, so the suite would still be green if the real-hw return-code semantics were wrong.  
- **Evidence:** test_well_behaved_flow: `h = check_maintenance(MaintenanceDevice(), op_class=MaintenanceClass.PPR); assert h.ok` and `assert h.checks == {... all True}`. The asserted dict is exactly what the Mock's fixed return strings produce. (I verified check_maintenance does correctly FAIL a deliberately broken device, so the verdict logic itself is fine — the gap is purely that nothing but the matched Mock is ever tested.)  
- **Fix:** Add a negative-path test that injects a non-conforming device (e.g. one that mutates on query, or never reports busy) and asserts h.ok is False with the specific failing check — and a test that drives the flow through mailbox.py return codes so the binding to the spec values is covered.  
- **Verifier:** The finding is factually accurate. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/cxl/maintenance.py, MaintenanceDevice is the sole implementation (no ABC/hierarchy), and its perform() returns hard-wired literals "busy"/"success"/"background" that check_maintenance compares against verbatim. In tests/test_cxl_maintenance.py, every check_maintenance call uses a fresh MaintenanceDevice(), and test_well_behaved_flow (lines 18-23) asserts checks == the exact dict the Mock produces — a closed loop that passes by construction. There is no negative-path test against a non-conforming device, and the flow is never bound to mailbox.py's numeric CciReturnCode values (SUCCESS=0x0000, BACKGROUND_STARTED=0x0001, BUSY=0x0009); maintenance.py uses bare strings and does not import from mailbox.py at all, so a wrong spec mapping would not be caught.

I independently verified the finding's parenthetical: subclassing MaintenanceDevice with a device that mutates on query and never reports busy yields h.ok=False with checks {'query_no_mutation': False, 'second_returns_busy': False}, so the verdict LOGIC is correct — the gap is purely test coverage.

I downgrade medium -> low. Real-world impact is limited: (1) the verdict logic is demonstrably sound, so there is no wrong PASS/FAIL on the current code; (2) this is not a real-hw/pragma path or a parser — it is an explicitly-scoped representative mock abstraction; (3) honesty discipline is observed — the module docstring expressly warns that only Opcode 0600h/PPR=01h are confirmed and that other codes and the binding to spec values must be taken from the CXL spec/CTS before being encoded as assertions, so the code does not fake or claim a conformance it cannot back. The finding identifies a legitimate but minor weak-test/coverage gap (tautological happy-path, no spec-binding test), not a defect that would mislead a hardware test today. The suggested fix (negative-path test + a test routing the flow through mailbox.py CciReturnCode values) is a reasonable improvement.  

**L40. Eye verdict ignores retimer.set_eye_thresholds while temperature honors set_tj_max — inconsistent threshold sourcing**  
`src/computetest/cxl/retimer.py` · src/computetest/cxl/retimer.py:49-53 (check_cxl_retimer) · _api-consistency_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** check_cxl_retimer drives the eye verdict from its own ui_min/mv_min args (CXL module defaults) via the module-level eye_quality_verdict, bypassing the per-instance _eye_ui_min/_eye_mv_min set by Retimer.set_eye_thresholds; yet temperature_ok() uses the per-instance _tj_max. An operator who tightens eye limits via the documented set_eye_thresholds API gets them silently ignored, while their set_tj_max takes effect — a confusing split that can pass a board the operator intended to fail.  
- **Evidence:** eyes_ok = all(eye_quality_verdict(retimer.read_eye(lane), ui_min=ui_min, mv_min=mv_min) ...)  # ui_min/mv_min are the function args, not retimer._eye_ui_min\n"temperature_ok": retimer.temperature_ok()  # this DOES use retimer._tj_max\nRuntime check: rt.set_eye_thresholds(ui_min=0.5, mv_min=200.0) then check_cxl_retimer(rt, ...) still returns ok=True because the 0.5/200 limits are never consulted.  
- **Fix:** Pick one source-of-truth and document it. Either default the eye thresholds from the retimer instance when the caller does not override (e.g. ui_min=ui_min or retimer._eye_ui_min) so eye and temperature both honor per-instance config, or add an explicit tj_max param so all three checks are arg-driven and symmetric.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced at runtime in src/computetest/cxl/retimer.py:49-53. check_cxl_retimer drives the eye verdict from its own ui_min/mv_min args (CXL defaults 0.25/30.0) via the module-level eye_quality_verdict, while temperature_ok() reads the per-instance _tj_max. Concretely: rt.set_eye_thresholds(ui_min=0.5, mv_min=200.0) on a board with 0.32UI/75mV eyes still returns ok=True (eye limits ignored), but rt.set_tj_max(50.0) with Tj=60C correctly yields temperature_ok=False. So the within-function sourcing IS asymmetric and undocumented — set_eye_thresholds is honored by base.py lane_status() (line 228) but NOT by the CXL check, whereas set_tj_max is honored by the CXL check. That is a real api-consistency wart and a plausible operator foot-gun, so it is confirmed rather than refuted/intentional. However I downgrade severity from medium to low: (1) the eye-via-args pattern is the consistent house convention — ddr5/eye.py and gmsl/checks.py also pass their own domain thresholds to eye_quality_verdict and likewise bypass the per-instance eye fields, and base.py:72 explicitly documents set_eye_thresholds as a lane_status/per-instance lever, not a module-check lever; (2) check_cxl_retimer already exposes ui_min/mv_min as explicit parameters, so the correct and conventional way to tighten CXL eye limits is right there on the same function signature — the wrong-PASS only occurs if an operator ignores those args and relies on set_eye_thresholds instead; (3) nothing fakes a PASS, hides a skip, or mislabels a verdict — every check reports honestly against the thresholds it was actually given. The suggested fix (ui_min=ui_min or retimer._eye_ui_min, or add a symmetric tj_max arg) is reasonable polish, not a field-critical defect.  

**L41. No test covers the empty-lanes false-PASS path**  
`src/computetest/cxl/retimer.py` · tests/test_cxl_retimer.py:31-34 (test_lane_subset) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** test_lane_subset only exercises lanes=[0,1]; no test passes lanes=[], so the vacuous-PASS behavior (finding #1) is uncovered and would not be caught by the suite even though it produces a wrong verdict.  
- **Evidence:** def test_lane_subset(self):\n    rt = MockRetimer(part_number='PT6161L')\n    h = check_cxl_retimer(rt, expect_part='PT6161L', lanes=[0, 1])\n    assert h.ok  — there is no lanes=[] case asserting a fail/raise.  
- **Fix:** Add a test asserting check_cxl_retimer(..., lanes=[]) either raises or returns ok=False, locking in whichever guard finding #1 adopts.  
- **Verifier:** Factually verified and reproduced. In src/computetest/cxl/retimer.py:48-50, when lanes=[] is passed (lanes is not None), lane_iter is empty and eyes_ok = all([]) = True vacuously. I reproduced this: check_cxl_retimer with injected_eye_ui=0.01/eye_mv=1.0 and lanes=[] returns ok=True with all_lanes_eye_open=True despite every lane's eye being far below threshold. The test-coverage claim is correct: tests/test_cxl_retimer.py is the ONLY test file exercising check_cxl_retimer, and its test_lane_subset (line 33) only passes lanes=[0,1]. No test in the suite passes lanes=[] to check_cxl_retimer (the lanes=[] hits in test_lmt_adapter.py/test_ocpdiag.py are an unrelated MarginResult dataclass). So a vacuous-PASS regression on the empty-lanes eye path would not be caught. This is not refuted by any guard, not intentional (the honesty-discipline convention argues against silent vacuous PASSes), and not a pragma real-hw path. Severity stays low rather than medium: triggering it requires an atypical caller bug (explicitly passing []), the default/normal path is lanes=None which checks all lanes and is well-covered, and part-identity + temperature checks still run — only the eye dimension goes vacuous. It is a genuine but minor coverage gap on a non-default input path. The suggested fix (add a lanes=[] test asserting fail/raise) is appropriate, though it depends on finding #1 first adopting a guard for the underlying behavior.  

**L42. Bias 95% CI uses fixed 1.96 normal quantile instead of t_{0.975,n-1}, understating the CI for small n**  
`src/computetest/msa/station_correlation.py` · bland_altman lines 137-140 · _correctness_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** ci95_bias = bias +/- 1.96*se_bias uses the normal quantile for all n. For the documented golden-DUT use case (small n; minimum n=2), the correct multiplier is t_{0.975,n-1} (e.g. 12.71 at n=2, 4.30 at n=3), so the reported bias CI is too narrow and could make a real bias look statistically insignificant.  
- **Evidence:** `ci_lo, ci_hi = bias - _LOA_Z * se_bias, bias + _LOA_Z * se_bias` with `_LOA_Z = 1.96`. The inline comment acknowledges this: 'We approximate t_{0.975,n-1} via the normal ... for small n add a t-table lookup later.'  
- **Fix:** Use a t-quantile for ci95_bias (small embedded t-table or an inverse-t approximation). Note _LOA_Z=1.96 is correct for the limits-of-agreement themselves (bias +/- 1.96*sd) per the Bland-Altman convention; only the CI on the bias should use t.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced directly in the code. In `bland_altman()` (station_correlation.py), the bias 95% CI is computed at lines 140/146 as `bias +/- _LOA_Z * se_bias` with `_LOA_Z = 1.96` (line 31, the normal 0.975 quantile), used for ALL n. The function enforces a minimum of n=2 (lines 129-130), where the statistically correct multiplier is t_{0.975,n-1} = 12.71 (n=2) or 4.30 (n=3), so the reported CI is materially too narrow for small n -- the finding's quoted t-values are correct. The inline comment at lines 137-139 explicitly acknowledges this ("We approximate t_{0.975, n-1} via the normal ... for small n add a t-table lookup later"), and the dataclass doc labels the field "95% CI for the bias (paired-t form)" -- a mild mislabel since it is actually a normal-approximation interval. The finding's note that 1.96 IS correct for the limits-of-agreement themselves (loa_lower/loa_upper, line 145) is also right; only the CI on the bias should use t.

Mitigating facts that cap severity: ci95_bias does NOT drive any automated PASS/FAIL verdict. StationPair.ok and DemingFit.agrees (lines 81-84, 104-105) use the Deming bootstrap CI, not the Bland-Altman bias CI. ci95_bias is a human-read diagnostic only (to_dict / summary). No verdict flips, so this is not critical/high. It is a wrong reported statistical number for the documented small-n golden-DUT use case, but it is an explicitly-documented approximation consistent with the toolkit's other normal/chi-squared approximations, and the gates pass. The slight "(paired-t form)" mislabel keeps it above a pure nit. Low severity is correct; not "intentional"-dismissed because it is a genuine numeric inaccuracy on the advertised small-n path, but the impact is limited to a non-verdict diagnostic.  

**L43. JSON fallback cannot parse genuine YAML; the 'works without PyYAML' claim is false for the bundled example**  
`src/computetest/msa/calibration_registry.py` · calibration_registry.py:113-121, docstring lines 15-16 and 96-98 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The `except ImportError` path falls back to json.loads on a .yaml file, but JSON is only a subset of YAML for JSON-shaped text. The bundled configs/calibration_registry.example.yaml uses YAML-only syntax (comments, unquoted strings, bare dates), so without PyYAML installed load_registry raises json.JSONDecodeError. The comment 'the registry works without PyYAML installed in CI' and docstring 'falls back gracefully' overstate this.  
- **Evidence:** `except ImportError:\n    import json\n    data = json.loads(raw_text)` with comment '# JSON is a strict subset of YAML 1.2; try it as a fallback so the # registry works without PyYAML installed in CI.' — verified json.loads on the example's `entries:\n  - role: rx_eye_scope` raises JSONDecodeError.  
- **Fix:** Either make PyYAML a hard dependency and drop the misleading fallback comment, or guard the JSON fallback and re-raise with a clear message ('install PyYAML to load YAML registries; JSON content only is supported as a fallback'). Reword the docstring to say JSON-shaped content only.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced end-to-end. load_registry (calibration_registry.py:112-121) falls back to json.loads(raw_text) on ImportError for `.yaml`/`.yml` files. The bundled configs/calibration_registry.example.yaml uses YAML-only syntax (leading `#` comment, bare keys `entries:`, block sequences `- role:`, unquoted bare dates), none of which json.loads can parse. Monkeypatching `import yaml` to raise ImportError and calling load_registry("configs/calibration_registry.example.yaml") raises json.JSONDecodeError ("Expecting value: line 1 column 1"). So the docstring claims (line 15-16 "falls back gracefully if PyYAML isn't installed"; line 97-98 "optionally JSON if PyYAML isn't installed") and the inline comment "the registry works without PyYAML installed in CI" overstate the behavior for the shipped example.

This is NOT covered by an existing guard/test: test_load_registry_from_yaml loads the example but the env always has PyYAML (6.0.3, pinned in dev deps), so it hits yaml.safe_load, never the fallback; test_load_registry_from_json_fallback writes JSON-shaped content to a .json path, which parses fine and also never tests YAML+no-PyYAML. PyYAML is genuinely optional (pyproject.toml line 14: `yaml = ["pyyaml"]  # ... JSON works without it`), so "without PyYAML" is a real supported install, not just a CI artifact — making the overstatement a legitimate honesty-discipline concern.

Severity stays low (matches the finding): the failure is loud (raises; does not fake a PASS, hide a skip, mislabel a verdict, or corrupt data) and only manifests in the PyYAML-absent path. The comment "JSON is a strict subset of YAML 1.2" is itself true, but the code relies on the invalid inverse (parsing YAML syntax with a JSON parser). Suggested fix is sound: reword docstring/comment to "JSON-shaped content only," re-raise with an actionable message, or make PyYAML a hard dependency.  

**L44. No test pins the UNC_* uncorrectable bit values to the PCIe spec (unlike the correctable bits)**  
`sim/qemu/inject.py` · tests/test_qmp_inject.py:107-110 (test_correctable_bit_values_match_engine) vs sim/qemu/inject.py:39-42 · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The correctable bits are guarded by test_correctable_bit_values_match_engine, but there is no equivalent assertion pinning UNC_DLP/UNC_POISONED_TLP/UNC_COMPLETION_TO/UNC_MALFORMED_TLP to their spec bit positions, so a wrong uncorrectable constant (the value injected on real hardware) would not be caught by the suite.  
- **Evidence:** Test pins only correctable: `assert (inject.COR_RX_ERR, inject.COR_BAD_TLP, inject.COR_BAD_DLLP, inject.COR_HDR_LOG_OVF) == (1 << 0, 1 << 6, 1 << 7, 1 << 15)`. The only uncorrectable check is incidental: `assert server.hmp_lines() == ["pcie_aer_inject_error nvme0 0x40000"]` which would still pass if UNC_MALFORMED_TLP were changed AND the same wrong constant were passed in the test (the test passes the constant explicitly, so it is self-referential for the value). UNC_DLP/POISONED_TLP/COMPLETION_TO have no value assertion at all.  
- **Fix:** Add `def test_uncorrectable_bit_values_match_spec(): assert (inject.UNC_DLP, inject.UNC_POISONED_TLP, inject.UNC_COMPLETION_TO, inject.UNC_MALFORMED_TLP) == (1 << 4, 1 << 12, 1 << 14, 1 << 18)` mirroring the correctable test. (The current values ARE correct per PCIe AER Uncorrectable Error Status reg; this just guards them against regression.)  
- **Verifier:** The core gap is real: tests/test_qmp_inject.py:107-110 (test_correctable_bit_values_match_engine) pins COR_RX_ERR/COR_BAD_TLP/COR_BAD_DLLP/COR_HDR_LOG_OVF to (1<<0,1<<6,1<<7,1<<15), and there is NO dedicated test pinning the UNC_* constants. I confirmed via grep that test_qmp_inject.py is the only test for sim/qemu/inject.py, and the only UNC constant it touches is UNC_MALFORMED_TLP. So UNC_DLP, UNC_POISONED_TLP, and UNC_COMPLETION_TO have zero value assertions anywhere — a regression in any of those three would not be caught. The current values (bits 4/12/14/18) are spec-correct per the AER Uncorrectable Error Status register, as the finding itself concedes.

However the finding is partly wrong and overstates impact: (1) Its claim that test_inject_uncorrectable_builds_hmp_command (line 98) is "self-referential for the value" is incorrect — the assertion compares against the hardcoded literal `0x40000`, NOT against inject.UNC_MALFORMED_TLP, so changing UNC_MALFORMED_TLP to a wrong value WOULD make that test fail.
