UNC_MALFORMED_TLP (the one constant wired as the inject_uncorrectable default) is therefore already effectively pinned. Only the three unused-as-default convenience constants are truly unguarded. (2) Real-world impact is low, not medium: these UNC_* constants live entirely on the QEMU/sim injection side (sim/qemu lane), and the actual hardware verdict engine (c/pcie_bert.c) masks uncorrectable with 0xFFFFFFFFu and counts via a persistent latch (account_uncorrectable) — it never decodes individual UNC bit positions, so a wrong UNC constant cannot flip a hardware PASS/FAIL verdict. The worst case is injecting the wrong error TYPE during a simulation exercise, which is a test-quality regression-guard gap, not a verdict/data-corruption risk. The suggested fix is reasonable and harmless, but this is a minor coverage nit, not a medium-severity weak test on a verdict path.  

**L45. Export tests are hasattr-only and would not catch a name silently dropped from __all__**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/__init__.py` · tests/test_nvme_mi.py:313-322 and tests/test_nvme_mi_extras.py:195-203 (test_public_exports) · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Both test_public_exports tests only assert hasattr(mi, sym) for a hardcoded expected set; they never assert that the package __all__ equals the imported set, so a regression that imports a symbol but drops it from __all__ (breaking `from ... import *` and tooling) would still pass.  
- **Evidence:** Test body is `for sym in expected: assert hasattr(mi, sym)`. hasattr succeeds for any module-level binding regardless of __all__ membership, and there is no reciprocal check that __all__ contains no extras or that star-import equals __all__. The module is currently correct (verified: star-import symdiff __all__ == empty, no missing, no dups), but the test does not guard it.  
- **Fix:** Add an assertion that `set(mi.__all__) == expected` (catches both drops and stray additions) and a `from computetest.nvme.mi import *` round-trip check that the resulting public namespace equals set(mi.__all__).  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced. Both export tests use only `for sym in expected: assert hasattr(mi, sym)` over a hardcoded set — tests/test_nvme_mi.py:313-322 and tests/test_nvme_mi_extras.py:195-203 — and neither references __all__ nor does a star-import round-trip (grep for "__all__" and "import *" in both test files returned NONE; no test anywhere in tests/ checks __all__). hasattr checks the module namespace, not __all__: in src/computetest/nvme/mi/__init__.py the symbols are bound via `from .submodule import (...)` statements (lines 28-68) independently of the __all__ list (lines 70-89), so removing an entry from __all__ while keeping its import leaves hasattr() True. I empirically confirmed this: a synthetic module with a symbol bound as an attribute but absent from __all__ still passes hasattr while `from ... import *` omits it. The module is currently correct (verified: len(__all__)==27, star-import symdiff __all__ == empty set, no dups), so this is not a live defect — it is a real but narrow test-quality gap that would let a future regression dropping a name from __all__ (breaking `from ... import *` and re-export tooling) pass undetected. Not intentional per conventions (no honesty-discipline/mock/pragma rationale) and not handled elsewhere. Impact is confined to import-star/tooling ergonomics, not a hardware PASS/FAIL verdict or spec value, so low severity is correct. The suggested fix (`assert set(mi.__all__) == expected` plus a star-import round-trip) is valid and one line.  

**L46. check_preemption discards all Verify/Respond handshake failure reasons from PreemptionHealth.notes**  
`src/computetest/tsn/preemption.py` · preemption.py:106-120 (check_preemption) · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** When the verify_respond_handshake check fails, the verdict flips to FAIL correctly but PreemptionHealth.notes contains no explanation, so a field engineer reading notes/to_dict() cannot tell why preemption failed (bad verifyTime vs. no Respond vs. late Respond).  
- **Evidence:** `frag_ok, notes = check_fragmentation(fragments, add_frag_size)` then `return PreemptionHealth(checks=checks, notes=notes)` — `notes` is sourced ONLY from check_fragmentation. run_verify_respond returns a bare bool and its three distinct failure paths (invalid verifyTime, got_respond False, respond_delay too large) emit no note. summary() lists the failing check key but to_dict()['notes'] stays empty for handshake-only failures.  
- **Fix:** Have run_verify_respond (or check_preemption) emit a reason string into notes for each handshake failure path, e.g. 'verifyTime out of 1-128ms range', 'no SMD-R Respond received', 'Respond after 0.8x window'. The verdict is correct; this is a reporting-completeness/honesty gap, not a wrong PASS.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced the core claim against /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/tsn/preemption.py. In check_preemption (lines 106-120), notes is sourced ONLY from check_fragmentation (line 114: `frag_ok, notes = check_fragmentation(...)`), and run_verify_respond (lines 71-80) returns a bare bool for all three failure paths (invalid verifyTime, got_respond False, late respond) with no string emitted. Verified at runtime: for a handshake-only failure with valid fragmentation, to_dict()['notes'] == [] while fragmentation failures DO emit descriptive notes (e.g. 'first fragment is not SMD-S', 'mCRC failure'). That asymmetry is real and confirmed. No guard/test covers it — test_handshake_failure_fails only asserts the bool/check key, never a reason note.

Two corrections to the finding that bear on severity: (1) The "bad verifyTime" case IS already distinguishable — verify_time_valid is a separate key in checks, so out-of-range verifyTime shows checks['verify_time_valid'] is False. (2) summary() and the checks dict already report that verify_respond_handshake failed, so the failure is NOT hidden or mislabeled and the verdict is correct (ok=False). The genuinely indistinguishable pair is only "no Respond" (got_respond False) vs "late Respond" (respond_delay too large): both yield identical checks and empty notes.

This is therefore a reporting-completeness gap, not a wrong PASS/FAIL, data corruption, wrong spec number, or real-hw crash. The failing check is still correctly named to a field engineer; they just cannot tell no-Respond from late-Respond from to_dict() alone. Per the severity rubric that is minor, so I downgrade from medium to low. The suggested fix (emit a reason string per handshake failure path) is reasonable and would make handshake diagnostics symmetric with the fragmentation path.  

**L47. run_verify_respond accepts a negative respond_delay_ms as a valid (in-window) Respond**  
`src/computetest/tsn/preemption.py` · preemption.py:80 · _error-handling_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A nonsensical negative respond_delay_ms passes the window test instead of being rejected as invalid measurement.  
- **Evidence:** Probe: run_verify_respond(10.0, respond_delay_ms=-5.0, got_respond=True) -> True, because `respond_delay_ms <= RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION * verify_time_ms` is satisfied by any negative value. A negative delay (Respond 'before' the Verify was sent) is physically impossible and likely indicates a measurement/parse error from real hardware.  
- **Fix:** Guard with `0 <= respond_delay_ms <= RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION * verify_time_ms` (or assert respond_delay_ms >= 0 and return False / note on negative), so a bad timing capture cannot silently produce a PASS.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced directly: run_verify_respond(10.0, respond_delay_ms=-5.0, got_respond=True) returns True. At preemption.py:80 the only window check is `respond_delay_ms <= RESPOND_WINDOW_FRACTION * verify_time_ms`, which any negative value satisfies. No lower-bound guard, abs(), or normalization exists anywhere in the module, and the tests (test_tsn_preemption.py lines 33-43) only exercise non-negative delays, so nothing handles or covers this case. A negative delay means the Respond (SMD-R) arrived before the Verify was sent — physically impossible and a sign of a measurement/parse error — yet the handshake reports PASS, which falls in the conventions' "fakes a PASS / mislabels a verdict" category rather than being intentional design (the docstring/header only document the 0.8x upper bound). Severity stays low: run_verify_respond/check_preemption are public conformance entry points with no in-repo caller that parses a measured respond_delay_ms from real hardware, so today the bad input can only arrive via a future/external measurement source; the impact is a latent edge-case that could silently convert a corrupt timing capture into a PASS, not a wrong verdict on data the codebase currently produces. Suggested fix (guard 0 <= respond_delay_ms <= window, or reject negatives with a note) is sound.  

**L48. Docstring claims a real-hw sysfs backend that does not exist**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/ras.py` · src/computetest/ddr5/ras.py:8-9 (module docstring); cross-checked ppr.py/ecs.py/ecc.py/soak.py signatures · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The docstring asserts 'the real backend reads/writes the sysfs nodes behind the same surface,' but there is no real backend, no mock_mode()/COMPUTETEST_BACKEND split, and no 'pragma: no cover - real-hw path' reading ecs_fruX/scrubX/mem_repairX anywhere; all consumers are typed to MockDdr5Ras only, so these DDR5 RAS checks can never run against real hardware.  
- **Evidence:** ras.py:8-9 'Modeled as a deterministic in-memory device (MockDdr5Ras) ... the real backend reads/writes the sysfs nodes behind the same surface.' grep for RealDdr5Ras/mock/Backend across ddr5/ras.py,ppr.py,ecs.py,ecc.py returns only this docstring line. By contrast memory.py:66 has '_read_edac() # pragma: no cover - real-hw path' behind a genuine mock_mode() split; check_ppr(ras: MockDdr5Ras,...), check_ecs(ras: MockDdr5Ras,...), soak_snapshot(ras: MockDdr5Ras) take the mock directly.  
- **Fix:** Either implement the real EDAC ecs_fruX/scrubX/mem_repairX read/write path (with the standard 'pragma: no cover - real-hw path' marker behind mock_mode()) as memory.py does, or correct the docstring to state plainly that only the mock is implemented and no real-hardware path exists yet, so a field engineer is not misled into thinking the ECS/PPR checks touch hardware.  
- **Verifier:** The finding's facts check out. src/computetest/ddr5/ras.py:7-9 says "Modeled as a deterministic in-memory device (MockDdr5Ras) ... the real backend reads/writes the sysfs nodes behind the same surface." That present-tense "the real backend reads/writes" asserts a real path as a current fact, but it does not exist: grep across src/ and tests/ finds no RealDdr5Ras, no mock_mode()/COMPUTETEST_BACKEND split in ddr5/, no "# pragma: no cover - real-hw path", and no code that reads/writes ecs_fruX/scrubX/mem_repairX. MockDdr5Ras is a concrete standalone class with no ABC/Protocol, and every consumer is typed directly to the concrete mock — check_ppr(ras: MockDdr5Ras), check_ecs(ras: MockDdr5Ras), check_ce_reporting/check_ue_reporting(ras: MockDdr5Ras), soak_snapshot(ras: MockDdr5Ras) — so there is not even a "surface" for a real backend to sit behind without changing signatures. The memory.py contrast is correct: src/computetest/memory.py imports mock_mode (line 19), branches on it (lines 82, 106), and has genuine real-hw EDAC sysfs readers behind "# pragma: no cover - real-hw path" (lines 66, 93-94, 108, 111, 123, 131).

This is also a real deviation from the project's own honesty convention: every sibling mock-only module hedges honestly — gmsl/serdes.py "room for a real ADI-register backend to drop in behind the same surface" (and it has a real SerDesLink ABC at line 253), tsn/phy.py "room for a real MDIO/register backend behind the same surface", cxl/mailbox.py "real ... backends plug in by subclassing" (MailboxTransport ABC). ras.py is the lone module stating the real backend already reads/writes hardware. So it is not intentional design nor already-handled; confirmed.

Severity downgraded from high to low. The rubric reserves high for a wrong number/spec value or a real-hw path that crashes/misparses; this is neither. There is no wrong spec value, no mislabeled verdict (the mock checks compute correctly), no data corruption, and literally no real-hw code that could crash. The only harm is a documentation overstatement that could mislead a field engineer into thinking ECS/PPR checks touch hardware. That is a documentation/honesty nit-to-low issue. I keep it at low (not nit) because it touches the project's explicitly-prized honesty discipline and the present-tense phrasing is materially more assertive than the careful "room for a real backend" wording its peers use. Suggested fix: change the docstring to match the peer convention, e.g. "and room for a real EDAC sysfs backend behind the same surface" rather than asserting one exists.  

**L49. Fabricated 0xF0-0xFE "reserved" EID range contradicts DSP0236; test locks in the wrong value**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:30,50-51 (is_reserved) and tests/test_nvme_mi.py:58-59 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The code classifies EIDs 0xF0-0xFE as MCTP "Reserved", but DSP0236 (MCTP Base) defines 0x08-0xFE as the full range of valid, assignable endpoint IDs; only 0x00 (null), 0x01-0x07 (reserved), and 0xFF (broadcast) have special meaning. The 0xF0-0xFE carve-out does not exist in the spec.  
- **Evidence:** Line 30 comment: `# * 0xF0–0xFE — Reserved`; line 51: `return (1 <= self.value <= 7) or (0xF0 <= self.value <= 0xFE)`; line 55 `is_routable` correspondingly truncates to `0x08 <= self.value <= 0xEF`. The test reinforces it: tests/test_nvme_mi.py:58-59 `for v in (0x01, 0x07, 0xF0, 0xFE): assert MctpEndpointId(v).is_reserved`. A real EID assigned in 0xF0-0xEF..0xFE would be mislabeled as reserved/non-routable.  
- **Fix:** Remove the 0xF0-0xFE reserved range: make `is_reserved` return only `1 <= value <= 7`, and `is_routable` return `0x08 <= value <= 0xFE`. Update the line 27-31 comment and fix tests/test_nvme_mi.py:58-59 to assert 0xF0/0xFE are routable (or at least not reserved).  
- **Verifier:** The finding is factually correct. mctp.py:30 comments "0xF0–0xFE — Reserved", line 51 returns reserved for (1<=v<=7) or (0xF0<=v<=0xFE), and line 55 truncates is_routable to 0x08<=v<=0xEF. Two independent searches of the authoritative DMTF DSP0236 (MCTP Base) content agree the spec EID map is: 0x00 null, 0x01-0x07 reserved, 0x08-0xFE assignable, 0xFF broadcast — there is NO 0xF0-0xFE reserved carve-out. So valid assignable EIDs 0xF0-0xFE are wrongly classified reserved and excluded from routable (0xEF cutoff). tests/test_nvme_mi.py:58-59 reinforces the wrong value (asserts 0xF0/0xFE are reserved), which also trips the project's honesty-discipline rule against tests locking in a spec value the code cannot back. Refutation attempts failed: no guard corrects it, the carve-out appears nowhere else in the repo (not an intentional convention), and it is not a mock/pragma exception. (Minor: the code comment misattributes the range to DSP0239; the EID table is in DSP0236, but the carve-out is wrong either way.) Severity downgraded from medium to low because grep shows is_reserved/is_routable are NOT consumed anywhere in src/ — they are dead helpers exercised only by the test, so no real-hardware PASS/FAIL verdict or routing decision is currently driven by the misclassification. It is a genuine latent spec-conformance defect plus a test asserting a wrong spec value, but with no active wrong-verdict impact today.  

**L50. EID ranges and IC bit cited to DSP0239, but they are defined in DSP0236 (MCTP Base)**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:26, 61, 85 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Line 26 attributes the EID address-range table and line 85 attributes the IC (integrity-check) bit at "§6.4" to DSP0239, but EID assignment ranges and the IC bit are normative parts of DSP0236 (MCTP Base Specification). DSP0239 is "MCTP IDs and Codes" — it owns the message-type code table (line 61 is defensible) but not EID ranges or the IC bit. The module's own opening docstring correctly cites DSP0236 for these, so the later references are internally inconsistent.  
- **Evidence:** Line 4: `MCTP (DSP0236) is the OOB wire ... The base spec defines a small fixed header ... an optional fragmentation+reassembly layer`. Then line 26: `# Per DSP0239 — MCTP Endpoint IDs (8-bit):` and line 85: `integrity_check: bool = False    # IC bit per DSP0239 §6.4`. A reader cross-checking conformance against DSP0239 will not find an EID range table or an IC-bit §6.4 there.  
- **Fix:** Re-attribute the EID-range comment (line 26) and the IC-bit comment (line 85) to DSP0236 (e.g. DSP0236 §8.2 EIDs, §7 header/IC bit). Leave the message-type table reference (line 61) as DSP0239 since code assignments live there.  
- **Verifier:** Factually correct spec-citation inconsistency, confirmed against the cited code at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py. Line 26 attributes the MCTP Endpoint-ID address-range table and line 85 attributes the IC (integrity-check) bit "§6.4" to DSP0239, but both are normative in DSP0236 (MCTP Base Specification): DSP0236 defines the MCTP transport/message header including the IC bit, and the special/reserved EID ranges (Null 0x00, reserved 0x01-0x07, routable 0x08-0xEF, reserved 0xF0-0xFE, broadcast 0xFF). DSP0239 (MCTP IDs and Codes) is a code registry; it owns the message-type code table only, so line 61/82's DSP0239 attribution for the message-type byte is correct and the finding rightly does not flag it. The misattribution also contradicts the module's own line-4 docstring, which correctly credits DSP0236 with the fixed header and IC layer — genuine internal inconsistency. Not protected by any project convention: the honesty-discipline carve-out covers deliberate None/raise and scheduled-debt placeholders, not an erroneous citation. However, severity stays low: the issue is entirely in comments — the encoded EID range values and IC semantics are themselves correct, and no verdict, numeric limit, parser, or runtime path is affected. It can only mislead a documentation/conformance-traceability reviewer (who would look in DSP0239 and find no EID-range table or IC-bit §6.4), never a hardware test result. Suggested fix is sound: re-attribute lines 26 and 85 to DSP0236, leave line 61 as DSP0239.  

**L51. Module docstring claims "encode + decode of headers" but no serialization exists**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:10-13 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The docstring says "This module models the framing layer (encode + decode of headers, EIDs, control vs message-type discrimination)", but the module contains no encode/decode/serialize logic at all — `MctpMessage` is a plain dataclass that is never turned into or parsed from DSP0236 wire bytes (no version byte, dest/src EID bytes, SOM/EOM/pkt-seq/TO/tag flag byte, or message body packing). The wording overstates what is implemented.  
- **Evidence:** grep for `encode|decode|struct|to_bytes|from_bytes|pack` in mctp.py matches only docstring prose (lines 6, 10) and a comment (line 78); there is no function that produces or consumes a byte string. Contrast messages.py which really does `encode_mi_request`/`decode_mi_response` with `struct.pack`.  
- **Fix:** Reword the docstring to match reality, e.g. "models the framing-layer data types (header field containers, EIDs, message-type discrimination) and a transport ABC; on-wire serialization is each binding subclass's responsibility." This is a doc fix, not a logic change — the absence of packetization is itself intentional (see below).  
- **Verifier:** Confirmed and accurately scoped. The module docstring at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:10-11 says the module "models the framing layer (encode + decode of headers, EIDs, control vs message-type discrimination)". A full read plus grep show the module contains NO serialization: it defines only data types (MctpError, the MctpEndpointId frozen dataclass with range/classification logic, the MctpMessageType IntEnum, the MctpMessage dataclass with tag validation + __len__) and the MctpTransport ABC + MockMctpTransport. There is no function that produces or consumes DSP0236 wire bytes — no version byte, no src/dest EID byte layout, no SOM/EOM/pkt-seq/TO/tag flag byte, no IC/type byte packing. grep for encode|decode|struct|to_bytes|from_bytes|pack matches only the docstring prose (lines 6, 10) and the comment on line 78. By contrast messages.py really does serialize (encode_mi_request/decode_mi_response with struct.pack/unpack at lines 123-137/140-150), and even that is the NVMe-MI NMP/MIC layer, not the MCTP header. MockMctpTransport.send/recv just shuttle dataclass objects in memory; the so-called "round trip" test (test_nvme_mi.py:86-110) never produces a byte string. So the wording "encode + decode of headers" overstates what the module implements.

Adversarial checks all hold: the claim is factually correct about the code; no guard/test covers it (none could — the logic doesn't exist, and this is a doc-accuracy issue not a code path); and while the ABSENCE of packetization is intentional and well-documented (line 78: reassembly is the transport's job; binding subclasses own wire framing), the docstring phrasing itself still claims active serialization the module does not perform.

Severity correctly stays low: this is purely descriptive module-docstring prose, not a verdict, number, faked PASS, or spec-compliance claim that could mislead a hardware test or corrupt data. Real-world hardware impact is none; the only harm is mildly misleading a maintainer into thinking serialization lives here. The suggested doc reword is appropriate.  

**L52. hmp() declared '-> str' but returns object from execute(); inject_aer calls .strip() on it**  
`sim/qemu/inject.py` · sim/qemu/inject.py:113-115 (hmp), 100-111 (execute), 121-124 (inject_aer) · _type-safety_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** execute() is annotated '-> object' and returns reply['return'] (any JSON type), but hmp() is annotated '-> str' and returns that object directly; inject_aer then calls out.strip(). mypy reports 'Incompatible return value type (got "object", expected "str")' at line 115, and this file is outside the mypy gate (files=["src"]) so it is never caught.  
- **Evidence:** Running `mypy sim/qemu/inject.py` reports: `sim/qemu/inject.py:115: error: Incompatible return value type (got "object", expected "str") [return-value]`. pyproject.toml has `[tool.mypy] files = ["src"]`, and this module lives under sim/qemu/, so CI never type-checks it. In practice QEMU's human-monitor-command always returns a JSON string so it won't crash, but the type contract is unsound.  
- **Fix:** Have hmp() coerce/validate: `ret = self.execute(...); if not isinstance(ret, str): raise QMPError(f'expected text from HMP, got {ret!r}'); return ret`. This also hardens the real-hw path against an unexpected non-string return rather than failing later inside .strip().  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly. At /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/sim/qemu/inject.py:115, hmp() is annotated `-> str` but returns `self.execute(...)` directly; execute() (line 100) is annotated `-> object` and returns untyped JSON `reply["return"]` (line 108). Running mypy on the file yields verbatim: `sim/qemu/inject.py:115: error: Incompatible return value type (got "object", expected "str") [return-value]`. inject_aer then calls `out.strip()` (line 123). The gate-exclusion claim also holds: pyproject.toml [tool.mypy] has `files = ["src"]` (lines 69-71), and `mypy` with the project config checks 81 source files and matches zero paths under sim/qemu/, so CI never type-checks this module — the unsoundness is real and uncaught. This is a genuine type-contract defect, not refuted/already-handled/intentional. However, severity is correctly low (borderline nit): QEMU's `human-monitor-command` contractually returns text, the test fake (tests/test_qmp_inject.py:58-64) always returns a JSON string, and the finding itself concedes it "won't crash" in practice. No wrong verdict, no data corruption, no crash under realistic operation — purely a latent type-annotation gap. The suggested isinstance-guard fix is a reasonable hardening but its protective value is speculative given HMP always returns a string.  

**L53. main(): --correctable '' (empty string) raises raw TypeError and is mis-classified, instead of a clean argparse error**  
`sim/qemu/inject.py` · sim/qemu/inject.py:152-153 · _edge-case_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `int(args.correctable or args.uncorrectable, 0)` crashes with TypeError when the chosen status string is empty, because `'' or None` evaluates to None and int(None, 0) is a TypeError; separately `bool('')` is False so an empty --correctable would be treated as uncorrectable if it didn't crash.  
- **Evidence:** Reproduced: `int('' or None, 0)` -> `TypeError: int() can't convert non-string with explicit base`. Line 152: `status = int(args.correctable or args.uncorrectable, 0)`; line 153: `correctable = bool(args.correctable)`. A user passing `--correctable ''` gets an unhandled traceback rather than ap.error(...).  
- **Fix:** Resolve the value explicitly by which flag was given (not truthiness): e.g. `raw = args.correctable if args.correctable is not None else args.uncorrectable; correctable = args.correctable is not None; try: status = int(raw, 0) except ValueError: ap.error('status must be an integer (e.g. 0x40)')`. This also fixes the correctable/uncorrectable misclassification for status '' and gives a clean error.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced through the real main() entry point at sim/qemu/inject.py:152-153. `inject.main(["--qmp","127.0.0.1:1","--id","rp0","--correctable",""])` raises an unhandled `TypeError: int() can't convert non-string with explicit base`. Mechanism is exactly as claimed: the other mutually-exclusive arg defaults to None, so `"" or None` -> None, and `int(None, 0)` is a TypeError. The misclassification half is also real in principle (`bool("")` is False, so an empty --correctable would be tagged uncorrectable), though the TypeError fires first so it's never reached for the empty case. No guard or test covers it: the only main() tests (test_qmp_inject.py:128-139) cover a valid status and bad --count. This is NOT a real-hw pragma path and NOT an honesty-discipline raise, so no convention excuses it. Severity stays low (not critical/high): this is a developer-facing CLI sim helper in sim/qemu/, and the impact is an ugly traceback instead of a clean ap.error() message on malformed CLI input — no wrong verdict, no data corruption, no hardware misparse. Worth noting the issue is broader than the empty-string slice: any non-numeric status (e.g. --correctable notanumber) likewise raises a raw ValueError, so the proper fix is wrapping int(raw, 0) in try/except and resolving the flag by `is not None` rather than truthiness.  

**L54. Bad-GPU test asserts only not ok, never that the row-remap or replay check is the one that failed**  
`src/computetest/gpu.py` · tests/test_gpu.py:9 (test_bad_gpu_reports_rma_history_separately_from_failures) and tests/test_interfaces.py · _test-quality_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The bad-GPU tests assert not h.ok and check history strings, but never assert checks["no_row_remap_failure"] is False or checks["replay<100"] is False, so a regression that broke the row-remap-failure or replay gate (while any other check still failed) would not be caught.  
- **Evidence:** test_bad_gpu_reports_rma_history_separately_from_failures: asserts `not h.ok` and history substrings only; the row_remap_failure=1 and replay_count=500 fields in _mock_metrics are never individually asserted against their checks.  
- **Fix:** Add explicit assertions that h.checks["no_row_remap_failure"] is False and h.checks["replay<100"] is False (and the temp/throttle checks) for the index==99 mock, so each individual gate has a regression guard.  
- **Verifier:** Partially confirmed at reduced scope; the finding overclaims. Verified against tests/test_gpu.py, tests/test_interfaces.py, tests/test_gpu_format.py and src/computetest/gpu.py.

ROW-REMAP HALF = REFUTED / already-handled. The finding claims a regression breaking `no_row_remap_failure` "would not be caught," and even cites tests/test_interfaces.py as a location. But test_interfaces.py:28 already has `assert h.checks["no_row_remap_failure"] is False` against gpu.check_gpu(99) (the index==99 mock with row_remap_failure=1). So that gate IS guarded, and the suggested fix to "add" that assertion duplicates an existing one.

REPLAY HALF = CONFIRMED, but narrow. No test anywhere asserts `checks["replay<100"] is False` (grep of tests/ confirms zero such assertions). Because GpuHealth.ok = all(checks.values()) (gpu.py:44-45) and several other checks for index==99 are already False (ecc, temp, throttle, row-remap, link gen/width), a regression to the replay comparison (`replay_count < replay_limit`, gpu.py:71) would not flip h.ok, so the `not h.ok` assertion in test_gpu.py:11 would still pass and the bug would slip through. The good path uses replay_count=0 (trivially passing), so it is no guard either. (link_gen_ok and link_width_ok are likewise individually unguarded, though the finding did not raise those.)

Real but minimal impact: the replay-gate logic is simple and correct today, and the production path is exercised; this is only a missing regression guard on one gate, not a wrong verdict or data corruption. Original "low" severity is accurate. The finding as written is materially inaccurate on its row-remap claim, so it should be narrowed to "replay gate lacks an individual regression assertion."  

**L55. no_thermal_throttle check is redundant under default thresholds and has no independent failing test**  
`src/computetest/tegra.py` · tegra.py:161,165-167 ; test_tegra.py:66 test_hot_fails_overtemp_and_throttle · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** With defaults throttle_temp_c=97 > max_temp_c=85, `no_thermal_throttle` can only fail when `max_zone>=97`, which simultaneously fails `max_zone<=85C`; the throttle check never fails independently at defaults. The only test that exercises a throttle failure (HOT at 99C) also fails the overtemp check, so a regression that broke throttle-by-inference alone would not be caught.  
- **Evidence:** throttle_inferred = max_zone >= throttle_temp_c (97); checks both include max_zone. HOT line is `GPU@99C ... CPU@90C` (99>=97 and 99>85) -> both checks fail together. No test sets max_temp_c above throttle_temp_c to isolate the throttle verdict.  
- **Fix:** Add a test that passes max_temp_c=100, throttle_temp_c=97 with a 98C line and asserts no_thermal_throttle is False while max_zone<=100C is True, isolating the throttle path; or document that throttle is intentionally a stricter-than-overtemp inference and only independent for custom thresholds.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced the finding's core claims against src/computetest/tegra.py and tests/test_tegra.py. At default thresholds throttle_temp_c=97 > max_temp_c=85, so throttle_inferred = max_zone >= 97 (tegra.py:161) can only be True when max_zone is also >85, meaning the overtemp check `0 < max_zone <= 85` (tegra.py:165) already fails. The sole throttle-failure test, test_hot_fails_overtemp_and_throttle (test_tegra.py:66) uses HOT = GPU@99C/thermal@98C/CPU@90C, where max_zone=99 trips BOTH checks together. I confirmed via a runtime probe that the throttle path can only fail independently (overtemp PASS, throttle FAIL) under custom thresholds, e.g. max_temp_c=100, throttle_temp_c=97, line GPU@98C -> checks {'max_zone<=100C': True, 'no_thermal_throttle': False}. No test in the suite exercises that combination (grep shows only nvme/gpu use custom temp args; tegra tests never pass max_temp_c/throttle_temp_c, though cli.py:292 does, so the path is reachable in the field). So the throttle verdict is never validated in isolation from overtemp — a genuine test-quality gap, correctly low severity (the code logic itself is correct; no field verdict is wrong). One caveat: the finding's evidence overstates impact by saying "a regression that broke throttle-by-inference alone would not be caught" — test_tegra.py:70 directly asserts h.checks['no_thermal_throttle'] is False for the HOT case, so a regression that fully disables throttle inference (or pushes its threshold above 99) WOULD be caught. The truly untested scenario is narrower: throttle failing while overtemp passes (the 85<temp<97-style band under custom thresholds). The suggested fix (a test with max_temp_c=100/throttle_temp_c=97/98C line isolating no_thermal_throttle=False while the overtemp check is True) is valid and cheap. Not intentional design and not covered by mock/pragma/honesty conventions — nothing fakes a pass; it is simply a missing isolating test.  

**L56. in_progress treats 0xF as an idle code, which is not a spec-defined 'no operation' value**  
`src/computetest/nvme/health.py` · health.py:175 (_real_self_test_log) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `in_progress = cur not in (0, 0xF)`. Per the NVMe Base Spec, Current Device Self-Test Operation bits 3:0 use 0h = no self-test in progress; 1h short, 2h extended, Eh vendor-specific. There is no 0Fh idle code (only the low nibble is defined; the full byte's value 0x0 is idle). Treating 0xF as idle is a benign superset today, but if a future/vendor code ever lands on 0xF (a running op) it would be wrongly classified as completed, flipping the passed verdict.  
- **Evidence:** in_progress = cur not in (0, 0xF)  
- **Fix:** Mask to the operation nibble and compare to 0 only: `in_progress = (cur & 0xF) != 0`.  
- **Verifier:** Verified against /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/health.py:175. The code is `in_progress = cur not in (0, 0xF)`, where `cur = raw.get("current_operation", 0)` comes from nvme-cli's `self-test-log -o json` (byte 0 of the Device Self-test log page 06h). The finding's spec claim is correct: per the NVMe Base Spec, the Current Device Self-Test Operation status lives in bits 3:0 with only 0h = "no operation in progress"; 1h short, 2h extended, Eh vendor-specific, and 3h-Dh + Fh are Reserved. There is no 0Fh "idle/no-operation" code, so including 0xF in the not-in-progress set is spec-unjustified. I could not refute it: no comment, commit message (last touched in Sprint 2.3 / c9d5d39, originally 131cc3a), or nvme-cli convention treats 15/0xF as a sentinel. This is a real-hw-only path (# pragma: no cover), and per project conventions a spec/logic bug inside such a path is a legitimate finding (no test exercises it; the lack of test is itself fine). Real-world impact is narrow, matching the reported "low": for any spec-compliant device the operation nibble is only 0/1/2/E, so the 0xF exclusion is inert and never changes a verdict. It can only bite a non-compliant/vendor device that reports the Reserved 0xF nibble while a test is actually running — then in_progress would be wrongly False and `passed = (not in_progress) and result == 0` could latch a stale prior result==0 into a premature PASS. The suggested fix `(cur & 0xF) != 0` is correct and strictly safer: it compares only the operation nibble to 0 and biases unknown/reserved codes toward "still in progress" (never a false pass). Confirmed, severity low (inert on all conformant hardware; only a non-compliant Reserved-code device triggers it).  

**L57. Corpus snapshot is a self-round-trip of the decoder against the mock, not a spec/real-device fixture**  
`src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py` · corpus/nvme_ocp_internal_log/synthetic-mock-1tb/{telemetry.json,strings.json,expected.json}; tests/test_nvme_ocp_telemetry.py:111-129 · _test-quality_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The corpus 'snapshot' test cannot detect a decoder bug: telemetry.json is byte-identical to the in-module _MOCK_TELEMETRY and strings.json to _MOCK_STRINGS, and expected.json holds exactly what the decoder emits for that input, so the assertion is tautological with respect to wrong key names/units/widths.  
- **Evidence:** corpus telemetry.json == _MOCK_TELEMETRY (same logId/header/dataAreas/sizeBlocks); expected.json total_blocks=48 == 32+16, data_areas=={'1':32,'2':16,'3':0,'4':0} — i.e. expected derived from the decoder, not from the OCP spec or a real dump. The directory is honestly named 'synthetic-mock-1tb' (not flagged as faked), but the test still gives false confidence in real-hw decode.  
- **Fix:** Add a corpus case captured from `nvme ocp internal-log` on a real OCP drive (or from the OCP spec's worked example) with hand-derived expected values, so the snapshot can fail if key names/units change. Keep the synthetic case but label it as a mock-only regression fixture.  
- **Verifier:** Factually accurate. The corpus inputs are content-identical to the in-module mocks: corpus/nvme_ocp_internal_log/synthetic-mock-1tb/telemetry.json matches _MOCK_TELEMETRY (ocp_telemetry.py:89-99) — logId 7, header version 1, host_initiated_data_generation_number 12, dataAreas {1:32,2:16,3:0,4:0}; strings.json matches _MOCK_STRINGS (lines 101-111). expected.json is exactly the decoder's own projection of those inputs (total_blocks 48 = 32+16, data_areas {"1":32,"2":16,"3":0,"4":0}, version 1, log_ids 7/201). The snapshot test (tests/test_nvme_ocp_telemetry.py:111-129) runs _parse_telemetry/_parse_strings on those inputs and asserts the parser's own output, so it is a self-round-trip that cannot fail if key names/units/widths drift — provided the corpus and expected are edited in lockstep. This fits the project's own 'weak test that would pass even if code were wrong' = medium definition.

Downgraded from medium to low for two reasons. First, the parser logic is NOT solely guarded by this redundant fixture: TestParserTelemetry/TestParserStrings provide independent unit tests with hand-written inputs AND hand-written expected values (data_areas=={"1":64,"2":0}, total_blocks==64, missing-header->None, non-dict body->0 blocks, default log_id 0xC9), which do catch key/unit/width regressions — so the practical 'false confidence in real-hw decode' risk is limited. Second, there is no wrong PASS/FAIL verdict, no wrong spec value, no real-hw crash — the only concrete defect-with-teeth is an honesty overclaim in corpus/nvme_ocp_internal_log/README.md, which states each subdirectory is 'one known drive family's verbatim nvme ocp internal-log -o json output,' while synthetic-mock-1tb is a copy of the module mock, not a verbatim real-device capture. The directory name (synthetic-mock-1tb) is honest, but the README label is not. Suggested fix stands: add a real-device or OCP-spec worked-example case with independently hand-derived expected values, and relabel/scope the README so the synthetic case is described as a mock-only regression fixture rather than 'verbatim' device output.  

**L58. Chain upstream segment with no error source is reported PASS, not skip (honesty gap mirroring the --no-bert fix)**  
`src/computetest/diagnostics.py` · diagnostics.py:245-252 (diagnose_chain segment loop) / ChainSegmentResult.status · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** If an upstream chain BDF exposes neither AER nor Device Status, aer.read_errors returns ErrorReading(0,0,'none'); the segment is then scored 'pass' even though no error measurement was possible — the same false-PASS the single-device path explicitly fixed for --no-bert.  
- **Evidence:** r = aer.read_errors(backend, m.bdf, aer.error_source(backend, m.bdf))\n        ... cor = [...]; unc = [...]\n        ok = not unc and len(cor) <= upstream_correctable_ok\n        segments.append(ChainSegmentResult(m.bdf, m.direction, cor, unc, "pass" if ok else "fail"))\nWith error_source=='none', read_errors returns ErrorReading(0,0,'none') (aer.py:221), so cor=unc=[] and ok=True -> 'pass'. ChainSegmentResult.status comment even says only '"pass" | "fail"' — there is no skip channel. Contrast bert.BertResult.status which returns 'skip' when aer_available is False, and PcieDiagnostic.status which returns 'skip' for an unmeasured device.  
- **Fix:** Capture r.source (or aer.error_source) per segment; when it is 'none', mark the segment status 'skip' (and surface it in reasons/summary), and let ChainDiagnostic.status fold 'skip' through to a skip verdict rather than pass — matching the single-device honesty rule. Update the ChainSegmentResult.status docstring to include 'skip'.  
- **Verifier:** The mechanic is real and reproducible. In diagnose_chain (diagnostics.py:245-252), each upstream segment is scored via `ok = not unc and len(cor) <= upstream_correctable_ok` then `"pass" if ok else "fail"`. If a segment BDF exposes neither AER nor Device Status, aer.error_source returns 'none', aer.read_errors returns ErrorReading(0,0,'none') (aer.py:221), so cor=unc=[] and the segment is reported "pass". ChainSegmentResult.status is documented as only "pass"|"fail" (line 140) and ChainDiagnostic.status (line 170-174) only folds the *endpoint* BERT's skip through — a segment skip has no channel at all. This is a genuine asymmetry against the single-device path, which was explicitly fixed for exactly this honesty gap (PcieDiagnostic.status returns "skip" when bert is None or bert.status=="skip", with dedicated tests test_diagnostics.py:88 and :113) and against bert.BertResult.status which returns "skip" when aer_available is False. No existing test exercises a 'none'-source chain segment.

HOWEVER, severity must be downgraded from high to low because the triggering condition is not realistically reachable on conforming hardware. error_source returns 'none' only when find_ext_cap(AER) is None AND read_device_status is None; the RealBackend.read_device_status (backend.py:369-371) returns None only when find_cap(bdf, CAP_PCIE) is None, i.e. the device has no PCI Express Capability at all. But every member of a PCIe link chain — root port, switch upstream/downstream port, endpoint — has a mandatory PCIe Express Capability (that is what makes it a PCIe device on the path); link_chain itself is derived from the sysfs PCIe realpath (backend.py:383-389), so a non-PCIe device cannot be a chain member. Thus a 'none'-source upstream segment is a degenerate, essentially-impossible hardware state. Every realistic segment failure (correctable/uncorrectable accrual) is still caught, and the endpoint-skip path is honest. So this is a real but low-impact honesty/consistency gap against the project's own standard — it would not mislead a real hardware test in any conforming topology. The suggested fix (capture r.source, mark 'skip' when 'none', fold through ChainDiagnostic.status, update the docstring) is correct and cheap, but its field value is minor.  

### Nit (45)

**N1. Gen6 FEC burst-length histogram over-counts symbols (shares sum to 1.004, leftover guard never subtracts)**  
`src/computetest/backend.py` · MockBackend.read_fec_stats, backend.py:646-656 · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The per-bucket share expression assigns 0.002 to buckets 6, 7 AND 8 (the chained-ternary final `else 0.002` covers all three), so the eight shares sum to 1.004, not 1.0; the histogram therefore reports MORE bursts than there are pre-FEC symbol errors, and the `if pre > counted` leftover guard only adds to bucket 8 when under-counting, never trims the over-count.  
- **Evidence:** share = (0.6 if b == 1 else 0.25 if b == 2 else 0.10 if b == 3 else 0.04 if b == 4 else 0.008 if b == 5 else 0.002)  # b in 6,7,8 all get 0.002 -> sum=1.004 ; then `counted = sum(hist.values()); if pre > counted: hist[8] += pre - counted`. Verified: pre=1000 yields hist sum 1004.  
- **Fix:** Give buckets 7 and 8 distinct (smaller) shares or set the >=8 bucket as the explicit remainder, and assert/normalize so sum(hist.values()) == pre_fec_symbol_errors. E.g. compute buckets 1..7 from shares that sum to <1 and set hist[8] = pre - sum(hist[1..7]).  
- **Verifier:** The arithmetic claim is factually correct and reproducible. In MockBackend.read_fec_stats (backend.py:646-656) the chained ternary `... else 0.008 if b == 5 else 0.002` assigns 0.002 to buckets 6, 7 AND 8, so the eight shares (0.6+0.25+0.10+0.04+0.008+0.002+0.002+0.002) sum to 1.004. For pre=1000 the histogram sums to 1004, and the `if pre > counted: hist[8] += pre - counted` leftover guard only fires on under-count, so it never trims this over-count. I reproduced this directly.

However, the real-world impact is negligible, so I downgrade from low to nit:
- This is the MockBackend (laptop simulation), not a `# pragma: no cover - real-hw` parser, and lines 643-645 explicitly label the histogram "a model — real silicon would surface a vendor burst counter." Under the mock-fidelity/honesty conventions it is a deliberate placeholder, not a claimed spec or hardware value.
- No verdict, validator, or test depends on the histogram sum. The only FEC value that drives a PASS/FAIL (fber, with the LESS_THAN_OR_EQUAL 1e-6 validator in ocpdiag.py:288) is computed from post/flits, entirely independent of the burst histogram. The OCP-diag emitter (ocpdiag.py:290-292) emits each bucket count as an unvalidated standalone measurement. The only histogram test (test_bert_fec.py:75-83) asserts that hist[1] dominates and never checks the sum, so it passes regardless. No PASS/FAIL is mislabeled and no real-hw reading is corrupted.
- The finding's implied invariant (sum of burst counts == symbol errors) is itself conceptually loose: for a length-keyed burst histogram, symbols relate to sum(length*count), not sum(count). The code's own leftover guard already conflates burst count with symbol count, so the inflation is one small slip atop an already-simplified model.

Net: a genuine but cosmetic arithmetic slip that slightly inflates the simulated/displayed burst counts (by ~0.4%) in mock-only output, with no effect on any hardware verdict. Worth a one-line fix (make bucket 8 the explicit remainder) but properly a nit, not low.  

**N2. Mock Device-Status always reports uncorrectable as Non-Fatal, never Fatal**  
`src/computetest/backend.py` · MockBackend.read_device_status, backend.py:582-591 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** An injected uncorrectable fault (inject_uncorr_bit) always maps to DEVSTA_NONFATAL (bit 1) in the no-AER Device-Status path; the mock can never set DEVSTA_FATAL (bit 2), so the coarse decode mislabels a fatal uncorrectable as 'NonFatalDetected'. Verdict is unaffected (any uncorrectable already triggers an immediate fail in bert.py:261 and aer.read_errors masks both bits), but the reported error class is wrong.  
- **Evidence:** if d._uncor_status:\n    val |= DEVSTA_NONFATAL  # never DEVSTA_FATAL, regardless of which uncorr bit was injected  
- **Fix:** Map known fatal AER uncorrectable bits (e.g. SurpriseDown bit 5) to DEVSTA_FATAL, or add a MockDevice flag to mark an injected uncorrectable as fatal vs non-fatal, so the devstatus-fallback decode matches the real hardware's bit.  
- **Verifier:** The code behavior is exactly as described. In MockBackend.read_device_status (backend.py:586-591) any non-zero _uncor_status maps only to DEVSTA_NONFATAL; DEVSTA_FATAL is never set, even when inject_uncorr_bit names a default-fatal AER error (e.g. bit 5 SurpriseDown, bit 4 DLP). The real-hw read_device_status (backend.py:369-371) reads the raw register and CAN return the Fatal bit, and the decode table (aer.py:160-162) defines FatalDetected for bit 2, so the coarse devstatus-fallback decode will report 'NonFatalDetected' where real hardware could report 'FatalDetected'. The verdict is genuinely unaffected: bert.py:261 fails on any unc_total>0, and aer.read_errors masks DEVSTA_NONFATAL|DEVSTA_FATAL together (aer.py:219). So this is real but purely a cosmetic label gap in the coarse no-AER fallback path. Downgraded from low to nit because: (1) it changes no number, verdict, or spec-compliance claim and never fakes a pass; (2) the toolkit nowhere models the AER Uncorrectable Error Severity register, which in the PCIe spec is configurable, not fixed per-bit, so the mock has no principled basis to declare any bit 'fatal' and defaulting to NonFatal is a conservative (never over-claiming) choice; (3) the finding's own suggested fix (hardcode SurpriseDown->Fatal) would itself be a fidelity simplification since real severity is software-configurable. Real but negligible-impact mock-label cosmetic gap.  

**N3. MockDevice._min_speed_seen is written every read_link_status but never consumed**  
`src/computetest/backend.py` · MockDevice._min_speed_seen backend.py:440; write at backend.py:541 · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `_min_speed_seen` is updated on every read_link_status (d._min_speed_seen = min(d._min_speed_seen, speed)) but no code in the package ever reads it, so the transient-downtrain low-water-mark it is meant to record is dead state and cannot be asserted on or surfaced.  
- **Evidence:** grep shows only the initializer (=99) and the single min() write; no reader anywhere in src/. The downtrain verdict relies solely on the _bw_latched LBMS/LABS flag, not on _min_speed_seen.  
- **Fix:** Either expose _min_speed_seen via a test helper / LinkStatus field so a slow poller can detect a dip that recovered before snapshot, or remove the field if the LBMS/LABS latch already covers the case.  
- **Verifier:** Factually reproduced. `MockDevice._min_speed_seen` is initialized at backend.py:440 (=99) and written every read_link_status at backend.py:541 (d._min_speed_seen = min(d._min_speed_seen, speed)). An exhaustive grep across the whole repo (src, tests, scripts) shows NO reader of this field — only the initializer and the single min() write. No dynamic access (vars/__dict__/getattr/asdict) consumes it either. It is not surfaced in the returned LinkStatus dataclass (backend.py:104, fields are speed/width/training/bw_changed/autonomous_bw/dl_active), so a caller cannot observe it. The genuine transient-downtrain low-water-mark is implemented independently in linkstate.py: LinkHealth.min_speed is updated at linkstate.py:124 (health.min_speed = min(health.min_speed, ls.speed)) from the RETURNED LinkStatus.speed value, fed into speed_degraded (linkstate.py:57) and tested at test_linkstate.py:71 ("the dip was caught"). So the intended functionality already exists, fed by the visible speed field rather than this mock-internal one — confirming _min_speed_seen is genuine write-only dead state, not a mock-fidelity feature that models consumed dynamics. Real-world impact is nil: it lives only in Mock-backend internal accounting, never affects a verdict, returned value, or hardware path; it cannot cause a wrong PASS/FAIL, data corruption, or real-hw crash. Hence a true defect but correctly rated nit. Suggested fix (remove it; LBMS/LABS latch + LinkHealth.min_speed already cover transient dips) is sound.  

**N4. Result reports n bits while verdict was computed on max(n,1.0) in the conductor**  
`src/computetest/bert.py` · bert.py:455 and bert.py:460 · _api-consistency_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** run_conductor computes the verdict with `ber.assess(cor_total, max(n, 1.0), ...)` but constructs BertResult with the raw `n`, so BertResult.bits and BertResult.verdict.bits can disagree when n is 0 — unlike run_bert which deliberately reports verdict.bits for consistency (see its comment 'Report the SAME bits the verdict was decided on').  
- **Evidence:** Line 455: `verdict = ber.assess(cor_total, max(n, 1.0), target_ber, confidence, unc_total)`; line 460: `return BertResult(bdf, elapsed, n, ...)`. run_bert instead passes `verdict.bits` (line 300) precisely to keep them equal (test_bert.py::test_deterministic_clock_injection asserts `r.bits == r.verdict.bits`). The conductor path has no equivalent assertion and would violate it at n=0.  
- **Fix:** Pass `verdict.bits` (not `n`) into the BertResult in run_conductor, matching run_bert; or compute assess on `n` directly and guard division-by-zero inside assess. n=0 only arises in degenerate runner output, so impact is low, but the inconsistency defeats the stated P0-2 invariant for the C path.  
- **Verifier:** Factually verified: bert.py:455 computes the verdict via ber.assess(cor_total, max(n, 1.0), ...) so verdict.bits == max(n,1.0), while bert.py:460 builds BertResult with raw n. At n==0 these disagree (BertResult.bits=0.0 vs verdict.bits=1.0). run_bert deliberately keeps them equal by passing verdict.bits (line 300, comment line 299), and test_bert.py:59 asserts r.bits == r.verdict.bits for run_bert only. No conductor test asserts that invariant, and tests/test_conductor.py never exercises n==0 (its fake runner always returns bits=BPS*secs). So the inconsistency is real and reproducible.

However the finding overstates impact and partly misdescribes the code: (1) There is no documented "P0-2 invariant" anywhere — grep finds no such reference; it is only an inline comment in run_bert, not a project-wide contract. (2) The suggested "guard division-by-zero inside assess" rationale is moot — assess/confidence_le/ber_upper_bound already guard bits<=0 (ber.py:170, 200); I confirmed ber.assess(0, 0.0, ...) returns CL=0.0, ber_ub=inf with no crash. So max(n,1.0) is not a divide-by-zero guard. (3) verdict.status is overwritten on line 456 regardless, so neither n nor max(n,1.0) affects pass/fail. The divergence is purely the reported bits field, 0.0 vs 1.0, on a multi-Gb/s link, and only in a degenerate runner-output case (loop always runs >=1 window; bps<=0 returns early). It cannot mislead a hardware verdict. Real but cosmetic — a nit, not low.  

**N5. error_source recomputed twice per upstream BDF**  
`src/computetest/diagnostics.py` · diagnostics.py:234 and 246 · _performance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** aer.error_source(backend, m.bdf) is evaluated once to arm/clear (line 234) and again to read (line 246); each call does find_ext_cap and possibly read_device_status. It is harmless on the mock and the source is stable across the window, but it is redundant config-space traffic on real hardware and a place the two calls could disagree if a cap appeared/vanished mid-window.  
- **Evidence:** Arm: aer.clear_errors(backend, m.bdf, aer.error_source(backend, m.bdf)). Read: r = aer.read_errors(backend, m.bdf, aer.error_source(backend, m.bdf)). Two separate error_source() evaluations for the same BDF.  
- **Fix:** Compute `src = aer.error_source(backend, m.bdf)` once per BDF (store alongside the member) and reuse it for both clear_errors and read_errors; this also gives you the value needed to emit a 'skip' for source=='none' (Finding 1).  
- **Verifier:** Factually accurate: in diagnose_chain (src/computetest/diagnostics.py), aer.error_source(backend, m.bdf) is evaluated twice per upstream BDF — line 234 (arm/clear, first loop over `upstream`) and line 246 (read, second loop over `upstream`). Each call runs aer_base -> backend.find_ext_cap (a config-space capability walk) and possibly backend.read_device_status (aer.py:192-198), so the redundant config-space traffic on real hardware is real. However, this is purely a performance/cleanliness nit with no real-world test impact: it cannot change a PASS/FAIL verdict, the source is stable across a BERT window, and the cost (a few extra TLPs per BDF) is negligible against the 30s stress window. The "two calls could disagree if a cap appeared/vanished mid-window" framing is not a credible hardware failure mode — PCIe extended capabilities do not materialize/vanish mid-window absent a hot-reset/re-enumeration, which would already invalidate the topology/BDF list captured at line 224. Per project conventions, pure-style/perf nits are low value. The suggested fix (compute src once per member and reuse) is sensible refactoring but fixes no functional defect. Severity correctly remains nit.  

**N6. summary() prints cosmetic 'Gen0' for a transient zero observed during watch**  
`src/computetest/linkstate.py` · linkstate.py:78,84,72 (summary min/GT formatting) · _correctness_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** If a mid-watch read returns speed/width 0 (transient parse glitch), min_speed/min_width become 0 and summary renders '/min Gen0' or '/min x0' and 'Gen0(0GT/s)', which is misleading text though the verdict (degraded) is correct.  
- **Evidence:** `health.min_speed = min(health.min_speed, ls.speed)` can drive min to 0; summary then does `lo = f"/min Gen{self.min_speed}"` and `LINK_SPEED_GTPS.get(self.speed, 0)` -> '0GT/s'. Verified output: 'Gen4/min Gen0<Gen4'. Verdict is still DEGRADED so no false pass.  
- **Fix:** Render 0/unknown mins as 'unknown' rather than 'Gen0'/'x0' in summary(), e.g. special-case min<=0.  
- **Verifier:** Reproduced exactly. Via the watch loop in check_link (linkstate.py:124-125), a transient real-hardware LnkSta read of speed/width 0 (link drop / Recovery with no negotiated speed; RealBackend decodes ls & 0xF at backend.py:365, which can be 0) drives health.min_speed/min_width to 0. __post_init__ (lines 35-39) only SEEDS these and is bypassed because the watch loop mutates the field after construction, so min CAN be 0. The summary() degraded branch (lines 78, 84) then renders "/min Gen0" and "/min x0" — e.g. "DEGRADED(SPEED Gen4/min Gen0<Gen4,WIDTH x16/min x0<x16)", matching the evidence. The Mock caps dips at max(1, speed-2) (backend.py:540) so this manifests only on real hardware. So the cosmetic rendering claim is technically correct.\n\nBut the impact is purely cosmetic, correctly a nit: the verdict is right (speed_degraded/width_degraded True, ok False, no false PASS, no honesty-discipline violation). The text is arguably NOT misleading — "min Gen0"/"min x0" accurately conveys that the link momentarily dropped to no negotiated speed/width, which is genuinely distinct from and worse than a partial downtrain like "min Gen2"; the suggested "unknown" fix would conflate a mid-watch link drop with a sysfs/enumeration parse failure and lose information. The secondary evidence about the header "Gen0(0GT/s)" only occurs in the speed_unknown branch, which is already explicitly labeled "SPEED unknown (sysfs/enumeration)" honestly. Confirmed mechanism, nit severity, debatable whether it should change at all.  

**N7. matches() re-coerces with base-10 int(), inconsistent with load_config's base-0 to_int**  
`src/computetest/topology.py` · topology.py:49-53 (DeviceExpectation.matches) · _api-consistency_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** matches() calls int(m['vendor_id']) (base 10), so a DeviceExpectation constructed directly with a hex-string match value (e.g. '0x10DE') raises ValueError, whereas the same string survives load_config's base-0 to_int.  
- **Evidence:** Lines 49/51/53 use `int(m['vendor_id'])` etc. `int('0x10DE')` -> ValueError (base 10), while load_config's to_int uses `int(v, 0)`. Through load_config the values are already ints so int(int) is a harmless no-op; the inconsistency only bites a caller who builds DeviceExpectation directly with hex strings. Tests only pass ints to direct construction (test_topology_edges.py:15), so this path is untested.  
- **Fix:** Use a single coercion helper (base-0) in both places, or store match values already-normalized to int in DeviceExpectation and have matches() compare ints directly without re-calling int().  
- **Verifier:** The factual claims hold. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/topology.py:49-53, matches() calls int(m['vendor_id']) (base 10); verified that int('0x10DE') raises ValueError while load_config's to_int (line 88) uses int(v, 0) which yields 4318. So a DeviceExpectation built directly with a hex-string match value would crash in matches(), an inconsistency with the load_config path. This is technically a real api-consistency wart, hence not refuted. However the real-world impact is near-zero, so I downgrade to nit: (1) The only supported/documented construction path is config files via load_config (the sole production caller is harness.py:66), which always normalizes match values to int through to_int before constructing DeviceExpectation, making the int() in matches() a harmless no-op (int(int)). (2) No production code path constructs DeviceExpectation directly with hex strings; tests only pass already-int values to direct construction. (3) Crucially, if the path were ever hit it raises a loud ValueError, not a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict or silent mismatch, so it cannot mislead a hardware test in the field. The finding's own confidence is medium and it acknowledges the no-op nature. A redundant base-10 re-coercion on an unused, undocumented path that would crash rather than fake a result is a minor cleanup, not a hardware-impacting defect.  

**N8. load_fixture_map leaks the file handle**  
`src/computetest/fixtures.py` · fixtures.py:166 load_fixture_map · _error-handling_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `text = open(path).read()` never closes the file object; it relies on CPython refcount GC and would warn/leak under PyPy or with __debug__ ResourceWarning enabled.  
- **Evidence:** text = open(path).read()  # file handle never explicitly closed  
- **Fix:** Use a context manager: `with open(path, encoding='utf-8') as fh: text = fh.read()` (also pins encoding so a non-UTF-8 locale on a station doesn't misread the map).  
- **Verifier:** Code at fixtures.py:166 is verbatim `text = open(path).read()` — the file object is never explicitly closed and no encoding is pinned. The factual claim is correct: it relies on CPython refcount GC to close the FD, and the rest of the codebase consistently uses the `with open(...) as fh:` idiom (memory.py:125/133, backend.py:273/316, cli.py:367/369, topology.py:75, tegra.py:213, gmsl/video.py:86/91), so this and msa/calibration_registry.py:112 are the two outliers. So it is a real, accurately-described code-quality issue, not refuted/already-handled/intentional. However, the real-world impact is negligible, so the original "low" overstates it: (1) load_fixture_map runs once at station startup to read one small config file, not in any real-hardware measurement or verdict path; (2) under CPython — the realistic deployment — the temporary file object is refcount-GC'd immediately after .read() returns, so the FD closes deterministically and there is no actual leak; the PyPy/ResourceWarning concern is theoretical for a single once-per-session read; (3) it cannot produce a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict, data corruption, crash, or spec/units error. The encoding pin in the suggested fix is a reasonable defensive add but addresses an edge case (config files are effectively always UTF-8/ASCII), not an observed bug. Net: a legitimate cleanup (use a context manager + encoding="utf-8"), correctly downgraded to nit per the impact-based severity rubric.  

**N9. Module/stress docstrings claim a soak 're-read' that stress_memory does not perform**  
`src/computetest/memory.py` · src/computetest/memory.py:6 and 105 (module docstring + stress_memory docstring) · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=uncertain  
- **Claim:** Both docstrings imply stress_memory provokes errors and then counts/re-reads EDAC, but the function only returns stressapptest's exit code and never re-reads EDAC; no caller in the module re-reads after the soak.  
- **Evidence:** Module docstring: 'Optionally drives a stressapptest soak and re-reads.' stress_memory docstring: 'Run a stressapptest soak (provokes errors EDAC then counts). Returns success.' Yet `_real_stress_memory` returns only `subprocess.run(...).returncode == 0`; grep shows no source caller does check_memory() after stress_memory(), and ddr5/soak.py uses its own MockDdr5Ras.read_edac(), not this module.  
- **Fix:** Reword to 'runs a stressapptest soak; caller should call check_memory() before and after to diff CE/UE,' or add an orchestrator that snapshots EDAC, runs the soak, re-reads, and returns the delta — matching what the docstring promises.  
- **Verifier:** The finding's factual claims are all verified true: in src/computetest/memory.py, stress_memory (line 104) / _real_stress_memory (line 111) only run stressapptest and return `returncode == 0` — they never re-read EDAC; no module/CLI caller chains check_memory() after stress_memory() (grep confirms only test callers exist); and src/computetest/ddr5/soak.py:30 uses a separate MockDdr5Ras.read_edac(), not this module's check_memory(). So the docstrings (module line 5-6 "drives a stressapptest soak and re-reads"; stress_memory line 105 "provokes errors EDAC then counts. Returns success.") are loosely worded relative to what the single function does.

However, this is NOT the kind of honesty defect the project conventions target (faking a PASS, hiding a skip, truncating coverage, mislabeling a verdict, or claiming unbacked spec compliance). No verdict or hardware data is affected: stress_memory honestly returns the soak's exit code, and check_memory honestly computes EDAC limits. The module docstring fairly reads as describing the module's collective capability — it does provide both the soak driver (stress_memory) and the EDAC re-read (check_memory) — and the function's "(provokes errors EDAC then counts)" reasonably describes the soak's PURPOSE (provoke errors so EDAC's counters catch them) with an accurate "Returns success." The re-read is the caller's step, exactly as ddr5/soak.py frames it ("a caller can snapshot ... and diff before/after"). It would not mislead a hardware test into a wrong pass/fail. This is at most a minor wording-clarity item; per conventions, pure-wording nits are low value and downgraded. The suggested reword is a reasonable polish, but I cannot demonstrate a real defect, so this does not rise above nit.  

**N10. check_ethernet has no docstring at runtime — the intended docstring is a dead string statement placed after the first executable line**  
`src/computetest/ethernet.py` · ethernet.py:78-83 (check_ethernet) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The triple-quoted block documenting throughput gating sits AFTER `_validate_iface(iface)`, so it is not the function docstring; check_ethernet.__doc__ is None and the explanation is invisible to help()/tooling.  
- **Evidence:** def check_ethernet(...):\n    _validate_iface(iface)\n    """Check link/speed/errors...""" — verified check_ethernet.__doc__ is None.  
- **Fix:** Move the docstring to the first statement of the function body (above `_validate_iface(iface)`).  
- **Verifier:** Factually confirmed. At ethernet.py:78-83, `_validate_iface(iface)` is the first executable statement and the triple-quoted block follows it on lines 79-83, making it a no-op string expression, not a docstring. Runtime check confirms `check_ethernet.__doc__ is None`. The throughput-gating explanation is therefore invisible to help()/introspection tooling. This is a real defect, but impact is purely cosmetic/documentation: the runtime behavior is unchanged (the string is evaluated and discarded), no verdict, number, spec value, or real-hw parse is affected, and the actual throughput-gating logic (lines 85, 113-114, only checking throughput_ok when iperf=True) is correctly implemented. Ruff's enabled rule set (E,F,W,I,B,UP) does not include pydocstyle D-rules, so passing gates don't contradict the finding. Severity correctly stays at nit per the convention that pure documentation/style issues are low value.  

**N11. XID 92 mislabeled as "contained ECC" (it is "High single-bit ECC error rate")**  
`src/computetest/gpu.py` · gpu.py:28-29 (_XID_CRITICAL) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The _XID_CRITICAL table labels XID 92 as "contained ECC", but per NVIDIA's published XID reference 92 = "High single-bit ECC error rate"; 94 = "Contained ECC error" and 95 = "Uncontained ECC error". The displayed/reported reason for code 92 is therefore wrong.  
- **Evidence:** _XID_CRITICAL = {... 92: "contained ECC", 94: "contained ECC", 95: "uncontained ECC"}  — 92 and 94 carry the same "contained ECC" label even though 92 is a high-SBE-rate event, not a contained ECC event.  
- **Fix:** Relabel 92 to "high SBE rate" (e.g. 92: "high SBE rate", 94: "contained ECC", 95: "uncontained ECC"). The FAIL verdict for 92 can stay (a high SBE rate is worth flagging), but the technician-facing reason must match the spec.  
- **Verifier:** The spec claim is factually correct: per NVIDIA's published XID reference, code 92 = "High single-bit ECC error rate", 94 = "Contained ECC error", 95 = "Uncontained ECC error". At /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gpu.py:28 the _XID_CRITICAL table labels 92 as "contained ECC" — a duplicate of 94's label — which mislabels a high-SBE-rate event. So the inaccurate label genuinely exists (confirmed).

HOWEVER the finding's stated impact is wrong, which forces a severity downgrade. The label STRINGS in _XID_CRITICAL are dead values — they are never displayed, reported, or surfaced to any technician. The only two consumers are gpu.py:66 (`bad_xid = [c for c in m.get("xid_errors", {}) if c in _XID_CRITICAL]`) and tegra.py:162 (`bad_xid = [c for c in xids if c in _XID_CRITICAL]`); both test KEY membership only and never read `.values()`/`.items()`. There are no `_XID_CRITICAL[...]` lookups anywhere in src/ or tests/. The technician-facing output surfaces only the integer code: GpuHealth.summary() at gpu.py:52 does `xids = ",".join(str(c) for c in m.get("xid_errors", {}))` -> e.g. `xid=92`, and TegraHealth.summary() (tegra.py:75) does the same; to_dict() likewise emits only codes. So the finding's central premise — "The displayed/reported reason for code 92 is therefore wrong" / "the technician-facing reason must match the spec" — is false: no reason string is ever displayed.

The verdict is also unaffected and correct: XID 92 stays in the FAIL set (membership only), and a high SBE rate is legitimately worth flagging — the finding itself concedes the FAIL should stay. There is no wrong PASS/FAIL, no wrong reported value, no real-hw misparse. This is purely an inaccurate inline documentation string in a lookup table whose values are never read. The fix (relabel 92 to "high SBE rate") is worthwhile housekeeping, but the real-world impact is nil, so this is a nit, not medium.  

**N12. pcie.replay.counter treated as a per-test/volatile metric but it is cumulative since driver load**  
`src/computetest/gpu.py` · gpu.py:8-11 (docstring) vs gpu.py:71/148/158 (replay_count) · _honesty_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The docstring states pass/fail uses VOLATILE counts ("errors since the last reset — i.e. during THIS test"), and replay_count participates in that pass/fail (replay<replay_limit). But nvidia-smi's pcie.replay.counter is a lifetime/since-driver-load counter with no per-test reset, so a healthy board with historical replays can fail a fresh test, or the threshold can mask new replays accumulated on top of a large baseline.  
- **Evidence:** "pcie.replay.counter" queried at gpu.py:148, used as m.get("replay_count",0) < replay_limit at gpu.py:71; docstring at gpu.py:8 promises volatile/this-test semantics.  
- **Fix:** Either snapshot replay_count at test start and compare the delta over the window, or document that replay_count is a cumulative counter and that replay_limit is an absolute (not per-test) ceiling, so the field reading is not misrepresented as volatile.  
- **Verifier:** The finding's factual core checks out. (1) nvidia-smi's pcie.replay.counter (queried at gpu.py:148) is indeed a cumulative counter that resets only on driver reload/reboot, not per-test. (2) replay_count does participate in pass/fail via `m.get("replay_count", 0) < replay_limit` at gpu.py:71 (default replay_limit=100, used by both callers cli.py:308 and harness.py:106). (3) The docstring at gpu.py:8 literally says "Pass/fail uses VOLATILE counts (errors since the last reset — i.e. during THIS test)." So there is a genuine mismatch: the blanket "Pass/fail uses VOLATILE counts" overstates, since replay (and also temp/link-gen/width/throttle, which the finding omits) are not per-test/volatile.

But the real-world impact is minimal, so I downgrade to nit rather than the claimed low:
- The "VOLATILE" wording is NVIDIA's own ECC term, and the docstring's second sentence (lines 9-11) is entirely about ECC volatile-vs-aggregate and row-remap RMA history — its actual subject. Replay is not even mentioned there.
- It does not cause a wrong PASS/FAIL in normal use: test stations boot fresh, so the counter starts near 0; treating replay_limit as an absolute link-quality ceiling (100 replays => degraded link) is a defensible, arguably correct design, and the mock's 500-bad/0-good values are consistent with absolute-ceiling semantics. The "mask new replays on a large baseline" scenario requires a long-running un-rebooted driver session — an edge case.
- No other doc/code propagates a "replay is per-test" claim.

Net: a real but minor documentation-imprecision (honesty) issue, not a verdict-affecting defect. The suggested fix (document replay_limit as an absolute, not per-test, ceiling — or snapshot a delta) is reasonable. Relevant: /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gpu.py:8-11, :71, :148; tests/test_gpu.py (only ECC volatile/aggregate semantics are tested, not replay semantics).  

**N13. Setpoint formatting with %g silently rounds to 6 significant figures**  
`src/computetest/instruments.py` · instruments.py:417,419,500,508,512,630,634,742 (set_voltage/current/etc. using {x:g}) · _correctness_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** All numeric setpoints use Python's default %g (6 significant figures), so a 7-sig-fig setpoint is silently truncated before it reaches the instrument (e.g. 3.3000001 V -> 'VOLT 3.3', 1234567.89 -> '1.23457e+06').  
- **Evidence:** Verified: PowerSupply.set_voltage(3.3000001) writes 'VOLT 3.3'; set_voltage(1234567.89) writes 'VOLT 1.23457e+06'. For most bench resolutions this is within tolerance, but a margining loop expecting sub-uV/high-precision steps loses the request silently.  
- **Fix:** If higher precision is ever needed for margin steps, use an explicit format such as {x:.9g}; otherwise document the 6-sig-fig limit in the setter docstrings so callers know the instrument-side resolution floor.  
- **Verifier:** The mechanical claim is factually correct and reproducible. All setpoint setters in /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/instruments.py use `{x:g}`, which is Python's default %g = 6 significant figures: `f"VOLT {3.3000001:g}"` -> "VOLT 3.3", `f"VOLT {1234567.89:g}"` -> "VOLT 1.23457e+06" (verified). This applies to PowerSupply.set_voltage/current (417,419), ElectronicLoad.set_current/voltage/resistance/power (502,506,510,514), SMU.set_voltage/current/set_compliance (632,636,647), SMU.sweep_voltage step writes (688), and ExternalBERT.set_rate/set_amplitude/run_window (742,746,758).

However, the real-world impact is effectively nil, which is why I downgrade to nit:
1) 6 significant figures exceeds the programming resolution of every instrument this module targets. Even the precision SMUs named in the docstring (Keysight B2902 family) cannot set a sub-uV step at a 3.3 V operating point; the discarded 7th sig fig (3.3000001 V) is below the instrument's own DAC resolution, so nothing is "lost" that the hardware could have honored. The 1234567.89 example (1.23 MV) is not a realizable setpoint on a bench supply/DMM/load/SMU.
2) The set_rate case (line 742) is safe for all standard rates: 53.125 Gbps PAM-4 is only 5 sig figs, 64/32/16/8 are trivial.
3) This is purely on the write/program path. Readback uses parse_scpi_float on the instrument's actual returned bytes, and Reading.raw preserves the verbatim SCPI string, so measurement fidelity and every PASS/FAIL verdict are unaffected — nothing fakes a pass, hides a skip, or mislabels a verdict.
4) The finding itself already concedes "For most bench resolutions this is within tolerance" and its suggested fix is to document the limit, i.e. it is acknowledged as a doc nit, not a defect.

Tests (tests/test_instruments.py:189, tests/test_instruments_hal.py:118-120, tests/test_gmsl_shmoo.py:25) only assert on values within 6 sig figs, so they neither catch nor are misled by this. Confirmed as a true (if minor) behavior, but true severity is nit, not low — it would not mislead a hardware test in the field.  

**N14. metadata dicts bypass the scalar guard and are silently stringified by json.dumps(default=str)**  
`src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py` · src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py:97 (_write json.dumps default=str), 171-172/206-207/190-191 (metadata=dict(metadata)) · _type-safety_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** measurement/series values go through _scalar (raise on non-scalar), but metadata values do not; a non-JSON-serializable metadata value (e.g. an enum, a numpy scalar, a datetime) is silently coerced to its str() instead of raising, so the emitted value can be wrong without any error.  
- **Evidence:** self._stream.write(json.dumps(obj, default=str) + "\n"). metadata is passed through verbatim via m["metadata"] = dict(metadata) with no per-value check; default=str swallows any non-serializable value into a string rather than failing loudly.  
- **Fix:** Either drop default=str (let json.dumps raise on bad data so the bug surfaces) or shallow-validate metadata values to scalars/JSON-native types before emitting. At minimum document that metadata values must be JSON-serializable.  
- **Verifier:** The mechanism is factually accurate and I reproduced it. In _write (src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py:97) the emitter calls json.dumps(obj, default=str). measurement value / series_element value are guarded by _scalar (lines 161-163, 199-201) which raises on non-string|bool|number, but metadata is passed verbatim via dict(metadata) at lines 171-172, 190-191, 206-207 with no per-value check. I confirmed with a repl that a plain (non-str/non-int) Enum, datetime, or similar non-JSON-native metadata value is silently coerced to its str() (e.g. {"v": "C.A"}) by default=str instead of raising, so the asymmetry the finding describes is real and untested (tests/test_ocpdiag.py only asserts native int metadata like {"lane": 0} at line 412).

However, real-world impact is minimal, so I downgrade to nit: (1) No current caller can trigger it. The only external metadata caller is cxl/emit.py:67 which passes rec.record_type.value (EventRecordType is a `str, Enum`, so .value is a plain str) and int(rec.log) — both JSON-native; the two internal metadata call sites (emit_margin_series {"lane": int(...)}, emit_chain {"downstream_bdf": li.downstream_bdf} typed str) also pass native scalars. (2) metadata is purely descriptive context — it never feeds a verdict, a measured value, or a validator comparison (validator values ARE guarded at line 64), so even an oddly-stringified metadata value cannot flip a PASS/FAIL or corrupt a measured number. It is a latent type-safety gap on a public API surface, not a defect that would mislead a hardware test verdict in the field.
