The suggested fix (validate metadata to scalars or document JSON-serializable requirement) is a reasonable hardening, but this does not rise above nit given no trigger and no verdict/data impact.  

**N15. Constructor accepts out-of-range / non-7-bit I2C address without validation**  
`src/computetest/pcie/retimer/aries.py` · aries.py:66-78 (__init__), self.address · _real-hw-path_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** address is stored verbatim with no bounds check; a 7-bit I2C target address must be 0x00-0x7F, so an invalid value (e.g. 0x155) is silently retained and would only surface as an opaque SDK/transport error once _open_sdk is wired up on real hardware.  
- **Evidence:** `def __init__(self, part_number: str = "PT5161L", *, address: int = 0x55, ...)` ... `self.address = address` — no range check, while part_number IS validated against _KNOWN_PARTS two lines above.  
- **Fix:** Validate in __init__: `if not 0 <= address <= 0x7F: raise RetimerError(f"I2C address {address:#x} out of 7-bit range [0x00,0x7f]")`, mirroring the existing part-number guard.  
- **Verifier:** Factually accurate: aries.py:78 stores `self.address = address` verbatim with no bounds check, while `part_number` IS validated against `_KNOWN_PARTS` at lines 70-72. No guard or test covers address range (grep confirms `address` appears only at lines 66/78/91 in the module, and TestAriesStub has no address test). The repo even has a direct precedent for this: nvme/mi/mctp.py validates MctpEndpointId against `0x00 <= value <= 0xFF` in __post_init__, so range-validating an I2C/endpoint address is an established in-codebase convention the constructor here omits. So the finding is not refutable. However, severity is correctly a nit, not higher: (1) it has zero current impact because `_open_sdk()` always raises NotImplementedError before `self.address` is ever consumed (the address is dead today); (2) even once the SDK is wired, an out-of-range address fails LOUD at the SDK/transport layer (no device responds), it does not produce a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict or corrupt data, so the honesty-discipline and wrong-verdict concerns do not apply; (3) this is a robustness/input-validation gap, not a parsing/units/spec-math bug. The suggested fix (mirror the part-number guard with a 0x00-0x7F check) is reasonable and consistent with the mctp pattern. Confirmed at nit severity.  

**N16. _normalize_smart_keys mutates the caller's dict in place**  
`src/computetest/nvme/health.py` · health.py:109-113 (_normalize_smart_keys) · _api-consistency_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The function adds keys to the dict argument and returns it, so callers sharing the dict see side effects. In check_nvme the dict is freshly parsed so it is harmless, but the tests pass dict(raw) defensively, indicating the in-place mutation is a known footgun.  
- **Evidence:** for src, dst in _SMART_KEY_ALIASES.items():\n    if src in smart and dst not in smart:\n        smart[dst] = smart[src]\n    return smart — mutates the input.  
- **Fix:** Operate on a copy: `out = dict(smart); ...; return out`, so the function is pure.  
- **Verifier:** The factual claim is accurate: `_normalize_smart_keys` (health.py:109-113) mutates its `smart` argument in place (adds canonical keys) and returns the same object, so it is not pure. However, the real-world impact is nil, as the finding itself concedes. The sole production caller, `check_nvme` (real-hw path, lines 126-128), creates the dict fresh via `json.loads(...)` and immediately rebinds `smart = _normalize_smart_keys(smart)`; no other reference to that dict exists, so no caller observes a surprising side effect. The function is module-private and idempotent (line 111 guards `dst not in smart`, confirmed by test_normalize_is_idempotent), so even repeated calls are safe. The tests using `dict(raw)`/`dict(once)` are reasonably explained by wanting to reuse the original literal for later assertions, not necessarily a documented footgun. There is no wrong verdict, data corruption, misparse, or units bug — nothing that would mislead a hardware test. The suggested copy-on-write fix is harmless and slightly cleaner, but this is a pure-style purity observation against passing gates, so it is a nit, not low.  

**N17. data_units_written history flag fires at ~51 GB written — far below any 'significant lifetime' bar**  
`src/computetest/nvme/health.py` · health.py:75-76 (_history) · _correctness_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** data_units_written is in units of 1000*512 bytes per the NVMe spec, so the >100000 threshold trips at only ~51.2 GB written — burn-in or a brief test write on a new TB-class drive would tag it as 'significant lifetime writes'. This is a soft history signal (not a fault gate), so it cannot cause a wrong PASS/FAIL, but it will over-report used-stock.  
- **Evidence:** if smart.get("data_units_written", 0) > 100000:\n    h.append("significant lifetime writes")  # 100000 units = 51.2 GB  
- **Fix:** Raise the threshold to a meaningful fraction of drive capacity (e.g. derive from id-ctrl tnvmcap) or document the 51 GB intent; consider reporting the actual GB written in the history string.  
- **Verifier:** Spec citation and arithmetic check out. Per the NVMe Base Spec, SMART data_units_written is reported in thousands of 512-byte units (1 unit = 1000*512 = 512,000 B), so health.py:75 `> 100000` trips at 100000*512000 = 51.2 GB written — confirmed. A TB-class drive after burn-in or even a short test write would easily exceed that, so the "significant lifetime writes" line over-reports used-stock. The defect is real and reproducible.

However impact is minimal. _history() is a soft, non-verdict signal by explicit design: the module docstring (lines 8-10) and the field comment at line 31 ("used-stock flags (not faults)") establish that history never feeds `ok`, which is derived solely from `_apply_limits` checks (line 36). The finding itself concedes "it cannot cause a wrong PASS/FAIL." So there is no false PASS/FAIL, no data corruption, no crash, no faked spec-compliance claim, no real-hw misparse — none of the high/critical triggers in the severity rubric. The only effect is a possibly-spurious informational string in the history list (test test_nvme_format.py:34-42 only checks the >threshold/empty branches, not calibration).

This is a genuine but cosmetic calibration weakness on an advisory output, below the "low = minor real impact" bar, so I downgrade from low to nit. The suggested fix (derive a threshold from id-ctrl tnvmcap, or report actual GB) is reasonable polish but not required for correct hardware testing.  

**N18. emit_to_ocpdiag always emits a hardcoded PASS diagnosis even when the drive recorded a fatal error**  
`src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py` · src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py:240-243 (emit_to_ocpdiag) · _honesty_ · conf=medium · verdict=uncertain  
- **Claim:** The diagnosis verdict is unconditionally '.pass' / type_='PASS' regardless of decoded content, so a drive whose C9h strings include a non-empty last_fatal_error (or zero data areas) still produces a step-level PASS verdict; an RMA/MT pipeline that keys on the diagnosis verdict rather than re-parsing each measurement would treat a faulted drive as healthy.  
- **Evidence:** em.diagnosis(verdict=f"nvme.ocp_internal_log.{device}.pass",\n                 type_="PASS",\n                 message=f"OCP internal-log decoded for {device}")  
- **Fix:** Either keep PASS but rename the verdict to make clear it only asserts 'decode succeeded' (e.g. message stays but verdict 'nvme.ocp_internal_log.decoded'), OR derive the verdict from content: emit FAIL/UNKNOWN when strings.get('last_fatal_error') is non-empty or telemetry.total_blocks == 0. Today the verdict claims more than the decode actually verifies.  
- **Verifier:** The finding is factually correct about the code: emit_to_ocpdiag (src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py:240-243) unconditionally emits verdict=...pass / type_="PASS" / step_end("pass") independent of decoded content, and it is the lone adapter in ocpdiag.py that does NOT derive its verdict from a content status (cf. emit_bert/emit_pcie_diagnostic/emit_health which key on result.status, diag.status, h.ok).

But the framing as a real defect is substantially overstated, and the suggested fix is spec-wrong: (1) This is an informational ingest/decode step, not a health gate — the module docstring and docs/research/2026-05-29-roadmap.md:138-141 describe it as shelling out and "ingesting the result into the ocp-diag schema" to surface decoded telemetry/strings (model, firmware, boot_count, build_signature, sled_id) as measurements. Actual NVMe health gating lives in nvme/health.py and nvme/mi/controller_health.py keyed on critical_warning, not here. (2) The proposed content-based FAIL conditions would introduce false-FAILs: C9h last_fatal_error is a HISTORICAL string (the last fatal error the drive ever recorded — a healthy qualified drive routinely carries one from a past power-loss/reset), and total_blocks==0 is a normal healthy state (the mock itself has data areas 3 and 4 at 0 blocks). Gating FAIL on either would be incorrect. (3) The OcpStringsResult/OcpTelemetryResult dataclasses intentionally carry no ok/health concept, so there is no content-derived status to faithfully render — unlike BertResult/Health. (4) No internal consumer gates on this verdict; it is emitted only to the external ocp-diag stream.

What survives is a mild honesty looseness: type_="PASS" asserts slightly more than "decode succeeded," and a neutral diagnosis (the _diag_type UNKNOWN default already exists in ocpdiag.py:40,248-249) or a renamed verdict (...decoded, per the first half of the suggested fix) would be more honest. That is a low-severity labeling nit, not a medium-severity wrong-PASS/FAIL or data-corruption defect — it corrupts no real check because no pass/fail check is defined for this debug/inventory log page. Marking uncertain (genuine but minor honesty kernel; defect framing and the content-based fix are not independently reproducible/correct) and downgrading severity to nit.  

**N19. log_id measurements labelled unit='hex' but emitted as decimal integers**  
`src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py` · src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py:220-221,232-233 (emit_to_ocpdiag) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** ocp.telemetry.log_id and ocp.strings.log_id are emitted as value=int(...) (7 and 201) with unit='hex'; a consumer trusting the 'hex' unit would mis-read 201 as the literal value when the spec log id is C9h, so the unit label contradicts the decimal value actually emitted.  
- **Evidence:** em.measurement(name="ocp.strings.log_id", value=int(strings.log_id), unit="hex")   # strings.log_id == 201 (0xC9)  
- **Fix:** Drop the misleading unit (omit unit, since a log identifier is dimensionless) or emit the value as a hex string (e.g. f"0x{strings.log_id:02X}") if the 'hex' presentation is intended. As-is the unit is purely cosmetic and the value carries no base information.  
- **Verifier:** The code does what the finding describes. In emit_to_ocpdiag (src/computetest/nvme/ocp_telemetry.py:220-221 and 232-233) both log_id measurements are emitted as JSON integers — value=int(telemetry.log_id)==7 and value=int(strings.log_id)==201 (0xC9) — while carrying unit="hex". The emitter (src/computetest/io/ocpdiag.py:156-166) treats unit as a free-form annotation written verbatim into the measurement; it does not transform the value. Every other unit in the codebase is an actual physical unit ("count","lane","ns","GT/s","bitmask"), so labeling a base-10 integer "hex" is inconsistent and mildly misleading to a human reader. So the core observation is real and reproducible.

However the finding's escalated harm model is not realistic: in the ocpdiag schema unit is a human-readable label with no machine semantics for numeric radix, and a JSON number 201 is unambiguously decimal — no conformant consumer would re-read 201 as 0x201/513 based on the unit string. The value emitted is correct; only the cosmetic label is off. This never affects a PASS/FAIL verdict, never corrupts data, does not fake a pass/hide a skip/mislabel a verdict, and is not even on a real-hw parsing path (the log_id integer is parsed correctly by both mock and real parsers; tests test_emit_strings_only / test_emit_telemetry_only confirm the measurements emit fine). Per the project conventions, pure presentation nits are low value. The finding rated it "low" by leaning on an unrealistic mis-parse scenario; the true severity is a nit (cosmetic metadata inconsistency, zero impact on any verdict, number, or hardware behavior). Suggested fix (drop unit or emit f"0x{log_id:02X}") is reasonable polish but optional.  

**N20. IC bit and message-type byte cited to DSP0239 instead of DSP0236**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:61, 85 (and EID allocation comment line 26) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** Lines 61/85 cite 'DSP0239 §6.5' for the message-type byte and 'DSP0239 §6.4' for the IC bit, but the IC bit and the MCTP message body/type-field layout are defined in DSP0236 (MCTP Base Specification); DSP0239 (MCTP IDs and Codes) is only the message-type *code* registry. The module's own header docstring (line 4) correctly attributes the base spec to DSP0236, so the inline citations are internally inconsistent and would mislead a reviewer checking traceability.  
- **Evidence:** `MCTP_CONTROL = 0x00 ... Per DSP0239 §6.5.` and `integrity_check: bool = False    # IC bit per DSP0239 §6.4`, versus line 4 `MCTP (DSP0236) is the OOB wire...`.  
- **Fix:** Cite DSP0236 for the IC bit and message-body structure; keep DSP0239 only for the numeric message-type code assignments (and even there, EID allocation is a DSP0236 §8.2 concept).  
- **Verifier:** The core spec-attribution claim is factually correct, verified against the DMTF specs. The IC (integrity check) bit and the message-body first-byte layout (IC bit as MSB, 7-bit message-type field in the LSBs) are defined structurally in DSP0236 (MCTP Base Specification), while DSP0239 (MCTP IDs and Codes) is only the registry of the numeric Message Type *values*.

Confirmed against the code at /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:
- Line 85 `integrity_check: bool = False  # IC bit per DSP0239 §6.4` — clear misattribution; the IC bit is a DSP0236 concept, not DSP0239.
- Line 26 `# Per DSP0239 — MCTP Endpoint IDs (8-bit)` with null/reserved/routable/broadcast ranges — these EID-range semantics are a DSP0236 (§8.x endpoint-ID) concept, also loosely attributed to DSP0239.
- Line 4 header docstring correctly says "MCTP (DSP0236) is the OOB wire," so the inline §6.4/§6.5 DSP0239 citations are internally inconsistent with the module's own header — a reviewer checking traceability would be misled.

Partial caveat that lowers severity: line 61 `Per DSP0239 §6.5.` sits on the MctpMessageType IntEnum, which actually enumerates the message-type *code* assignments (0x00 control, 0x01 PLDM, 0x04 NVMe-MI, etc.) — those codes genuinely ARE DSP0239. The imprecision is only that the same docstring also describes the DSP0236 byte layout. So line 61's citation is partly defensible.

Impact is comment/documentation-only: every value the code uses (EID ranges, message-type codes, IC-bit semantics) is correct; there is no wrong verdict, no wrong numeric spec value fed into a check, and no real-hw parsing bug. It does not fake a PASS, hide a skip, or claim unbacked spec compliance. This is a minor traceability imperfection, so I downgrade the finding's self-rated "low" to "nit." Suggested fix is sound: cite DSP0236 for the IC bit and message-body layout, and keep DSP0239 only for the numeric message-type code assignments.  

**N21. No guard on non-monotonic timestamps despite 'monotonic ms' contract**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/aem.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/aem.py:66, 89-106 (timestamp_ms handling) · _error-handling_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** AsyncEventMessage.timestamp_ms is documented as 'monotonic ms since subscription open' and sequence as 'monotonic per-subscription', but deliver() never validates that timestamp_ms is non-decreasing; a backwards timestamp silently produces negative deltas that are always < coalesce_window_ms (so it coalesces) and never resets the rate window, which can wedge delivery.  
- **Evidence:** Lines 96/105 compute `timestamp_ms - self._window_start_ms` and `timestamp_ms - self._last_emit_ms` with no lower bound. Reproduced: HEALTH@1000 (True) then HEALTH@500 returns False (coalesced via negative delta). For a deterministic mock this is benign, but on the real-hw path a stale/clock-skewed timestamp would stall events with no diagnostic.  
- **Fix:** Either assert/raise on timestamp_ms < self._last_emit_ms (since the contract says monotonic), or clamp deltas to >=0. At minimum document that callers must supply monotonic timestamps.  
- **Verifier:** The mechanical behavior is real and I reproduced it from src/computetest/nvme/mi/aem.py. deliver() (lines 89-117) computes timestamp_ms - self._window_start_ms (line 96) and timestamp_ms - self._last_emit_ms (line 105) with no lower bound, and __post_init__ guards coalesce_window_ms/rate_limit_per_s but never validates timestamp monotonicity. Repro: with coalesce_window_ms=1000, deliver(HEALTH, timestamp_ms=1000)->True then deliver(HEALTH, timestamp_ms=500)->False (negative delta -500 < 1000 => coalesced). I also confirmed the rate-window wedge: after _window_start_ms=10000, a backwards ts=500 gives delta -9500 which is not >=1000, so the per-second counter never resets and events stay dropped. So the claim is factually accurate and unguarded — not refuted, and no existing test covers a backwards timestamp.

However the real-world impact is negligible, so I downgrade from low to nit: (1) The field is explicitly documented as a precondition — 'monotonic ms since subscription open' (line 66) and 'monotonic per-subscription sequence' (line 68) — so a backwards timestamp is caller contract violation, not code misbehavior. (2) deliver() has ZERO callers anywhere in src/ (grep for '.deliver(' returns nothing outside the definition); it is exercised only by deterministic unit tests in tests/test_nvme_mi.py. The docstring (lines 76-80) states the real implementation lives in the management endpoint/BMC over MCTP and that this Subscription is an in-process mock 'so the subscription state machine can be tested.' There is no '# pragma: no cover - real-hw path' here feeding clock-skewed hardware timestamps; the finding's 'on the real-hw path ... would stall events' is speculative since no such wired real path exists. (3) The finding itself concedes 'For a deterministic mock this is benign.' This is not a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict, data corruption, spec-value error, or a real-hw misparse — it is a missing defensive guard on an already-documented precondition. The suggested 'at minimum document the precondition' is in fact already done in the field comments. A clamp-to-0 or assert would be a reasonable robustness hardening, hence not fully 'intentional', but the severity is nit.  

**N22. Temperature conversion uses 273.0 instead of 273.15**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/subsystem_health.py` · subsystem_health.py:48 (temperature_c) · _correctness_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `temperature_c` subtracts 273.0 rather than 273.15. This is internally consistent with controller_health.py:35 and the in-band path in health.py:130, and integer-Kelvin SMART temps make the 0.15 immaterial, so it is a low-severity precision nit — but combined with the Kelvin-vs-Celsius issue above it should be revisited when the real parse path is written.  
- **Evidence:** Line 48: `return float(self.composite_temperature_k) - 273.0`. Mock 314 K -> 41.0 C (test asserts exactly 41.0); 273.15 would give 40.85.  
- **Fix:** If the field truly stays Kelvin, use 273.15 for correctness; but per the Celsius finding above the NSHSP byte should likely not be converted from Kelvin at all.  
- **Verifier:** Factually accurate but immaterial. subsystem_health.py:48 does `float(self.composite_temperature_k) - 273.0`, and the 273.0 offset is internally consistent with controller_health.py:35 (also -273.0) and the real-hw in-band path health.py:130 (`smart["temperature"] -= 273`). The strictly correct Kelvin-to-Celsius offset is 273.15, so the conversion is technically off by 0.15 C (314 K -> 41.0 instead of 40.85; tests at test_nvme_mi_extras.py:44/107 and test_nvme_mi.py:295/307 lock in 41.0). However the impact is effectively nil: the NVMe(-MI) Composite Temperature field is reported as a whole integer in Kelvin (1 K quantization), so a 0.15 C correction is below the source granularity. No verdict depends on it — NvmSubsystemHealth.healthy (lines 56-61) never reads temperature, and the only temperature limit comparison (health.py max_temp_c) operates on integer Kelvin where 0.15 cannot flip a pass/fail near a 70 C threshold. The finding already self-rates this as a precision nit and explicitly defers the substantive concern to a separate Kelvin-vs-Celsius finding. Real bug, but a true nit, not low: it would not mislead a hardware test in the field. Downgraded from low to nit.  

**N23. Reverse-channel EOM read mislabels mode as the forward PAM4 mode**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/serdes.py` · serdes.py:471 (MockSerDes.read_eom) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A REVERSE-direction EOM read on a PAM4-mode link returns EomReading.mode = GMSL3-PAM4-12G even though the reverse control channel is always 187.5 Mbps NRZ (and correctly returns a single eye). The mode field describes a channel the reading did not measure.  
- **Evidence:** read_eom always stamps `mode=self._mode`: `return EomReading(link=link, direction=direction, mode=self._mode, vertical_mv=vertical, ...)`. Verified at runtime: MockSerDes(injected_mode=PAM4_12G).read_eom(0, REVERSE).mode == GmslMode.PAM4_12G while vertical_mv has len 1. The reverse channel per the module's own docstring (line 6, 64) is '187.5 Mbps NRZ'.  
- **Fix:** When direction == REVERSE, report a mode that reflects the reverse channel (e.g. an NRZ marker) or omit/None the mode, so a station logging reverse EOM does not record a PAM4 label against an NRZ measurement. At minimum, the EomReading.mode docstring should state it reflects the forward negotiated mode, not the measured channel.  
- **Verifier:** The code behavior is exactly as described and I reproduced it at runtime: serdes.py:471 stamps `mode=self._mode` unconditionally, so MockSerDes(injected_mode=PAM4_12G).read_eom(0, REVERSE) returns mode=GmslMode.PAM4_12G with a single eye (len(vertical_mv)==1), even though the reverse control channel is 187.5 Mbps NRZ (docstring lines 6, 63-64). No guard or test addresses the mode label on a reverse read (test_nrz_reverse_has_single_eye only asserts the single-eye structure), so it is not already-handled, and there is no comment declaring the intent, so it is not documented-intentional. Hence: confirmed, not refuted.

However, the finding overstates severity. The impact is purely a metadata/labeling imprecision: I traced every consumer of EomReading.mode (checks.py:241 -> EomHealth.mode; lmt.py:74 -> _base_fields label) and it NEVER feeds a verdict, threshold, step-mapping, or computation. The eye_open verdict is derived solely from worst_vertical_mv and horizontal_ui, and the physically load-bearing fact for an NRZ reverse channel (a single eye) IS returned correctly. So no PASS/FAIL is wrong and no data is corrupted on real hardware. Additionally, `mode` is reasonably interpreted as the per-link NEGOTIATED mode (a single GMSL link mode, mirroring LinkLock.mode), not a per-direction modulation; the reverse channel being NRZ is a fixed protocol property, and the GmslMode enum has no value for the 187.5 Mbps reverse control channel (its NRZ_6G is the forward 6G fallback), so the suggested fix has no clean representation. This is a documentation/label nit, not a low-severity field defect; the finding's own fallback fix (clarify the docstring) is the appropriate response.  

**N24. Module docstring/comment internally contradict the filter constant (100 MHz vs 50 MHz)**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/channel.py` · channel.py:5, 92 ('100 MHz') vs line 7 and line 24 ('50 MHz' / GMSL3_FILTER_HZ = 50e6) · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The same documented split frequency is stated three ways: '100 MHz filter split' (lines 5 and 92) while the inline text (line 5 cont. 'below 50 MHz'), line 7-related prose, and the actual constant GMSL3_FILTER_HZ = 50e6 say 50 MHz. A reader transcribing AN-2585 masks against the wrong split frequency would partition the trace at the wrong boundary.  
- **Evidence:** Line 5: '100 MHz filter split (data used unfiltered below 50 MHz, filtered above)'; line 24: 'GMSL3_FILTER_HZ = 50e6'; line 92: 'Partition a sweep at the GMSL3 100 MHz filter split'.  
- **Fix:** Pick the spec-correct value and make docstring, inline comment, and constant agree (the code uses 50e6 = 50 MHz; fix the '100 MHz' prose in lines 5 and 92, or correct the constant if 100 MHz is the true AN-2585 boundary).  
- **Verifier:** The internal documentation inconsistency is real and reproducible. channel.py line 5 says "100 MHz filter split" but immediately adds "(data used unfiltered below 50 MHz, filtered above)"; line 92 again says "GMSL3 100 MHz filter split"; while the constant (line 24: GMSL3_FILTER_HZ = 50e6), its inline comment, and the rest of line 5 all say 50 MHz. The phrase "100 MHz" is inconsistent prose.

However, this is a pure documentation/wording defect with NO behavioral impact, so the medium severity is overstated. The actual code partitions at filter_hz, which defaults to GMSL3_FILTER_HZ = 50e6 (lines 89-97), and tests/test_gmsl_channel.py::test_filter_partitions_at_50mhz (lines 69-73) confirms the boundary is exactly 50 MHz (49e6 -> below, 50e6 -> above). There is no wrong number used in any computation, no wrong PASS/FAIL verdict, and no spec value baked in incorrectly.

The finding's claimed impact ("a reader transcribing AN-2585 masks against the wrong split frequency would partition the trace at the wrong boundary") does not hold: masks are just (freq_hz, limit_db) breakpoint lists supplied to the framework, and the partition boundary is the GMSL3_FILTER_HZ constant (50 MHz), not something the transcriber sets. The misleading "100 MHz" word cannot cause a mispartition. Also note the AN-2585 masks are all None (honesty discipline: refuse-to-pass without supplied masks), so no compliance verdict is being produced from this module at all today.

Net: a confirmed but cosmetic docstring inconsistency (the "100 MHz" on lines 5 and 92 should read "50 MHz" to match the constant/comment/test). True severity is nit, not medium.  

**N25. lock_status() recomputed for all links on every inner iteration**  
`src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py` · src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py:77 · _performance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** serdes.lock_status() (which a real backend implements as a multi-link register read) is called V*T times and the full list is rebuilt each iteration only to read a single element, an unnecessary per-corner register transaction on real silicon.  
- **Evidence:** Line 77 calls `serdes.lock_status()` inside the `for volt in voltages` loop nested in `for temp in temperatures`. lock_status in the ABC returns 'one entry per info().links' — a real backend reads all links' lock registers each call.  
- **Fix:** Once the field-based lookup fix is in place, this is moot for one link; if multi-link shmoo is ever added, fetch lock_status() once per corner and index the needed link from that single read.  
- **Verifier:** Factually accurate about the code. At src/computetest/gmsl/shmoo.py:77, `relocked = serdes.lock_status()[link].locked` is inside `for volt in voltages` nested in `for temp in temperatures`, so it runs V*T times. The ABC contract (serdes.py:288-289) defines `lock_status()` as returning one LinkLock per `info().links`, and MockSerDes.lock_status() (serdes.py:439-446) builds the full per-link list each call — a real backend would read every link's lock register, then the code discards all but `[link]`. So the claim that the full multi-link list is rebuilt per corner to read a single element is correct, and there is no guard/cache or single-link accessor used here (negotiated_mode() exists but isn't relevant; no per-link lock_status helper exists). It is not intentional design protected by the conventions, nor handled by a test. However the real-world impact is negligible and the nit rating is correct: each shmoo corner is already dominated by thermal-chamber settling (set_temperature) and AEQ re-training (force_relock) plus read_eom/read_temperature; one extra multi-link register read (microseconds-to-milliseconds over I2C) per corner is lost in the noise versus those, and a shmoo is only a few dozen corners. No wrong verdict, no data corruption, no crash — purely a micro-optimization. Confirmed as a true-but-trivial nit.  

**N26. check_preemption drops handshake/verify-time diagnostics from notes; only fragmentation notes survive**  
`src/computetest/tsn/preemption.py` · preemption.py:106-120 (check_preemption) · _error-handling_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** PreemptionHealth.notes only receives the fragmentation notes; when the failure is verify_time_valid or verify_respond_handshake, notes is empty even though those checks are False, so to_dict()/notes give no diagnostic reason for the failing handshake. The verdict itself is still correct (summary names the failing checks), so this is a diagnostics gap, not a faked pass.  
- **Evidence:** `frag_ok, notes = check_fragmentation(...)` then `return PreemptionHealth(checks=checks, notes=notes)` — handshake/verify-time produce no note. Reproduced: verify_time_ms=200, got_respond=False -> checks show two failures but notes == [].  
- **Fix:** Append explanatory notes for the non-fragmentation checks too, e.g. when verify_time invalid add f'verifyTime {verify_time_ms} ms outside 1-128', and when handshake fails add the reason (no respond vs late respond).  
- **Verifier:** The factual core is reproducible. In check_preemption (preemption.py:106-120), `notes` is bound ONLY from `check_fragmentation` (line 114). The verify_time_valid and verify_respond_handshake results (lines 116-117) are recorded as booleans in `checks` but contribute nothing to `notes`. Reproduction confirmed by tracing the cited case (verify_time_ms=200, got_respond=False): run_verify_respond returns False at line 77 because verify_time_valid(200) is False; if fragments are valid, check_fragmentation returns (True, []), so notes==[] while checks={'verify_time_valid': False, 'verify_respond_handshake': False, 'fragmentation_valid': True}. So to_dict()['notes'] is empty even though two checks failed. The finding's own statement that the verdict is still correct is accurate: ok is False (line 60) and summary() (lines 62-65) emits FAIL(verify_time_valid,verify_respond_handshake), naming the failing checks by key. This is NOT a faked pass, hidden skip, or mislabeled verdict — those honesty-discipline concerns do not apply.

Severity downgraded from low to nit: the failing-check IDENTITY is already fully exposed two ways — the per-check booleans in the `checks` dict (surfaced verbatim by to_dict()) and the failing-key list in summary(). A field engineer reading to_dict() sees verify_time_valid: False and verify_respond_handshake: False explicitly. The missing `notes` entries would only add redundant free-text (e.g. a verify_time note duplicates the verify_time_valid bool). No docstring claims check_preemption's notes carry handshake reasons (only check_fragmentation documents notes for fragmentation). The suggested fix (adding explanatory notes) is a reasonable diagnostic nicety but provides no information not already structurally available, so real-world impact on a hardware test is negligible.  

**N27. PMA thresholds duplicate phy.py constants and bypass the PHY's own per-instance thresholds**  
`src/computetest/tsn/pma.py` · src/computetest/tsn/pma.py:23-24, 61-62 · _api-consistency_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** check_pma_electrical defines its own DEFAULT_PMA_MAX_LINKUP_MS/DEFAULT_PMA_SQI_MIN and compares raw reads directly, ignoring EthPhy.linkup_ok()/sqi_ok() and any thresholds a caller set via phy.set_thresholds(); a caller who tightened the PHY's thresholds would silently get the PMA module's looser defaults.  
- **Evidence:** `DEFAULT_PMA_MAX_LINKUP_MS = 100.0` / `DEFAULT_PMA_SQI_MIN = 5` mirror phy.py's `DEFAULT_MAX_LINKUP_MS = 100.0` / `DEFAULT_SQI_MIN = 5`; the checks call `phy.read_link_up_time() <= max_linkup_ms` rather than `phy.linkup_ok()`. The values happen to agree today, so there is no live wrong-verdict bug, only a divergence/override-bypass risk.  
- **Fix:** Either reuse phy.sqi_ok()/phy.linkup_ok() (and let set_thresholds drive them), or import phy.py's DEFAULT_* constants instead of re-declaring, so the two definitions can never drift apart. Document that the PMA params intentionally override per-instance thresholds if that is the intent.  
- **Verifier:** The factual claims are accurate and reproducible from the code. pma.py:23-24 declares DEFAULT_PMA_MAX_LINKUP_MS = 100.0 and DEFAULT_PMA_SQI_MIN = 5, duplicating phy.py:43-44 (DEFAULT_MAX_LINKUP_MS = 100.0, DEFAULT_SQI_MIN = 5). check_pma_electrical (pma.py:61-62) compares raw reads — phy.read_link_up_time() <= max_linkup_ms and phy.read_sqi() >= sqi_min — instead of the PHY's own linkup_ok()/sqi_ok() helpers (phy.py:139-145), which are the only consumers of the per-instance thresholds set via set_thresholds() (phy.py:131-137). So a caller who tightened the PHY via phy.set_thresholds(sqi_min=6) and then called check_pma_electrical without re-passing sqi_min=6 would indeed get the PMA default of 5, not the tightened value. That divergence/override-bypass is real.

However the impact is minimal, and the finding itself concedes it: 'there is no live wrong-verdict bug, only a divergence/override-bypass risk.' (1) The constants agree today, so no current PASS/FAIL is wrong. (2) No code in the repo exercises the bypass pattern — tests/test_tsn_pma.py drives thresholds exclusively through check_pma_electrical's own kwargs, and tests/test_tsn_phy.py exercises set_thresholds only against sqi_ok()/linkup_ok(); the two paths are never crossed. (3) This is plausibly intentional: TC8 L1 is a distinct conformance program, and passing program-specific limits explicitly at the call site (rather than inheriting hidden per-instance mutation state) is the more auditable design — though that intent is undocumented. This is a pure api-consistency/maintainability concern (future constant drift + a confusing override surface), not a wrong verdict, misparse, wrong number, or faked PASS. Downgrading from low to nit: the claim holds but there is no behavioral defect and no realistic path to a wrong hardware verdict as the code is actually used. A docstring note or importing/reusing the phy.py constants would resolve it.  

**N28. to_dict() omits has_correctable while including has_uncorrectable (asymmetric serialization)**  
`src/computetest/cxl/ras.py` · src/computetest/cxl/ras.py:81-85 (CxlRasSnapshot.to_dict) · _api-consistency_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** to_dict serializes has_uncorrectable but not has_correctable, an inconsistency that could mislead a downstream consumer relying on the dict form to gauge correctable activity.  
- **Evidence:** return {"ue_raw": ..., "ce_raw": ..., "uncorrectable": [...], "correctable": [...], "has_uncorrectable": self.has_uncorrectable}  # no has_correctable key  
- **Fix:** Add "has_correctable": self.has_correctable to the dict for symmetry, or document why correctable presence is intentionally not surfaced.  
- **Verifier:** The asymmetry is real and reproducible: src/computetest/cxl/ras.py:81-85 (CxlRasSnapshot.to_dict) emits "has_uncorrectable" (line 85) but never "has_correctable", even though both are symmetric properties (lines 73-79). So the structural claim is factually correct.

However the impact is overstated and the severity is below "low":
1. No PASS/FAIL verdict depends on to_dict(). The real RAS verdict logic in emit.py:48-54 (emit_cxl_ras) reads the live properties directly (snapshot.has_uncorrectable, snapshot.uncorrectable, snapshot.correctable), not the serialized dict. So there is zero risk of a wrong hardware verdict.
2. Correctable activity is NOT actually hidden from the dict — line 84 always emits the full "correctable" list of decoded CE error names. A consumer can trivially gauge correctable activity via len(dict["correctable"]) > 0, which is exactly has_correctable. The missing key is only a redundant boolean shortcut.
3. No consumer anywhere in the codebase reads a "has_correctable" key (grep across src/computetest/cxl confirms none), and the only to_dict() test (tests/test_cxl_ras.py:34) asserts on the "uncorrectable" list, not the boolean flags.

This is a genuine but purely cosmetic API-symmetry inconsistency with no field-test consequence (no wrong verdict, no data loss, no crash, no spec-compliance claim affected). Per the severity rubric it does not even rise to "low" (edge-case mishandling / weak test) — it is a nit. The suggested fix (add "has_correctable" for symmetry) is reasonable but optional.  

**N29. No spec gating that 256B flit requires >=32 GT/s, so contradictory inputs pass**  
`src/computetest/cxl/link.py` · src/computetest/cxl/link.py:55-60 (check_cxl_link) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The four checks are independent, so speed_gt=8 with flit_mode=CXL_256B_STD and cxl_negotiated=True would pass speed against a lowered expect_speed_gt while reporting a 256B flit, even though 256B flit mode is only defined at 32/64 GT/s in the CXL/Flex Bus spec (68B is the flit at 8/16 GT/s). The verdict cannot catch a physically impossible / mis-parsed combination.  
- **Evidence:** checks dict computes flit_mode_ok and speed_ok independently; there is no cross-check that flit_mode in {256B-*} implies speed_gt >= 32. Docstring states 'at the expected speed (32/64 GT/s) ... in the expected flit mode (68B vs 256B...)' but encodes no coupling.  
- **Fix:** Add a consistency check (e.g. a checks entry 'flit_speed_consistent') that fails if a 256B flit is reported below 32 GT/s, since that combination indicates a parse/negotiation-report bug on real hardware.  
- **Verifier:** Verified against src/computetest/cxl/link.py:55-60. The mechanical claim is accurate: the four checks (cxl_alt_protocol_negotiated, speed_ok, width_ok, flit_mode_ok) are computed independently and there is no cross-check coupling flit_mode to speed_gt. The spec premise is also correct — 256B flit mode is only defined at >=32 GT/s (PCIe 6.0/CXL 3.0 at 64 GT/s; 68B flit is used at 8/16 GT/s), so a 256B flit reported at 8 GT/s is physically impossible. A useful flit_speed_consistent cross-check is genuinely absent and would add defense-in-depth against a garbled/mis-parsed negotiation report.

HOWEVER, the finding's framing materially overstates impact and its "contradictory inputs pass" scenario only occurs under deliberate misconfiguration. I reproduced both cases (PYTHONPATH=src): with the DEFAULT expect_speed_gt=32, the impossible combo 256B@8GT/s already FAILS via speed_ok (ok=False). The combination only passes if the caller explicitly lowers expect_speed_gt to 8/16 while still reporting/expecting a 256B flit — i.e., an operator self-inflicted, contradictory expectation. So the claim "the verdict cannot catch a physically impossible combination" is false for normal/default usage; the default config does catch it. The only caller in src uses the function via re-export with no lowered expectation (no other call site sets expect_speed_gt). There is also no parsing logic in this file — values are supplied by the caller — so this is not a real-hw parser bug; it would only help as a sanity assertion if a caller passed mismatched parsed values.

Net: a legitimate but minor robustness/spec-hardening enhancement, not a verdict-corrupting or data-corruption defect. Severity 'low' in the finding is too high given defaults already reject the impossible case; downgraded to nit. Suggested fix (add a flit_speed_consistent check that fails when a 256B flit is reported below 32 GT/s) is reasonable and would harden against a mis-parsed hardware report regardless of expect_speed_gt.  

**N30. speed_gt stores GT/s here but the 'extended' linkstate module stores PCIe Gen number — easy cross-module unit confusion**  
`src/computetest/cxl/link.py` · src/computetest/cxl/link.py:25,48-57 vs src/computetest/linkstate.py:22-24,104-113 · _api-consistency_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The module docstring says it 'extends the linkstate.check_link expected-speed/width pattern', but linkstate.LinkHealth.speed is a PCIe generation integer (e.g. 5) compared via LINK_SPEED_GTPS lookup, while CxlLinkHealth.speed_gt is a raw GT/s integer (32/64). A caller reusing the linkstate mental model could pass speed_gt=5 and silently fail speed_ok against the 32 default.  
- **Evidence:** linkstate: `gt = LINK_SPEED_GTPS.get(self.speed, 0)` (speed is a Gen index). link.py: `"speed_ok": speed_gt >= expect_speed_gt` with expect default 32 and summary `f"{self.speed_gt}GT/s"`. Same conceptual field, different units, no shared type.  
- **Fix:** Document the unit difference prominently in the check_cxl_link docstring and/or validate speed_gt is one of the known CXL link rates (e.g. >= 8 and in the GT/s domain), so a Gen-number mistake produces a clear error rather than a quiet FAIL.  
- **Verifier:** Every concrete claim is factually accurate. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/linkstate.py the LinkHealth.speed field is a PCIe generation index (line 72: `gt = LINK_SPEED_GTPS.get(self.speed, 0)` with LINK_SPEED_GTPS={1:2.5,...,5:32.0,6:64.0}; summary prints `Gen{self.speed}({gt}GT/s)`). In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/cxl/link.py the CxlLinkHealth.speed_gt field is a raw GT/s value (default expect_speed_gt=32, tests pass 32/64, summary prints `{self.speed_gt}GT/s`). The docstring (line 4) does invoke "the linkstate.check_link expected-speed/width pattern." There is no validation that speed_gt is in the GT/s domain, so a caller reusing the Gen-number mental model and passing speed_gt=5 would get speed_ok = (5 >= 32) = False — a quiet FAIL. So the unit divergence is real and unguarded.

However, severity is overstated as low; the true severity is a nit. (1) The failure direction is conservative: a Gen-number mistake produces a FAIL, never a false PASS — the project rubric reserves higher severity for wrong-PASS/data-corruption, and a caller-induced wrong-FAIL on misuse is the lowest-impact class. (2) The API is self-documenting and the confusion is well-mitigated already: the field is named speed_gt (explicit unit suffix), the parameter is expect_speed_gt, the emit consumer tags unit="GT/s" (cxl/emit.py:23), and the summary string says "GT/s". check_cxl_link is keyword-only, so a caller must literally type speed_gt=, which signals the unit at the call site. (3) There is zero code coupling — cxl/link.py does not import from linkstate and uses a different field name (speed_gt vs speed), so the only link is the docstring prose. This is an api-consistency/documentation observation, not a behavioral defect: under correct usage the code does exactly what its names say. Not intentional honesty-discipline or a pragma path, but the conservative fail-direction and strong in-band naming reduce real-world impact to nit. The suggested fix (clarify the unit in the docstring; optionally validate speed_gt is in the GT/s domain) is a reasonable low-priority polish.  

**N31. Verdict upper boundary at exactly 30.0% disagrees with the documented '>30% not acceptable' rule**  
`src/computetest/msa/gage_rr.py` · gage_rr.py:213-215 (verdict ternary) vs docstring lines 16-20 · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** A study with pct_rr exactly == 30.0 is classified 'not_acceptable', but the docstring rule states '10-30%: marginal' and '>30%: not acceptable', which makes 30.0 the inclusive top of the marginal band.  
- **Evidence:** Code: verdict = ('capable' if pct_rr < AIAG_PASS_PERCENT else 'marginal' if pct_rr < AIAG_MARGINAL_PERCENT else 'not_acceptable'). At pct_rr==30.0 the 'pct_rr < 30.0' test is False so the result is 'not_acceptable'. Docstring line 18 lists '10-30 %: marginal' and line 19 '> 30 %: not acceptable'.  
- **Fix:** Either change the second comparison to 'pct_rr <= AIAG_MARGINAL_PERCENT' to match the documented inclusive-30 marginal band, or update the docstring to say '>=30% not acceptable'. The current behavior is conservative (errs toward rejecting), so impact is low; align doc and code regardless.  
- **Verifier:** The factual claim is reproducible: at pct_rr == 30.0, the ternary at gage_rr.py:213-215 evaluates `pct_rr < AIAG_MARGINAL_PERCENT` (30.0 < 30.0) as False, yielding "not_acceptable", while the docstring (lines 16-20) reads "10-30%: marginal" / "> 30%: not acceptable", literally placing 30.0 in the marginal band. So a genuine doc-vs-code boundary mismatch exists. But the real-world impact is essentially nil: (1) the docstring itself calls these the AIAG "rule of thumb" (line 12) and AIAG MSA does not rigorously fix the closed/open treatment of the exact 30.0 boundary; (2) the test at tests/test_msa.py:80 documents the INTENDED behavior as "%R&R >= 30 -> not_acceptable", matching the code, so the code reflects developer intent and only the prose is imprecise (the lower boundary similarly: code gives "marginal" at exactly 10.0, consistent with docstring); (3) pct_rr is a continuous float (sqrt(var_rr)/sqrt(var_total)*100), so hitting exactly 30.0 is a measure-zero event; (4) most importantly, the `ok` property (line 82) is True only for "capable", so a study at exactly 30% is "not ok" whether labeled marginal or not_acceptable — this boundary only swaps a label between two non-passing states, never flips a PASS/FAIL gate, never fakes a pass, and errs conservatively toward rejection. This is a trivial doc-precision nit, not the "low spec-conformance" defect claimed; recommend just aligning the docstring (or using <=) but it cannot mislead a hardware verdict.  

**N32. File handle leaked in load_registry (open without context manager)**  
`src/computetest/msa/calibration_registry.py` · calibration_registry.py:112 · _correctness_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `open(path).read()` never closes the file; the descriptor is reclaimed only by GC/refcounting, which is non-deterministic and flagged by ResourceWarning under -W. Trivial but real for a long-lived process loading many station registries.  
- **Evidence:** `raw_text = open(path).read()`  
- **Fix:** Use a context manager: `with open(path, encoding='utf-8') as f: raw_text = f.read()` (also pins encoding instead of relying on the platform default).  
- **Verifier:** Factually accurate. Line 112 reads `raw_text = open(path).read()` verbatim — no context manager and no explicit encoding, so the descriptor is reclaimed only by CPython refcounting (non-deterministic on other runtimes, and flagged by ResourceWarning under `-W error`), and decoding relies on the platform-default locale encoding (cp1252 on Windows vs utf-8 on Linux). The suggested fix (`with open(path, encoding="utf-8") as f: raw_text = f.read()`) is correct and also pins encoding. This is not a pragma real-hw path, spec-mask, or fake-pass, so the conventions do not exempt it. However, severity is overstated as a correctness defect: `load_registry` is invoked once per station-config load (instruments.py / tests), not in any handle-accumulating loop, and the registry schema fields (role/manufacturer/model/serial/ISO dates) are effectively ASCII, so neither the leak nor the encoding produces a wrong PASS/FAIL verdict or data corruption in practice. Under the project's rubric (low = minor; corruption/wrong-verdict = high/critical) this is a genuine but minor code-quality issue — a nit. Worth fixing for hygiene/portability, but it would not mislead a hardware test.  

**N33. Empty HMP output is treated as a successful injection (potential false PASS if QEMU ever returns no text)**  
`sim/qemu/inject.py` · sim/qemu/inject.py:122-125 (inject_aer) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The success gate `if out.strip() and not out.strip().startswith('OK')` treats an empty/whitespace reply as success. For pcie_aer_inject_error, real QEMU always echoes a non-empty 'OK id: ...' on success and a non-empty error string on failure, so an empty reply indicates an unexpected condition (version mismatch, command silently dropped, truncated read), yet it would be reported as a successful inject and downstream the BERT would expect a count that never arrived.  
- **Evidence:** Code: `if out.strip() and not out.strip().startswith("OK"): raise QMPError(...)`. The FakeQMPServer docstring even claims 'returns "" for human-monitor-command (QEMU's success = no output)', which contradicts the real pcie_aer_inject_error contract (it returns 'OK id: ...'). Empty -> no raise -> silent assumed-success.  
- **Fix:** For this specific command require a positive ack: `if not out.strip().startswith("OK"): raise QMPError(f"pcie_aer_inject_error did not ack OK: {out.strip()!r}")`. This turns an empty/anomalous reply into a loud failure instead of a silent pass.  
- **Verifier:** The code claim is factually accurate. At sim/qemu/inject.py:121-125 the gate is `if out.strip() and not out.strip().startswith("OK"): raise`. For an empty/whitespace reply, `out.strip()` is falsy, the condition is False, no exception is raised, and the empty reply is treated as a successful injection. The finding's premise about the real contract is also correct: QEMU's HMP `pcie_aer_inject_error` handler emits non-empty text on BOTH paths (success = "OK id: <id> root bus: ..."; error = a non-empty error string), so an empty reply is anomalous (truncated read / dropped command / version drift). No guard inside inject.py covers the empty case, and no test pins it as intentional — the FakeQMPServer (tests/test_qmp_inject.py:58-64) returns "OK id: ..." or an error string, never "" — so the empty branch is genuinely unhandled rather than deliberately designed. It is mildly at odds with the project's honesty discipline (no silent assumed-success).

However, the severity must be downgraded to nit, and the "false PASS" framing is overstated: (1) inject.py is an injector, not a verdict producer — the actual PASS/FAIL gate is the guest-side AER count assertion in aer_test.sh with a documented +/-1 tolerance plus a setpci post-injection diagnostic (sim/qemu/README.md:105-118), which catches a silently-dropped injection regardless of the ack string; the ack check is only a best-effort early warning. (2) The README documents that the one real failure they actually hit (QEMU 8.2.2 TCG echoing "OK" while NOT writing the AER register) returned a NON-empty "OK", which both the current code and the suggested fix (`require startswith("OK")`) would pass — so tightening empty->fail does not close the demonstrated real-world failure mode. (3) Empty replies do not occur for this specific HMP command on a healthy QEMU. The suggested fix is reasonable defensive hardening and harmless, but the real integrity protection lives downstream. Note also: the cited "contradiction" (FakeQMPServer docstring saying it returns "") is a stale docstring at tests/test_qmp_inject.py:27 in a different file than the audited inject.py; the server body and inject.py actually agree on the "OK id: ..." success contract.  

**N34. inject_aer does not validate status range/sign; a negative status renders an invalid HMP literal**  
`sim/qemu/inject.py` · sim/qemu/inject.py:121 and main():152 · _error-handling_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** status is formatted with `0x{status:x}` without bounds/sign checking; a negative int (e.g. from int('-0x40', 0) in main) formats as '0x-40', and a value > 0xffffffff is silently passed though AER status is a 32-bit register.  
- **Evidence:** `out = self.hmp(f"pcie_aer_inject_error {flags}{qdev_id} 0x{status:x}")` with `status = int(args.correctable or args.uncorrectable, 0)`. f"0x{-64:x}" -> '0x-40'. QEMU will error, so it is not a false pass, but the failure mode is a confusing QEMU parse error rather than a clear local validation message.  
- **Fix:** Validate `0 <= status <= 0xFFFFFFFF` in inject_aer (or in main before connecting) and raise a clear ValueError/argparse error; reject status==0 as a no-op injection.  
- **Verifier:** The mechanical claim is factually correct and I reproduced it. inject.py:152 does `status = int(args.correctable or args.uncorrectable, 0)`, and argparse base-0 accepts a leading-minus literal: `int('-0x40', 0)` == -64. inject.py:121 then formats `f"0x{status:x}"`, and `f"0x{-64:x}"` yields the malformed HMP literal `"0x-40"`. There is no bounds/sign guard in inject_aer or main (confirmed by grep), and no test covers a negative/oversized status. So the finding accurately describes the code.

However, the severity is overstated and I downgrade it to a nit:
- The finding itself concedes this is "not a false pass." There is no wrong PASS/FAIL verdict and no data corruption — the project's primary severity drivers do not apply.
- The failure is cleanly surfaced, not silent. For a negative status, QEMU returns a non-"OK" error report, and inject_aer (lines 122-124) raises `QMPError("pcie_aer_inject_error failed: ...")`. For an oversized status (>0xFFFFFFFF), QEMU's HMP parser (qemu_strtoui) returns -ERANGE and errors rather than silently truncating to a wrong bit pattern, so the "silently passed through" framing overstates the risk — there is no silent mis-injection.
- This is an operator-driven developer CLI tool; `status` is a hand-typed arg. The only real-world consequence is a QEMU parse error instead of a friendlier local message — a usability nicety, not a correctness or hardware-test defect.
- The sub-suggestion to "reject status==0 as a no-op" is itself wrong: `0x0` is a valid HMP literal and QEMU would simply inject no bits; that is not a bug.

Net: a real but minor input-validation/ergonomics gap on a dev tool, with no impact on test verdicts, no silent corruption, and a clean error path already in place. Confirmed as described, but nit-level, not low.

Yet `__all__` (len 53) and the import block cover device/emit/events/hdm/link/maintenance/poison/retimer as well. Verified: all 53 names import, no duplicates, IMPORTED_NOT_IN_ALL=[] and IN_ALL_NOT_IMPORTED=[], and every symbol's __module__ matches its import source.

The docstring uses two illustrative bullets and never asserts "only these two submodules exist," and the __all__ block itself carries clear per-submodule section comments that document the full API surface in code. It does not fake a PASS, hide a skip, truncate coverage, mislabel a verdict, or claim unbacked spec compliance. No effect on any hardware test path. Confirmed but correctly classified as a nit (matching the finding's own severity and its note that it is purely a doc edit with no code change).

- **Claim:** The package intentionally re-exports six DEFAULT_* constants (each explicitly imported under a `# noqa: F401` block), but only DEFAULT_CSI2_DATA_TYPE is listed in __all__; DEFAULT_MAX_LOCK_MS, DEFAULT_PRE_FEC_TARGET_BER, DEFAULT_BIST_DURATION_S, DEFAULT_EOM_MV_MIN, and DEFAULT_EOM_UI_MIN are imported but omitted from __all__, so `from computetest.gmsl import *` and any tooling/docs that read __all__ will not see them, even though they remain attribute-accessible (e.g. gmsl.DEFAULT_MAX_LOCK_MS works).

The checks-group __all__ lists only `"DEFAULT_CSI2_DATA_TYPE"` (line 92) and the serdes-group __all__ (lines 84-86) lists every dataclass/enum but none of the three serdes DEFAULT_* constants. (If the intent were truly to keep them private, they should not be imported into __init__ at all.)

In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/gmsl/__init__.py, five DEFAULT_* constants are imported under `# noqa: F401` (the deliberate re-export idiom) but omitted from __all__: DEFAULT_MAX_LOCK_MS (line 23), DEFAULT_PRE_FEC_TARGET_BER (line 24), DEFAULT_BIST_DURATION_S (line 49), DEFAULT_EOM_MV_MIN (line 50), DEFAULT_EOM_UI_MIN (line 51). Only DEFAULT_CSI2_DATA_TYPE appears in __all__ (line 92). The sibling-convention claim also holds up: name-by-name comparison shows cxl (2/2), ddr5 (4/4), and tsn (11/11) all list every imported DEFAULT_* in __all__, so gmsl is the lone outlier at 1/6. (Note: a naive `grep -c "DEFAULT_"` on tsn's __all__ returns 8 because two lines pack two names each, but the actual name set is the full 11 — the finding's 11/11 figure is correct.) However, the real-world impact is minimal: the constants stay attribute-accessible (gmsl.DEFAULT_MAX_LOCK_MS works), no code anywhere uses `from computetest.gmsl import *`, no test asserts on __all__, and grep finds no consumer of these names outside their defining modules. This affects only star-import visibility and __all__-based doc/tooling introspection — it does not fake a PASS, hide a skip, mislabel a verdict, change any number/spec value, or affect any real-hw parsing path. Per the project's severity rubric (critical/high require wrong verdicts, wrong numbers, or real-hw crashes/misparses), this is a cosmetic API-consistency cleanup, not a defect that would mislead a hardware test in the field. Confirmed as a real inconsistency but downgraded to nit. Suggested fix is correct: add the five names to the checks/serdes groups in __all__ to match the cxl/ddr5/tsn convention.
