**N38. to_dict()/SpdInfo typed as plain dict / mislabels per-die density as module density**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/spd.py` · spd.py:24-31, 59 · _api-consistency_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** density_gbit is documented/named as if it were a single scalar 'Gb' but byte 4 is per-die density; module capacity needs die-per-package, ranks, channels, and bus width (all of which memtest86plus multiplies in). summary() prints '<density_gbit>Gb' which a field engineer will read as module size, off by orders of magnitude.  
- **Evidence:** summary(): `f"DDR5 SPD {self.info.module_type} {self.info.density_gbit}Gb "`. Corpus dir 'synthetic-ddr5-32gb' vs density_gbit field shows the conflation; memtest86plus computes module_size = density * die_per_pkg * channels * bus_width / io_width * ranks.  
- **Fix:** Rename to density_gbit_per_die and/or compute true module capacity from bytes 4/6/234/235, and have summary() print the unit it actually means.  
- **Verifier:** The factual core of the finding is accurate and reproducible from the code/corpus. In spd.py:18,41 byte 4 (`data[4] & 0x1F`) decodes the JESD400-5 per-die SDRAM density in Gb (DENSITY_GBIT[4]=16), NOT module capacity. The corpus dir corpus/ddr5_spd/synthetic-ddr5-32gb/ proves the conflation: spd.hex byte 4 = 0x04 -> density_gbit=16 (per-die Gb), while the dir name and expected.json (density_gbit:16) describe a 32 GB module. So summary() at spd.py:59 (`f"...{self.info.density_gbit}Gb "`) prints "DDR5 SPD RDIMM 16Gb ecc=True" for a 32 GB module, which a reader could misinterpret as module size. The name density_gbit and the bare "Gb" token do not disambiguate per-die vs module. That part is real.

HOWEVER the real-world impact is far below "low": (1) No wrong PASS/FAIL and no data corruption — check_spd compares parsed density_gbit against expected.json's density_gbit (also per-die, =16), so the verdict is internally consistent (parsed.get(k)==v); the parsed value itself is the correct per-die Gbit. (2) The module docstring (spd.py:9-10) is explicit and honest: "a scaffold, not a full SPD decode" decoding only the fields the conformance check needs — it never claims to report module capacity, so nothing fakes a pass or claims compliance it cannot back (consistent with the honesty discipline). (3) The to_dict()/SpdInfo "-> dict" typing portion of the title is a pure style nit that already passes mypy files=src. The only residual risk is a human misreading a cosmetic summary string label — a naming/labeling nit, not a logic/parsing/units defect. Confirmed as accurate but downgraded from low to nit; suggested fix (rename to density_gbit_per_die and/or print the unit it means) is a reasonable clarity improvement but not a hardware-misleading bug.  

**N39. set_ecs accepts any mode string; documented enum not enforced**  
`src/computetest/ddr5/ras.py` · src/computetest/ddr5/ras.py:88-101 (set_ecs) and 22 (EcsConfig.mode) · _spec-conformance_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** EcsConfig.mode is documented as the enumerated Linux ECS ABI field 'counts_codewords | counts_rows', and set_ecs strictly validates threshold against ECS_THRESHOLDS, but set_ecs(mode=...) accepts any arbitrary string; the real sysfs node would reject an invalid mode, so the mock is more permissive than hardware.  
- **Evidence:** ras.py:22 'mode: str = "counts_codewords"  # counts_codewords | counts_rows'; set_ecs validates threshold ('if threshold is not None and threshold not in ECS_THRESHOLDS: raise ...') but 'if mode is not None: self._ecs.mode = mode' with no membership check.  
- **Fix:** Validate mode against the allowed set ({'counts_codewords','counts_rows'}) and raise Ddr5RasError on an unknown value, mirroring the threshold validation, so tests exercising mode programming match real-sysfs rejection semantics.  
- **Verifier:** The factual claim is reproducible at src/computetest/ddr5/ras.py:88-101: set_ecs validates `threshold` against ECS_THRESHOLDS and raises Ddr5RasError, but assigns `mode` (`if mode is not None: self._ecs.mode = mode`) with no membership check, while line 22 documents the field as an enum `counts_codewords | counts_rows`. test_ddr5_ras.py only exercises the happy-path mode="counts_rows" (line 22) and a bad-threshold rejection (line 35); there is no test asserting mode rejection, so no existing guard/test covers it. The asymmetry is genuine.

However, the real-world impact is essentially nil, so I downgrade low->nit: (1) `mode` is never consumed by any verdict — check_ecs in ecs.py only evaluates `ecs.threshold == threshold and ecs.enabled` and the scrub-time budget; an invalid mode string cannot produce a wrong PASS/FAIL or corrupt data. (2) There is NO real backend in the codebase (only MockDdr5Ras; no RealDdr5Ras / sysfs writer / ecs_fruX path exists), so the finding's premise that "the mock is more permissive than hardware" has no real-hw counterpart to actually diverge from — it is speculative. (3) Per honesty-discipline conventions this does not fake a pass, hide a skip, or mislabel a verdict; the documented enum is a descriptive comment, not an enforced contract. The suggested fix (validate mode against {'counts_codewords','counts_rows'}) is reasonable input-hygiene that improves mock fidelity, but it is a minor nit, not a defect that would mislead a hardware test in the field.  

**N40. MockMctpTransport: responder-produced responses can interleave behind pre-queued responses, breaking req/resp pairing**  
`src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py` · src/computetest/nvme/mi/mctp.py:156-166 · _test-quality_ · conf=medium · verdict=uncertain  
- **Claim:** When both `queue_response` (pre-seeded) and a `responder` are used together, `send` appends the responder's reply to the same FIFO list after any pre-queued items, so a later `recv` may return a stale pre-queued message instead of the responder's reply for the just-sent request. A test mixing both mechanisms could silently pass against the wrong response.  
- **Evidence:** Line 161 `self._responses.append(resp)` and line 166 `return self._responses.pop(0)` share one list with `queue_response` (line 148). There is no association between a sent request and the response returned; FIFO ordering is the only contract. The docstring even presents the two mechanisms as alternatives without warning they don't compose.  
- **Fix:** Either document that `responder` and `queue_response` are mutually exclusive, or have the responder path bypass the pre-queued list (e.g. a separate deque or returning the responder result directly from `recv`). At minimum add a test asserting the intended ordering when both are used.  
- **Verifier:** The technical mechanics are accurate and reproducible from the code: queue_response appends to self._responses (line 148), send with a responder appends its reply to the SAME FIFO list (line 161), and recv pops from the front (line 166) with no request/response association beyond FIFO order. So if a caller both pre-queues a response and uses a responder, recv would return the stale pre-queued message first. The MCTP recv intentionally does no message_tag matching, so the claim is not factually wrong.

However, the real-world impact is negligible, warranting a downgrade to nit:
- The responder parameter is never used anywhere in NVMe-MI tests or production code; only queue_response is exercised (tests/test_nvme_mi.py:90-110). No existing test mixes the two mechanisms, so "a test could silently pass against the wrong response" describes a hypothetical, not-yet-written test where the author controls the assertions.
- This is an in-memory test double, not a real-hardware path (no # pragma: no cover - real-hw). A defect here cannot produce a wrong PASS/FAIL on actual hardware in the field; per the severity rubric that rules out medium/high/critical.
- The sibling CXL MockMailbox.send_command (src/computetest/cxl/mailbox.py:114-122) is synchronous and DOES prioritize the responder, falling through to the FIFO only on None, with an explicit test (test_responder_falls_through_to_queue). That shows the "responder-or-queue, responder-takes-precedence/falls-through" contract is intentional design. The MCTP transport splits send/recv (genuinely async wire), so it cannot return the responder result directly from recv; routing it through the shared queue is a reasonable, if composition-fragile, choice.
- The docstring frames the two as alternatives ("queues responses... Use a custom responder for stateful..."), consistent with every existing call site using exactly one mechanism.

Disposition: not "refuted" (the behavior is real), not strictly "already-handled" (no guard or test pins the ordering when both are combined), and not a deliberate honesty-discipline/pragma case. It is a genuine but trivial latent foot-gun in a mock that no current code exercises. The suggested fix (document mutual exclusivity, or add a test) is reasonable hygiene but low value. True severity is nit.  

**N41. FakeQMPServer docstring contradicts its own success-output behavior (stale 'success = no output')**  
`sim/qemu/inject.py` · tests/test_qmp_inject.py:25-27 (FakeQMPServer docstring) vs lines 62-64 · _honesty_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The FakeQMPServer class docstring claims it 'returns "" for human-monitor-command (QEMU's success = no output)', but the actual _serve body returns the non-empty string 'OK id: dev root bus: ...'. The documented success model is wrong; only the code is right.  
- **Evidence:** Docstring: 'returns "" for human-monitor-command (QEMU\'s success = no output), and errors on "boom".' — but the body does: `else: # QEMU success echoes "OK id: <id> ..." conn.sendall(b'{"return": "OK id: dev root bus: 0000:00, bus: 0 devfn: 3.0"}\r\n')`. Verified against qemu-system 11.0: hmp_pcie_aer_inject_error prints 'OK id: %s root bus: %s, ...' on success and a non-OK error string on failure, so the BODY (and inject.py's startswith("OK") check) is correct; the docstring is stale.  
- **Fix:** Update the FakeQMPServer docstring to state that success echoes an 'OK id: ...' line (matching QEMU 11.0 and inject.py's startswith("OK") parse) and failures return a non-OK error string. The current docstring would mislead a maintainer into 'fixing' the server to return "", masking the real parsing contract.  
- **Verifier:** Factually verified. In /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/tests/test_qmp_inject.py the FakeQMPServer docstring (lines 26-27) states it "returns \"\" for human-monitor-command (QEMU's success = no output)", but the _serve body (lines 62-64) actually returns the non-empty success string '{"return": "OK id: dev root bus: 0000:00, bus: 0 devfn: 3.0"}' and an error path (lines 60-61) returning '"invalid id: nonexistent"'. The docstring describes the opposite of what the code does and omits the error-report path entirely. The body is the correct model: sim/qemu/inject.py inject_aer (lines 122-124) parses success via out.strip().startswith("OK") and raises on any other non-empty text, and inject.py's own module docstring (lines 8-17) cites the qemu-system 11.0 "OK id: ..." syntax. So the body and production parser agree; only the docstring is wrong. Git history (commit e2521bc) shows the docstring and body were committed together, so it was inconsistent from inception rather than drifting, but the inconsistency is real either way. Not protected by any project convention (it is not a None-then-raise mask, scheduled-debt budget, or pragma path) and no guard/test references the prose. All 9 tests pass. However impact is purely documentary: it is a comment in a test-helper class, has zero effect on any verdict, parse, or hardware path, and cannot fake a pass or corrupt data. The only risk is a maintainer "fixing" the server to return "" per the bad comment. Real but minor — downgraded from low to nit.  

**N42. self._sock None-access in _recv_json/_send is invisible to the type gate (sim/ outside mypy files=src)**  
`sim/qemu/inject.py` · sim/qemu/inject.py:88-98 (_recv_json line 90, _send line 98) · _type-safety_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** _sock is typed Optional[socket], and _recv_json/_send dereference it without a None guard; mypy flags union-attr on lines 90 and 98, but the src-only mypy scope means this never gates. Runtime-safe today (connect() sets _sock first) but the type is unsound and a future caller using _send before connect() would get an AttributeError, not QMPError.  
- **Evidence:** `mypy sim/qemu/inject.py` -> 'line 90: Item "None" of "socket | None" has no attribute "recv"' and 'line 98: ... has no attribute "sendall"'. _send does `self._sock.sendall(...)` with no None check; _recv_json does `self._sock.recv(4096)`.  
- **Fix:** Add an assertion/guard in _send and _recv_json (e.g. `if self._sock is None: raise QMPError('not connected')`) so misuse surfaces as a QMPError, and to satisfy strict-optional if this file is ever added to the mypy scope.  
- **Verifier:** All checkable claims reproduce. `sim/qemu/inject.py:58` types `self._sock: socket.socket | None = None`; `_recv_json` (line 90) does `self._sock.recv(4096)` and `_send` (line 98) does `self._sock.sendall(...)` with no None guard (the only `_sock is None` check is in `close()` at line 134, unrelated). Running `python3 -m mypy --strict-optional sim/qemu/inject.py` emits exactly the cited errors: line 90 `Item "None" of "socket | None" has no attribute "recv" [union-attr]` and line 98 `... has no attribute "sendall" [union-attr]` (it also surfaces a line-115 return-value error, beyond this finding's scope, reinforcing that sim/ is genuinely unchecked). The mypy gate in pyproject.toml is `files = ["src"]` with `strict_optional = true`, and `sim/` is a sibling of `src/`, so these errors never gate in CI — claim confirmed. Runtime-safe-today is also confirmed: every entry point (`__enter__`, `connect`, `inject_*`->`hmp`->`execute`, and `main`) reaches `_send`/`_recv_json` only after `connect()` (line 69) sets `_sock`; `CxlInjector` in cxl_inject.py merely wraps an already-connected client and adds no pre-connect path. So the type is unsound and a guard is missing, exactly as described. However, real-world impact is minimal: this is a sim/ QMP test-injection helper, not a real-hardware PASS/FAIL verdict or parsing path; there is no incorrect behavior today; the failure mode is purely hypothetical misuse of private methods (`_send`/`_recv_json`) yielding AttributeError instead of QMPError; and the file is intentionally outside the project's src-scoped mypy gate. Genuine latent type-safety nit, not a defect that would mislead a hardware test. Downgraded from low to nit.  

**N43. MockDevice._min_speed_seen is dead state (tracked but never consumed)**  
`src/computetest/backend.py` · src/computetest/backend.py:440,541 (MockDevice._min_speed_seen) · _api-consistency_ · conf=high · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** _min_speed_seen is updated on every read_link_status but no module ever reads it; linkstate.py computes its own min_speed from the per-poll LinkStatus.speed values, so this field is unused accounting.  
- **Evidence:** grep shows _min_speed_seen written at :541 (d._min_speed_seen = min(d._min_speed_seen, speed)) and initialized at :440, but no reader anywhere in src/ or tests/. linkstate.py:124 does health.min_speed = min(health.min_speed, ls.speed) off the returned LinkStatus, not off this field.  
- **Fix:** Either remove _min_speed_seen, or expose it via a helper if it was intended as a ground-truth cross-check for tests. Harmless as-is; flagged only for clarity.  
- **Verifier:** Independently reproduced: MockDevice._min_speed_seen is initialized at src/computetest/backend.py:440 (_min_speed_seen: int = 99) and written at :541 (d._min_speed_seen = min(d._min_speed_seen, speed)) inside read_link_status, but grep across src/ and tests/ finds ZERO readers — only those two write/init sites. The consumer-facing minimum-speed tracking is computed entirely independently in linkstate.py:124 (health.min_speed = min(health.min_speed, ls.speed)) off the returned LinkStatus.speed, confirming the field is write-only dead state.

It is NOT an intentional ground-truth cross-check: the analogous "ground-truth" field _true_errors IS deliberately exposed via the true_error_count() test helper at backend.py:664-665 (used to verify AER undercounting fidelity), whereas _min_speed_seen has no accessor and no test. So this is most likely a vestigial half-implementation of a parallel cross-check that was never wired up, not deliberate mock-fidelity design.

Severity is correctly a nit (matching the finding's own assessment). This is dead accounting in the Mock simulation layer only — it cannot affect a real-hardware verdict, produce a wrong number, corrupt data, or crash a parser. It is purely cosmetic. The suggested fix (remove it, or expose it via a helper like true_error_count if a ground-truth min-speed cross-check was intended) is reasonable but low value.  

**N44. _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C boundary suppresses a real -40.0C reading (automotive low-temp limit)**  
`src/computetest/tegra.py` · tegra.py:38,150 `_MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C = -40.0` ; `elif t <= _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C:` · _edge-case_ · conf=medium · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** The offline-sensor filter uses `<=`, so a sensor reading exactly -40.0C — a legitimate AEC-Q100 grade-1 low operating temperature — is treated as an offline (-256C) sensor and dropped, rather than only the clearly-offline sentinel values.  
- **Evidence:** `elif t <= _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C: notes.append(... ignored (offline sensor))`. -40.0 <= -40.0 is True, so a valid cold reading at the low limit is excluded along with the -256C offline marker.  
- **Fix:** Use strict `<` (t < _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C) so -40.0C survives, or lower the offline threshold well below the operating range (e.g. -100C) since real offline zones report -256C and there is no legitimate reading between -100C and -40C to worry about.  
- **Verifier:** The mechanism is factually correct: at tegra.py:150 `elif t <= _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C:` with `_MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C = -40.0`, a zone reading of exactly -40.0C satisfies `-40.0 <= -40.0` and is routed to the "ignored (offline sensor)" branch, mislabeled as offline alongside the real -256C sentinel. I reproduced this directly.

But the real-world impact is negligible, so the original "low" overstates it and it should be a nit:

1. No verdict effect in any realistic scenario. The pass/fail logic gates ONLY on the hot end: `max_zone<=max_temp_c` (overtemp) and `no_thermal_throttle` (lines 161,165). `max_zone = max(meaningful)` is set by the HOTTEST zone, so dropping a cold -40.0C value cannot change it. I confirmed with `_lines=['GPU@-40C CPU@55C']`: ok=True, max_zone=55.0 — identical to what strict `<` would give. There is no undertemp/low-temp gate anywhere in the module (grep confirmed), so a dropped cold reading cannot create a wrong PASS or wrong FAIL.

2. The reading is NOT lost. TegraHealth is built with the unfiltered `thermal` dict (line 179 / merged["thermal_c"], line 143), so -40.0C is still preserved in `thermal_c` and surfaced via to_dict()["thermal_c"]. The `meaningful` filter only affects which zone counts toward max_zone/hot_zone.

3. The only scenario where `<=` vs `<` flips a verdict is every die sensor reading exactly -40.0000C simultaneously (then meaningful is empty -> max_zone=0.0 -> FAIL). I reproduced this with `_lines=['GPU@-40C CPU@-40C']` (ok=False), but it is physically implausible: a running SoC under test does not have all zones at the exact float boundary, and the documented purpose of the threshold (lines 35-36) is to suppress the far-away -256C offline sentinel.

So this is a genuine but trivial cosmetic/edge-case nit (note text mislabels a hypothetical exactly-(-40.0C) reading as "offline"); the suggested fix (strict `<`, or lower threshold to ~-100C) is reasonable hygiene but does not fix any verdict error. It does not fake a pass, hide a skip, or mislead a hardware verdict.  

**N45. Completion-percent JSON key is likely wrong ('completion'), so reported percent is always the 100 default on real hw**  
`src/computetest/nvme/health.py` · health.py:176 (_real_self_test_log) · _real-hw-path_ · conf=low · verdict=confirmed  
- **Claim:** `raw.get("completion", 100)` reads a top-level `completion` key. nvme-cli reports the in-progress completion under `current_completion` (the percent-complete of the running operation). With the wrong key the returned `percent` silently defaults to 100 even mid-test. This is cosmetic (percent is not used in the pass/fail decision or by _real_poll_self_test) but mislabels progress in logs.  
- **Evidence:** return {"percent": raw.get("completion", 100), "in_progress": in_progress, ...}  
- **Fix:** Read the actual key: `raw.get("current_completion", raw.get("completion", 0))`; verify against a real capture (see the corpus-test finding).  
- **Verifier:** Verified against /Users/kywwilson/Desktop/Projects/Zoox/toolkit/src/computetest/nvme/health.py:176. The code does read `raw.get("completion", 100)`. This is a genuinely suspect key: line 172 correctly reads the companion field `current_operation` (the canonical nvme-cli snake_case key), and line 173 reads `Self-test Log`, but line 176 reads a bare `completion`. nvme-cli's self-test-log JSON renders the in-progress percent as `current_completion` (the snake_case sibling of `current_operation`), not a top-level `completion`. The mixed naming styles in this function are a tell that the schema was hand-guessed, and there is NO corpus capture for self-test-log to validate it (corpus/nvme has only smart-log.json and id-ctrl.json), while the path is `# pragma: no cover - real-hw`, so nothing catches it. So on real hardware `percent` would silently default to 100 even mid-test. However, the finding's own caveat is correct and load-bearing for severity: I traced every use of `percent` (grep across src/computetest/nvme/) and confirmed it feeds NO verdict — `_real_poll_self_test` (health.py:188-196) inspects only `log["in_progress"]`; `passed` derives from `in_progress` and `result`; `summary()` (health.py:38-48) does not even print it. The value only appears in the returned dict / to_dict() output, i.e. a progress number in logs. There is no wrong PASS/FAIL, no data corruption, no crash. Real but cosmetic, so I downgrade from the finding's 'low' to 'nit'. The suggested fix (`raw.get("current_completion", raw.get("completion", 0))`) is reasonable; defaulting to 0 instead of 100 is also more honest about unknown progress.  

## Completeness-critic gaps (modules flagged for the second pass)

- **spd.py** — 6 findings but the spec byte-offset map is self-validated only against corpus/ddr5_spd/synthetic-ddr5-32gb/expected.json, which appears to have been generated to match the parser — so a wrong JESD400-5 offset cannot be caught by the corpus test. ECC is read from data[13] & 0x08, but in JESD400-5 byte 13 is 'Module Organization' (ranks / device width); the bus-width / ECC extension lives elsewhere (byte 235 region in classic DDR4 SPD; DDR5 relocates it). Density at data[4] & 0x1F and module type at data[3] & 0x0F also need confirmation. On real hardware a wrong ECC-present verdict is a silently wrong PASS for a RAS-critical module attribute. → _recheck:_ Cross-check each offset/bitmask in parse_spd against the actual JESD400-5 (DDR5) SPD layout: byte 2 = DRAM type (0x12 DDR5), byte 3 module type nibble, byte 4 density/banks, and CRITICALLY which byte+bit reports ECC/bus-width-extension. Confirm the corpus expected.json was authored from the spec, not from the parser output. Verify the DENSITY_GBIT table (0..8 -> 0/4/8/12/16/24/32/48/64 Gbit) matches the SDRAM Density and Banks byte encoding.
- **preemption.py** — 5 findings. ADD_FRAG_SIZE_BYTES = {0:64,1:128,2:192,3:256} is presented as the 802.3br/802.1Qbu addFragSize -> minimum non-final fragment payload mapping. The IEEE definition is minimum fragment = (addFragSize+1)*64 octets of the frame, but the conventional minimum-fragment-size values account for the mCRC/overhead (often cited as 60/124/188/252 additional octets, or 64/128/192/256 including header depending on how 'fragment' is measured). check_fragmentation compares f.payload_bytes directly to this floor, so an off-by-4 (mCRC) or header-inclusion mismatch would wrongly pass/fail a real Clause-99 fragmentation conformance run. → _recheck:_ Confirm against IEEE 802.3br / 802.1Qbu-2016 whether addFragSize 0-3 yields minimum NON-FINAL fragment sizes of 64/128/192/256 of the same quantity (payload vs full mPacket) that check_fragmentation measures as f.payload_bytes. Verify the -4 octet mCRC adjustment is correctly included or excluded so the floor comparison is apples-to-apples.
- **ras.py** — 3 findings. CxlRasRegisters hardcodes UE_STATUS=0x00 and CE_STATUS=0x0C. In the CXL.cachemem RAS capability the layout is Uncorrectable Error Status (0x00), Uncorrectable Error Mask (0x04), Uncorrectable Error Severity (0x08), then Correctable Error Status — so 0x0C is plausible but must be confirmed against the CXL 3.x spec offset, not assumed. The bit maps are sourced from rasdaemon ras-cxl-handler.c (comment), but a wrong CE_STATUS offset would clear/read the wrong register on real hardware (here the mock uses the same constant so tests are self-consistent and cannot catch it). → _recheck:_ Verify UE_STATUS / CE_STATUS offsets and the full UE/CE bit-name maps against the CXL RAS capability register layout in the CXL spec (and confirm rasdaemon's bit numbering matches). Confirm 0x0C is CE Status and not UE Severity.
- **messages.py** — 7 findings (highest count). MessageType enum values (CONTROL_PRIMITIVE=0x00, NVME_MI_COMMAND=0x01, NVME_ADMIN_COMMAND=0x02, PCIE_COMMAND=0x04, AEM=0x05) and the NMP byte packing in encode_mi_request (MT bits0-3, CSI bit4, SLOTID bits5-6) are concrete spec-field claims. decode/encode are inverse-tested against each other, so a wrong bit position or NMIMT value would round-trip cleanly in tests yet mis-frame a real wire message. Also _crc32c uses reflected poly 0x82F63B78 with init/final 0xFFFFFFFF and little-endian MIC packing — verify byte order of the MIC matches NVMe-MI. → _recheck:_ Confirm NMIMT field values and NMP bit layout (MT/CSI/SLOTID) against NVMe-MI 1.x/2.x spec section 3. Independently verify _crc32c against a known CRC-32C test vector (e.g. '123456789' -> 0xE3069283) and confirm the MIC is transmitted little-endian per spec.
- **mctp.py** — 4 findings, but the audit kind says 'CRC, packetization, message assembly' — this file contains NONE of those: no FCS/CRC, no packetization into MTU-sized fragments, no reassembly. It is only endpoint-ID/message-type framing types plus an in-memory mock. Either the real packetization+CRC logic lives in an un-audited location, or the module's claimed responsibility (and the MctpMessage.__len__ '+1 for type byte') is incomplete relative to DSP0236. A missing/mislocated packetization+FCS path is a real-hw correctness gap. → _recheck:_ Locate where MCTP packetization (SOM/EOM, packet sequence number, per-packet header, MTU fragmentation) and the per-binding FCS/CRC actually live. If they don't exist, confirm that is intentional (mock-only transport) and that nothing claims DSP0236 packet-level conformance it doesn't implement.
- **inject.py** — 6 findings. inject_aer success detection (line 123) treats any non-empty HMP output not starting with 'OK' as failure. QEMU's pcie_aer_inject_error HMP command typically prints NOTHING on success and an error string on failure; it does not emit 'OK id: ...' the way some other HMP commands do. If real QEMU prints an informational non-error line, this raises a false QMPError; if it prints nothing on a real failure mode, errors slip through. This is the live real-path injection client whose fidelity gates the whole QEMU AER lane. → _recheck:_ Verify against qemu-system 11.0 exactly what pcie_aer_inject_error prints on success vs failure (the docstring claims 'OK id: <id>'). Confirm the FakeQMPServer in tests/test_qmp_inject.py models the REAL success/failure output, not an idealized 'OK' that masks the parsing assumption. Also re-check that injecting a raw status with bit positions matches QEMU's expected error_status encoding.
- **backend.py** — 5 findings. Two real-hw concerns inside non-mock paths: (1) _encoding_efficiency returns 242/256 for Gen6 PAM4+FLIT/FEC as 'approximate payload efficiency' — link_bits_per_second feeds ber.py's time_estimate and the mock's lambda accrual, so a wrong Gen6 number skews bit-count math; verify 242/256 is the intended FLIT payload efficiency. (2) RealBackend.link_chain derives the PCIe path purely from BDF-shaped components in os.path.realpath of the sysfs node — confirm sysfs realpath actually nests parent bridges as path components on modern kernels, else the AER chain walk silently returns just the endpoint and misses upstream links. → _recheck:_ Confirm Gen6 242/256 efficiency intent and that GEN6_X16_BPS isn't used where a strict spec number is claimed. Validate RealBackend.link_chain's realpath assumption against a real /sys/bus/pci/devices/<bdf> symlink target structure (or the fixture tree) and read_port_type's PCIE_CAP_FLAGS>>4 nibble extraction.
- **gpu.py** — 4 findings. Real-hw parser _query_nvidia_smi positionally indexes f[0..10] from a CSV split on ','; nvidia-smi's clocks_throttle_reasons.active is parsed as hex via int(f[10],16). Two risks on real hardware: (1) GPU names containing a comma (rare but possible) shift every positional index; (2) _query_row_remap's grab('Correctable Error')+grab('Uncorrectable Error') for row_remap_count assumes those exact labels in -q -d ROW_REMAPPER output and sums them — verify the label strings and that summing CE+UE remap counts is the intended 'remapped rows' metric, not double counting. → _recheck:_ Verify nvidia-smi --format=csv,noheader,nounits never emits an embedded comma for the queried fields (or split with a limit), confirm clocks_throttle_reasons.active is reported in hex, and confirm the ROW_REMAPPER label strings ('Pending','Failure Occurred','Correctable Error','Uncorrectable Error') and that summing them is correct.
- **tegra.py** — 5 findings, newest module. The real collection path is pragma-no-cover but the regex parsers run on real DUT output. _RAIL_RE = [A-Z][A-Z0-9_]{2,}\s+(\d+)/(\d+)(?=\s|$) is meant to catch power rails in mW but the lookahead-vs-MB exclusion is fragile across JetPack versions; _TEMP_RE captures any '<word>@<num>C' which on Orin includes spare zones reading -256C (handled by _MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C) but throttle_inferred = max_zone >= throttle_temp_c with default 97C and PMIC@100C ignored — verify a real hot zone can't be silently dropped or a dummy zone gate the verdict. gpu_temp = thermal.get('GPU') or thermal.get('tj') or max_zone uses 'or', so a legitimately-0.0... actually 0C GPU reading would fall through. → _recheck:_ Confirm _RAIL_RE/_TEMP_RE against real tegrastats output from the target JetPack/L4T version (field names and units vary), verify _IGNORE_ZONES/_MIN_PLAUSIBLE_C don't suppress a real hot sensor, and check the 'gpu_temp = GPU or tj or max_zone' falsy-0 edge.
- **health.py** — 6 findings. Real-hw subprocess parser of nvme-cli smart-log/self-test JSON with a Kelvin->Celsius conversion and JSON key mapping called out as risk areas. A wrong Kelvin offset (temperature is in Kelvin in the SMART log) or a mis-mapped JSON key would yield a silently wrong temperature or health verdict on real drives; this is a no-cover path so unit tests won't catch a units bug. → _recheck:_ Verify Kelvin->C conversion (subtract 273, not 273.15 per NVMe spec convention) and the exact nvme-cli JSON key names for composite/sensor temperatures, percentage_used, critical_warning bits, and self-test result codes. Confirm critical_warning bit decode matches the NVMe SMART/Health Information log.
- **ocp_telemetry.py** — 7 findings (highest count, tied). OCP telemetry decode is a binary/structured real-hw parser with the most flagged issues; struct offsets, field widths, and endianness in an OCP datacenter NVMe telemetry/internal-log decode are exactly the class of bug a mock corpus can mask if the corpus was generated to match the decoder. → _recheck:_ Validate every struct offset/width/endianness against the OCP NVMe Cloud SSD spec (and the OCP internal-log format), and confirm the corpus expected values were derived from the spec or a real device dump rather than from the decoder itself. Check for silent truncation or sign-extension on multi-byte counters.
- **diagnostics.py** — 4 findings. This is the verdict-aggregation orchestrator (diagnose_all/diagnose_chain, skip vs fail). Per the honesty discipline, the highest-value defect class here is a skip being counted as a pass or a per-link/per-device FAIL not propagating to the aggregate verdict. Aggregation logic that ORs/ANDs many sub-verdicts is easy to get subtly wrong (e.g. empty result set -> vacuous PASS, or a skipped check masking a real fail). → _recheck:_ Trace how diagnose_all/diagnose_chain fold individual verdicts: confirm an empty set or all-skipped set does NOT yield PASS, that any FAIL on any link in a chain fails the chain, and that 'skip' is reported distinctly and never silently upgraded to pass. Verify exit-code mapping in cli.py consumes this correctly.

## Module health summaries

- **backend.py** (4 kept) — Solid HAL: BDF validation, AER offsets/bits, LnkSta decode, link-rate physics, and W1C/latch undercount modeling are spec-correct and well-tested; only minor mock-fidelity issues (burst histogram over-counts; fatal-vs-nonfatal collapse) and a couple of cosmetic/dead-state items remain, none of which affect a real-hardware PASS/FAIL verdict.
- **bert.py** (2 kept) — Verdict/counting logic and AER reuse are correct and well-tested; the one material issue is that on Gen6+ links the post-FEC FBER (the module's own stated "actual reliability signal") is collected but never feeds the verdict, so a catastrophic Gen6 link can report PASS / exit 0.
- **ber.py** (0 kept) — Clean: all Poisson/chi-squared statistics are exactly correct vs scipy, the pure-Python gamma fallback matches to machine precision across all regimes, spec/units claims are honest, and the tests are strong (no tautologies). No material defects found.
- **aer.py** (3 kept) — W1C clear logic, AER register offsets, and PCIe bit maps are spec-correct and the latch/undercount semantics are faithful; the one real defect is that AerSnapshot decodes Device-Status fallback data through the AER bit tables, blanking/mislabeling the operator-facing error names (verdict stays correct, evidence does not).
- **margining.py** (1 kept) — Logic, spec constant (ECAP 0x0027), UI/step math, and honesty (real paths raise instead of faking) are sound; one real mock-fidelity bug masks marginal NARROW links, plus two minor edge/test-quality notes.
- **diagnostics.py** (3 kept) — Single-device verdict aggregation (skip vs fail vs pass) is sound and well-tested; the whole-chain diagnostic has two real honesty/correctness gaps — an unmeasurable upstream segment is silently reported PASS, and per-link downgrade is not checked against the link's maximum when no expectation is supplied (the single-device path catches both).
- **linkstate.py** (2 kept) — Logic and verdict math are sound and fail-safe on unknown reads, but the LBMS/LABS bandwidth-change watch reads the latch at the endpoint BDF where PCIe defines those bits as RsvdZ — so the module's headline feature (catching a recovered mid-soak downgrade) silently never fires on real endpoint hardware.
- **topology.py** (3 kept) — Chain decomposition and enumeration logic are sound, but load_config crashes on the documented bdf match key, and greedy expectation matching can flip a verdict to a spurious FAIL.
- **cli.py** (4 kept) — Dispatch/exit-mapping is mostly sound and well-tested, but two real verdict-honesty gaps (ber --bits "continue" reported as PASS in both exit code and ocp-diag; --via-pci-lmt always exits PASS) and one operator-facing traceback (bad --ocpdiag path escapes the error mapping) need fixing.
- **harness.py** (3 kept) — The runner is logically sound for the happy path, but a single raising check on real hardware aborts the entire plan and leaves a stale "running" heartbeat — the orchestrator does not isolate per-target failures the way diagnostics._try_margin deliberately does.
- **results.py** (1 kept) — SQL is fully parameterized (no injection) and lifecycle/summary math is correct; the one material defect is that non-finite measured floats (inf/NaN, which BERT skips legitimately produce) are stored and re-served as non-RFC-8259 JSON tokens that break standards-strict consumers like the dashboard browser.
- **fixtures.py** (5 kept) — Functionally sound registry, but the IDN "wrong-box" guard it advertises is only a manufacturer check — it silently accepts a wrong same-vendor model and is silently disabled by a leading-comma typo; open_all leaks sessions on a mid-sequence failure.
- **dmesg.py** (2 kept) — Severity classification is sound for the common AER/bus-error formats, but link-down detection is inconsistently cased (filter case-sensitive, classifier case-insensitive) creating a real missed-fail risk, plus a same-second dedup undercount from the --ctime + set-diff choice and an ARI function-number regex gap.
- **memory.py** (2 kept) — Overall pass/fail verdicts are correct, but the real-hw EDAC parser never reads per-DIMM UE and silently degrades to no per-DIMM data on legacy-layout kernels, weakening the module's central "actionable RMA" promise; plus a docstring overstates that stress re-reads counters.
- **ethernet.py** (6 kept) — Pure parsers and CAN path are solid and well-tested, but the real-hw TDR cable-test parser has two field-breaking bugs (decimal distance truncation + multiline-output miss) and the Ethernet role parser reports configured preference instead of negotiated status — all in untested # pragma real-hw paths.
- **gpu.py** (2 kept) — Solid module; throttle-bit map, temp/replay/link checks and XID regex are all correct, but one XID label is wrong per NVIDIA's table and the real-hw nvidia-smi parsers have a couple of silent-miss / index-crash robustness gaps.
- **tegra.py** (4 kept) — Parser and verdict logic are mostly sound and well-tested, but the gpu_temp_c fallback uses truthiness (drops a legitimate 0C or reports an offline -256C GPU), and the pass-check's "0 < max_zone" guard spuriously FAILS sub-zero-but-plausible readings that the code itself admits as valid down to -40C.
- **lmt_adapter.py** (4 kept) — Schema-parity is the module's whole purpose, but two emitted columns (sample_count / sample_count_bits) are inverted and mis-computed vs. the OCP pci_lmt reference, and max_voltage_offset is mislabeled/out-of-range; these would corrupt any pipeline that ingests computetest and real pci_lmt runs interchangeably.
- **instruments.py** (4 kept) — Solid mock/real split and correct SCPI formatting; main defects are int(inf) OverflowError leaks in two real-hw count parsers and a BER that reads 0.0 when the bits counter overflows — all violate the module's documented "every fault surfaces as InstrumentError" / overflow-sentinel discipline.
- **ocpdiag.py** (3 kept) — Schema-faithful OCP v2.0 JSONL emitter; field names/enums/required-keys all conform and tests are strict. One real honesty gap: run_end can only ever emit status=COMPLETE, so a test-program error is reported as a DUT FAIL; plus a few low-severity robustness/test-coverage gaps.
- **base.py** (3 kept) — Contract is well-formed and the EyeMeasurement/EqLevels/eye_quality_verdict reuse by CXL/DDR5/GMSL is correct; the one real concern is lane_status() reporting a structurally-always-zero error_count while its docstring claims an error read, which could mislead a field operator.
- **aries.py** (2 kept) — Clean, honest SDK-gated boundary stub — no subprocess/parse logic to misfire; lane bounds checked before SDK open, part metadata matches Astera's real lineup, every telemetry path raises NotImplementedError rather than faking telemetry. Only material issue is a docstring that points the wire-up engineer at a method/env-var that do not exist.
- **health.py** (4 kept) — Mostly sound; the self-test codes, Kelvin offset (matches nvme-cli convention), and key-aliasing are correct, but the temperature lower-bound and missing-temp default produce a false-FAIL verdict, and the spare-threshold normalization is dead code (spec-correct comparison is never made).
- **ocp_telemetry.py** (3 kept) — Decode/emit logic is correct and spec log-IDs (07h/C9h) are right; the real-hw parser has unguarded int() casts and a possibly-wrong block-summing semantic, and the emit path hardcodes a PASS verdict that ignores recorded fatal errors.
- **mctp.py** (4 kept) — Framing/EID dataclasses and a mock transport are sound mechanically, but the MCTP Endpoint-ID allocation invents a non-existent 0xF0-0xFE "reserved" band (wrong spec value that mislabels valid EIDs), the test bakes that error in, and several inline spec citations point at the wrong DMTF document.
- **messages.py** (4 kept) — CRC-32C and decode bounds are correct, but the on-wire NVMe-MI frame layout is structurally wrong vs the spec/libnvme: missing the fixed MCTP/IC type byte, message-type packed into the wrong byte, and CSI/SlotID at the wrong bit positions — frames will not interoperate with real hardware.
- **aem.py** (3 kept) — Compact, mostly-correct AEM mock state machine, but coalescing is implemented globally (last-event-only) which contradicts the module's own per-event-type contract and silently lets duplicate events through when types interleave; tests never exercise that path.
- **vpd.py** (1 kept) — Bounds logic, spec opcodes, FRU header byte, and tests are all correct; the one real concern is a deliberate package-wide pattern where mock=False without a transport silently returns fabricated mock VPD data instead of raising.
- **controller_health.py** (1 kept) — Logic is internally consistent and the mock/real split is honest (real path raises rather than fakes a pass); main concerns are a CHSP-vs-SMART spec-modeling mismatch, pending events not affecting the verdict, and untested healthy() failure branches.
- **subsystem_health.py** (3 kept) — Logic is internally consistent and the mock path is correct, but the data model bakes in a Kelvin assumption that contradicts the NSHSP wire format (signed Celsius), and the healthy verdict drops the numeric available_spare cross-check that the sibling controller poll enforces — both will mislead real-hardware testing.
- **reset.py** (2 kept) — Clean code mechanically, but the docstring/operator-note semantics are self-contradictory and the RSF enum is mislabeled against NVMe-MI §5.7, with the sole backing test being tautological for a "Mandatory" conformance row.
- **serdes.py** (1 kept) — Clean, faithful mirror of pcie/retimer/base.py; mode-aware lock, PAM4 three-eye EOM, dual-rate PRBS and ERRB modeling are all correct. Only two low/nit-severity labeling/edge issues; no verdict-affecting or spec defects.
- **checks.py** (3 kept) — Mostly sound verdict layer with correct ber/safety reuse, but check_eom bypasses the per-instance EOM thresholds the SerDesLink API exposes — a real wrong-PASS path on tightened hardware margins.
- **video.py** (5 kept) — Single-link check_gmsl is sound; the multi-link check_deserializer real-hw path is materially weaker than its mock — it reads one shared sysfs node per link (no per-link discrimination) and its frame-sync check degenerates to "all locked", so a real desync silently passes.
- **channel.py** (4 kept) — The mask/raise honesty discipline is sound (masks=None, check refuses to pass), but the headline orchestrator silently skips the two behaviors the module advertises (the GMSL3 filter split and the PoC carve-out), and a saturated/overflow RL point passes silently — both can mislead a real-hardware channel-compliance verdict.
- **lmt.py** (2 kept) — Honest, faithful PCIe-LMT reuse with no faked passes; main real-world risk is that FORWARD/REVERSE EOM rows collide on every identifier column, and eye openings beyond the PCIe-derived max-offset ceilings saturate the step and lose resolution at the healthy end.
- **shmoo.py** (3 kept) — Algorithm and verdict logic are honest (empty sweep fails, unlocked corner reads worst_eye=0.0), but two real-hardware defects: positional indexing of lock_status() can verdict the wrong link, and no chamber/rail settle before reading attributes eye margin to the wrong corner.
- **phy.py** (3 kept) — Clean, pure-Python ABC + deterministic Mock (no real-hw/subprocess paths); SQI/linkup/TDR/PRBS logic is correct, but the PRBS `locked` heuristic is exposed with no BER verdict wrapper and TdrResult.ok treats any non-"fault" status as OK — a few low/medium mock-fidelity and test-tautology issues, no critical/high defects.
- **gptp.py** (0 kept) — Math and emit are correct, but the verdict computes max|TE| over the ENTIRE window including the pre-lock acquisition transient, which contradicts the 802.1AS/Avnu "hold ±80ns after lock" criterion and can produce a wrong FAIL on real hardware; secondary gaps in window-adequacy and lock_acq_s validation.
- **gptp_proto.py** (3 kept) — One real spec bug: the mean-link-delay formula applies neighborRateRatio to the wrong interval; masked by all tests using rate_ratio=1.0, but wrong by ~100 ns on real hardware at the allowed ±200 ppm. BMCA tiebreak omits a 1588 comparison field.
- **qbv.py** (3 kept) — Two real spec-conformance bugs in the timing math: guard-band false-positives when a TC gate stays open across adjacent GCL entries, and signed edge-jitter that lets large "early" excursions pass — both undetected by the tests.
- **preemption.py** (4 kept) — Core mechanism logic (addFragSize mapping, verifyTime range, non-final-fragment floor, SMD sequencing) is spec-correct; the load-bearing defect is the unsupported 0.8x Respond-window criterion presented as Clause-99 conformance, which can wrongly FAIL a standards-conformant device.
- **frer.py** (3 kept) — Non-wrapping window logic is correct, but the recovery function ignores the 16-bit R-TAG sequence-number wrap defined by IEEE 802.1CB, so a continuously-running stream that wraps produces a false FAIL; one verdict check is structurally dead.
- **gptp_hotstandby.py** (2 kept) — Mechanically sound and reuses bmca_elect correctly, but the failover TE metric is one-sided max|TE| while its own budget is documented as peak-to-peak — under-reporting excursions can yield a wrong PASS; empty TE samples also silently pass.
- **pma.py** (3 kept) — Module logic is clean and honest (masks mandatory, empty traces fail-safe, correct RETURN_LOSS direction for both RL and mode conversion); the only material issues are test-quality gaps in the suite, not defects in the production code.
- **cable.py** (2 kept) — Logic is correct and the ethernet.py reuse is faithful; the only material issues are test-quality: the honesty-critical "skipped" path is documented but completely untested (MockPhy can't emit it), and fault_distance_m can KeyError on a real PHY backend.
- **adapter.py** (0 kept) — Small, correct mirror of lmt_adapter: subprocess calls are shell-safe list-form, pmc/ttworkbench argv matches the cited tools, TSN_COLUMNS exactly matches the dataclass field order; only minor caller-dependent edge cases (unvalidated verdict string, non-strict JSON NaN) and a thin real-hw-path test surface.
- **ras.py** (0 kept) — Constants and ABI semantics (ECS thresholds, persist_mode, mem_repair fields) are correct and faithful to the Linux 6.15 EDAC sysfs; the issues are mock-fidelity gaps (finite hPPR pool never depletes; live-reference aliasing on read_ecs/read_scrub; scrub accumulators survive power_cycle) that could let a real over-allocation or diff bug pass undetected, plus a sparing/PPR conflation. No outright faked PASS.
- **ecc.py** (1 kept) — Core CE/UE delta logic is correct against the mock and the reuse of MockDdr5Ras is consistent; the material issues are real-hardware verdict risks where the checks are stricter/looser than real EDAC semantics, plus a docstring safety claim the UE path does not actually verify.
- **ecs.py** (1 kept) — Logic is small and correct for what it checks, but the ECS verdict can return ok=True on a clearly-failing DIMM: the corrected-error count and max-error row are read and reported yet never gated, and a disabled/zero-duration scrub passes the 24h budget check.
- **ppr.py** (2 kept) — Logic is correct for the common 0/1 case and the verdict is sound on real hardware, but the hPPR resource gate is global-not-per-bank (real-hw false-PASS risk), persist_mode is unvalidated (silent sPPR mislabel), and the mock/tests decouple resource exhaustion from repair success so a no-resource hPPR is never exercised against an actually-failed repair.
- **lmt.py** (2 kept) — Adapter correctly reuses the PCIe lmt step-mapping and record schema; main issues are metadata/spec flags that overclaim "independent left/right / up/down" semantics the single-value DDR5 data doesn't back, plus thin tests that wouldn't catch unit/scale regressions. No false PASS/FAIL.
- **eye.py** (0 kept) — Logic and retimer reuse are correct; main concerns are a "DFE check" promised in the title/docstring that is never actually verdicted, and tests too coarse to catch a UI/mV axis swap.
- **crc.py** (2 kept) — Clean, honest config-plumbing verifier with correct PASS/FAIL semantics; the only real gap is that a negative/sentinel retry count slips through the budget check, plus a couple of minor test-strength nits.
- **rfm.py** (2 kept) — Small, pure boolean enablement/alert-path checker; scope-honesty claim (no disturbance stress) holds, but the "not-required" branch silently rewrites per-check values to True so serialized reports can claim RFM/PRAC are enabled when they are actually disabled, and that branch's policy parameter is untested.
- **spd.py** (5 kept) — Parser is structurally sound and the DDR5 key byte / module-type / density decodes match JESD400-5, but the ECC field is read from the DDR4 byte-13 location instead of the DDR5 byte-235 bus-width-extension field, so the RAS "ecc" verdict is wrong on real DIMMs — and the corpus is hand-built to ratify the bug.
- **soak.py** (0 kept) — Clean: a thin, correct RAS-state snapshot wrapper over MockDdr5Ras; reuse is correct in arity/field names, no spec/units/parse/subprocess surface, no material defects found.
- **mailbox.py** (2 kept) — Clean, faithful mirror of the NVMe-MI MCTP mock pattern; the only material issues are two wrong CXL CCI spec constants (Get Partition Info opcode and Busy return code) that a real-hw backend would import and mis-encode/mis-decode — both are docstring-disclaimed and untested, so honest but still incorrect.
- **ras.py** (3 kept) — W1C clear/snapshot logic is sound and faithfully mirrors aer.py, but the Uncorrectable Error Status bit map is off-by-two for bits 12+ (InternalError/IDE Tx/IDE Rx mislabeled and IDE-Rx silently dropped), and the tests bake in the wrong mapping so they cannot catch it.
- **link.py** (3 kept) — Logic is sound for a pure-data verdict function, but the flit-mode check uses exact-equality while speed/width use >=, so the default expectation FAIL-verdicts a link that negotiated the better latency-optimized flit; a couple of edge cases (no spec gating of 256B-at-low-speed, FlitMode value collision risk) are lower-impact.
- **events.py** (0 kept) — Small, clean in-memory CXL event Get/Clear model; spec encodings correct and design consistent with sibling modules — main issues are a vacuous-PASS on an empty clear request and a deliberate-but-undocumented divergence from CXL's Invalid-Handle semantics.
- **poison.py** (2 kept) — In-memory model is internally consistent, but the poison round-trip verdict is structurally tautological (can never FAIL on the Mock), the PoisonSource enum values diverge from the CXL spec Error-Source encoding, and clear() ignores length so it cannot model partial/length-scoped poison clears.
- **hdm.py** (1 kept) — HPA->DPA math is correct, but calc_interleave_pos is documented/tested in the WRONG chain direction relative to the kernel it mirrors (silent wrong position), and hpa_to_dpa crashes on a zero granularity/ways read from real hardware.
- **emit.py** (3 kept) — Link/RAS projections are correct and faithfully mirror the PCIe emit pattern, but the events projection unconditionally emits a PASS verdict even when Fatal/Failure device-event records are present, and the RAS fail message can be empty for undecoded UE bits.
- **device.py** (2 kept) — Coherency classifier is mostly correct, but it wrongly requires HDM-D[B] + BISnoop on Type-1 (cache-only, no device memory) devices, producing a FAIL verdict on spec-conformant hardware; the test suite hides this by feeding Type-1 physically-impossible HDM=True inputs.
- **maintenance.py** (1 kept) — Verdict logic is sound and the honest "only confirmed codes" posture is correct; the material gaps are fidelity/consistency: the model uses bespoke strings instead of the sibling mailbox CciReturnCode framing it claims to verify, and the whole flow is a closed Mock-tests-Mock loop with no spec or real-hw linkage.
- **retimer.py** (3 kept) — Reuse of the Retimer ABC is correct, but an empty explicit lanes list produces a false PASS (eye check vacuously true with zero lanes measured), and the eye-threshold path silently ignores the retimer's per-instance set_eye_thresholds while temperature still honors set_tj_max — an internal inconsistency.
- **gage_rr.py** (3 kept) — Mathematically sound crossed-ANOVA Gage R&R: EMS divisors recover known variance components, SS decomposition is exact, percentages use the correct AIAG study-variation (sigma-ratio) form and quadrature-sum to 100%; only minor issues are a doc/code boundary discrepancy and a test-quality gap (no pinned reference ANOVA table despite a docstring claim).
- **station_correlation.py** (4 kept) — Math is largely correct (Deming point estimate, Bland-Altman, percentile interpolation all verified against numpy/reference), but a degenerate-resample handling gap in the Deming bootstrap produces a wrong PASS verdict at the documented minimum sample size (n=3).
- **calibration_registry.py** (3 kept) — Small, correct core logic; one real runtime crash path when YAML emits datetime timestamps, plus a misleading "graceful fallback" claim and a minor file-handle leak.
- **inject.py** (3 kept) — Solid, faithful QMP/AER injection client: bit constants, flag construction, and OK/error parsing all match the PCIe spec, the C engine, and real QEMU semantics; only minor edge-case/test-coverage gaps remain.
- **cxl_inject.py** (3 kept) — Three of four QMP commands are spec-correct, but inject_general_media_event omits four QMP-required fields and is hard-rejected by real QEMU 11.0.0, and the test that "covers" it cannot catch this because the fake server never validates arguments.
- **__init__.py** (0 kept) — Clean re-export hub: import set and __all__ are exactly symmetric (32/32), every name resolves, no duplicates or leaked attrs, and all 48 ddr5 tests that import the public surface pass; no material defects.
- **__init__.py** (1 kept) — Clean re-export module: all 53 __all__ names resolve, no duplicates/missing, imports exactly match __all__, each symbol resolves to its claimed submodule, and docstring mirror claims (ras→aer.py W1C, mailbox→NVMe-MI MctpTransport) are accurate; only a minor docstring-completeness nit.
- **__init__.py** (0 kept) — Clean public-API re-export surface: __all__ is exhaustive and consistent with the imports, every name resolves, and the surface is exercised by tests; no material defects found.
- **__init__.py** (1 kept) — Re-exports are correct and import cleanly; one API-consistency defect — 5 deliberately imported DEFAULT_* constants are omitted from __all__, breaking star-import and deviating from every sibling package's own convention.
- **__init__.py** (2 kept) — Clean re-export module: all __all__ names resolve, no missing/duplicate/leaked symbols, star-import matches __all__ exactly, spec-section/opcode annotations consistent with submodules, all 49 tests pass; only minor doc-completeness nits.
- **__init__.py** (0 kept) — Clean: the package re-export surface is fully consistent — all 13 __all__ symbols exist, no missing/extra/duplicate exports, and the test suite validates the complete set.
